S77 182- V. 2 utitiibutrdibuterubuurlurdetedlbiirbiede GENERAL PRINCIPLES. PART THREE. 7N8 CHAPTER ONE. DE LA GREFFE. The art of grafting has been known since antiquity, but the name of its inventor has not come down to us. It is likely that chance provided the first example, and that mankind merely perfected this discovery. The branches of two similar trees," says Dumont de Courcet (1), "intertwined and tightened immediately. The wind that agitated them tore and removed their bark at the point of their junction; the sap reunited the two parts together, and one of the two changed into the nature of the other. Whether you then cut off one of these branches below their junction, and all those on the stem of the other, you may have been astonished to see one almond tree bearing the peaches of its neighbor, or the latter producing almonds. Thus, we can believe that the grafting approach is the first (1) Le Botaniste cultivateur. 2. M734939 velles. 3 Whether this is the case or not, it is no less true that one of the world's most ancient peoples, the Phoenicians, knew this operation, by which the nature of almost all plants can be changed at will. They passed on their knowledge of grafting to the Carthaginians and Greeks. The Romans received it from the latter and spread it to Europe, where it first made great progress, but was soon lost almost entirely, because no one knew how to apply it properly, and it was regarded more as a curious and amusing experiment than as something useful. In fact, if we take the trouble to read what ancient authors had to say about it (1), we'll see that they only suspected the immense advantages that could be derived from it. It was La Quintinie who was the first of the modern authors to give a glimpse of the importance that grafting could have in agriculture: his work caused a great sensation, and the art of grafting became fashionable. It was then thought that all we had to do was to subject all trees, whatever their nature, to this operation, to metamorphose them into fruit trees, and turn our forests into immense orchards. These dashed hopes soon led to a second complete abandonment of this (1) Among Greek authors, Aristotle, Theophrastus and Xenophon; Magon among the Carthaginians; Virgil, Pliny, Varron, Constantine Caesar and Columella among the Romans; in more recent times, Kuffner, Agricola and Sikler in Germany; more recently still, Miller, Bradeley and Forsyth in England; Olivier de Serre, La Quintinie, Duhamel, Rosier, Cabanis, etc., etc., etc., in France. My first master and friend, the venerable M. Thouin, whom death has just snatched from the sciences and his many friends, has given the public, in the Annales du Musée, a monograph dos grefses in which he has recorded the progress of this astonishing art. And Baron Tschudy, in publishing his method of herbaceous grafting, has completed the history of this means of multiplying varieties. Such is the nomeuclature of ancient and modern authors who have given really interesting details on this subject. method, as had already happened eight or nine hundred years before. A graft," says M. Thouin, "is a living plant part which, when joined to another, or inserted into it, identifies with it, and grows there as on its natural foot when the analogy between the individuals is sufficient. It follows from this definition, which is perfectly accurate, that the art of grafting aims to change, according to the will of the person performing the operation, the trunk, or just the branches of one plant, into the trunk or branches of another plant; at least this is what is most commonly intended. But the results are extremely varied, and it is perhaps for this reason that this method of multiplication has so many charms for all those who devote part of their lives to cultivation, and who make it their sweetest enjoyment. Indeed, what more appealing spectacle for the thinking man, than that of a wild nature, producing at first only lacklustre flowers and bitter fruits, suddenly obeying the powerful hand that directs it, and almost immediately adorning itself with the most brilliant gifts of Flora, and delicious fruits. There's no denying that grafting, through which these miracles are performed, is one of the most lovable and useful conquests that art has made over nature. By grafting, we can preserve and multiply varieties and sub-varieties obtained by chance or by artificial fertilization, when seedlings would be powerless to reproduce them. This is how the amateur can be sure that his collections will always include those beautiful roses which are admired by all who see them, and those magnificent fruits whose exquisite flavour rivals the most seductive color. Even when these precious varieties can be multiplied by other means, we still have recourse to this one to ensure and, above all, hasten our enjoyment. If a disease, or a bizarre accident, changes the nature of a plant to a certain extent, but in a curious or pleasing way, it's through the use of chaff that we fix this monstrosity, and perpetuate it. This is why we have trees with variegated or laciniate leaves, with semi-double, double or full flowers; with fruits remarkable for their flavor, their colors or their singularities. Mossy roses with prolific lettuce or hemp leaves; laciniate, variegated or spotted maples; hermaphroditic orange trees, cluster cherry trees and a hundred other anomalies, each more singular than the last, cannot be preserved or multiplied in any other way. Grafting also has the invaluable advantage of hastening fruiting by several years. If an amateur notices in his nursery a young tree without thorns, whose leaves are developing more fully, and whose buds are closer together than in others of the same species, he can look forward to obtaining a new fruit; but this flattering expectation will not be realized, or disappointed, until the plant has reached ten to fifteen years of age (the average time for a frank tree to bear fruit); and this long wait makes a result sometimes of little value pay dearly. So what does he do? He cuts a branch from his young pupil, grafts it onto an old plant; and the second, or at the latest the third year, he can judge the merit of his new acquisition. And there's more. Although we have said that grafting preserves varieties, it does even more: it perfects them. The more an ornamental tree or shrub is grafted, the more its flower increases in volume and brilliance; this improvement is less noticeable in fruit, but it does exist. The good thing about fruit is that it increases their quality the first time it is grafted, and facilitates ripening by accelerating the time of ripening. The phenomenon of graft recovery is easily explained by physiologists. Gems, they say, are rudimens of buds, just as seeds are those of complete individuals; since the former have the faculty of making themselves clean and assimilating to their nature the fluids supplied to them by foreign roots, grafting will take place whenever the vessels designed to carry these fluids from the root to the branches are not clogged in one of their parts, and the nourishing juices can easily circulate from the subject to the graft. To achieve this, the truncated part of the graft's vessels must be in precise contact with the truncated part of the subject's vessels; the orifices of these vessels must be positively pressed together, so that the sap can pass from one to the other without encountering obstacles. The nourishing liquors deposit, as they pass over the wound, a sufficient quantity of organic matter to weld the edges of the wound; the overabundance passes into the bud, which it develops, and recovery takes place. In the absence of discerning and accurate observation, it was once believed, and authors are still printing today (1), that "whatever the method (of grafting) used, it is always a question of uniting the liber of the two individuals". The liber (first bark joining the sapwood) plays no more essential a role in the success of this operation than any other part of a plant in which nourishing fluids circulate. We ask these authors whether they have ever seen grafts of fruit, leaves or herbaceous stems performed with complete success; they (1) See L'HORTICELTEUR FRANÇAIS. O° know that these parts have neither bark nor bast: so how can it be the bast that has brought about the recovery? In their organization, plants offer us a phenomenon that's easy to observe: they have parts, which we'll call living, in which reside all the principles of vegetation or growth; sap, through extremely fine and untidy channels, circulates to feed them: so they're the only ones that grow by their own energy. This is particularly true of leaves, fruiting bodies and bark, as well as the stems of herbaceous succulents. Woody plants have dead parts that take no growth by their own energy; these are sapwood and wood. For example, if a tree trunk grows, it owes this only to the overabundance of fluids produced by the bark. The quantity in excess of what is needed to nourish the living parts extravasates between the wood and the bast, thickens there, and becomes the so-called cambium, which soon hardens, lignifies and forms a new layer of sapwood. If it finds an opening through which it can pass to the outer surface of the bark, it coagulates by contact with the air, and forms new gems, which soon develop into vigorous branches, or roots if placed in favorable circumstances. If we thought we had to assign to the graft a particular principle that acts to revive it, we would say that this principle is nothing other than the cambium, or, if we prefer, the sap that has reached a certain degree of thickening; and by this we would explain all species of grafts, since sap is in circulation in all living parts. We conclude that the best way to ensure the recovery of a graft is: 1° to place it on a plant in the part where the sap, being more elaborate than elsewhere, has the greatest tendency to organize itself; 2° to make the subject's sap vessels coincide as closely as possible with those of the graft; 3° to choose, for grafting, the period when the sap also has the greatest propensity to organize itself. However, even with all these precautions, we still won't succeed in carrying out the recovery unless the two individuals we subject to this experiment have a certain degree of analogy between them, which we haven't yet been able to calculate. Some authors say that, to ensure the success of this operation, it must be carried out on two congeneric subjects: but what do they mean by congeneric subjects? That's what we don't understand, and even what they'd be at a loss to explain. As is all too common today, they avoid a difficulty by equivocation, and this method ends up confusing science to such an extent that its progress is singularly retarded. We have carried out many experiments on this subject, which have led us to the knowledge of a large number of specific facts; but these same facts, far from enlightening us on the general principle, seem on the contrary to cover it in our eyes with a more impenetrable veil. For example: the analogy between pear and apple trees is far more striking than that between pear and quince trees; yet pear trees grafted onto quince trees are perfectly successful, whereas apple trees vegetate for a year or two without bearing fruit, and then perish. What could be the cause of this? This pear tree, which we have never been able to get to recover completely from the apple tree, will succeed to a certain extent if we graft it onto medlar, azero and even thorn, all species which seem to have much less in common with it. Cherry trees cannot unite with plum trees, with which they are closely related, nor with apricot, peach and almond trees; and Virginia chionanthus, whose fruit is a berry, does very well on ash, whose fruit is a capsule. An estimable author is wrong when he says that the theory of the art consists in "only grafting varieties of the same species, species of the same genus and, by extension, genera of the same family" onto each other. If we put this theory to the test in practice, we'll see that the exceptions, or rather the facts that contradict it, are a hundred times more numerous than those used to establish it. It is true that the author adds that it is still necessary to: "Observe the analogy of trees in the "epochs of the movement of their sap, in the perma" nence or fall of their leaves, and in the quali" tés of their own juices, in order to put all these "things in relation between the subjects and the grafts." In the majority of cases, the analogy between the permanence or fall of leaves is of no use at all; there is more: in some, circumstances an evergreen tree takes over from another whose leaves fall every year; but it is not this error that we need to concern ourselves with at present. We will only point out that, when he speaks of seeking analogy between the proper juices, he is much more consistent than the authors with congeneric species, without having taken science one step further; for how is it possible to recognize analogy in the proper juices? If we were to subject them to chemical analysis, the result, which is very difficult to obtain, would be useless. Should we rely on analogies that the eye can grasp, all limpid juices resemble each other, as do all milky, gummy and resinous juices. If we could reasonably recommend to gardeners a study which would perhaps be more harmful than useful, because of the loss of precious time it would entail, we would advise them to look for these analogies in the vascular system of plants, in the shapes of medullary cases, etc., and perhaps this research, aided by experience, would lead them to make some valuable discoveries in the art of grafting. As for us, we shall confine ourselves to the theory we gave above, and add only that, to ensure success, the operation should be carried out very lightly, so as not to give the amputated parts time to dry out; as much accuracy as possible should be used in reassembling them; they should be protected from meteorological influences that could harm them, and we should try to help them benefit from those that could be favorable. Further details will be given in the article on each individual crush, and the subjects to be chosen for placing the species and varieties will be listed in the cultivation of each individual. Lacking knowledge of the degrees of affinity necessary between two trees for the success of a cranesbill, a great many ridiculous tales were once told on this subject. For example, it was claimed that grafting a peach tree onto a willow tree produced a fruit of prodigious size, but whose flesh was inedible. It has been claimed that, to protect the orange tree from the effects of frost, all you had to do was graft it onto the holly; that a rose grafted onto the blackcurrant bush produced black flowers; and finally, even today, some authors have allowed themselves to be persuaded by charlatans that a rose grafted onto the holly bush produces a green rose. Dumont de Courcet himself, while refuting these errors, seems to believe them to a certain extent; and, in fact, it takes many years of experience not to be tempted by the appearance of naivety with which many people tell you they have seen it. One has, either foolishly or out of ignorance, put forward a false fact; one supports it out of self-love: such is the spirit of ordinary men: one prefers to support a silliness against the very evidence, than to admit that one has been mistaken, or that one may have been! The results of grease are perhaps even more difficult to explain than the relations of analogy it needs to resume. This operation so modifies the character of certain trees, that it seems to change their nature. For example, apple trees grafted onto franc trees grow to prodigious heights; on paradis they reach just a few feet; and it's much the same for all trees grafted onto scions. The scissor sorb grafted on hawthorn grows, in a few years, to a height of twenty-five or thirty feet; from seed, it remains a mediocre shrub for a long time. The ragouminier produced by its seeds crawls on the ground and rarely reaches more than two feet high; grafted onto a plum tree, its stems stand upright, join together in bundles and grow to four or five feet. What is even more singular is that many trees that cannot withstand the cold of our winters when they are free-standing, cease to be sensitive to it when they are grafted; For example, the Japanese medlar, grafted on hawthorn, has spent the winter for some years in the open ground, with the simple precaution of covering its branches with a little straw, whereas those from seed have not been able to withstand the frost, even though more meticulous precautions have been taken for them. The true pistachio tree, grafted on terebinth, is not sensitive to a cold of ten degrees; if it is produced by its seeds, it perishes at six, says a famous professor (M. Thouin.) Finally, all trees, without exception, produce more fruit, of greater volume and better quality, when grafted; and the more often they are grafted, the more these qualities increase. As this is the most useful and ordinary result we intend to achieve, it is also the only phenomenon D. will try to explain. If we succeed in lifting a corner of the veil with which nature likes to cover her most essential operations, we will believe we have done science a real service, because our conjectures will perhaps point the way to someone more skilful than us, who will later complete the task of shedding full light on this part of the gardener's art. Then, as experience will always agree with theory, we'll no longer be groping our way along, and we'll suddenly obtain the precious results that may still be hidden from us. First and foremost, we need to specify the effects of grafting on the fruiting bodies, and admit only those observed by experience. 1° It is certain that the fleshy pericarps of all fruits with pepins and most others are almost always, by a fifth, a quarter, and sometimes even a third, larger on grafted individuals, even of the same variety, than on those resulting from seeds. 2° Seeds, far from being altered by this development of the pericarp, are on the contrary larger, in equal numbers, and just as fertile. There are exceptions to this principle, which we'll discuss later. 3° Grafting influences the flavor of the fruit, making it sweeter, more fragrant, and more agreeable to the taste, but simply by altering the stems, without involving the subject's own juices. Some authors claim that the subject modifies the flavor of the grafted fruit, or even changes it. They say that the Queen Claude plum, grafted on different varieties of wild plants of its species, is tasteless on some and delicious on others; that cherries grafted on mahaleb, cherry laurel or wild cherry have a completely different taste. But several experiments have taught us that this is a mistake. We'll explain the results of our observations below. In the great majority of plants, and particularly in those that take cuttings or marcottes, and which lend themselves to grafting (perhaps in all of them), all the parts that compose them are endowed, by the effect of their particular organization, with the faculty of elaborating the nourishing juices that are transmitted to them by any matter whatsoever, and of assimilating them to their own nature, without these juices ever retaining the slightest analogy with what they were before (1). The only influence they can have on a plant is to produce a greater development of vegetation, if they are abundant, and this for the sole reason that the plant appropriates a greater quantity of them. (1) This is not to say that terroir has no influence on the quality of harvests; experience gives us proof to the contrary in every place, and it will certainly never be denied that Bordeaux wine is better than Surène. But these differences are only slight modifications in the flavor of the fruit, and cannot be used to characterize varieties. What's more, if we were to take into account the influences of climate, exposure, meteorites, cultivation, wine-making methods, time of ripening and species of plant, the contribution of the land would be reduced to very little, perhaps even nothing; it's almost certain that, if soil from the Clos Vougeot were brought to Paris, the vines planted there would produce no better wine than Surène. We are aware that other objections may be raised: for example, vegetables grown in soil smoked with excrement are usually tasteless; at least those grown on some of the plains around Paris are reputed to be so. This is not, as is often thought, due to the nourishing juices supplied by the fertilizer, but rather to the miasma it exhales, which attaches itself to the surfaco of the leaves and other parts of the plants, impregnating them with a bad odor. It would be more difficult to explain the so-called "terroir taste" in certain wines. However, it has often been remarked that vines placed in the vicinity of a lime kiln impart to the wine an unpleasant taste, caused by the sediment that the smoke deposits on the grapes, and not at all by nourishing juices; would it not be the same in this case, and could we not attribute this so-called terroir taste to mephitic mists that act in the same way as the lime kiln smoke? 9 For example, a tree planted in good soil will grow much larger than one planted in poor or mediocre soil. This principle of plant physiology is proven by experience, and we really can't imagine how anyone could have doubted it, when they wondered whether the sap of a subject on which a graft had been placed would influence the shape, color and flavor of a fruit, to the point of making it a new species, or at least a new variety. The sap of the subject will act on the graft in precisely the same way as the nourishing juices of the earth act on a cutting or a marcotte. Whether these juices are supplied to a plant by clay, silica or alumina, by calcareous, sandy or granitic soil, by humus derived from animal or vegetable decomposition, from the moment they are absorbed by the cutting, they will metamorphose into its own substance, they will become of the same essence as it, regardless of what they were before absorption; and the most trained eye will see no difference in the forms and tissues of two cuttings of the same species growing in different types of soil. Now, grafting is nothing other than a cutting, which, instead of being made in the ground and absorbing nourishing fluids through roots, is placed on a bark whose nourishing fluids it absorbs by bringing its sap vessels into contact with those of the subject. Finally, to cut a long story short, a graft is nothing more than a parasitic plant, living at the expense of another, without ever forming a single individual with it. To be sure of the truth of this assertion, all you have to do is take a grafted tree of any age and remove the grafts. You will see that the fibers of the serous vessels of the subject and the graft are superimposed, but never continuous. Even more 4 convincing proof: if the graft were not parasitic, when, for example, it is placed at half stem height on a three-year-old subject already having six woody layers, twenty years later it could no longer detach, because there would be forty uninterrupted woody layers, from the root of the tree to its head, and the first six superimposed layers would be enclosed under the other forty continuous layers. But this is not the case: each woody layer starting from the root retains the nature of its subject until it is grafted, at which point continuity is broken; not only does its nature change, but it is abruptly interrupted, and another layer, completely distinct and of the nature of the graft, is superimposed in such a way as to seize its nourishing juices without there being any prolongation of the first, or even the slightest inter-crossing in their reciprocal fibres. Lastly, a graft is held to the subject only by a kind of gearing and a particular sticking. We conclude from all this that the subject's sap has no influence on the shape, flavour or color of the fruit; that it cannot modify the nature of the graft, and that the subject influences it only by the greater or lesser quantity of food it supplies; which can be estimated on roughly the same scale as the influences of a plot of land on the bonture placed there, according to its good or bad quality. 4° Trees whose grafting has the greatest effect on fruit development live much shorter lives than others. The apple from paradis is the largest, and the tree lives only twenty-five years at most; the apple from doucin is often smaller, and the tree more vigorous; the apple from franc is smaller still, and the tree lives up to one hundred and twenty years; the apple from sauvageon is the smallest, and the tree lives two hundred years. 5° The more the graft develops the size of the fruit, the more it also stops the general development of the tree: and this phenomenon follows the same progression as longevity. It has been feared that certain foreign trees seem to evade the general principles we have just established; for example, red and yellow poppies, grafted on horse chestnut, are said to live longer than those grown from seed; bird mountain ash and Lapland celni are in the same situation, etc.; but it would be easy for us to give you more details.But it would be easy for us to establish that, if these facts are true, they are mere anomalies that must be attributed to particular causes, and we can affirm that individuals from seed are always the most vigorous and longest-lived. Now let's try to find the causes to which we can attribute these phenomena. You'd have to have never observed nature not to have noticed an invariable law to which it is subject: it is to sacrifice everything, even individuals, for the preservation of the species; and this law applies to all organized beings. It is as a consequence of this principle that we see the largest and most robust animals, those which can therefore most easily avoid danger, or resist accidents which would destroy them, produce only a few young. Their individual strength is enough to preserve the species, so there was no need to multiply them. But when creatures share only weakness, are constantly subject to all kinds of accidents, and succumb to the slightest shock, the species would soon be destroyed if the individuals were not very numerous. The whale and the elephant produce only one young, the herring a million, the butterfly a hundred, and the mouse five to eight every month. It's much the same with plants. Annual plants, which are consequently the most hazardous D8 in terms of preservation, are those that generally produce the greatest number of seeds. A poppy, for example, will provide a hundred or two hundred times as many seeds as an oak or a chestnut; and these can be counted among the trees that multiply their seeds in greater numbers; and the reason for this is that, being found only in the north, they have to fear the rigors of winters that trees in the south have nothing to fear. The latter are generally less fruitful, although their fruits are usually larger. It follows from this principle that, since nature has in mind the multiplication of species and nothing else, it must also ensure that each individual reproduces several times before it is destroyed. So we see that, when any being, animal or vegetable, is altered in its constitution, the organic principles which should, in its healthy state, be distributed to all its parts to maintain them in their proportions and vigor, take another course and concentrate, as it were, on the organs of reproduction, in order to ensure the future existence of the small beings that will replace the one it abandons to death. This general law also applies to human beings. Naturalists and physicians have noted that people who are stunted, phthisic, or suffering from a chronic disease caused by a defect in organization, are those most prone to the venereal act. Animals that have been domesticated for a long time are the most altered in nature, and also those that reproduce in greater numbers. This observation is particularly striking in plants: When a tree is very vigorous, it bears little fruit; but when it has been mutilated by pruning, by the amputation of some of its roots, by several transplants, or even by a wound made to its trunk; when it has been subjected to ring incision, to bowing; when it has been altered at last, nature makes an effort; it seems to apprehend the destruction of the individual before it has fulfilled the purpose it expects of it, and it covers it with flowers and fruit. From this, we can conclude that experience has proven that the number, size and often the quality of fruits are inversely proportional to the vigor of the individual that produces them; and that, consequently, the more altered the plant's habit, the more it will bear fruit. However, this alteration must not be carried to the point of disorganization, as exhausted nature would make futile efforts, and the tree would bear aborted fruit and promptly perish. Grafting is an operation by which the constitution of a tree is altered, by hindering the flow of sap from the roots to the branches; the more the tree is grafted, the more it will be altered. So, according to the established principle, grafting should increase the size and number of fruits. This is all the more true as we can see that the very alteration of the subject on which we graft already influences at least their volume. Let's follow our first comparison: take, for example, two apple trees on the same branch; place the first on paradise, the second on franc; you'll get a tree from the first that rarely rises to the height of four or five feet, but the fruit will be much larger. The reason for this is that paradis is a natural marcotte detached from the base of a tree and already much altered, therefore providing less sèvè. The second will rise from twenty to thirty feet, and the fruit will be much smaller than on the first. To succeed perfectly in the grafting operation, there must also be other analogies between the two subjects, but these are much easier to grasp. If we 2. 7 D6 grafted a strong individual onto a weak or languishing subject, the result would be that the roots and trunk below the graft would never be in proportion to the head of the tree; The former, too spindly, would not attach the tree strongly enough to the earth, and would leave enough room for the winds to uproot it, or at least shake it badly; the latter, slender and thin, would eventually, after a certain length of time, no longer be able to transmit nourishment to the branches, and the tree would perish. In addition, the place where the graft would be attached to the subject would form an enormous bulge, which would soon become cankerous, present an unpleasant look, and finally lead to the loss of the plant. Varieties should also be grafted onto subjects of other varieties which experience has shown to be more suited to them, either because of their own affinities, or because of the quality of the soil in which they will be planted. For example, Provence apricots can be grafted onto stone plums, albergiers onto peach almond, and Portuguese apricots onto large damas. In dry, sandy soils, peach trees can be grafted onto almond trees; in wet soils, they can be grafted onto plum trees; and again, the varieties of grafts must be matched to the varieties of trees. All these special cases will be dealt with in detail in the articles on particular crops. Now that we've covered the general effects of grafting, we'd like to talk about the lesser-studied effects of grafting, which are more specifically related to plant physiology, but which are nonetheless essential for even the simplest practitioner to know. 1° Grafting, whatever the subject on which it is placed, as we have said, changes little or nothing in the fruit and size of the tree. Shape, growth habit, health and disease remain unchanged, with very few exceptions. When you want to multiply a species or variety by this ingenious means, it is important to choose grafts from vigorous subjects with no particular vices, and possessing all the qualities you want to fix in your offspring. By the same token, if you wish to perpetuate a disease or anomaly, such as variegation, double flowers, etc., you can do so by grafting; and you will be all the more successful if you choose the branch on which these defects are best determined. An essential observation is that diseases are only perpetuated by this operation when they are constitutional, i.e. when they belong to a modification of organization whose cause is almost always unknown, and not to an accidental lesion. Success will be certain when the disease is general; but if it modifies only a part of the plant, it will become doubtful, unless we are certain of having inserted a modified gem. Let's suppose that in a nursery there is a subject that has developed two branches, one of which will bear single flowers, the other double flowers, and that you want to propagate the double-flowered variety. If a bud is taken indiscriminately for grafting, even on the double-flowered branch, it is possible that it will be chosen below the place where the organization is altered, even if it is the bud closest to the anomaly, and the operation will be missed. But if you take a bud from the same branch and place it above, success is guaranteed. Not only are these changes transmitted by grafting, but also those due to the nature and vigour of the gems; hence the need, as far as possible, to choose the eyes to be grafted onto healthy branches, of a size proportionate to the type of graft and the size of the subject. 2° The graft, by a particular reaction on the subject I which receives it, can modify its nature to an extent which is not yet known, for lack of experiments on the subject. From some of the facts we have observed, it seems that patch grafting acts on the plant receiving it, in the same way as inoculation on animals. On several occasions, we have grafted a variegated variety onto a species that was not variegated; the graft, after having grown for some time, perished by accident, or was detached entirely, and the subject that had received it nonetheless contracted variegation. Diseases can be perpetuated by this kind of inoculation, but we don't believe the same is true of anomalies. Before going into the details of the different ways of grafting, we need to describe the instruments most needed for the operation, and indicate the substances used, either to hold the parts in place, or to facilitate wound healing. There are two types of grafting instrument. 1° The ordinary grafting knife; this is a kind of small knife, with a blade one and a half to two inches long, slightly rounded at the edge. The handle should be made of stag horn and rough, so as to fit more easily into the hand. The tah is fitted with a spatula made of boxwood, ivory, bone, and sometimes, but mistakenly, copper, forming an oval, flattened at the edges, but not sharp. This instrument is too well known to require any further description, but it must be razor-sharp to cut very cleanly, and the blade must always be kept very clean. Care should be taken to wipe it clean each time it is used on a tree of one species, and then on another of a different species. 2° The angular grafting tool, which we have invented and illustrated, pl. 6, fig. 7. It consists of a blade fifteen lines long, hollowed out into a triangular gutter a, the wider, truncated end of which is sharp; it is screw-fastened into a handle four inches long, and a small copper cover or case b is used to defend it when the instrument is put in one's pocket. Fig. 8 shows the same instrument turned upside down for more convenient cutting close to the ground. Blade a is placed, by means of a screw, on a pivot, which turns on a spring when pressed. This mechanism is used to replace the blade perpendially on the handle, so that it can be covered with a sheath, as shown by the dots. Fig. 7 is reduced by a quarter, while Fig. 8 is drawn at natural size. The other instruments are limited to a few wooden wedges, of various sizes, to place in the slot made in a strong subject, and hold it open while the graft is being driven in; and a hammer to open this slot by striking on a sharp instrument, when operating on a tree trunk that has already reached a certain size. The materials most commonly used to make the bindings that hold the parts in place until they are welded together are: 1° coarsely spun, loosely twisted wool. It is highly advantageous, because its elasticity allows it to relax as the subject grows, thus preventing strangulation and nodosities. It also has the advantage of taking moisture with difficulty, and when impregnated, retains it for a long time. This substance seems preferable to all those used to date. 2° bark strips. They have the disadvantage of being difficult to tie, of rotting too quickly, and of not loosening when the subject gropes. 3° hemp, never loosens, and, for this reason, almost always produces strangulations which not only cause nodosities, but even hinder the recovery of the cocks. 4° rushes retain too much moisture, rot quickly and lack strength. 5° wicker, split and soaked in water for some time to soften it, is used for very strong subjects, whose bark is not very delicate, 6° tar cloth: this offers the precious advantage of holding the parts together, and preserving them from humidity and the drying contact of atmospheric air. As it is not yet in use, we'll show you how to prepare and use it. Take some fine cloth, cut it into ribbons of varying widths, as required; then melt two parts black pitch, one part tallow and one part wax in an earthen vase, and cover the ribbons with it, but only on one side. Thus prepared, they can be kept for a very long time; when you want to use them, simply warm them with your hands or breath, and apply them with the precaution of running your fingers over them several times to attach them to the bark of the grafted plant. 7° Finally, in the absence of the substances we have just mentioned, anything that can be used to make a ligature can be employed with varying degrees of success. Sometimes it is not necessary to bind to maintain the graft on the subject, but it must always be protected from atmospheric influences, and particularly from humidity caused by rain for too long. In this case, we use grafting wax, or what gardeners call Saint-Fiacre's ointment. The former is composed in several ways: 1° a mixture of pitch resin and yellow wax, in equal quantities, is melted in an earthen vase; 2° or five eighths of black pitch, one eighth of resin and one eighth of yellow wax; 3° or one pound of Burgundy pitch, one quarter of black pitch, two ounces of yellow wax, two ounces of resin and half an ounce of sheep's tallow. This composition, used in our establishment and in that of Luxembourg, is excellent: it is melted over a very small fire in a cast-iron pot, and heated on a portable stove, to be used as needed; 4° or two-thirds of yellow wax and one-third of tallow, 5° or finally one-third of black pitch, one-third of yellow wax and one-third of tallow, to which is added a roughly equal quantity of bricks pulverized as finely as possible. This last method also seems very good to us, because brick dust gives solidity as well as economy. Grafting wax is used when it is heated enough to become liquid and spread easily over the bark with a brush; but care must be taken that it is too hot, as it will dry out the edges of the bark, and the operation will be missed. Saint-Fiacre ointment is nothing more than a mixture of clay and cow dung. In rainy weather, it has the serious disadvantage of retaining too much moisture and rotting the graft; in dry seasons, it has the advantage of maintaining freshness, and consequently helping recovery. Finally, some people simply use pure clay, once it has been beaten enough not to crack as it dries. Each of these methods is to be preferred in particular circumstances, but the one that offers the most general advantages is the use of wax. If you had to graft in a place far from habitation, where you wouldn't have the convenience of a fire to soften the various waxes we've just described, you'd prepare in advance the following recipe: one pound of yellow wax, one pound of fat turpentine, half a pound of white or Burgundy pitch, and a quarter of sheep's tallow. When the composition is melted, well mixed and cooled, we knead it, wetting our hands so that it doesn't stick, and we put it into small loaves, which we take with us to use as needed. All you have to do is knead it again to soften it and make it suitable for attaching perfectly to the bark to which it is applied. Few authors have attempted to methodically classify the different types of graft, and only one has succeeded in providing truly analitical divisions: Professor Thouin. Duhamel was the first to try: he divided them into five sections, to which he gave the names of approach, cleft, crown, flute and escutcheon grafts. These sections have no characters to distinguish them from one another, and are therefore arbitrary and bad; but it is already a great achievement to have indicated to the authors who were to write after him, the methodical route they should follow to reach a useful goal that he himself missed. Rosier, who came after him, should perhaps have seized on Duhamel's conception and perfected it; all he did was add a sixth section, just as arbitrary, to which he gave the name of grafts by juxtaposition. Finally, M. Thouin included his analitical classification (1) in the memoirs of the Musée d'Histoire Naturelle, the best that we believe could be adopted in a work that would be less practical than this one. We shall extract it verbatim from his excellent work, before giving our own, based on quite different foundations. M. Thouin says: "We restrict the type of grafting to three sections, and call them: the first, approach grafts; the second, scion grafts; and the third, gem grafts. (1) Monographie des greffes, ou Description technique des diversa sortes de greffes employées pour la multiplication des végétaux. 18 "The first covers all types of grafting carried out by means of some of the parts of plants that are attached to their rooted feet. "The second includes all those made with woody parts separated from one individual and transported to another. "The third and last section includes all those performed using gemma or eyes, lifted with the portion of bark surrounding them, from one plant and placed on another. "These three sections are themselves divided into series, each of which has its own secondary characteristics to distinguish them from one another. "These in turn are divided into types, each with its own distinctive characteristics. "Finally, the various varieties and sub-varieties offered by some of these types are distinguished by particular definitions, and are arranged after their main types. "Grafting by approach is divided into five series, or five different groups, according to the various parts of the plants with which they are made: " 1st series, grafts by approach on stems. "2° series, approach grafts on branches. "3° series, approach grafts on roots. "4° series, approach grafts on fruit. "5° series, approach grafts on leaves and flowers. "Scion grafts are made from young woody shoots, such as buds, twigs, small branches and roots, which are separated from their individual parts to be placed on another, to live and grow at its expense. "The kinds of grafts belonging to this section being numerous, they have been divided into five series, according to the parts of the trees with which they are performed, and the operations they require." 000 "The first includes those known as split grafts, which are usually made using young shoots produced by the first sap. "The second is usually called crown grafting, and is almost always carried out with young shoots produced by the penultimate sap, aged between twelve and eighteen months. "The third category includes branch-tip grafts, or those made from twigs with their twigs, leaves, often flower buds, and sometimes young fruit. "The fourth category includes side grafts, which are made on stems without requiring amputation of the head of the individuals on which they are made. "The fifth and last category consists of grafting roots onto aerial plant parts, and young scions onto root stocks. Grafting by gemma consists of an eye, bud or gemma, carried on a plate of bark of varying size and shape, transported from one place to another on the same or other individuals. "As this section offers a fairly large number of different types and modes of grafting, it has been divided into two series: "The first includes all the scape grafts carried out by means of an isolated gemma, or several joined into a single button. "The second includes all flute and juxtaposition grafts, which can bring together several gemmas, separated from each other, on the same bark tube. The reader can see from this excerpt how precisely and accurately Mr. Thouin has created divisions to which the zoa grafts he knew 90, those that were unknown to him, and even those that may be invented in the future, can easily relate. If we have not adopted his classification, it is because we wanted to present the graft species as far as possible in the order of their use. Our intention being to produce a useful work, we must always ascribe the theoretical approach to the practical one; moreover, it will be easy to classify all our grafts in M. Thouin's divisions, and it is with this aim in mind that we have reported the characters he assigns to each of his main divisions. bbb FIRST SECTION. GRAFTING FRUIT TREES. FIRST DIVISION. GENERAL GRAFTING OF FRUIT TREES. When we graft a fruit tree, we propose one or more aims, but these can only be the following: 10 to multiply a variety or species, by placing it on a different variety or species; 2° to place several species or varieties on a single subject; 3° to rejuvenate a tree in whole or in part; 4° to repair a shape defect in its bearing; 5° to increase its vigor; 6° to obtain fruit on a very young tree; 7° to increase the quality and size of the fruit in a particular way; 8° to repair a wound made to the bark of a precious tree; 9° to obtain a large number of francs on a single stump. 08 Grafting for variety propagation. (L. ESCUTCHEON GRAFTS. An escutcheon is a small plate of bark, imitating a shield, on which is a gem commonly known as an eye or bud. Several types of grafting, which we will describe below, have taken their generic name from this plate, and are carried out according to the same general principles. They are usually used on young trees, from one to five years old, and even older if their bark has remained thin, smooth and healthy. They are suitable not only for fruit trees, but also for all other non-coniferous trees, shrubs and bushes, whose bark has the qualities we have just described. They are done in spring, when vegetation is beginning and the trees are well into sap, which can be recognized by the great ease with which the bark can be detached from the wood, or during the sap of August. The gardener's first step is to choose carefully the branch of the tree whose variety he wishes to multiply, and on which he is to raise the crest. This branch must be a shoot from the previous year, well budded, with perfectly formed eyes. If you fear that the buds are not ripe enough, pinch the end of the branch to stop its growth, force the sap to reach the eyes, and delay cutting until perfect maturity. When the branch is cut, the leaves are removed, but with the precaution of leaving a portion of the petiole, two or three lines long, attached to the wood; this will serve to hold the escutcheon with two fingers, when it is removed, and to place it more conveniently in the incision prepared to receive it: moreover, when, after being dried out, it falls from the graft, separating cleanly and without effort, it will be the most favourable augury for the success of the operation. 61 If you don't need to graft right away, and have to keep the cut branches for a day or two, stick them in damp earth and keep them in a cool place. If they are to be kept for several days, greater care is needed: wrap them in damp cloth or moss. Finally, if they are to travel, they should be pricked into a ball of moist earth, wrapped in wet moss, and enclosed in a tin can. Mr. Thouin recommends, for a journey of four or five days, to prick them in a cucumber or other watery fruit; and, for more distant distances, to put them in a honey bath: but these precautions seem to us excessive and even risky. If you need to graft immediately, you can remove the buds and throw them into a vase of water, to prevent the air and heat from drying them out: at least this method is used by some gardeners. But the safest way is to dip only the branches into the water, and remove them one after the other, as needed to lift the escutcheons and place them in their incision. This is done by cutting the bark of the tree from the epidermis to the sapwood, first by a transverse cut, then by a longitudinal one which starts in the middle of the first and extends, downwards or upwards, in a length proportionate to that of the crest, so as to form the shape of a straight or inverted T. To place the escutcheon, start at the top and spread the two lips of the incision with the spatula or ivory blade of the grafting machine, lifting the bark with great care to avoid tearing or injuring it. The escutcheon, which had been placed between the lips to keep the hands free, is grasped by the petiole and slid under the bark: the bast of the transversely cut part is made to coincide perfectly with the bast of the transversal incision of the subject; then the lips of the subject's bark are brought closer together, so that there is no gap between the parts, through which foreign corps could enter. The ligature is made by wrapping the whole, except for the bud, with several turns of wool or hemp; not too tightly, so as not to injure the bark by compressing it, and the operation ends there. All that remains is to visit the grafts from time to time to make sure that the ligature has not formed any constrictions or bulges; and, if this happens, the grafts are undone on the spot to make them looser again. After a fortnight or a month, the bark is reunited, and the graft resumed. We will now list and describe the escutcheon grafts most commonly used to propagate fruit species or varieties). Nora. All grafts marked with an asterisk" are used in daily practice; those marked with two " are generally used. 10** Ecus graft its woody. Lenormand graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1re, fig. 3. c, incision; a, open incision; b, escutcheon (1). An escutcheon is raised on a branch. To do this, we make a transverse incision above a healthy, vigorous eye (1) We have shown the sizes of all the grafting species we know, and we have referred to M. Thouin's Synonymy all those known to this learned professor. However, we must warn our readers that they will sometimes find differences in cuts, if they compare our figures with those cited by this respectable man; here is the reason: M. Thouin, in writing his Monographie des grefses, was writing the history of the art, rather than wishing to teach its new application to current practice. He therefore had to quote the authors' figures without allowing himself to make any changes. Our aim being different from his, we felt it necessary to illustrate our grafts as they are used in current practice, either in our establishment or in other growers' establishments, without bothering much with the sigures of the former authorsa. The first incision is a deep, vertical cut; then, bringing the blade of the grafting knife a little higher than this cut, a strip three to four lines wide and an inch or an inch and a half long is removed; it is pointed at the bottom, and the first incision causes it to be cut transversely at the top. The eye should be about one-third of the way up, and the stipules or other membranes that sometimes accompany the stalk that has been left behind, the prickles or other appendages, should be carefully removed. Using the tip of the grafting tool, the wood is removed from the escutcheon, leaving a slight blade about a third of the way along. This escutcheon is then inserted on the tree, and tied as described above. This graft is suitable for all seeded and stone fruit trees, as well as for most forest and ornamental trees, and is the most widely used in the Paris area. ** Graft in the form of a shield with a growing eye. Jouette graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1re, fig. 3. a, subject incision; b, escutcheon. The escutcheon is cut and placed in the same way as for the previous graft; but as soon as it is placed, the head of the subject is cut off, and all buds growing on the stem are cut off daily. This grafting, carried out in spring, offers the real advantage of forcing the inserted bud to develop immediately, and thus hastening a year's enjoyment; however, it sometimes happens that, if the grafting does not take place, the sap, not finding a way to emerge, causes the subject to perish from fullness, at least in a large part of its length. Done in August, it rarely succeeds, because the young shoots of the escutcheon, not having time to harden off, perish in the frost, often leading to the subject's ruin. 14 3°** Sleeping-eye escutcheon graft. Vitry graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1re, fig. 3. a, subject incision; b, escutcheon. It is done in the same way as the previous ones, but at the time of the August sap, and nothing is removed from the subject until the following spring, in order to prevent the bud from developing before that season. By postponing enjoyment for a year, this type of crane secures it even more. It also has the advantage of not harming the subject if it doesn't come back. It is used almost exclusively by the inhabitants of Vitry, the largest fruit tree grower in the Paris region. It also appears to be the most suitable for peach tree propagation. 4° Reverse crest graft. Knoop graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1re,. sig.5. .a, reverse incision; b, the crest cut backwards. The escutcheon is raised in such a way that the tip of the eye, when placed on the subject, is inverted and facing the ground, whether the incision of the subject is made in the ordinary way, or in Trenversé (L). By this method, the buds are forced to grow in the opposite direction to the one they should have naturally; but they soon straighten up too, and the aim of this graft, to increase the size of the fruit by hindering the flow of sap, has been missed or more or less. (He. SPLIT GRAFTS. These are made from branches or young shoots of the last sap, well grown, with from one to six or seven eyes. One of these shoots is cut at the bottom, in the shape of a knife blade covered with its bark on the side where thickness has been preserved, or beveled with bark on both sides. The subject's head is cut, the wound is joined together, and lateral slits are made, which are brought down straight, 906 to a more or less considerable length. Using the tip of a serpette, or a wedge of wood, this slit is held ajar, while the bevel of the graft is inserted. To ensure recovery, the liber of the branch and that of the subject must be made to coincide exactly, regardless of the outer part of the bark. All that remains is to make a ligature to prevent the cracks in the subject from letting the grafts escape as they spread, and to cover the wound with grafting wax or Saint-Fiacre's ointment. When the latter composition is used, it is wrapped in a piece of cloth or canvas, bound from underneath, to prevent rainwater from washing away or at least degrading the apparatus; in this case, the graft is sometimes called a doll. Slot grafting is performed in spring, when sap begins to appear. It is used for all fruits with pips, and for a large proportion of those with stones, berries, etc. It is the most common method of grafting. It is most commonly used to form high-stemmed trees) 5.** Simple split grafting. Grafting! Atticus, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 1. a, subject split to receive the graft; b,b, branches pruned in bevel, d, or in bevel with retreat, c;c, e, branch adjusted in the split. We prepare a young branch of a smaller diameter than that of the subject, leaving two or three buds, and pruning it to three or four lines below the last eye; we then cut the stem of the subject, either at the root collar, or at different heights, up to that of eight feet; we split it in the middle of its diameter, and insert the graft with the precautions indicated above. This graft, being one of the easiest and most 2. 2 0 reliable, is generally widespread, especially in the provinces. It is perfectly successful on seed trees, less so on stone trees, with the exception of cherry and some plum trees. It is suitable for trees that need to be grafted underground, and for those that need to be raised to high stems to form orchards. It is also used for many ornamental shrubs and bushes, because it offers the precious advantage of flowering and forming a pretty head in the course of a year. Roses grafted with a single cleft almost always flower in the same year and often in the same spring. But enthusiasts, who are more interested in owning a robust tree of good growth than in premature enjoyment, are careful to remove the flower bud as soon as it appears, whether from a rose, a shrub for pleasure or a fruit tree; the sap, which would have been carried to the flowering and fruiting organs, benefits the branches and twigs. 6. * Split graft Ferrari. Thouin. Pl. a, fig. 3. Take a young branch of exactly the same size as the stem of the subject; cut it bevelled on both sides, or rather in the shape of an oboe's mouthpiece, taking care to preserve the bark on each side as it was preserved on one side in the previous operation. The subject is cut and split in the middle of its diameter; then the graft is inserted, with the precaution of making the bark of the branch and the subject coincide perfectly on both sides. This method of grafting, widely used in Italy, and particularly in Genoa, is ideal for young fruit trees, as well as many ornamental shrubs, such as jasmines from the Azores, Spain and Arabia. It hastens the fruiting of the former, the flowering of the latter, and does not have the disadvantage of often causing unpleasant bulges; however, the trees on which it is used gain less growth and last less time. 7.r Single-stem lateral split graft. Pl. 2, fig.1. This is done in the same way as the previous method, except that, since the pith of the subject must remain intact, the slit, instead of cutting the subject positively in the middle of its diameter, cuts it about a third of the way through its thickness, so as to pass alongside the pith without damaging it. This method is used for the same purposes as the previous one, and is perfectly suited to young fruit or ornamental trees, whose thick, spongy pith needs to be protected. 8.** Split graft with two branches. Palladius graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 7. as for the simple split graft, n° 5, two branches are prepared instead of one; then the stem of the subject is split in the middle of its diameter, and the two branches are inserted opposite each other, i.e. on the two outer edges of the split, so that each occupies half the circumference of the cut of the subject. This grafting technique is used on subjects whose stems are already an inch in diameter, or even considerably larger. The chance of success is doubled, because it is rare for both grafts to perish, if there is no particular cause for this to happen. If desired, two different varieties of fruit or flower can be placed on the same tree. This method of grafting is as widely used as the simple split graft. 9.* Lateral split graft with two branches. Like the previous method, with the difference that the subject is split slightly sideways to spare the pith. 508 This technique is used for the same purposes as the previous one, and is merely a modification of it. 10.** Split graft with four branches. Gresse Laquintinie, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 8. four branches are prepared in the same way as for the previous ones, then two slits are made on the cut of the subject, crossing in the middle of its diameter and dividing it into four equal parts; the two slits are extended, going downwards, by one to three inches in length, and the four branches are introduced into them; after which the whole is wrapped in a dolly. It is invaluable for grafting old trees whose head you want to change into a more pleasant or more useful species or variety. It quadruples the chances of success and, better than the previous methods, provides a well-filled head in a short space of time. It can also be used to bring several species to the same individual. It can only be carried out on subjects or branches that have reached a certain size. (III. SLOTTING BY ROOTS AND ON ROOTS. These are made in several ways; sometimes they are twigs that are grafted onto roots left in their place; sometimes they are roots separated from their stumps that are grafted onto stems or branches; or finally they are roots of different trees that are grafted onto each other. They are done at the beginning of the spring sap, and in the same way as other split grafts. Although not widely used, they might be worth adopting, as several plant species are very well suited to them. In addition, they would undoubtedly improve fruiting, because they impede sap flow more, and therefore force it to focus more particularly on the fruiting organs. But to achieve this goal, the operation would have to be twofold. For example, we would graft a stem of a different species onto the roots of an individual; then, when this stem was strong enough, we would graft it in turn by placing another variety on it) 11. Cleft graft on a root collar. Gresse Guettard, de Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 10. One or more branches are prepared as for other slit grafts; then the subject is cut on the root collar, one or more slits are made, into which the branches are placed in the usual way. You can also simply make incisions in the bark, as for crown grafting. When a strong, well-established tree has a counterfeit or diseased stem, or one on which grafts cannot be placed for some other reason, far from pulling it out, as is usually done, you should take advantage of its vigorous root to graft onto it, and in a very short time obtain a robust tree of very fine growth. If an accident breaks a tree on the ground, this graft can still be used. (IV. APPROACH GRAFTING. Approach grafts are somewhat similar to marcottes; like them, they feed on their mother's roots until they have recovered to the point where they can do without them. They are very well suited to young trees, and even to those that have reached a quarter, a third or half their size. They can be carried out at any time of year, as long as there is no excessive heat or frost, but the best time to ensure prompt recovery is during the sap flow, especially at the beginning. These grafts are nothing more than the welding together of two parts of the same plant, or of two different plants, examples of which are often found in nature. To perform the operation, the part to be grafted is given a clean wound proportionate to its size, and the subject is given a similar and corresponding wound. The two are then joined so that they overlap, so that the libers of the graft and the subject are in contact at as many points as possible, and so that there is no gap between the two. The parts are secured with strong bindings, and held together with strong stakes, to prevent any movement that might cause them to come apart. Finally, we protect them from contact with air, water and even light, by covering them with a plaster of Saint-Fiacre ointment when they are made on large, robust subjects, or with a thick layer of grafting wax if they are delicate. As with other grafts requiring a ligature, careful attention must be paid to any nodosities or constrictions that may form, and the ligature must be undone and reattached less tightly if any are found. This operation, if carried out before the graft has been separated from its base, requires a great deal of care, as the stems, which have been bent to bring them together, will spring back; and, if care is not taken, they will come unstuck and untie the graft to return to their natural position. Once the weld is firmly in place, the next step is to wean the graft from the branch or stem that supplied it. If you were to cut it off all at once, you'd run the risk of losing it, because the plant's sap wouldn't yet be sufficiently abundant to nourish it completely; so you start by making a small cut underneath, and from time to time enlarge it, until finally you cut it off completely. The wound is then brought closer to the graft, by removing the heel it forms at its base; the wound is joined as close as possible to the weld, and the operation ends there. Grafting by stem approach is carried out on stems or trunks of different sizes). 12. Grafting by tongue approach. Gresse Pradeley, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 5. a, stem to be grafted; d, its splinter inserted in the slit of the subject; b, the subject; c, the splinter, formed by cutting the bark, inserted between the splinter and the wound of the stem to be grafted. The head of a young tree is cut; the cut area is slit as for a split graft, then a piece of bark is removed from one side, as for an ordinary approach graft. Once this has been done, take the branch or stem to be grafted, make a splinter in it and remove a sheet of bark of the same size as that removed from the subject; then press the splinter into the slit in the subject, join the wounds together and finish with a ligature. Very good for hardwood trees, and above all very solid. (V. BRANCH-APPROACH GRIFFES. These differ from the previous method in that, instead of welding trees by their stems or trunks, they are made by grafting a branch or twig to a trunk, stem or branch. They are easier and safer than stem grafting, and provide greater opportunities for propagation). 13.a Ordinary approach grafting. Agricola graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 9. a, a, subject and graft; b, junction. This involves bringing a branch of the variety to be grafted up against a branch or stem of the subject. Both are cut longitudinally up to the medullary sheath or a little less, and the two wounds are joined as we have said. The angular grafting machine, of our own invention, is particularly useful for this type of cress, making it much easier to join the libers, thanks to the accuracy with which it allows 08 to be placed in the two notches. It saves a lot of time and ensures good recovery. Ordinary approach grafting is widely used to propagate all delicate species and varieties of trees, shrubs and bushes, on which slit and escutcheon grafts are difficult to propagate. It is most commonly used on those with thin bark, hard wood and gems without a scaly envelope. 14. Grafting by notch approach. Cabanis graft, by Thouin. This is done in the same way as the previous method, with the difference that a simple notch is made, which must penetrate to the marrow of the subject. It is also used for the same purposes. Grafting to obtain different fruits on the same plant. It often happens that, either as an object of experiment or curiosity, we wish to have fruits of different species on a single individual. To ensure satisfactory success, you must not take the first varieties that come along indiscriminately: on the contrary, you must be very discerning in your choice, so as to establish a balance of sap in the various grafts, without which the most vigorous would soon choke the others. If, for example, you had placed a sour cherry and a sour cherry dwarf on the same subject, or a calville apple and an apis apple on a franc, there's no doubt that the sour cherry and the calville, despite all the precautions you'd take when pruning, would soon starve the other two by taking all the sap, and promptly cause them to perish. You must also choose the subject on which to operate, and give preference to one whose vigour will be in harmony with the strength of vegetation of the grafts you will place on it. Moreover, despite the care taken when pruning, and despite the precaution of only pruning trees that are suitable for this operation, you should never expect a long-lasting product, especially when you have placed more than two species. Another serious disadvantage is that, since each variety of fruit tree has its own particular habit, and its buds do not grow at the same angle, it is almost impossible to subject these heterogeneous individuals to a regular shape. To achieve this goal, all grafting techniques can be used, enabling several buds or branches to be placed on the same tree: but there are a few more specifically designed for this purpose. 15. Grafting in escutcheon at the end of branches. Jansein graft, by Thouin. This graft is done in the form of a budding or dormant bud, and is applied to branch tips whose bark is still smooth and green. If carried out in May, the branch tips are cut off as soon as the operation is completed, but with the precaution of leaving a wood eye slightly above them for some time, in order to attract the sap, or at least prevent it from leaving the branch tip, and consequently the bud, to retreat to lower buds. If you have grafted in August and then cut back the branch tips the following year, this method is not necessary, because, the welding having been perfectly carried out, the graft bud develops at the same time as the others, and is sufficient to maintain the balance of the sap. This procedure is used for all fruit species. 16. Split grafting at the end of branches. This is carried out at the same time and in the same way as ordinary split grafting, but with the precaution of leaving no buds below the buttress. 08 It is most commonly used to obtain plums or cherries of different shapes and colors, ripening at different times. 17. Grafting by compression approach. Pl. 5, Jig. 1. several plants of different species of the same height are planted in the same hole; they are spaced as little as possible, so that when recovery is certain, they can be joined into a single bundle, which is held together by means of a sheath of fresh lime bark or other material. As the trees grow, their stems are compressed and welded into a single trunk. The ancients, and Olivier de Serres among them, believed that, as the saps mixed, the result was mixed-race fruit, participating in the nature of all, without belonging more specifically to that of one than to that of another. But this error has been corrected; each species retains its own stem and roots, and produces its own fruit. 18. Approach graft, spiral graft. Diane graft, by Thouin. Pl.5, fig. 3. As with the previous method, several plants of different species of the same age, height and, as far as possible, strength are planted in the same hole. When they are perfectly established, and especially well rooted, their stems are twisted one on top of the other, much like the strands of a thick rope, following the course of the sun, i.e. from sunrise to sunset, and at a height of seven or eight feet, more or less. The stems weld together to form a single trunk, imitating a torso column. The head offers the most varied view in its foliage, flowers and fruit, and can eventually provide extremely strong twisted wood. 43 If one wished to obtain varieties on francs, one could use marcottes and cuttings on certain subjects; but, as this kind of propagation is always long, and often difficult, one can use grefse with much greater advantage. (V. CROWN GRAFTS. These are slit grafts, but differ from ordinary grafts in a number of respects. 1° They are made with branches from the last sap but one, and sometimes even with eighteen-month-old wood. 2° They are placed on subjects that are not split in all their diameter, and whose heart is not attacked. They are mainly used for grafting old fruit trees of the seedling division, whose stems or branches are too large to be budded or to receive the ordinary split graft. They can also be used on young trees with thin bark and very hard wood). 19.* Small crown graft. Liébault graft, by Thouin. A tree is dug up to its roots and cut. Using small wooden wedges, the bark is lifted, and as many twigs as can fit are placed between it and the wood, trimmed as for the split graft. Cover with soil, taking care to leave only a third, or so, of the length of the grafts above the soil surface. They grow very vigorously, and the lateral buds are removed as they grow. After three years, we dig them up and remove the rooted ones. The others are marcotte and, by strangulation, are made to emit roots very easily. The free-standing plants obtained by this method make very good mother plants, which for a long time produce young free-standing offspring. (VII. SIDE GRAFTINGS. These grafts are distinguished from other scion grafts by the fact that they do not require amputation of the subject's head, and that, like approach grafts, they are made on the side of the stem. They also differ from approach grafts in that the branch to be grafted is detached from its mother. They are more commonly used to replace a missing branch than to propagate varieties, and are much rarer than the others, although they are fairly easy to perform. They must be strictly carried out at the first sap flow of spring, before the buds have begun to develop). 20.* Grafting by cutting approach. Pepin graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 6. a, the cutting planted in a pot; b, c, cut. A branch of sufficient length to make a cutting is cut and planted next to the subject. It is then grafted to three-quarters of its height, and trimmed to three eyes above its union with the subject. The humidity of the soil sustains life in the graft until it is welded; and, when it is taken up, the subject supplies sap to the branch until it has taken root. The result is that by cutting the branch below the weld, we have a free-standing subject, and the subject, wild before the operation, is changed into an individual of a good species; and we have two trees instead of one. O08 Grafting to obtain fruit from young trees. A very young tree can be forced to bear fruit by various means, but almost always at the expense of its lifespan. However, the grafting method described here only alters the tree to a certain extent, and when pruned carefully, it can continue to bear fruit for several years. 21. Side graft, inserted like an escutcheon. Girardin graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 8. a, bevel of graft; b, incision and insertion of graft. A branch bearing fruit buds is selected, and its base is cut with an extended bevel. A T-shaped incision is made on the subject, as if to place a crest; the bark is lifted with the spatula of the grafting machine, and the bevel of the graft is inserted. Ligature is then applied in the same way as for the previous graft. This scion graft belongs to the side graft series. Grafting to increase fruit quality. Accredited authors such as Olivier de Serres, Miller, Duhamel, Rozier, etc., believed that it was simply a matter of placing several grafts one on top of the other, to hasten the fruiting of a tree, greatly increase the volume and flavor of the fruit, and hasten the time of ripening. Most growers today are still imbued with this belief, which becomes an error if we give it the same extension as the ancients. In our Theory of Grafting we said that the more a tree was altered, the more its fruit gained in quality, and it is through the abuse of this true principle that a false consequence has been drawn by saying: If a first graft increases the volume of an O00 fruit by altering a plant, a second graft, increasing the alteration, will increase its size; a third, even more; a fourth likewise, and so on indefinitely, or at least until, nature having made its last efforts, the fruits have reached monstrous dimensions. They did not consider that this alteration, once reached a certain degree, would produce an effect absolutely opposite to the one they supposed, and that the tree, at the fourth, perhaps even at the third graft, would cease to bear fruit, languish for some time in exhaustion, and perish in the long run. But supposing it resisted these mutilations, thanks to the long intervals of time that we could put between each operation, we would still only obtain the degree of alteration that we usually obtain by other means, such as cuttings and layering, and that we naturally find in offshoots. Let's suppose, for example, that we graft an apple tree onto a wild tree: The first graft will produce a degree of deterioration; a second graft will produce a second degree of deterioration, which we compare to that of the franc; a third will produce the degree of deterioration of the doucin; a fourth, that of the paradis, and a fifth will produce nothing, since the deterioration of the paradis, reducing its size to two or three feet, is the last degree of deterioration that a tree can withstand without perishing, or at least without remaining sterile. The proof is that paradise trees that do not reach this level of development - and this happens quite often in poor soils - cease to bear fruit, which means they have to be uprooted. If we were to compare the fruit obtained by grafting with that obtained by selecting subjects, their increase in volume would follow the same pattern, and the fifth graft would produce precisely the same fruit as the first one obtained from paradise; after which no more results would be expected, as the tree would be entirely exhausted. Even if the alteration by grafting did not work in exactly the same proportions as that from wild to frank, from frank to doucin, and from doucin to paradise, the two extreme points being the same, the final result would also be the same. Assuming, therefore, that grafts-on-grafts have the influence on fruit that the authors attribute to them, which seems more than doubtful to us, the product would be of little interest, since it can be obtained immediately by another, simpler and, above all, much less time-consuming method. We are therefore only giving these grefses to complete our monograph, and at the same time to provide growers with the means to ascertain the truth of our assertion. 22. Graft-on-graft in slit. Slot grafting is done in the ordinary way. When the graft is sufficiently advanced, we take one of our own shoots and graft it onto the previous year's growth. The operation is repeated, always taking the branch from the shoots of the last graft, until there are several, one on top of the other. 23. Graft-on-graft in escutcheon. Duroy graft, by Thouin. Grafting is carried out in the same way as above, with the difference that the escutcheon graft is used instead of the slit graft. It seems that Mr. Thouin does not positively deny the usefulness of these two grafts, as he recommends leaving fruiting branches below each of them, in order to have irrefutable points of comparison, which will easily resolve this important question. 009 Grafting to increase a tree's vigor, or rejuvenate it.
