The descriptions of these grafts, most of which are rarely used, will provide sufficient indication of the more or less advantageous benefits that the grower can derive from them, 24. Noël graft by approach. Thouin. Several trees of different heights, but of the same species and variety, are planted a year in advance. They are grafted onto each other using the following grafting procedure, by wedge-shaped approach. Mr. Thouin claims that this method gives the trees extraordinary vigor, and modifies the flavor and size of their fruit. This last point does not seem sufficiently proven to us, and we believe even less that new breeds can be obtained. 25. Grafting by wedge-shaped approach. Monceau graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 11. a, triangular cut; b, wedge-shaped end. The head of a very long wedge is cut, then a deep triangular notch is made in the tree to be grafted, in the opposite direction to the wedge: the wedge is inserted into the notch, and the parts are tied together with a ligature. This gives extraordinary vigour to a tree that receives nourishment from two root systems, even though it has only one head. The preceding graft is said to increase vegetative strength even more, because it gives several root systems to the same head, whereas this one gives only two. 26. Greffe Buffon, by approach. Thouin. When a fruit tree is spreading its branches horizontally, a savage of a certain strength is planted under each of the largest branches; the tops of the branches of large trees are arched; the end is cut off and beveled. Once this has been done, the wildings are notched, the bevels joined to the notches, and held in place with a ligature. In no time at all, you have an extremely singular tree, whose head is all the better for being fed by several trunks. This grafting technique could be very advantageously employed in landscape gardens, where picturesque effects are sought: in addition, it produces a greater number of more beautiful and better fruits. (VIII. APPROACH GRAFTING ON ROOTS. Their name indicates what they are, and how they differ from other approach grafts. They could be very useful for re-establishing a tree that is ill due to exhaustion or any other alteration of its roots; but so far they have not been used in the usual practice of horticulture). 27. Grafting by root approach. Lemonnier graft, by Thouin. Two holes are dug, one on each side, at the foot of a diseased tree whose roots have been slightly uncovered. Two root stocks are planted in these holes, chosen from species known to provide good subjects for grafting the species or variety of tree affected by the disease: then, on the cut surface of the two root stocks, the ends of the two main roots of the individual to be cured are grafted, by inlaying, so as to preserve its stem and all its ascending parts. This inlay is made by making a notch in the subject, into which the beveled root tip is inserted, as for an ordinary slit graft. Cover with soil, and the operation is complete. It is claimed that this method, by providing the tree 2. 9 with healthy, vigorous roots, not only restores it to full health, but also increases its fruiting. 28." Crown grafting for rejuvenation. Pliny's graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 3. a, flute beak with its notch. Pliny described this graft, calling it incisio inter corticem et lignum. The branches or even the trunk of an old tree, which is beginning to bear no more fruit, are cut at various points; the bark is lifted around the cut area; the branches to be grafted are cut in the shape of a flute-beak; a notch is made at the upper part of the notch where the flute-beak begins, then they are inserted between the bark and the sapwood of the subject. These new branches grow very vigorously, and in a short time produce a beautiful head, or vigorous branches that bear fruit for several years. 29. Crown graft in the bark. Théophraste graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 4. As in the previous method, the branches or trunk of the subject are cut off, but instead of lifting the bark, it is split vertically in all parts of the perimeter where grafts are to be placed. The branches are pruned in the same way, and inserted between the sapwood and the bark where the latter has been split, so that the two lips of the split cover both sides of the flute mouthpiece made at the base of each graft. It is more advantageous and safer than the previous method, in that it allows a greater number of branches to be placed, and because the bark is not detached from the wood next to the grafts, air cannot get in to dry out the cambium and prevent repriscation. It also serves the same purpose. D4 30. Crown side graft. Gresfe Richard, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 8. a, bevel of the graft; b, T-shaped incision into which the branch is inserted. A branch is taken and trimmed at its base with a long, flattened bevel. A T-shaped incision is made in the subject, as if to place a crest; the bark is detached from the sapwood with the spatula of the grafting machine, and the bevel of the graft is slid between the two, held in place with a ligature. It is ideally suited to replacing the escutcheon graft on trees whose old bark has become thicker and stronger. It can be used to place branches where they are missing, or to replace those that have had to be cut off due to accident or disease. Before the discovery of herbaceous grafts, they were used quite advantageously for resinous trees. 31. Crown side graft, ame sorte. Pl. 3, fig. 8. c, T-shaped incision from which the circular piece of bark is removed. This is done in the same way as the previous one, but a small circular piece of bark is removed from the T-bar, so that the bast of the upper part of the subject's incision coincides with the bast of the upper part of the graft's bevel. It is suitable for the same uses as the previous one. 32. Peg side graft. Terence graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 9. c, branch inserted in hole. A small branch, twig or twiglet is cut; it is headed at one end, and the base is trimmed and thinned to form a dowel. Then use a crankshaft 000 to make a hole in the stem or trunk of a tree, and press the graft into it, always taking care to match the bark. The wound is covered hermetically with grafting wax. It is used for the same purposes as the previous ones, but is much stronger. 33. Grafting from the side and backwards. This is done in exactly the same way, but on the opposite side.

Instead of pruning the branch at its base, it is pruned at its upper end, so that it is inserted backwards. The Romans preferred to use these two grafts to propagate the most precious varieties of vine and olive. Terence especially recommends their use. 34. Side graft, simple, by notching. Pl. 5. fig. 7. Take a branch slightly smaller than the stem to be grafted, cut it into a thin wedge elongated at its base, and cut its upper part two or three eyes above the bevel. A deeper cut is made as it is lowered to an inch or an inch and a half, but never deep enough to reach the heart of the wood. The graft is placed so that the bark on either side of the notch coincides perfectly with the bark on either side of the wedge, and is secured by a ligature. This method, which is simple and easy to perform, offers a high chance of success, and produces fairly solid heads. 35. Side grafting, double, by notching. The twigs are cut into wedges as for the previous method, but bark is left on one side only; or better still, they are cut into knife blades, with bark only on the thick part opposite the cutting edge. On a tree much larger than 906 the grafts, make a notch similar to the previous one, just as long but shallower, and insert two grafts, one on each side of the notch, taking care to ensure that the bark coincides perfectly. The width of the notch and the size of the grafts must be combined in such a way as to leave no gap between the two branches when they are placed in pace. Ligature is used to hold the branches together. As easy as simple grafting, this method deserves preference, because it offers a double chance of success. 36. Side grafting by transverse notching. Gresse Roger Schabol, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 10. a, subject cut; b, spatula-cut branch; c, insertion. The branch is cut in the shape of a flute-beak or spatula, and the subject is notched crosswise, reaching directly to the middle of its diameter without raising or lowering the notch; then the graft is inserted in the same way as a tenon in its mortise. The wound is covered with grafting wax. This method, much more difficult than the previous ones, is not as successful, and is rarely used. It is used for the same purposes. These seven grafts are included in the series of crown grafts, because several can be placed around the same stem when it is strong enough. 37. Die-cut escutcheon graft. Mus tel graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, Jig. 4. a, wound; b, bark blade. One must have a specially made punch, with which one removes a plate of bark from a subject. Using the same tool, or the blade of the grafting machine, we lift the escutcheon or bark blade, in the middle of which is a vigorous O09 eye. It must be exactly the same size as the wound made in the subject, in order to fill it with the greatest precision. When it is well adjusted, it is held in place with grafting wax, or soft wax. This method is excellent for placing escutcheons on old trees, whose thick, rough bark would not lend itself to ordinary escutcheon grafting. Grafting to maintain a tree's shape and vegetation balance. In the absence of well-placed branches to bend into the shape intended for a tree (cattail, goblet, espalier, etc.), we are sometimes forced to maintain a vicious shape, or to place buds where nature has denied them. To do this, we employ several types of grafting, according to the various circumstances that chance may present, and with time and patience, we manage to restore to a precious individual all the regularity and grace of which it was deprived. Quite often, a tree loses its regular shape, because the sap, carried away in some branches without anyone being able to guess at the cause, abandons other parts which initially lose weight and end up withering away after a while. 38. Grafting by approaching gourmands. Malesherbes graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig.1. a, gourmand branch; b, its insertion in the stem. We measure the length of branches commonly known as gourmands, which grow on some parts of a tree at the expense of the useful branches they starve. We see whether their tops can easily reach the main stem, or the suffering branches, by means of a slight inclination or arch. 906 A notch is made in these parts, to receive the end of the gourmands that have been bevelled, and they are welded together by means of inlay or braid in the ordinary approach. The result is that the sap, which was too abundant in the gourmands, is restored by them to the parts that were deprived of it, and the balance is quickly re-established. 39. Grafting by approaching branches. Forsyth graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4,sig. 2, a, branch lacking twig; b, twig grafted to it by approach, at c; d, twig cut when perfect recovery is achieved. On the stems and branches of a subject, wherever twigs are missing, notches are made from the sapwood down to the wood. Select nearby twigs, cut them in opposite directions, join the parts wound on wound, taking care to ensure that they overlap exactly, and that the bark coincides; then hold the unit together with ligatures. When the graft is complete, the twigs are cut off below the graft, and the tree is well stocked with them wherever they were lacking. Grafting is particularly used on fruit trees pruned into goblets, espaliers, fans and especially cattails, to add branches where they are lacking, make them more regular and more pleasing to the eye, and thus increase their yield. 40." Approach grafting for head replacement. Cauchoise graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 4. a, triangular notch of the subject; b, notch of the young subject which is to provide the new head; c, the same notch seen from the front. 009 The head of a subject is cut off, or the wound is joined when the tree has been broken by an accident, then a triangular notch is made on the cut area. As close as possible to its foot, plant a young tree whose head is beginning to form; tilt it over the cut of the first one; and, by means of a wedge-shaped notch made in its stem, fix it in the triangular notch of the tree, and make a ligature to hold everything together until perfect recovery. Then the young tree is cut below the graft, and the old trunk, supplying abundant sap to the new head, soon makes it develop dimensions equal to those of the first one it has lost. If the wind, or any other accident, breaks or destroys the head of a tree in an avenue, a stagger or an orchard, it is much better to replace it with another, using this graft, than to replace it with a young subject whose growth will be very long, even supposing that the neighbouring trees do not choke it. The inhabitants of Normandy, Picardy, etc., use no other means to repair the damage that the wind sometimes causes to their immense plantations of cider apple trees. 41. Grafting using a prop approach. Gresse Duhamel, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 8. a, an old tree, its trunk destroyed by age, in b, is replaced by young trees, c,c, c,c. Around the trunk of a large tree are planted several subjects of a certain strength. The following year, when they have fully recovered, their heads are cut off, and the ends of their stems are pruned into tenon shapes. Mortises are dug into the trunk of the large tree, into which the tenons are inserted and fixed. Young trees should be planted at a distance from the old trunk, calculated so that when they are bent to fix 4 them, they do not form an angle of more than 35 to 40 degrees. An extremely strong ligature is made over the wounds, with chissons and old ropes on top. If, when the trunk of an old tree has deteriorated, we wish to preserve it, either because we attach pleasant memories to it, or because of its product, we undercut it in this way, and greatly increase its vigor at the same time as prolonging its existence. 42. Grafting by shield approach. Denainvilliers graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 14. a, incision of subject and insertion of graft; b, bevel of young subject. An elongated, flat bevel is cut on the bud of the last or penultimate shoot of young trees planted the previous year around a tree that is not as old as the previous graft. On the bark of the tree to be grafted, two inverted T-shaped incisions (L) are made, and the bark is lifted with the spatula of the grafting machine; then, between the bast and the sapwood, the bevels of the stems of the subjects are inserted, and a solid ligature is made, as for the escutcheon graft. This graft offers the same results as the previous one, but has the advantage of being much easier and more reliable. It is used on young fruit trees, and even more often on ornamental trees whose growth is to be increased and given a picturesque shape. 43. Grafting using a brace approach. Fougeroux graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 15. a, subject; b, b, young subjects grafted in c, c. Young subjects are planted around a tree that is still young, as for the previous grafts. When1009 their recovery is perfect, they are bent over the middle tree, and their stems are notched (without cutting the head) from the first layer of bark to the sapwood. On the trunk of the tree, notches are made corresponding to those of the subjects, covering them one by one, and the ligature is made as for ordinary grafting. When the grafting is complete, the heads are cut off. This technique is used for the same purposes as the three preceding ones, and has the advantage over them that, if the operation is unsuccessful, there is little or no damage to the subjects; but it is also a little less solid and a lot less clean. 44. Complicated approach grafting. Rozier graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 5. b, 6, mother branches; a, a, etc., scction points where buds are grafted. Franc-grafted trees are planted in rows, and at pruning, two opposite mother branches are established, horizontally trellised and as close to the ground as possible. Buds are left to grow on the branches, and kept as close together as possible. As soon as they have reached eighteen inches or two feet in length, we incline them, one to the right, the other to the left, and so on, so as to make them form a sort of lozange grid on each mother branch; their inclination should never be more than 30 to 45 degrees. At each point of section, the buds are incised down to the medullary sheath, and the wounds are joined in the same way as for ordinary approach grafting. As the buds increase in length, their tips are grafted again. This is extremely useful for forming hedges and fruit-covering palisades in orchards, especially in landscaped gardens of a 10 rural type. Of all fruit trees, the apple tree is the most suitable for forming solid, well-defended fences with high yields. Grafting to cover an amputation. 45. Close bark grafting. Pl. 4, fig. 7. c, c, c, strips of preserved bark; d, cut of subject above graft e; b, strips applied to cut, above escutcheon a.. Before cutting the stem of a subject, above a graft or an eye, we begin by encircling the bark all around at a height (above the place where we want to cut) equal to the diameter of the stem. The bark is then longitudinally split into three, four or more strips, depending on the size of the subject; the strips are peeled off the sapwood and turned over; the wood is then cut to the desired height, and the scar is perfectly joined. The strips are brought closer together, and cut into triangles whose points should meet towards the center of the cut when they have been tilted. The libers are made to coincide as closely as possible, and the apparatus is held in place with grafting wax. This method welds the bark together very well, covers the amputation and prevents heat and humidity from penetrating the wood or pith. This type of grafting can be useful for precious trees with soft, supple wood, grown in open soil. Grafting to repair tree bark. If a tree is stripped of its bark by any kind of accident, such as an impact, the alternating contact of heat and humidity on the sapwood will soon cause it to crack, canker will set in, and the tree will be lost in no time unless promptly remedied. When the wound is large, it is futile to cover it with grafting wax, Saint-Fiacre's ointment or any other ingredient; it can only be covered by means of grease. 46. Graft in escutcheon without eyes. Tillet graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, sig. 1. a, wound; b, plate. On a useless tree, of the same species or only of the same genus as the injured tree, provided however that the analogies are sufficient, a patch of bark slightly larger than the wound of the tree to be preserved is removed and given a regular shape. The bark of the wound of the useful tree is cut to the same shape and exact dimensions as the plate, so that the latter can be placed and inlaid as accurately as possible. With the libers of the plate and subject perfectly joined all the way round, and the plate well applied to the sapwood at all points, the graft is held in place by a ligature, and the edges of the wound are covered with grafting wax. Needless to say, this operation can only be carried out during the sap flow, as a shock will only loosen the bark of a tree at this time. However, if it were otherwise, we would cover the wound with Saint-Fiacre's ointment, and wait for the right moment to operate; but then we would have to thoroughly clean the sapwood, and even remove all the dried-out or mouldy surface. MO SECOND DIVISION. SPECIFIC GRAFTS FOR CERTAIN FRUIT TREES. The grafts we have just listed are suitable for most fruit trees, but there are a few whose particular organization requires special methods if they are to be successfully grafted. Vines, walnuts, chestnuts, olives and oranges are particularly well-suited to this situation, and we'll be providing separate articles on each of them, supplemented as far as we can. However, we do not pretend to say that, apart from the methods we are about to describe, there is no procedure that can lead to the same end; we will simply give the most reliable of those most commonly used. Grafting vines. For a long time it was believed, and this error is still held by some growers, that the vine had no bark, and that consequently it was not necessary to make the libers coincide in order to carry out the recovery of its split graft. It is certain that this coincidence is as necessary in this plant as in all other tree species, whatever some authors may have said; and, far from grafting being easier on this shrub, which it should be if grafting could be carried out on all its parts as they claim, it is on the contrary all the more difficult, as the bark is thinner and the bast intimately attached to the sapwood. 47. Double V (W) split graft. Pl. 2, fig. 9. a, a, drying horns. The only difference is that mortality, which always occurs to a greater or lesser extent on the cut of an ordinary subject, descends in the vine much lower than on other fruit trees, and prevents recovery if this inconvenience is not counteracted. To achieve this, the graft is pruned into a knife-edge or wedge shape, without retreating so that it sits on the cut surface of the subject. This wedge, cut from an eye, will be very thin on the side that is to be inside, and the opposite side will be thicker and covered with bark. If the wedge is, for example, eighteen lines long from the eye where the cut begins, the subject is split two inches deep, so that the eye can be brought down to six lines below the upper end of the split; for experience has taught us that, in a subject of ordinary size, six lines or so of the two horns made by the split dry out and die before recovery. The graft is inserted in the usual way, i.e., by matching the bark as closely as possible, and the ligature is made starting from the bottom and working upwards, to just above the eye, where the two horns begin to stop touching the shoot. The following year, when the graft is perfectly fused, the two dried horns are cut off, as close as possible to the bud, and the wound is bound together to heal perfectly. This method is the safest of all, but as the operation is rather difficult and time-consuming, it is rarely used except for grafting trellises grown in gardens to supply table grapes. It is still used with great advantage for grafting walnut and all trees with thick, voluminous pith. 48.* Buried slot graft. Pl. 2, fig. 10. a, a, ground level. As this technique is used in large-scale cultivation, we O6 will describe it as it is practiced in the Lyon area. A number of branches proportionate to the quality of the stock to be grafted are prepared by wedge pruning, and placed in a basket carried by a woman. Once this has been done, a man with a pickaxe begins to dig up the first stock in a row of vines to a depth of six inches; he removes any small roots that may be found within five inches of the surface of the earth, and moves on to the second stock in the same row to prepare it in the same way, then to the third, the fourth, and so on; another worker follows him, and cuts the vine at its roots, to a depth of soil that circumstances require, but which cannot be less than three inches and more than six; he unites the cut area, and opens, by means of a strong, slightly curved serpette, one, two, three or four slits, according to the size of the subject. The woman who comes next places as many grafts as there are slits; finally, a third worker, who comes last, secures the grafts by compressing a little earth around them with his hands, then covers them with a pickaxe, but taking extreme care not to disturb them, and leaving two eyes out of the earth. These grafts, made on three- or four-year-old subjects, are very successful; but the vigor of their vegetation is incredible when they are established on old vines whose nature can be changed, for example, from red to white. Three men and a woman can easily graft ten to twelve ares in a day. 49. Split grafting on provins. Olivier de Serres graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 12. Around a vine stock, well-cultivated sarmens are placed, and their ends are cut four or five inches below the surface of the earth. These are split and girdled, in a single split, with slightly thinner, but equally well-aruged branches; then D6 cover with soil, leaving two eyes as for the previous method. This method is ideal for gradually changing the species in a vineyard, and only by replacement, as well as for multiplying valuable species more rapidly in a garden. It can also be used for foreign trees, after preparing their stock by cutting. 50. Buried slot graft with grooves. Vine graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 10. a, a, soil surface. It is done in much the same way as the penultimate graft. The stump is uncovered, the stem cut three or four inches into the soil, and two triangular grooves formed, one on each side. Two sarmens are cut into triangular points, one side of which is left with its bark. These are fitted exactly into the subject's grooves, with the bark coinciding, and covered with earth so as to leave only two eyes above the surface. This method is used, like the previous ones, to transform mediocre varieties into good ones, and to increase the yield; but the difficulty it presents in digging the grooves, and in establishing the grafts firmly enough not to be disturbed by the soil with which they are covered, means that it can only be used to advantage in gardens, and is not very suitable for large-scale cultivation. 51. ** Split graft in the middle of the wood. Pl. 2, fig. 16. a, the split shoot into which the blade has been placed b. On large, well-arched wood from the previous year, a slit is made which divides it in two throughout its thickness, and between two knots. Then cut a graft of the variety to be propagated, giving it the shape 009 of a knife blade, very flat, with a sharp point at both ends, thickening towards the middle where the eye is, and with bark on both sides. It is inserted into the slit with its lips parted, and the bark on both sides is made to coincide. To do this, the blade must be as wide as the subject is thick. A strong ligature is made with strips of bark or very flexible wicker. This graft is widely used in gardens around Paris to propagate precious varieties. 52. Split graft with double notch support. Pl. a, fig. 14. a, tip of bevel ending in a flute beak; b, notched notch at base of bevel. Select a branch to be grafted of exactly the same size as the subject. We cut it into a long bevel ending at its tip in a small flute beak, and make a notch at its top; we cut the subject in exactly the same way, but in the opposite direction, so as to be able to adjust both when laying them down. The ligature is made in the same way as the previous one. It is as widely used as the other method in the Paris area; but, while it is easier, it is also much less reliable. 53. crown graft, buried, with notch incision. Hervy graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 1. The stem of a vine is cut at the root collar, and a triangular incision three to four lines deep is made in the cut area. A very ripe vine shoot is chosen, and its large tip is cut into a wedge the same length as the notch, into which it is fitted as exactly as possible, so that the bark coincides on at least one side. Cover with soil as before. 2. 5 006 This graft has been recommended for large-scale use in vine-growing countries, but as it presents the same difficulties as the previous one, it has not been successfully put into use, and has remained relegated to gardens, where it is used to propagate young, hardwood trees whose grafts do not easily recover. 54. Aerial crown graft with notch incision. Pl. 3, fig. 1. This technique is performed in the same way as the previous one, except that it is carried out above ground on young trees at an arbitrary height, and the graft is attached to the tree by means of a ligature and grafting wax. This method is suitable for the same purposes, and has the same disadvantages as large-scale grafting. 55. Grafting by Chinese approach. Two vines of different grape colors are split lengthwise, to about one-third of their diameter; the thinner side is removed, and the two stems are joined wound to wound; then a ligature is made all along the two stems, which become one. This process produces vines that yield grapes of two colors or species. 56. Grafting using a complicated Chinese approach. Chinese grafting, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 4. Several young plants of approximately the same strength, but of different colors and varieties, are taken and planted as close together as possible. When they have recovered perfectly, they are split lengthwise into equal triangular quarters, and only one quarter is kept per plant. The size and angular dimension of the quarters must be calculated so that, when joined together, they form a round stem, with the bark joining well on the outside and the wood on the inside. This union is held together by a ligature, which is left in place until the weld is complete along the entire length. When this grafting is successfully repeated, fruit of several varieties can be obtained on the same stock, as many as the number of quarters joined together. But some people still believe that this method produces grapes of strange shapes and particular flavors on the same stock, a mixture of all the varieties combined in a single fruit: this error is all the more crude because it contradicts all the laws that nature has established for plant organization, and offends common sense as much as plant physiology. 57. Grafting by perforation approach. Gresse Virgile, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 9. A hole is drilled diametrically through the stem of a vine using a crankshaft. The branch of the species to be grafted is inserted, trimmed to two eyes above the point where it exits the hole, and both sides of the wound are luted with grafting wax. It used to be thought that this method greatly increased the volume of the grapes, and that the least result was to have them the size of plums. This wax has the same effect as the others, and the error has fallen into the disrepute it deserves. 58. Grafting by heterogeneous approach through perforation. This is absolutely the same as the previous method, but instead of choosing a vine stock as the subject, the graft is placed in a walnut branch perforated to receive it. Quite a few authors have written that the grapes obtained by this method grow to an O00 enormous size, but that they are not edible, because they contract the bitter taste of walnut stain. This absurdity no longer needs to be refuted today; for not only would the fruit not be as they say, even if the recovery were successful, but the latter is impossible. 59. Grafting by twisting approach. Caton graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 3. Open a pit and plant three to five crossettes of different varieties. The strongest bud of each plant is allowed to grow, and the others are destroyed. When they have reached a sufficient height, they are twisted slightly one on top of the other, and tied lengthwise to force them to weld together as they grow, forming a single stem. It was once thought that grapes obtained in this way should be variegated in color and have the mixed flavor of all the varieties making up the aggregation. This error has fallen into disuse, and we now know that only unmixed varieties can be obtained, but several on the same stem. 60. Slit graft of the laxative and unguentère vine. Constantin César graft, by Thouin. We have retained the name given to this graft by Constantine Caesar, liv. 4, chap. 7, as we report on the virtues, as marvelous as they are ridiculous, that he attributes to the fruit obtained. A vine stock is cut between two plots of land, split in the middle of its diameter, the pith removed, and replaced with colors, aromatics and medicines. Two shoots are prepared as for the simple split graft, inserted in the same way on the two split sides, and covered with soil, leaving two shoots protruding from the surface of the soil. According to the author, this method will produce 96 green, blue, white grapes, etc., depending on the colors used; they will taste of cinnamon, nutmeg, clove or other flavors; and they will be laxative or unguentère, to use the author's expression, depending on the virtue of the medicinal agents used. Certainly, we had to rely heavily on the credulity of our ancestors to dare print such things. If we have described these last three grafts, it is because in writing our work we have made it our duty not only to give all the true and interesting facts, but also to point out the numerous errors accredited, even today, by authors whose learned theories, all taken from books, have not been subjected by them to the tests of experience. Walnut grafts. The walnut, being a very soft tree, is very difficult to graft. However, it is quite often successful when grafted with the following grafts: crest with growing eye, ordinary flute, split, double W, and even better the following. (IX. FLUTE GRAFTS. These are gem grafts. The operation consists in bringing back one or more eyes placed on a ring of bark, without sapwood. They are commonly known as "whistle", "cannon", "ring", "pipe", "cornuchet", "flute" and "chalumeau" grafts. They are most commonly used for fruit trees grown in fields and large orchards, and for propagating some species of foreign hardwood trees. The best times to do this are in spring, when sap begins, and in August, when sap ends. To carry out the operation, a 006 tube of bark with good eyes is removed from the branches of the trees to be propagated; a young branch is selected from the subject, from which a ring of bark of the same length as the tube is removed, and this is placed exactly in the place of the removed ring. Other care consists of sealing the scissures to prevent air, water and other foreign bodies from entering the wound. The best time to operate is when the weather is mild, with no rain or sunshine, and when the sun's drying rays are not too strong. As soon as the eyes of the graft start to grow, all the buds growing underneath are cut off, and the subject is cut above the last eye of the tube. They are very solids and are not prone to being blown off by the wind). 61. ** Ring flute graft. Jefferson graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 11. a, wound made on subject; b, bark ring with eye. On the tree to be propagated, select a branch as big as, or bigger than, the subject to be grafted. Using a serpette, we circle the bark above and below an eye, in the shape of a ring, which we detach by splitting it perpendicularly on one of its sides, and lifting it with the spatula of the squeegee. The same operation is then performed on the subject, i.e. a ring of exactly the same bark width is detached from its stem in the same way, but without bothering whether it has eyes or not. The removed ring is returned to its place on the branch of the variety you wish to propagate, taking extreme care to ensure that the libers join at the top and bottom. No ligature is used, but the whole is covered with Saint-Fiacre ointment or grafting wax. Neither the branches nor the head of the tree are removed until the recovery is complete. The two most favorable times for this type of grafting are when the sap is at its peak in spring, and at the end of August. I This type of graft has the advantage of never mutilating the subject, because if it does not vegetate, the bark of the ring remains in place, and takes the place of that which was removed. Not only is it suitable for propagating walnut trees, but also all rare hardwood trees, such as American oaks and chestnuts. 62. ** Flute graft by juxtaposition, or whistle graft. Whistle graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 12. a, wound made on subject; b, bark pipe. The head of the tree to be grafted is cut off, and a bark tube one to three inches long is removed at the end. From the tree you wish to propagate, select a branch of exactly the same size as the subject, and remove a ring or tube of bark from the large end, a little shorter than that of the subject, but with two or three good eyes; fit it onto the subject in place of the one removed, and make sure the bark is joined at the bottom. By splitting in all directions the excess wood of the subject which extends beyond the ring of the graft, we reduce it, as it were, to lint, which we fold down on each side of the graft to hold it in place, and with the grafting wax we smooth out all the scissures. In most of France, this method is used almost exclusively for grafting walnut, chestnut, mulberry, fig and other fruit trees with thick bark and abundant pith; it is also used for seed and stone trees. Chestnut grafting. This tree can be grafted with a growing eye when the sap has risen, but success is more certain when the following grafts are used, especially the first. 208 a, subject stripped of its bark; b, b, b, b, strips of bark; c, pipe; f, f, strips raised and applied by means of ligature g; h, one of the pipe's eyes; d, bevelled amputation. The subject's head is cut off, but instead of removing a pipe of bark, as in the previous operation, it is split longitudinally into four or five strips two or three inches long. On a branch of the tree to be propagated, a pipe is removed, slightly shorter than the strips, but longer than in the whistle graft, and fitted with four or five eyes. The straps are detached from the subject, and turned over towards the ground while the tube is inserted; then they are raised onto the graft they are protruding from, and fixed to the top of the bark-free stem by means of a ligature. Make sure they don't cover any of the eyes. Grafting wax is then applied to all the scissures. This method serves the same purpose as the previous graft, and is almost as widely used. 64. "Split flute graft. Pl. 1, sig. 11. a, subject stripped of a ring of bark; b, split pipe of the graft. This is done in exactly the same way as the previous ones, with just one difference. If all you had to lift the graft pipe was a branch larger than the subject, you would split the pipe longitudinally on one of its sides before lifting it; then you would bring it back onto the subject, applying it around its entire circumference; and, bringing the two sides together, we would immediately see how much wider it would be, and we would remove a strap so as to make it very-fair, and to make it envelop the stem at the same time as its two edges would meet exactly. A 908 graft ligature would then be made, and finished off like the others. Less used than the others, because it is only used when necessary, it is suitable for the same purposes. 65.* Split flute graft, with strap. This is the counterpart of the previous one. When the branch on which the graft is to be placed is smaller than the stem on which it is to be placed, the pipe is split as described above, and applied to the subject. The space uncovered on one side is filled with one of the strips of bark from the subject, which has been trimmed to a width proportionate to the need, and which has not been peeled off the sapwood to overturn it with the others. It ends like the previous one, and is used for the same purposes. 66. Grafting by approaching several debarked stems. Magon graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 4. A pit is opened, into which several young plants of the same height and size, and above all of the same variety, are planted. When they are fully recovered, they are barked on one side, facing each other, along the whole length of their stems; they are brought together so that their wounds cover each other, forming a single stem, and they are held firmly in position by means of a ligature with strips of fresh bark. This grafting technique is said to considerably increase the volume and vigor of the tree, as well as the quantity of its fruit. Examples include the famous chestnut trees of Mount Etna and the ancient olive trees of Spain. 009 Olive tree grafts. This tree can be grafted in the same way as above, by side grafting in peg (n° 32), in escutcheon, and in the following manner: 67. Square escutcheon graft. Aristotle graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1,sig. 8. a, subject's wound; b, strap lowered; c, escutcheon on; dpl, escutcheon placed and strap raised. Three incisions are made on the subject, one transverse, the other two longitudinal, starting on either side of the horizontal one, and descending parallel to four or five lines. They will be three or four lines apart, and will represent a long square with the bottom line missing. This square strap is lifted and lowered onto the subject. Or, on a branch of the tree to be multiplied, cut a square escutcheon with a good eye, exactly the same size as the subject's plate, and apply it to the wound it is intended to cover with the greatest accuracy. Once this has been done, we lift the bark strip from the subject, cover the escutcheon with it up to the underside of its eye, smooth the scissures with grafting wax, and bind the whole like other escutcheon grafts... It seems that this grafting method was formerly widely used, and was perfectly successful; but, as it is a little meticulous in its execution, and takes a lot of time, it has almost entirely been abandoned; we nevertheless recommend it as one of the most advantageous. 68. Approach grafting on disgenic root. Columelle graft, by Thouin. A young fig tree is planted at the foot of an olive tree, and when it has fully recovered, the roots are dug up and the stem cut off at the collar. A triangular notch is made in the cut area, which is continued at some depth by a slit. An olive branch is lowered onto the stump, its wedge-shaped end inserted halfway into the cut. If necessary, the branch is held in place with a bark binding, and covered with earth. It used to be thought, and Columelle thought, that this graft proved that all trees, even those of absolutely different natures, could, with certain precautions, take over when grafted onto each other. But this experiment only proves what we already knew, that the olive tree easily takes root from the marcotte; for the graft only succeeds because it is buried, and grows roots that nourish it. We have therefore only mentioned this method to point out an error that is still quite widespread among amateurs and farmers with little knowledge of plant physiology. Orange tree grafts. This tree, interesting for its beauty, the sweet fragrance of its flowers and the delicious quality of its fruit, has always attracted the attention of growers. It has therefore been the subject of a great deal of experimentation, to ascertain the most suitable cultivation and the surest means of multiplying its many varieties. The result has been the invention of a fairly large number of grafts specific to this tree, in addition to approach, split and escutcheon grafts, which are more or less suitable for it and other fruit trees. (X. TWIG GRAFTS. These differ from ordinary split grafts in that the branch planted on the subject must be covered with leaves, flowering buds and even young fruit. The only way to ensure 008 their success is to carry them out when the first sap is most active, and with special precautions that we will list in their respective articles. They have the advantage over all others of producing fruit with surprising speed; for example, it is easy to sow a seedling, graft the stem it produces, and obtain flowers and fruit, all within the space of a few months, and, with certainty, before the year is out; but they are also less durable than the others, and less easy to recover. They require more or less submissive care to regulate the degree of heat, light, watering and even the quantity of air they absolutely require). 69.* Twig graft for orange trees, 1st type. Huard graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 5. b, triangular notch in subject; a, graft insertion. It is performed on a subject from eight months to three years old, or even much younger if it is strong enough. We cut off its head, and make a triangular cut on one side of its stem, extended longitudinally by five or six lines. Choose a branch with its leaves, flower buds and nascent fruit: prune it by the large end into a triangular point, leaving the bark on one side, and fit it into the notch so that it fills it exactly, without revealing any part of the wood of the subject or the graft. The pot in which the plant is placed is placed on a warm surface covered with a frame, and shaded for the first few days. The frame can be replaced very advantageously by a tarpaulin or a warm greenhouse with a layer of tannin, and then the graft is smothered for a few days with a glass funnel, which is covered until it has given the first signs of vegetation; the small opening of the funnel is plugged with a paper plug, which is removed some time before the funnel is removed, in order to gradually accustom the plant to the open air. This method, suitable for making very young orange trees produce fruit, is also suitable for propagating exotic evergreen trees from the hottest climates, which are grown here in hot greenhouses. 70. Twig graft for orange trees, 2' type. Riedlé graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 6. It is done in much the same way as the previous one, except that a triangular notch is made in the cut surface of the subject, leaving two recesses, one on each side. The twig is wedge-cut, leaving a notch on each side to press against the subject's recesses. The braid is adjusted by making the bark coincide as in the previous process, and is subjected to similar treatment until it recovers perfectly. It can be used for the same purposes, but has the added advantage of being suitable for fruit trees. 71. Graft in twig for orange trees, 3rd type; or heel graft, doe foot graft. Collignon graft, by Thouin. Pl.a, fig. 5. a, the subject; b, the subject's bell and the graft's tooth; c, the tongue. The end of a twig is cut into a very long tongue, and a sort of tooth in the shape of a very short wedge is made above the tongue. In front of this notch, a strip of bark the same size as the tongue of the graft is removed from the stem, and the two wounds are joined together by inserting the prong of the branch into the notch of the subject, and applying its tongue exactly to the part where the strip has been removed. Ligature is made in the same way as for the previous ones, the scissures are healed with grafting wax, and the plant is subjected to the same treatment. It is used in the same way as the others, and is particularly suitable for propagating hollies, myrtles and laurels. 72. Branch graft for orange trees, 4th type; or Daphne graft. Riché graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3. -Fig. 7. a, notch into which the base of the bevel enters. This graft is almost identical to the previous one; however, it differs in that the branch tongue, a little thicker than the other, is beveled at its tip. At the base of the wound made in the stem to remove the strap, a notch is cut to receive the bevel of the graft. The graft is then adjusted, the ligature made, and the same procedure followed. This graft is particularly useful for thin, slender subjects. It is very well suited to delicate shrubs with barely woody stems, such as most daphnes, and its recovery is so complete that flowering shoots can be used without aborting the fruit they are intended to produce. 73.* Twig graft for orange trees, 5th type. Pl. 5, fig. 10. a, triangular base of twig; b, bevelled subject. Three or four young plants are planted in the same pot, as close together as possible. Their heads are cut off at the same level, and a long bevel is made at the top of the stem. Choose a twig slightly larger than one of the subjects, and cut its base into a point the same length as the bevels, and triangular if there are three subjects, square if there are D0 to four, or offering as many sides as there are stems. We fit the bevels of the subjects onto the sides of the graft, so that they completely cover the wound at its base, so that the barks of the graft and the subjects coincide perfectly at the top of the graft, and the barks of the subjects with each other on their longitudinal cut. A strong ligature is made, and the apparatus is covered with grafting wax; the whole is then placed on a warm layer and under glass, until perfect recovery. This very curious graft produces very picturesque trees, as the young stems can be braided before the operation. It also produces very vigorous buds, all the more beautiful as they are nourished by several complete root systems. 74.* Woodless escutcheon graft. Poederlé graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 3. a, incision; b, escutcheon. This graft is made in exactly the same way as the ordiary escutcheon grafts (n°*1, a, 3.); only, instead of leaving a portion of wood in the escutcheon, it is removed with the tip of the grafting tool, so as to leave only the smallest possible amount of sapwood, precisely under the eye, in order not to remove its gem. The tree is then laid down, tied and driven like any other of its kind. In addition to its excellent suitability for orange trees, it is also suitable for grafting hardwood trees such as myrtles, hollies and other exotic plants similar to these. It can be done at will with a growing or dormant eye. 75." Inverted escutcheon graft. Gresse Schneewoogt, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 5. a, incision; b, escutcheon. The triangle-shaped escutcheon is removed as in the previous example, but instead of cutting the tip 006 below the eye, it is cut above. We realize that the incision in the bark of the subject must be reversed in the same way, i.e., instead of having the shape of a straight T, it will have that of an inverted I. To achieve this, the longitudinal incision is made above the transverse one, instead of below. Incidentally, it ends in the same way as the previous grafts. This graft is almost the only one used in the south of France, especially in Genoa and Hyères, to propagate orange trees, and all those sent to us by the trade in these countries are treated in this way. It is also suitable for multiplying trees whose sap is abundant and gummy, and we believe it could be used to ensure the recovery of crests on resinous trees. This is the end of the list of grafts most commonly used to propagate fruit trees. In the nomenclature of the following sections, you will find a few that can be applied to them in certain circumstances, just as most of the first ones can be given to ornamental and forest trees, as the occasion arises. duucbbubbb SECOND SECTION. GRAFTING FOREST TREES. To propagate forest trees, we could, in the greatest number of cases, use split, approach and escutcheon grafts, which are used to propagate fruit trees; but as a tree is always taller and more vigorous when it is free-standing, and it is these qualities in particular that we look for in those that populate forests, and not the rarity of the varieties, we will make little use of them. Either to decorate an ornamental park, and produce picturesque effects, or to obtain woods that nature must begin to shape, in order to make them suitable for use in the arts or in certain constructions, we are obliged to graft by particular methods, and it is these different grafts that we will deal with in this section. Grafting resinous trees (XI. HERBACEOUS GRAFTS. We had long been experimenting with herbaceous grafts, and were using them to great advantage in our establishment, when Baron Tschudy published his excellent Mémoire on this interesting subject in Metz. We are pleased to do justice to this esteemed man; although he is not the author of this discovery (as the author of L'Horticulteur français says, although he must have seen it on our premises long before Mr. Tschudy mentioned it), he gave it much greater scope than we did, by practicing it on vegetable plants. We have always restricted their use to resinous trees and succulents, which prompted Mr. Thouin to give our name to a graft of this species. (See his Mémoire already quoted, page 49.) In any case, herbaceous grafts belong to the division of split grafts; the only difference is that, instead of using a twig from the previous or older shoot, we use a bud that is a quarter or a third developed, and still has all the softness that its herbaceous nature gives it; once it has become woody, it is no longer suitable for regrowth. It should be inserted into a notch made on a branch of the same size, nature and degree of development. As 2. 6́ neither is yet barked, and the circulation of sap takes place in vessels that fill their entire thickness, it is necessary as far as possible to establish correspondence between these vessels, and to place the graft close to a shepherd's crook or a leaf that attracts sap to it until recovery has taken place, after which they can be cut down. Needless to say, this type of grafting should only be done during the peak of sap flow, as it is impractical at any other time) 76.* Herbaceous grafting of resinous trees. Gresses des Unitiges, by Tschudy. Pl. 5, fig. 11. b, a bud of the year; a, graft insertion. When the terminal bud of a green tree, such as pine, fir, larch, pear tree, etc., has reached about two or three inches in length, it is cut and wedged at its base; the subject is cut below the current year's bud, and a fairly deep, triangular notch is made in the cut area. The braid is inserted into the cut so as to fill it exactly. Next, we make a very loose ligature, which we take great care to loosen again once the graft has taken hold, as can be seen by the vegetation of the graft; or better still, we hold it in place with grafting wax into which we've put a lot of wax and tallow to make it softer, and give it the ability to spread as the stem increases in thickness. It is covered with a small paper cornet to protect it from contact with air and light until it fully recovers. This graft has the advantage over all others of being extremely solid; the reason is that it is held to the subject by all the wood fibers, whereas the others are held only by the bark and a few layers of sapwood. It can be used not only on green trees, but also on all plants, trees or grasses, which grow a vertical main stem. 77. Grafting by tongue approach. Aiton graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 12. The green trees to be grafted are planted or sown in pots. When they have reached the right height, they are brought close to the tree whose species you want to propagate. A longitudinal wound is made in the stem of the subject, down to the sapwood, as well as on the branch or twig to be grafted; and, if desired, a staple is made in the middle by means of a projecting notch and a hook to receive it. When the two wounds overlap perfectly, the ligature is made as in ordinary approach grafting, and the pot is firmly fixed, so that it cannot be disturbed and the parts loosened by watering or cultivating the soil. It is ideal for propagating precious species of resinous and evergreen trees. It is also used to bury evergreen trees on deciduous ones, which are difficult to regrow. 78. Crested grafting of resinous trees. Magneville graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 6. a, the incision with a triangular wound; b, the escutcheon with the angular point on which the bud is placed. A T-shaped incision is made on the stem of a young tree, as for an ordinary scallop graft, and a double incision in the shape of a broken chevron is made two or two and a half lines above the upper bar of the T, cutting the bark about a line wide and down to the sapwood. A bud eight or ten lines long, covered with bark like an ordinary escutcheon, is removed from the tree to be propagated; it is inserted into the wound after lifting the bark with the spatula of the grafting machine, and a ligature is made. As a result, the eye is positioned above the T-bar, and is less likely to be drowned by the sap. This graft works well not only on resinous trees, but also on those with gummy or very abundant sap. For coniferous trees, the crown side graft, n° 30, can also be used with some advantage. Grafting forest trees to make them suitable for use in the arts or for shipbuilding. 79. Grafting by approaching branches onto the tree providing them. Michaux graft, by Thouin. Pl.4, sig. 3. a, T-shaped incision, receiving the end of the branch. The longest branches of a tree are selected, bent in a circular shape, and carefully so as not to break them, so that their top touches the stem of the subject. The tip is trimmed in the shape of a flute beak, and inserted into a T-shaped incision in the bark of the stem, in the same way as for the inverted shield graft. A ligature is made to secure it, and the scissures are smoothed with gresfer wax. This produces curves suitable for art and the navy, and picturesque effects in gardens. 80. Approach grafting on trunk. Sylvain graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 8. a, a, the two subjects; b, one of the two with its e-notch. Two young trees with straight, high stems are planted side by side. They are bent one on top of the other, and at the point where they meet and cross, two transverse or slightly oblique cuts are made in the stems, penetrating as far as the medullary case 008; they are fitted one inside the other, with the bark coinciding as closely as possible, and the apparatus is held firmly in place by means of a wide ligature. The operation should be carried out as close as possible to the two heads, so as to bring them together as one. It can be used to produce angular woods, rustic one-piece frames for doors in country homes, and very picturesque effects in landscaped gardens. 81." Approach grafting by joining trunks together. Hymen graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 9. a, a, heads of two trees, joined by grafting, in b. It is done in much the same way as the previous method, but instead of crossing the stems of young trees, they are brought together in parallel. Two cuts are made longitudinally, facing each other and penetrating as far as the medullary case, and they are joined so that one is covered by the other. The heads are brought so close together that they are obliged to intermingle their foliage naturally, which does not happen in the previous method; and the stems, instead of rounding off in the same way, form a very sharp angle below their junction. Ligation is carried out in the same way as for ordinary grafts, by approach. It is quite commonly used to bring together the two saps of a dioecious species on a single individual, to provide the arts with very angular wood, and finally to produce very picturesque effects in landscape gardens. 82. Approach grafting with four splinters. Dumoutier graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 10. a, a, a, etc., the splinters seen separately on one of the subjects b, and joined on both in e. The stems of two young trees are brought together, as in the previous example, but instead of simply making a longitudinal cut, a piece of bark is removed from each tree at the same height; On each wound, two wooden splinters are placed in opposite directions, i.e., in one case, the upper splinter turned point down, and the lower point up; in the other, the upper splinter turned point up, and the lower point down. These are set into each other from the side, and the ligature is made. It is suitable for the same uses as the previous one; and, if it is a little more difficult to do, it is also much stronger. 83. Grafting by feather-beak approach. Gresse Vrigny, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 12. b, bevel of young subject; a, notch of rootstock tree. A young tree is planted at the foot of a mature tree. The following year, when the tree has fully recovered, the head is cut off, and the end of the stem is trimmed to a very long bevel, with only the bark in the last third of its length. In the bark of the trunk of the tree to be grafted, a cut is made down to the sapwood, exactly the same shape and dimensions as the bevel of the subject, and it is firmly attached by means of a ligature. This graft gives extraordinary vigor to a tree, because it feeds it with two sets of roots. In time, it can still provide angular wood for the navy, and can be replaced by the escutcheon approach graft (n° 42.) 84. Approach grafting of gems. Muséum de Thouin graft. Pl. 4, fig. 16. a, a, terminal branches of the two subjects; b, b, reunion of the two eye portions. Two young trees are planted at a short distance D04 from each other; the following year, their terminal buds are split into two parts, with the precaution of leaving a portion of gemma in the half retained. One of the split portions is removed, along with a small strip of bark and wood underneath. Bring the two half-buttons of the two subjects together, and fit them against each other so as to cover each other's wounds and form a single eye. Ligate with great care, starting lower than the graft and tightening firmly, then tightening less as you go up, and finally tightening only enough to reunite the two half-buttons when you have succeeded. The whole is covered with a good layer of grafting wax, except on the eye, where it is applied very lightly, and only up to the height of the lower third. It is more suitable than the others for bringing two individuals of different sexes together in a very intimate way. It produces a picturesque effect in gardens, and provides the arts with very solid angular woods of rare shapes. Its only disadvantage is that it is meticulous and difficult to do, but it is easily repeated, as we were able to see at the Jardin du Roi, where M. Thouin had it done for the first time in June 1805. 85. Arc grafting. Arc grafting, by Thouin. As in the previous method, two young trees are planted at a certain distance from each other, and then, after they have been transplanted, they are bent one over the other, rounding them in a semicircle. Their heads are cut off, and the stems are joined together by means of two corresponding notches, with or without staples, overlapping each other, or by two bevels, or finally by a split buttress. It provides curved wood for shipbuilding, and could be used, in a landscape garden, to form living bridges with an effect as singular as it is picturesque, especially if the lateral branches were grafted into lozanges, to make a natural grid that would replace the floor. We assume that two parallel arches would be established, and that the grid would be formed by the lateral branches growing between the two arches. Grafting evergreen and hardwood trees. This can be replaced by ordinary approach grafting, and even quite often by split or escutcheon grafting, but with much less chance of success. 86. "Approach grafting of a lateral branch onto a stem. Varon graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 13. a, insertion of the graft; b, its wedge-shaped notch; c, triangular notch of the subject. Young trees are raised in pots, and when they have reached a suitable size, their heads are cut off. A triangular notch is made on the cutting surface, and a slit is made at the bottom, cutting the plant across its entire diameter. Bring the pot close to the tree to be grafted, and select a suitable branch. Cut a wedge-shaped notch in half its thickness, and fit the notched half into the cut of the subject, so as to represent a slotted graft fitted on its side only. A ligature is made, and the grafting wax is applied. When the graft is complete, the branch is detached from the tree by cutting it below the graft, and the wound is joined. It is suitable for multiplying evergreen trees, such as blackcurrant, holly, phyllirea, etc., and hardwood trees, such as hornbeam, oak, beech and the like. O Mbb THIRD SECTION. GRAFTING FOR ORNAMENTAL TREES AND SHRUBS. 87. Grafting by branch fragment, without gem. This is performed in the same way as ordinary split grafting, but a branch without an eye can be used for grafting, such as the stalk of a rose from which the flower has been cut. The apparatus is placed on a warm surface and under a frame or glass, as recovery depends almost entirely on the deprivation of air and light. Once soldered, gems are soon formed, which develop in a very short time in the same way as other grafts. This method is useful for multiplying a species which, by chance, would only have a flower with its stalk in our hands. 88. English-style split graft. English graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 6. a, subject; b, graft; c, sketch of graft; d, sketch of subject. The head of a young subject is cut at a very long bevel, and a slit is made in the middle of the length of the wound, so as to form a splinter. We choose a branch from the previous year, cut it over two or three eyes, and cut its base like the head of the subject, but in the opposite direction, and slit the wound in the same way to form a splinter. The grefse is then fitted into the subject, by fitting the splinters one on top of the other in the two slits. Ligature is made, and lute is applied with wax. 908 This very strong graft is very suitable for propagating exotic and hardwood trees. 89. "English-style split graft. Miller graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 5. a, subject; b, subject's notch and graft tooth; c, base of graft cut into bird's tongue. This is done in almost the same way as the twig graft for orange trees, 3rd type, n° 71; but, instead of choosing a twig with leaves and flowers, we take a twig from the previous year, cut it at its base into a bird's tongue shape topped by a projecting notch; we hollow out a corresponding notch in the subject to receive it, make a longitudinal wound to be covered by the tongue, adjust and bind. This graft is suitable for most species, trees and shrubs with thin bark and hard wood. 90. Slot graft with bevelled subject. Bertemboise graft, by Thouin. Pl. a, fig. 2. The branch of the graft is cut to the shape of a knife blade, and inserted into a slit made in the cut surface of the subject, after having removed its head. It dissects an ordinary split graft, in that the cut of the subject that is not covered by the base of the branch is cut to a long bevel. This makes it easier for the wound to heal, prevents unpleasant swelling and produces straighter, defect-free stems. It should therefore be used whenever the graft is to provide a portion of stem before forming the head of the tree. It is particularly useful for avenue and alignment trees. 91. Split grafting by juxta-position. Kuffner graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 11. A branch of exactly the same size as the subject is chosen; it is cut cleanly at its base, then, six or eight lines above, it is cut transversely1 in half its thickness; the splinter is removed, by means of a slit, over half the diameter of the cut area, and the wound is well united. The same operation is performed on the subject, but in reverse, and the wound is joined so that what has been removed in one is found in the other. It is very rarely used, although it is quite suitable for shrubs with thin bark. If it is not widely used, this is because it can be advantageously replaced by others in all circumstances. 92. Split grafting with beveled juxtaposition. Pl. 2, fig. 12. This technique is very similar to the previous one. The subjects must be exactly the same size. The base of the twig and the stem of the subject are cut in the shape of a bird's tongue, and made to overlap one another. When making the ligature, great care must be taken not to disturb the coincidence of the bark, which is all too easy. As little used as the previous method, it is suitable for the same purposes. 93. Split grafting by juxta-position with bevel and notch. Pl. 2, fig. 13. Same size conditions as above, and same bird's tongue size; but the end of the tongue of the graft is beveled, and a notch is made at the bottom of the wound to receive it. If the subject were stronger than the branch, this grafting would still be practicable by leaving a retreat on the cut surface, as we have illustrated; but, for the barks to coincide, the stem of the subject must be a little flattened, which happens quite often in some part of its length. The stem is then trimmed and tied, and in the latter case, covered with wax. 906 and very suitable for delicate shrubs in pots. 94. Split grafting by juxta-position with bevel and tooth. Pl. a, sig. 15. This is done in much the same way as bevelled juxta-position grafting, except that a notch is cut in the middle of the length of the subject's cut, into which a projecting tooth or notch is fitted in the middle of the graft's bevel. Less widely used than the preceding techniques, it is suitable for the same purposes. 95. Slit graft with dormant eye. Maupas graft, by Thouin. Pl. 4, fig. 6. a, subject's slit, shown with its lips parted; b, branch cut into a wedge on one side, or rather into the blade of a knife, to be inserted enc. In August, when the sap begins to fall, we make a deep incision in the stem of a young tree, or rather a slit, without cutting its head. We choose a young shoot from the previous year's growth, and try to find one with a natural inflection, which is better because, adjusted, it will be less tight against the subject's stem; we cut it like the blade of a knife, implanting the sharp side into the slit; we make the ligature, and cover the wound, especially the top of the slit, with grafting wax. The following spring, all branches and buds above and below the graft are removed, to encourage the sap to flow to it. To carry out this grafting, we can also use the slit grafting method in the middle of the wood, n°, 51, and pl. 2, fig. 16. Although more widely used than the previous methods, its practice is nevertheless very limited; it could however be used with hope of success for the multiplication 90 of exotic, but robust and open-ground trees, whose gems would be scaly. 96." Split grafting with triangular notch. Lée graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 4. a, triangular notch not reaching the heart of the subject; b, branch cut into a triangular point, seen from the side opposite the bark. The head of the tree is cut, and a triangular, longitudinal cut is made on one side, not reaching the medullary case, which must remain intact. The base of a branch is triangularly tapered to match the subject's notch, so as to fill it as exactly as possible. The parts are then joined and tied together. This graft is generally used for very young or delicate trees, whose pith must never be attacked. It is also used for old trees whose hardened bark offers little sap. 97. Crown grafting by enfourchement. Dumont graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 2. After cutting the stem of a young tree, it is pruned into the shape of an extended wedge, leaving the bark on either side. Choose a branch of the same size, and cut a deep triangular notch in its base, so as to overlap the wedge and receive it in its entire length. The two wounds are joined together, and a ligature is made; or, if you don't mind a bit of shock, you simply cover with grafting wax. This method was previously indicated for grafting vines between two plots of land; but, as ordinary split grafting is equally and perhaps more advantageous, it has been relegated to the garden, where it is used to propagate exotic trees grafted onto very young stock. 98. Varin graft, in twig between bark and wood. Varin graft, by Thouin. Pl.3, fig. 4. The head of the tree is cut off and the bark is lifted on one side of the cut, after which it is split longitudinally. Choose a twig with its leaves and flower buds, cut a flute-beak shape at the base, and cut a notch at the top to seat it on the subject. Insert it into the incision, between the bark and sapwood, in the same way as for a shield graft, and tie it in place. This graft is carried out under a bell and on a layer. It is very well suited to exotic trees and shrubs with eyes not covered by scales, as well as to those with hard wood. 99. Grafting in twig onto cuttings and done at the same time. Choose a young, healthy and very vigorous branch, of an orange tree for example; cut off its tip, and make a slit on the cut surface, or a longitudinal triangular notch on the side. In the first case, cut the twig of the variety you wish to propagate, like a knife blade; in the second, cut it like a triangular point. It is then adjusted, tied and covered with grafting wax. Once this has been done, the base of the young branch is bevelled to prevent the pernicious action of humidity; the wound is covered with a little wax, then a hole is made in good soil prepared in a pot; a little sand is thrown into this hole, again to prevent humidity; the soil is covered, and the whole is carried in a tarpaulin on a warm layer; the cutting is covered with a glass funnel, and is stunted until it sprouts again: then gradually acclimatize it to air and light. Suitable for propagating rare and delicate varieties. 100. split graft on root holding the subject. Gresse Hall, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 12. A root is uncovered without detaching it from its subject, -1 and its tip is lifted from the ground, cut and split in the middle of its diameter, to establish a branch of the penultimate sap, in the manner of ordinary split grafting, i.e., cut at the base into the shape of a knife blade; then covered with soil. This method is very rarely used, because its usefulness is very accidental. It is suitable for propagating rare trees which have no analogues and which refuse other means of propagation. It has also been thought that it could serve as proof of the existence of descending sap, but this would only prove that sap does not have a regulated course of circulation. 101. Crown graft on root. Saussure graft, by Thouin. Pl. 2, fig. 10. A large root is detached near the stump of the subject; the large end is raised a little above the surface of the soil, the wound is united, and one or more slits are made to receive as many twigs cut in the shape of a knife blade, and inserted in the same way as the ordinary slit graft. Bind with Saint-Fiacre ointment and cover with soil. Like the previous method, it is suitable for propagating rare trees that have no analogues, but has the advantage of being quicker and easier to do, and of offering a much more reliable recovery. 102. Split grafting on separate roots. Cels graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 15. a, two plants joined; b, b, soil level. Roots are separated from their stump, transplanted and then cored using the same method as for English-style coring, n° 89. Care should be taken to bury them only up to the penultimate eye of the branch. It provides an easy way of multiplying plants for which there are no similar species, and could be used to pO6 multiply others more abundantly and more safely. 103. Root slit grafting under the stem collar. Bourgdorf graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 13. a, subject cut, and good root inserted b. The root collar of a tree is dug up, and a notch is made penetrating to half its thickness, a little above the furrow of the large roots, higher or lower, depending on the case usually resulting from disease. From another tree of a similar species, we select a good root, healthy and full of hair. Separate it and cut a wedge at its large end, so as to fill exactly the notch in the subject; fit it in, hold it in place with a ligature and St. Fiacre's ointment, and cover with soil. If a tree's roots have been broken by an accident, or destroyed by white grubs; if they are attacked by a disease whose progress threatens to destroy them entirely by spreading to their collar, this method is used, with almost certain success, to replace them with others. This method can also be used to increase and accelerate the vegetation of a valuable individual. 104. Split grafting of roots on roots. Chomel graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, fig. 16. a, insertion of the wild root. Without detaching it from its stump, lift the end of a tree root from the ground, cut it transversely at a point where it is at least the size of a feather pipe, and split it in the middle of its thickness. A vigorous root is taken from a wildflower of the same species, trimmed at the large end to the shape of a flute mouthpiece, inserted into the slit in the usual way, tied and covered with soil. It is used to increase the vigor of a young individual, or to replace its diseased roots. 105. Approach grafting of roots onto branches 1 holding on to their tree. Palissy graft, by Thouin. Pl. 3, Jig. 14. Choose a young, well-grown branch from the tree you wish to propagate, and make a notch in its bark, or simply a sliding incision. From the same tree, we take a vigorous root, very hairless, and about the same size as the branch. Detach it from the tree, cut it off at the large end, and carefully plant it in a pot three-quarters filled with good soil. Bring the pot closer to the branch, fit the tongue into the notch or slide in the bark, and make a ligature or cover with grafting wax. The pot is then filled with soil, so that the grafted area is covered by about a finger's worth; then, from time to time, water is applied to maintain humidity, but only in sufficient quantity to sustain the life of the root. Once the root has recovered, the branch is detached from the tree by cutting it close to the graft. This method is not widely used, but can become indispensable if you want to propagate an exotic tree that does not respond to cuttings or grafting, and for which you have no congeners on which to graft it. 106. Grafting in escutcheon by inoculation. Xenophon graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 2. a, wound on subject; b, eye with bark border. With the tip of a grafting knife, or better still a penknife, a bud is encircled, leaving a small strip of bark around it, and removing a portion of the wood that remains. On the subject to be grafted, we make a wound the same width as that of the bud and its border, and a depth equal to the length of the piece of wood left. The whole is adjusted so that the eye rem2. t perfectly folds the wound, and the scissures are covered with gresser wax. This graft is used to transport flowering buds from one place where there are too many, to another where there are too few. 107. Graft in escutcheon on roots. Sickler graft, by Thouin. In spring, at the time when growing-eye grafts are made, the roots of a tree are uncovered, and an eye taken from its branches is grafted onto them in the form of an escutcheon. The graft is left uncovered, but all other parts are covered with soil. In the spring of the following year, when the graft is well established and has grown a fine shoot, we cut the root above the graft, carefully pull it out and transplant it elsewhere. In this way, you have an individual that you would not have been able to multiply either by marcottes or cuttings, or by the ordinary dregs, for lack of suitable subjects. 108**. Opposite escutcheon graft. Descemet graft, by Thouin. This graft is done in the same way as the dormant and growing eye grafts; the only difference is that, instead of placing just one on the stem of a subject, two are usually placed opposite each other, sometimes three, or even more, laid in the form of a crown around the stem. It has the advantage of ensuring success by multiplying favorable chances, and is perfectly suited to forming beautiful heads on weeping trees, such as ash, locust, laburnum, etc. 109. Graft in ecus its cover. Gresse Sintard, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 7. a, eye growing through hole in plate; b, plate pierced.
