This is done in the same way as the ordinary escutcheon graft, but when the eye is in place, instead of making a ligature, the scissures are plugged with grafting wax; a plate of bark is removed from another tree, pierced with a small hole in the middle, and applied to the graft so as to cover it, except for the bud which is uncovered by the hole. A ligature is made to hold the device in place. This graft, which is far too meticulous, has been rejected from ordinary practice, because it can be replaced by several others with the same advantages. It was indicated for use on rare and delicate trees. 110. Shield graft by portion of terminal eyes. Gretfe Sennebier, de Thoum. Pl. 1, fig. 9. c, split terminal eye; b, the same eye seen from the front; a, its insertion. The tip of a twig is cut to the length of six or eight lines, and split in two, dividing the terminal eye exactly down the middle. A T-shaped incision is then made in the subject, and this eye half is inserted in the same way as a crest graft. If necessary, the terminal eye can be divided into four equal parts. If you want to be sure of success, you must graft with a growing eye; however, this graft can also be successful with a dormant eye. This method can be very useful if the tree to be multiplied does not offer young wood strong enough to remove a shield. It is particularly suitable for rare trees with scaly buds and opposite branches. 111. Flute graft. Pl. 5, fig. 14. a, subject cut into a flute; b, base of twig stripped of bark, fitting into the slit in the subject. The stem of a very young tree, such as a three- or four-month-old orange, is cut two or three inches from the ground. A longitudinal incision of five or six lines is made in the bark, the bark is detached from the wood along its entire length, and the bark is spread or inverted so that the wood can be amputated cleanly with the tip of a penknife or grafting knife, precisely where the incision ends. Take a twig of the species to be multiplied, exactly the same size as the subject; remove a ring of bark at its base, just as long as the incision in the subject, and press the stripped part of the wood into the flute of the subject, so as to precisely fill the place of the amputated wood, and make the bark coincide. This easy-to-perform grafting technique is highly advantageous for the rapid multiplication of delicate species. It is carried out on warm soil, in the same way as the cutting graft, n° 99. 112. Flute grafting with dormant eye. Pan graft, by Thouin. This is done in the same way as flute grafting by juxta-position, n° 62; but instead of in spring, it is done in August, and with buds produced by the first sap of the same year, whereas for the other, eyes from the previous year are chosen. It is rarely used in daily practice; however, it can be useful for multiplying trees with very hard wood. 113.* Flute and strip grafting. Faune graft, by Thouin. Pl. 1, fig. 13. It is done in the same way as the ordinary flute graft, no. 63; but once the ligature has been made, the bark and wood are cut in the shape of a flute beak, above the last eye of the graft, and luted with grafting wax; in addition, the tube to be grafted must have at least five or six eyes, which makes it very difficult to detach. This graft is excellent for multiplying foreign hardwood trees; it would be equally useful for fruit trees, if the time it wastes, and the difficulty encountered in doing it, had not led gardeners to abandon it and replace it with others. 114.* Grafting using water. On the tree you wish to propagate, cut a branch from one foot to eighteen inches in length; make a longitudinal side cut in its upper part, six or eight lines long, below two or three good eyes; make a similar one on the stem of a subject, and unite the parts by the ordinary procedure of approach grafting. Once this has been done, a small vase, cup or flask, depending on the circumstances, is attached to the subject's stem; it is filled with water, and the base of the grafted branch is left to soak until it recovers, to prevent it from drying out. This procedure is excellent for grafting two trees that are too far apart to be able to join their branches. 115. Approach grafting on cuttings. A cutting is planted in a pot; a longitudinal wound is made on one side of its stem, and the pot is approached by a branch from the tree you wish to propagate; a wound similar to that of the cutting is made on this branch, they are joined, the ligature is made and wax applied. The soil in the pot is kept moderately but continuously moist, to facilitate root emergence. Grafting provides a means of obtaining, in a very short time, complete individuals of the rarest and most difficult species to propagate. Grafting and cuttings are mutually helpful: the former to make roots in a species that is difficult to take as a cutting, the latter to maintain humidity and life until the cut has been made. The difference between this method and cutting grafting, n° 20, is that in the latter, the cutting becomes the subject, whereas in the former, it only provides the graft. -1 Grafting for trellises and palisades. 116. ". Grafting by cradle approach. Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 2. b,b, grafting the stems of subjects a, a;c, c, c, etc., grafting the branches at their junction point. Young trees of similar species are planted in two parallel rows; their heads are bent into a cradle shape, and held in place with a light framework. As their tops lengthen and cross, they are cut at their junction point, and grafted using the arc grafting process, n° 85. The lateral branches are arranged so as to form 45-degree angles with their stems, and grafted using the trunk grafting process, n° 80. In this way, all the trees in an arbor share the same sap, so that if the roots of some die, the stems are nourished by the others. In addition, the cradles are impenetrable, well furnished with greenery, and in time provide the arts with highly prized bentwood. 117 - Grafting by diamond-shaped approach. Thouin. Young trees are planted in rows, eight or ten inches apart. When they are fully established, they are inclined to form an angle of 4o to 45 degrees, one to the right, the other to the left, and grafted at all section points, one on top of the other, using the trunk approach grafting procedure, n° 80. The same result is obtained using another method. Young trees are planted in the same way as the previous ones, but cut six inches from the ground, on two eyes, one on the right, the other on the left, in the direction of the line. As the two buds grow, they are grafted at an angle of forty-five degrees (CI.), and they are grafted by approach, according to the same procedure, at all their meeting points. Graft again as the branches lengthen and cross. These two methods of grafting are excellent for forming impenetrable hedges, palisades and picturesque fences for landscaped gardens. FOURTH SECTION. HERBACEOUS GRAFTS OF PERENNIALS AND ANNUALS. Here we come to the herbaceous grafts of Mr. Tschudy, mentioned above. This skilful amateur used them on vegetable plants, with the intention of increasing the quantity and quality of their produce; but, death having taken him away from his useful work, the positive results of his experiments have not been published, and we are still unaware of the extent to which they could be used to perfect horticulture. We regret that the nature of our work has not allowed us to follow these experiments ourselves, and we urge amateurs to do so. 118 *. Grafting succulents. Noisette graft, by Thouin. Pl. 5, fig. 15. A young stem or leaf of a fat plant, such as a cactus or opuntia, is beveled at the base and implanted into a slit in the stem or leaf of another species of the same genus. M. Thouin, in classifying this cress in his monograph, was mistaken about its results, and the error of this 908 man, as learned in agriculture as he was respectable for his private virtues, undoubtedly stems from the fact that his gardeners, in carrying it out in the greenhouses of the Jardin du Roi, did not follow exactly the information they had to obtain from our establishment. M. Thouin says: "The inserted parts live and grow, not like grafts, but like parasitic plants. This may be the case if, as he recommends above, we graft crassula and cotyledons onto cacti and opuntia; the reason is that, as these species do not have enough analogy, the grafts, instead of fusing, grow roots. But when the species are analogous, the welding takes place, and the graft is real. 119.* Grafting on fleshy roots or tubers. Pl. 6, fig. 1. a, tuber; b, b, grafts. It often happens that a dahlia tuber, finding itself devoid of eyes, either because of an accident which has broken the stem collar, or because nature has denied it any, despite all the care of the amateur, remains for one or two years, or even longer, without growing, and ends up rotting. Nothing is easier than to notice this, if you look carefully at the neck, as this is where the gems are always placed. In this case, you wait until a bud from another dahlia has begun to develop, circle it with the tip of the grafting tool, and remove it, leaving a small piece of the tuber above. A small hole is made in the neck of the sterile tuber, into which the graft is inserted, but in such a way that the base of the eye is perfectly level with the surface of the tuber. The tuber is planted in a pot, taking care not to bury the neck where the graft is, and the vase is placed on a warm layer under a frame. When the plant has recovered perfectly, remove the rootball and plant in the open ground. 1. This graft is used for the purpose we have just described; it can also be applied to other tuberous plants, principally peonies. It can be used to multiply precious species at the expense of common ones. 120. Stem grafting of annual or perennial plants. Pl. 6, fig. 2. a, stem; b, graft; c, its insertion near a leaf. The time of greatest vegetation is chosen, i.e. a few days before flowering. The stem is cut cleanly, above a leaf, as close as possible to the petiole attachment, and a slit is made in the cut area. A bud of the species you wish to propagate is taken from the root, its base bevelled, and inserted into the slit in the subject, taking care not to damage the leaf, since it is the leaf which must nourish the bud until it is fully established, maintaining the circulation of sap. A ligature is made, and the scissures are covered with grafting wax. When recovery is certain, as can be seen by the growth, the ligature is loosened, the leaf cut off, and the lower buds removed. In this way, Mr. Tschudy grafted artichokes onto thistles, and other plants onto their congeners. 121. Grafting melons. Pl. 6, fig. 3. a, cucumber stem with fruit; b, insertion of watermelon graft, with young fruit c. On a cucumber stem, or that of another plant in the Cucurbitaceae family but similar to the melon, choose a vigorous spot with a well-developed leaf. A half-thick oblique cut is made in the leaf axil. On a melon branch, cut a bud that is sufficiently developed to already have its fruit fully formed, and bevel it two inches below the fruit. Insert it into the cut in the subject, always leaving the leaf untouched until it is perfectly attached; make the ligature, and carry out the operation in the same way as the previous one. This grafting technique is quite successful, but has it really served any useful purpose, at least so far? Tomatoes can be grafted onto potatoes, and other plants of different species, but with sufficient analogy; and this is what experience alone can teach us to recognize. Herbaceous grafts for fruit or ornamental trees. We've included them here because, until now, they've been little more than objects of curiosity. If we were to consider them as useful, we would find a large number of other grafts to compare with them, which would fulfil the same purpose, and which are at the same time safer and easier. They are performed on buds while their tissues are still succulent and herbaceous. 122. Herbaceous groove graft for omnitiges. Pl. 6, fig. 4. a, subject groove; b, graft base. Mr. Tschudy gives the name "omnitiges" to plants whose branches all have the same arrangement, without any of them seeming to want to rise more vertically than the others, and to take up a greater portion of sap. In this case, all branches are equally vigorous and can be grafted indiscriminately. The tip of a bud is cut off, at least one inch from a bud with its leaf, as in previous grafting techniques. An incision is made in the leaf axil, next to the bud, in the form of a triangular groove, dug down to near the middle of the stem, and descending to one inch or eighteen lines. A vigorous bud is selected from the tree, its base trimmed to a triangular O09 point, leaving one side intact, and inserted into the incision so that its tip is level with the subject's eye. Ligature is made and wax applied. As the graft grows, the ligature is undone, the bud and leaf of the subject are removed, and the lower buds are cut off. This graft is successful not only on tree buds, but also on the stems of annual or perennial plants. 123. Herbaceous grafting for opposite-leaved buds. Pl. 6, fig. 5. In the middle of the stem, between two opposite eyes, a longitudinal and angular incision is made, crossing the stem from one side to the other. The graft is wedge-shaped at its base and top, and inserted from the side, so that the two eyes are on the same level as those of the subject, and form a whorl with them. Ligature is made, wax is applied, and the process is repeated as for the previous ones. Specific to tree species and annual or perennial plants whose gems are opposite each other on the stem, which is particularly the case with multi-stemmed plants. M. Tschudy gives this name to those whose central braches, tending to rise more vertically than the lateral ones, are also more vigorous; and it is on these that one should graft. dio

dr FIFTH SECTION. EXPERIMENTAL GRAFTS. These are of no use in the usual practice of gardening, but they are curious and of amusing execution, besides serving to explain 900 some phenomena of plant physiology, knowledge of which may lead to valuable discoveries for agriculture. 124. Approach grafting of branches from several trees onto a single stem. Egyptian graft, by Thouin. Two young fruit trees are planted three feet apart from a tree of the same genus, but of a different species or variety. On the stem of the middle tree, two longitudinal wounds are made, corresponding to two branches of the young trees, which are grafted onto them in the usual way. None of the other branches are cut, and are left to grow as they should. It has been thought that this graft brings about a change in the size, flavor and color of the fruit, and even in the hardness of the wood, but this error is no longer worth refuting. 125. Approach grafting of roots together. Malpighi graft, by Thouin. The roots of two or more neighboring trees are dug up, brought together, and grafted using the trunk approach grafting procedure, n° 80, or trunk abutment approach grafting procedure, n° 81. Then put them back in place, and cover with soil. In this way, the roots of several trees are brought together by sap, and it was once thought that their fruit would undergo some advantageous changes as a result. This has now been recognized as a mistake. 126. Grafting by approaching fruits in their buds. Pomone graft, by Thouin. As soon as they are born, two fruit embryos are brought together, before or as soon as the flowers open, and pressed against each other, so that, unable to move apart, they are forced to weld together as they grow. This graft, like the following one, is often found naturally, produced by accident or because the fruits have been hindered in their development by a branch or some other cause. We have succeeded in imitating nature by reproducing these remarkable monstrosities at will. 127. Grafting by approaching the fruit of one tree onto another. Leberriays graft, by Thouin. Two trees of different but similar species or varieties are brought together; for example, an orange tree and a lemon tree; and, when their fruits have reached a fifth or a quarter of their size, two corresponding wounds are made, and they are joined together; but great care must be taken not to allow the cut to penetrate as far as the lodges containing the seeds: thus, in the example cited, only a small part of what is commonly called the fruit rind is removed. Grafting can also be carried out by simple compression. To do this, a young fruit is brought close to the branch of another fruit of a similar species; the two fruits, which in this case must be barely formed, are brought together and held together by some means so that they cannot separate as they grow. These two methods of grafting have no useful purpose, so they are only used as objects of curiosity. 128. Approach grafting of leaves and flowers. Adanson graft, by Thouin. On young herbaceous stems, leaves, flowers or fruit, small incisions are made in the most suitable places, into which young leaves or flowers are inserted. The operation is carried out with as much delicacy as precision, and maintained with grafting wax. Grafting rarely takes place, but when it does96 , it can lead to some very curious observations about plant physics. 126. Split graft with branch inserted upside down. Lenôtre graft, by Thouin. The head of a subject is cut off, and a longitudinal slit is made in the cut area. Choose a branch from the penultimate sap, and instead of cutting it off at the base, cut it off at the top like a knife blade, and insert it into the slit so that the eyes are upside down. Ligature and cover with grafting wax. In the past, it was thought that this method would produce weeping trees, but experience has shown that the branches immediately straighten out. A more important error to note is that almost all authors have indicated that this method should be used to hasten fruiting. We have often experimented with this method without obtaining any results other than those obtained with ordinary split grafting. The only remarkable consequence that can be drawn from it concerns the uncertain way in which sap circulates in plants; it is therefore only of any use for the study of plant physiology. 130. Grafted sideways by means of a seedling. Grew graft, by Thouin. A branch six or seven feet long is selected, sharpened into a triangular point at the base, and cut into a flute mouthpiece at the top. The triangular point of the branch is driven into the ground at the foot of a large tree; a notch is made in the trunk of the tree, penetrating as far as the medullary case, and filled exactly with the plancon's flute-tip. It is generally believed that this graft is used to propagate trees that have no congeners on which they can be grafted; and this error is due to the fact that we attribute the regrowth of the seedling to the sap supplied by the tree trunk, whereas in reality it is nothing more than the regrowth of an ordinary cutting. Thus, the consequences drawn from this, to prove the descent of sap to the roots, prove nothing, since they are false. 131. Graft of three pieces. Muzat graft, by Thouin. Pl. 6, fig. 6. A vigorous, healthy root with a good head is selected and planted in a pot three-quarters filled with good soil suited to the nature of the plant. The large end of the root, which protrudes out of the soil, can be trimmed into a wedge. A branch of a congenial species is chosen, splintered at the base so as to sit or rather straddle the corner of the root, and fixed there by means of a ligature and grafting wax. Once the first graft is complete, the top of the branch is split in the middle of its diameter. Choose a twig from a tree of the same family, complete with leaves, flower buds and even young fruit. It is trimmed at the base with a very long bevel, and fitted into the branch cleft in the same way as an ordinary cleft graft. Once the ligature has been made and the wax applied, the pot is filled with soil and placed on a warm layer, in a tarpaulin or frame. The plant is deprived of air and too bright a light by means of a frosted glass funnel, and is only gradually given back both, when it is known from its vegetation that the two branches have been perfectly re-grafted. This extremely curious graft is of little use in normal use, but is useful for the study of plant physiology. 132. Composite graft in escutcheon, approach and cleft. Lambert graft, by Thouin. Two young, vigorous wildings are planted eighteen inches or two feet apart. They are grafted 908 in escutcheon facing each other with gems taken from trees whose fruits will be remarkable for their flavor and fragrance. When the grafts have produced two beautiful scions, they are grafted by approach, joining them together over as great a length as possible. As soon as the union is perfect, cut the new stem at the point where it will be most closely joined, make a longitudinal slit in the cut area, and fit a branch of a species with insipid fruit and no flavor at all. Mr. Thouin proposed this method to find out "whether mixing the sap and juices of different trees would not modify the flavor of the fruit, and establish new domestic breeds, more perfect in fruit quality than those we possess. Experience has taught us that the subject's sap has no influence on fruit quality. 133. Duhamel compound graft. A young, robust wild pear tree is selected. A quince tree is grafted onto it, in a slit or escutcheon; a hawthorn is grafted onto the hawthorn, a medlar tree is grafted onto the medlar tree, and a good Christian pear tree is grafted onto the medlar tree. This graft is proposed by Duhamel for the same purpose as the previous one; and, although we have not experimented with it, the analogy makes us presume, or rather gives us the certainty that the result would not be more advantageous. 134. Grafting woody plants onto perennial roots. Nebulous graft, by Thouin. The collar of large perennial roots is dug up, and woody species of the same genus are placed either in a slit or in an escutcheon. If you have grafted in escutcheon, cover with soil up to the level of the graft, but do not bury it; if, on the contrary, you have grafted in slit, bury the graft, and leave only two eyes out of the ground. 1 Olivier de Serre recommends this method for obtaining carnation, viola, passe-roses and passe-velours flowers of different colors. We see here only perennial plants, not woody ones: so it would be almost impossible to graft them in escutcheon; and, supposing we succeeded, we are certain that they would not take root again. As for split grafting, we can well imagine that it can take root, even with woody species, but only in the manner of ordinary cuttings, i.e. it will grow roots, but will not weld. In all cases, the species or variety will remain intact. 135. Grafting non-analogous species. Butret graft, by Thouin. Choose a subject with non-evergreen leaves, and graft onto it a species of the same genus, but whose leaves do not fall off in winter; or use the latter as a subject, and place on it a braid from the former. The experiment can also be carried out in other ways: 1° on a subject whose vegetation is slow and late, we graft another whose sap starts moving actively and very early; 2° on an individual whose sap is sweet and insipid, we place a graft whose sap is acrid and corrosive. Mr. Thouin proposes these methods to prove that it is not enough to graft trees of the same family, genus and species onto one another, to obtain complete success in the operation; but that the movements of the sap, in its ascent and descent, as well as the qualities of the proper juices, must also be more or less the same; otherwise these mismatched grafts perish in a few years. We don't agree with Mr. Thouin at all, and we can assure you that some evergreen trees 2. 009 do very well on deciduous ones; for example, Japanese medlar on hawthorn, burning buiason on hawthorn, olive trees on privet, and so on. As for the difference in sap, we know that pears are delicious on quince, whose fruit is unbearably pungent, and a thousand times late-sucking species are successfully grafted onto early-sucking species, and vice versa.d 136. Grafting seed into escutcheon. Bonnet graft, by Thouin. This graft and the following one have been proposed by M. Thouin to resolve a number of plant physiological questions of importance to agriculture; we shall extract them verbatim from his monograph, and give our opinion on the proposed questions. "Make an incision in the bark of a plant, either an annual or herbaceous perennial, or a tree or shrub, abundant in sap, to the depth of the fibers or woody layers. Introduce into this wound either a whole seed with its envelopes or without its tunics, or one deprived of its cotyledons and reduced to its germ. Cover the wound with a plaster, and keep the parts in their place, by means of ligatures which cannot hinder the development of the germ. "Uses. To find out: 1° whether these germs will develop - We believe that the germs will develop, but in the ordinary way, i.e., by the effect of the humidity maintained by the sap, and vegetation will last only as long as this humidity; they will perish when the sap has passed. "2° Whether the plants born from them will live in the manner of "parasites or false parasites"-it is certain that, during the short time that the germs vegetate, their radicle will run between the bark and the sapwood, and perhaps grow a few roots, to seek ¡1. moisture over a larger surface area. They would thus live like false parasites. "3° And finally, what changes will they undergo "in the subjects on which they grow?"-No changes, whether they vegetate as grafts or as parasites. We have given the reasons for this on pages 11 and following. 137. Grafting leaves in the manner of ecus son. Gresse Bosc, by Thouin. "Choose young subjects in full sap, and old ones in pots; make Tet-shaped incisions in their stems proportionate to the size of the petioles they are to receive; take leaves from congeners with little sap, at a quarter, a third, or half their size, or about to reach it; separate them from their trees with their pedicel in all its length, and its appendix, but saus gemma; put these grafts in the incisions made in the subjects, and place them on a warm layer covered with a shaded shawl, and under which will be maintained a vaporous, humid and warm atmosphere, during the recovery of the grafts. " "Uses. To find out: 1° whether the leaves will come back on neighboring species, which is likely... " - They will resume under the same conditions as for ordinary escutcheon grafts, i.e., on similar subjects. "2° Will they refuse to graft on disgeners? -There's no doubt about it. "3° Whether these leaves will produce gemma in their axils, as if they had remained on their natural foot? As we said in the article on the eyeless crane, n° 87, all the living parts of plants, bark, leaves, peduncles, petals, etc., can with certain precautions form gems that will develop and reproduce a complete individual of the same species. Thus, there is no 4009 doubt that the leaf will produce, not only in its axils, but in all its parts, gems capable of perfect development. Experience has proven this several times, as will be seen in the chapter on cuttings. However, in the grafting operation, only the pedicles, in favorable circumstances, will form embryos. "4° What will be the nature of the buds that develop from these gemmas? According to the principles established on page 11, the buds can only be of the same nature as the grafts that supplied them. There is no reason to believe that a plant will produce beings different from itself, in one part rather than the other. "5° And finally, whether these gemma exist in the seed, and only develop through the act of vegetation; or whether they are produced, each year, by the leaves of the plants?" - We regard this solution as absolutely useless to agriculture: so we won't venture to give our opinion. This question belongs more to philosophy than to the subject we are dealing with, and the philosophers of all centuries have discussed it in turn, without ever having been able to resolve it. This concludes the nomenclature of the grafts we know and which we have almost all performed in our establishment, either as part of our usual practice, or as an object of experiment. It remains for us to present the table in the analytical order of their affinities; and that's what we're going to do, adopting M. Thouin's classification as the one that seemed most natural to us. Although we have described thirty-five more grafts than this patriarch of agriculture, they would all relate exactly to his classification, if, at O00 the time of publication of his monograph, he had known about herbaceous grafts; but this valuable discovery has forced us to change his main divisions a little, and to establish new ones in which some of his own naturally fall. Nora. The number placed after each graft is the order under which it is to be found in the descriptions. FIRST CLASS. WOODY GRAFTS. These are made from woody parts, or by application to wood. DIOMECSD( 123 --..-- CHAPTER II. PRUNING. FIRST SECTION. GENERAL PRINCIPLES. Under the title of this chapter, we will include not only pruning as such, but also all operations which have some connection with it; thus we will deal with disbudding, shearing, bowing, ring incision, etc. The purpose of pruning is to give a tree a different shape from that which nature has imposed on it. The purpose of pruning is 1° to give a tree a different shape from the one imposed by nature; 2° to maintain the balance of sap in all its parts, so as to keep it in robust health; 3° to force it to produce more abundant, better-quality harvests every year. Pruning is an entirely artificial operation, for which nature offers no model. This mutilation acts on plants in the same way as grafting, i.e. by altering their constitution it forces them, by the same principle of plant physiology, to give to fruit production the sap that nature intended for the maintenance and growth of wood. If it increases the quality or quantity of a harvest, it owes it to this cause, and to the hand of the grower who, by removing useless wood, retains or subtracts, according to circumstances, that destined to bear fruit. It seems that this operation was unknown to the ancients. At least, if we are to believe what little they have left us on 208 this subject, they only knew about pruning and shearing, and their only aim was to subject their trees to regular, pleasing and sometimes bizarre shapes. Many authors have written on this interesting subject, but almost all of them, carried away by blind routine and lacking sufficient knowledge of the laws of vegetation, have given incoherent, disjointed precepts which, not being founded on any principle, are always vicious and often impossible to apply. In 1795, this grower published a small treatise entitled Taille raisonnée des arbres fruitiers, in which he gives excellent rules based on the true principles of vegetation. It is unfortunate that this esteemed author confined himself to describing only the pruning of peach trees, and only that practiced in Montreuil. Instruments for pruning, and wood cutting. The serpette is the oldest instrument ever used, and still the best. To be of the most convenient proportions, its handle should be 3 to 4 inches long; it should be made of deer horn, so that the roughness of this material fixes it firmly in the hand. The blade, two inches long, should be curved at the tip, so that it can easily remove a branch, but without snagging too hard to break. It must be extremely sharp, and always very clean, as the sap of certain plants is known to be caustic enough to attack the temper if left on the steel for a long time. In recent years, attempts have been made to replace the O08 serpette with the secateur, invented by Mr. Marquis Bertrand de Molleville; but this instrument, whose only merit is to save a little time, has so many disadvantages that it cannot be used in good cultivation. It consists of two crossed branches, ending in the shape of curved scissors with two blades, one oval and sharp, the other crescent-shaped to provide a point of support. The other details of its shape vary according to the intelligence of the craftsman who makes it, or the whim of the amateur who has it made. When using pruning shears, the crescent is pressed against one side of the scion to be cut; and, by squeezing the branches, the blade is brought closer together, cutting more or less cleanly through the portion of wood interposed between its crescent and the blade: but the result of this operation is that, as the wood presents its fibers perpendicularly to the blade, its resistance is much greater and causes a pressure which, by crushing the wood, also detaches the bark up to a few lines below the wound. The mutilated branch tip, far from healing, dries out, and mortality usually spreads to the underside of the eye, which itself is often damaged. The pruning shears have other serious disadvantages, which we won't go into here, because the one we've just mentioned is enough to make them inadvisable for any good crop. In addition to the pruning shears, we recommend a hand saw or egohine, with a narrow, strong, elongated blade, for amputating large branches; an ordinary pruning shear; shears or scissors for trimming hedges and palisades; volans, loppers, pruning hooks, etc. The way in which the serpette is used, or rather the way in which the wood is cut, is not at all indifferent. Whenever you are working on a species with a hard, not very soft wood, and whose sap does not flow in too abundantly, the amputation should be made above and as close as possible to an eye, but with extreme care to avoid damaging it. To do this, place the blade of the serpette on the part of the bark opposite the eye, and a little higher than it. Bring the blade of the instrument up, cutting so as to form an oblique, bevelled wound, the upper end of which will end a little above, or about, the level of the upper end of the gem. Pl. 7, fig. 1. One of the principles of cutting is to always make a bevel cut when the point of section is within the length of a scion or a young stem; and, as far as circumstances allow, the wound should be turned towards the north, so that, being naturally shaded, it is not so quickly dried out by the sun's rays. The result of bevel cutting is that, should any sap flow, it flows along the wound onto the bark opposite the bud, and does not risk reaching it. On plants with soft, supple wood, and especially those whose sap will be very abundant, the principle is no longer the same. No matter how clean the wound, it never heals on the cut itself; the end of the wood dries out and dies; the mortality descends to form a snag of a certain length, and would destroy the eye if it reached it. Walnut, for example, is in this situation. The reason for this is undoubtedly that the wood's great porosity allows air and heat to penetrate to a certain depth into its fibers, destroying their organization by drying out the nourishing juices right down to their finest vessels. The moisture that then penetrates completes the destruction of the medullary substance. It seems that, in trees with a high sap flow, such as vines and fig trees, there is another cause in addition to 3 this one. Shortly after cutting, the sap flows into the wound in great abundance, escaping and overflowing it on all sides; despite the inclination of the chisel, it flows over the eye and drowns it. Therefore, whenever you operate on individuals of this type, you should make the same bevel cut as for the previous ones, but at least half an inch above the eye. Pl. 7, fig. 2. This will produce a small snag, which will disappear the next time the tree is pruned, because it has been carefully removed to repair an unpleasant deformity, and to allow the bark to cover the wound. There is another way of avoiding this problem: by pruning closer to the eye, you could cover the wound with soft wax; but the time this method would take makes it impossible when you have a large number of trees to prune. When a small branch or bud is to be cut off entirely, it should be cut as cleanly as possible at its base, i.e. at the bark of the stem or branch on which it was placed. Extreme care should be taken to perfectly unite the wound, so as to make it easier to cover it by bringing the bark of the subject closer together. Pl. 7, fig. 3, a, b, It is always with astonishment that we see a large number of cases where authors make exceptions to this rule, which in our opinion should not exist. For example, they state that, in gum-prone trees such as peach, if we cut a branch or bud while the sap is in motion, i.e., while the tree still has its leaves, we must leave a snag to prevent gum and the canker that follows. We have never left a snag on any of the trees we have grown, and we have never noticed the slightest inconvenience. It is above all for resinous trees, such as pines, firs, etc., that they recommend, for all pruning periods, snags, whose slightest effect is to deprive a tree of the pleasant look that makes up all its merit in a garden where it can only be considered as an ornamental object. The reason they give is that the sap extravasates and exhausts the subject. We believe this to be the case, if one were imprudent enough to prune during the vegetation period; but, in a favorable season, we are certain that no accident would result. Experience would not have convinced us of what we are saying, and reasoning alone would lead us not to act otherwise. It's certain that any amputation always causes a tree to suffer at the moment it's made: so why put ourselves in the position of having to perform two operations for one? Besides, in resinous trees, if there is to be sap loss, it will just as easily occur through a wound made on a stump six inches long, as through a wound closer to the trunk. The authors say that this stump should be left to dry, and then broken off; this is even more inappropriate, as it is impossible in this case for the scar to recover, and mortality will necessarily increase with the thickness of the stem. All the resinous trees that have come out of our nurseries in the last twenty-five years have been pruned flush with the trunk, and we have never complained about their vigour. If a branch to be cut off is too big to be cut with a serpette, we saw it as close as possible to the trunk; then we seal the wound with a sharp instrument, until the smallest trace of the saw has disappeared, because otherwise the amputation would never heal; decay would quickly set in, and lead to the loss of the subject. If the wound is not large, it is covered with grafting wax; otherwise, with 11 Saint-Fiacre ointment. If, in order to rejuvenate a tree, we were obliged to cut it back to its trunk, i.e., to amputate its largest branches, we would use the saw as we have just said; we would unite the scar with a sharp, strong instrument, such as a carpenter's chisel, and cover the amputation with St. Fiacre's ointment, held in place by a canvas headstock. Mr. John Robertson, a member of the London Horticultural Society, proposes to replace St. Fiacre's ointment or wax, in this case and whenever it is necessary to treat a wound caused by the extirpation of a canker or other disease, with a mixture of tar and pulverized charcoal. We believe that this composition may be as good as our grafting waxes; but we see no reason why it should be better. If the tree were precious, or if we were very keen to preserve it, we would spread a thick layer of grafting wax over the wound, covering it with an extremely thin sheet of lead, similar to that used to wrap tobacco. This must be thick enough to protect the scar from contact with meteors, but not thick enough to resist the new bark as it forms. Finally, when the head of a young tree has to be cut off, the wound can be covered using the close bark grafting method. See page 59, n° 45. Time of pruning. The most favorable time to prune trees is in spring, when vegetation begins. In the Paris climate, this operation is usually carried out from the beginning of February to the middle of April. But this cannot be taken as a general rule, for the reason that, 2. even without leaving France, there are regions where vegetation is much earlier than in the vicinity of Paris, and others where it is less so. So, it's in the very principles of vegetation that we need to study the precise period at which we should prune. When a plant is in a state of absolute rest, when the sap, stagnant in its woody vessels, cannot carry nourishment to any of its parts, it would be absurd to make a wound which, unable to cover itself immediately, would remain exposed for a long time to the pernicious influences of air, cold and frost. The disease would penetrate to the very depths of the trunk, forming incurable cankers that would soon lead to the total loss of the individual. If a tree is in vegetation, the disadvantage of mutilating it while nature is at work becomes just as great. The sap is imprudently opened up to channels through which it escapes and is lost outside, instead of reaching the gems it was supposed to develop into leaves, buds or fruit. In addition to this, the individual, suddenly interrupted in its vegetation, experiences a jolt that can cause it to perish. If an amateur were uneducated enough to prune a tree when it is in full sap, that is, when its gems have already metamorphosed into twigs with leaves, a greater danger would be added to those we have just mentioned. Stripped of the absorbent pores that nature has placed on the leaf surface for plant respiration, the tree would suddenly perish of asphyxiation. These two equally dangerous pitfalls must be avoided. To do this, take advantage of the moment when the tree begins to vegetate enough to hope that, after a few days, the sap, increasing in vigor, will easily repair the alterations caused by pruning, 1 moment when the sap is not moving enough to risk being stopped in its course, and when it is not flowing enough to be lost, and consequently exhaust the subject. It's easy to see that this favorable period cannot be precisely indicated, since not only does it advance or delay due to different climates and more or less precocious years, but also due to the health of the individuals and the hot or cold exposure in which they will be placed. For example, a peach tree in light, warm soil, facing south, will flower eight or even two weeks earlier than another in less favorable soil, facing east or west. There's more: two trees of the same species and variety may, for reasons that are little or not at all known, flower, and consequently vegetate, at fairly distant intervals from one another. But there is a very simple way of recognizing, in all climates and under all circumstances, the true moment of pruning; this means spying on the moment when the prodigiously swollen buds are going to develop into flowers and leaves. The gardener's trained eye can distinguish with certainty the species of buds, enabling him or her to direct the operation with ease and discernment. When pruning a large crop, it is very important to choose the right moment, firstly to maintain the vigour and health of the trees, and secondly to avoid wasting time. If, through forgetfulness or lack of knowledge, you neglected to prune the early species first, and started with the later ones, the result would be that you would find yourself surprised by the vegetation, and O6 that you would suddenly have more trees to prune than you could cope with. Forced to mutilate them during their first growth, not only would their constitution be greatly altered, but you would also be exposed to breaking and throwing down a quantity of good joists and flower buds. There isn't a gardener who doesn't know that wood becomes much more fragile when it's in sap, and that trellising is very-difficult then. To follow the order of vegetation, we prune apricot trees first, because they bloom first, then peach trees, followed by plum, pear and cherry trees, and finally apple trees, because they bloom last, usually in April. GENERAL PRUNING GUIDELINES. Until now, it has been believed that sap has two movements, one ascending, destined to the growth and maintenance of wood, the other descending, charged by nature with developing and nourishing flowers and fruit. This doctrine, professed by learned and respectable men, would be a very interesting subject for discussion in a treatise on physiology, but we won't deal with it here, because the consequences drawn from it with regard to pruning are poorly deduced and of no application, since all the phenomena of vegetation and fruiting can be explained very well without the help of these ascending and descending saps. So, whether sap rises and falls by a regulated or periodic movement, or whether it doesn't, which seems more likely to us, the principles of pruning and of horticulture as a whole are no less the same; what's more, they will be much easier to conceive, once we have rid them of the hypothetical reasoning deduced from this prejudice. 1 FIRST PRECEPT. A tree's vigor depends, to a great extent, on the equal distribution of sap in all its branches. If the sap leaves some branches to flow more freely into others, those left behind will first lose weight, sometimes run out of fruit, and become thin and spindly. Mortality starts at the tip of the twig, spreads to the branch, and soon to the trunk; and, either if the diseased part is left there or detached too late, it forms a canker which soon brings the tree to complete ruin. No one has ever failed to notice this, particularly on espaliers. When one of the two mother branches dies, it's impossible to re-form the tree on the remaining one, and we're still lucky if we can prolong its existence by dint of art and care. It is therefore necessary, if a tree is to retain its health and shape, to prune in such a way as to maintain a perfect balance in all its main branches; and the gardener must pay scrupulous attention to this; for, if he neglects for a year or two to remedy the weakness of a branch by the most effective means, he will no longer be able to recapture the sap carried away by the other parts. This rule should be followed most rigorously during the first few years, when sap is most resistant to the symmetrical, unnatural shapes we impose on plants to make them more pleasing or useful. However, this is also the moment when it is easiest to tame sap, because we only work on a few buds. 009 SECOND PRECEPT. The life and vigor of a tree depend, to a great extent, on the constant balance between its branches and roots. It follows from this principle that whenever a newly planted tree is pruned, and its roots have suffered, or even lost some of their parts in the process of replanting, which is almost always the case, its branches should be cut back close to the stem, so that they do not have to expend a greater quantity of sap than the roots can supply. The same procedure should be followed whenever the roots are affected by disease or old age. If we acted otherwise, the roots would soon be exhausted, the sap responsible for providing them with hair would be absorbed to their detriment by the branches, and the tree would perish after languishing for some time. But it sometimes happens that, through whim or some other cause, we want to amputate the large branches of a tree. If its roots are vigorous and healthy, which is easily recognized by the beauty of its vegetation, they should never be cut back to the stem or trunk, because the abundant sap supplied by the roots, finding no outlet to rush out and develop into leaves and buds, will remain in its vessels, deteriorating there, and dealing a blow to the tree's health from which it will never recover well; but that's assuming he doesn't die of repletion in the first, second or third year at the latest. The stem should therefore be left with enough branches and buds to keep the sap busy, and provide it with a passage to the outside. From this principle, we can also draw the 009 escential consequence that a young subject, with vigorous roots, can nourish a greater number of buds than an old one, and consequently be pruned longer. THIRD PRECEPT. As sap always tends to rise from roots to branches as vertically as possible, it abounds in straight branches, to the detriment of others. It is on the knowledge of this principle that we have established the arcure, a means by which we hinder the progress of sap, and force it to divert to other buds. On the other hand, when the trellised branch of an espalier, for example, becomes thin, the sap is forced to flow to it by straightening it into a more or less vertical position, as required. As a result, when we fear that a tree is thinning at the bottom, we prevent the sap from reaching the top by inclining the upper branches. Some authors recommend long pruning in such cases, and we have a comment to make on this subject. The reason for this is quite simple: instead of concentrating on one or two buds (which would have happened if we had pruned short), and developing them into gourmands, the sap will be forced to divide itself between twelve or fifteen to which it will provide nourishment, and these will only acquire a suitable development; the fact is certain. But won't the quantity of sap required for fifteen small branches be as considerable, and even da vantage, as that diverted for the benefit of two larger ones? We believe so; and then O00 the sum of expenditure being equal, the lower branches will find themselves weaned off an equal quantity of sap. This means of restoring balance is therefore vicious, and should not be used. If the vigour of a branch cannot be stopped by inclining it, it would be better to use a bow, or a half-twist, or perhaps even a ring incision, than to prune it long. FOURTH PRECEPT. Sap develops much more vigorous buds on a branch pruned short, than on one pruned long. The reason for this is extremely easy to understand: having to develop only one or two buds, it is clear that it will develop them much more strongly than if it had had to divide itself between fifteen or twenty. This precept is nonetheless very useful, because a vigorous branch will only very rarely bear fruit, so you should prune short whenever you want to have strong, well-nourished wood; but you must not believe, as some authors say, that the sap carries more force in a shortened branch than in another. It follows from this that, when any part of a tree is less vigorous than another, either because it has been exhausted by a large production of fruit, or because it is languishing due to disease, by pruning it short for two or three years, we will obtain a sufficient quantity of wood to bring back the sap, restore its original vigor, and by this means re-establish the necessary balance in all parts of the plant. O08 FIFTH PRECEPT. Sap, always tending to flow to the end of branches, develops the terminal bud more vigorously than the lateral buds. This is especially true of young trees. Whenever you want to extend a branch, you need to prune on the most vigorous wood eye, leaving no twig or stalk beyond that could divert its nourishing juices; it's on this eye that you need to focus all your attention. Choosing the right eye requires a great deal of thought. For example, if you're working on an espalier, regardless of the fact that it should be kept only where wood is needed, either to extend a mother branch or a limb, it must be placed above or below the branch, and never on the sides. In the case of a cattail, other considerations apply: if the species of tree tends to develop its lateral branches too horizontally, which is very rare, it would be necessary to prune on a bud placed on the side of the stem to bring the branches closer together. But, much more commonly, if the branches tended to grow too close to the trunk, to grow too vertically, we would always prune on an eye placed outside, i.e. on the opposite side to the stem. Finally, since trees are subject to thinning out in some of their parts, either through an accident, or through poor pruning in the previous year, or through mortality caused by disease, we try to fill the void by pruning on eyes facing the side we want to fill. 508 SIXTH PRECEPT. If a branch is entirely removed, the sap benefits the neighboring branches and twigs. When a branch is so exhausted that there is no hope of it recovering, or when it is affected by canker or any other disease with a long or doubtful cure, or when, on a tree with a regular shape, it occupies a place that disturbs symmetry, it should be felled without hesitation. Neighboring branches will soon replace it in a way that is more advantageous for the elegance of the form; and, if it has been cut off because of disease, this replacement will take much less time than if we had tried to cure it instead of cutting it off. However, we would always advise the grower to think carefully before deciding to amputate a large branch from a tree; for, despite all the precautions he may take, this mutilation will always harm the tree, and all the more so the older it is, and the larger the wound. Only extreme necessity should determine this. Our precept should only be applied generally to branches no older than two, three or four years. SEVENTH PRECEPT. Branches in which sap flows abundantly produce much wood and little fruit; those in which sap does not flow abundantly produce much fruit and little wood. The consequence of this principle is that, when a branch becomes too woody, all we have to do is divert the sap by any means, such as tilting it horizontally, to force it to bear fruit. If, on the other hand, we wanted to turn it into wood, we would straighten it and concentrate all its sap on two or three buds, by pruning it short and on two or three eyes. Experience has shown that if you prune over one or two eyes, you'll get strong, sturdy woody branches; if you prune to half length, the top third will provide woody buds, the middle third twigs, and the bottom third joists; at least that's the way nature works on mature trees, and on ordinary branches that are neither too weak nor too vigorous. Often, a tree will grow branches of extraordinary vigor, which gardeners rather inappropriately call "gourmands". They are quick to prune them very short, and by this clumsy operation, they keep them in extraordinary and useless vegetation. It's much simpler to prune them very long, or even just pinch their tips, bend them and force them to set fruit. These branches can become precious, and should only be cut back when they risk deforming the tree. EIGHTH PRECEPT. The more sap is hindered in its circulation, the more twigs and fruit buds it produces. This principle is based on the same physiological reasons as detailed in the article on grafting, i.e. on the natural tendency of all plants to hasten the moment of fruiting when some alteration seems to threaten them with premature destruction. He explains in a very natural way how a bow, a twist, an annular or longitudi1008 nal incision, by impeding the flow of sap, determines a hitherto sterile branch to set fruit. It seems that the ancients knew, or at least suspected, this phenomenon of vegetation; for, when they wanted to force a tree that was too vigorous to bear fruit, they made a prosaic notch in its trunk, or a hole into which they drove a dowel of dry wood. NINTH PRECEPT. Any branch that is disbudded or pinched will produce a large number of branches and fruit buds, due to the overabundance of sap that cannot be released by developing wood. But for this principle to have its full effect and application, the operation must be carried out at the time of disbudding, i.e. after the first sap and during part of the year; For, if it were done in the first days of spring, the result would be that either the cambium would emerge through the bark and create new gems which would soon develop into wood, or that the subject would perish through a kind of plenitude; and in this case the disease would be announced by gum or incurable cankers. So, when you have exhausted the ordinary means of very long pruning, inclination, bowing or twisting, without success, on a woody branch, pinch its tip and cut off its lateral buds; this will soon force it to set fruit. 908 TENTH PRECEPT. The more you force a tree to bear fruit, the more you exhaust it; the more you keep it in wood, the more you increase its vigor. This principle shows gardeners how they should behave if they want to obtain good harvests for a long time on robust trees; they will never overload them, and the fruit will be much bigger, of better quality, and more abundant. This last point might seem extraordinary if we didn't explain it. Everyone has noticed that, once a tree has produced a large quantity of fruit, it usually goes one, two or even three years without yielding anything. The reason for this is that the tired tree, having exhausted all its joists, is obliged to reform others on new wood. And since the joists usually take two or three years to form, it's clear that they won't bear fruit during that time. Can a tree, however overloaded with fruit, produce as much in a single harvest as it would in three ordinary harvests? This is not a difficult question to answer, and we can say with complete certainty that it cannot. If three harvests of better quality are equivalent, and more, to one whose fruit will be small, gravelly and unscented, it is therefore to the grower's advantage to establish, as far as possible, an annual balance of produce for as long as the tree lives. To achieve this, pruning should leave only as much fruit as the tree can feed without damaging the woody branches, and therefore without altering its constitution. We have often seen amateurs admire their gardener with a kind of enthusiasm, 009 because their trees were covered, under his hand, with a prodigious number of fruits: if this same gardener had performed a similar prodigy in our establishment, we would have thrown him out immediately; we would have had him pruned, and we would not have been in a position to conclude, as the amateurs do, that an espaliered peach tree only lives twelve or fifteen years, and that a pear cattail only lasts twenty or twenty-five. The health and longevity of a tree depend very much on the reasoned balance the gardener strikes in the production of fruit and wood branches; and, if some of the former are to be sacrificed, it would be better to cut them down. What little may be lost in one year will be recouped in subsequent harvests. ELEVENTH PRECEPT. Depending on the species, fruit buds appear either at the tip of the branch or along the branch. This means that not all fruit trees can be pruned every year, if they are to produce an annual crop. The medlar, for example, cannot be pruned; as its fruit is always borne at the end of the branches, it would have to be cut down every year if we wanted to correct the bizarre shapes it affects in its habit. The same applies to the quince tree. TWELFTH PRECEPT. Fruit buds, in seed-bearing species, are usually born on old wood, and in stone-bearing species on one-year-old wood. All good pruning methods are based on the recognition and rigorous application of this principle. We have said that the fruit buds of seed-bearing species usually develop on old wood, because apple and pear trees are sometimes exceptions to this general rule. However, in order to help the reader better understand the consequences we are about to deduce, we feel it our duty, first and foremost, to enable him or her to recognize perfectly the nature of wood and buds, in relation to their product, and their differences resulting from the various species. THIRTEENTH PRECEPT. In trees with seeded fruit, all the gems, as they develop, are organized in such a way as to be able to produce, according to circumstances, wood buds, twigs or lambounds. A wood bud is easily recognized by its position: it is always applied to the branch, without any particular support or foot that carries it on and off. Its shape is thin, slender and elongated; it is less enveloped in scaly membranes than the others, and its tip extends into a sharper point, a little curved at the beginning of its growth. It is most commonly found at regular intervals on the previous year's buds, particularly at their tips. (Pl. 7, fig. 3, c, d.) The fruiting or flowering buds are carried on small supports of a particular shape and naturalness, to which we have given the names lambourdes and brindilles. In seed trees, these supports are the true fruiting branches. Fruit buds can be recognized not only by their peduncles, but also by their shape. They are much larger than wood buds, more enveloped in scales, and more rounded in shape. (Pl. 7, fig. 3, e.) The twig (Pl. 7, fig. 3.) is a small branch, O two to five inches long, which first developed from a woody œeil, and would have produced a branch if some particular circumstance had attracted a sufficient quantity of sap to it. Its bark is usually smooth, especially when it is at its longest, and it can be found on all parts of a tree, provided they are neither too young nor too old. Nevertheless, it is not uncommon to see a new bud produce a twig on the second shoot, and to see others emerge through the rough bark of an old branch, or even a stem. The twig is almost always provided, especially at its base and tip, with two or three eyes, from which, if necessary, wood could be obtained; but, to do this, it would be necessary to bend it over two of these eyes, and attract the sap to it by cutting off the buds placed above and below its base, or even by shortening the whole branch. A good twig that has never been mutilated can last and bear fruit for many years. The gardener's pruner must therefore always respect it, unless it is misplaced and deforms the tree. If it grows a bud at its tip, as in f, the following year, break it off or cut it off near its exit. The lambourde (pl. 7, fig. 3, g.) is the immediate support for a fruit bud. It usually develops on a twig, but often on an old or young woody branch. It usually takes three years to form, and sometimes longer. In the first year, instead of developing a single leaf and a woody bud in its axil, a bud produces three leaves and a small support one to four lines long, which is the first rudiment of the lambourde (fig. 3, h.). In the second year, the bud, already rounder and larger than a wood bud, produces five leaves; the lambourde lengthens by half an inch, more or less, and its bark wrinkles circularly to form deep, irregular folds; (fig. 3. g.) The following year it produces seven leaves (and sometimes, but rarely, its flower). The bud is then considerably larger than a wood-eye, and can no longer be mistaken for one, even by the most inexperienced man; yet its shape is still elongated. Its scales, scarce and brown at the tip, are pale green at the base, (fig. 3, k, K.) The lambourde has elongated further, revealing on its sides, near the base of the bud, other gems which, the following year, will in turn produce other fruit buds (fig. 3, l.). Finally, in the fourth year, the prodigiously swollen, almost round bud (fig. 3, e.) develops an indeterminate number of leaves, and a corymb of flowers in the middle. The flap has reached its full length, the folds of its bark have become even more pronounced, and the new buds, if any, are already sufficiently characterized for us to be able to judge the year in which they will bear fruit. The new shoots on which they are placed are much shorter than those on which they are placed, and very rarely produce new shoots. These are the general rules of fruiting in pear trees, but exceptions are extremely frequent. Young, vigorous pear trees often develop fruiting shoots and buds on current-year wood. We have even sometimes seen a gem grow a vigorous scion for the first month, then suddenly develop, in the middle of its length, a flower bud whose fruit knotted in July and reached, before falling, a quarter or a third of its size. These anomalies are even more common on apple trees, particularly on apis grafted on paradise. It happens that, on the previous year's wood, one or more flower buds develop in April, blossom immediately and 2. 10 GOO bear fruit the following autumn. It follows that, if you want to make the most of these last trees, you must not prune them until vegetation is sufficiently advanced to recognize these precious buds perfectly and not cut them down; which would necessarily happen if you pruned in the ordinary way. This would necessarily happen if the pruning was carried out in the usual way, as it is then necessary to keep wood which would otherwise have been removed; but when disbudding, we start to remove the wood on which the flowers or fruit have aborted, and at the next pruning we repair the deformities. Moreover, accidental circumstances can easily change the destination of a fruit bud, whether in its first, second, third or fourth year. If sap suddenly flows into it, the characteristics that made it a fruiting bud disappear, and a woody bud takes its place. The clues we have drawn from the number of leaves developing on a fruit bud are even more uncertain, because it takes less influential circumstances to produce remarkable variations. FOURTEENTH PREGEST. In stone fruit trees, flower buds are usually born on wood of the year, and cannot metamorphose into wood buds. Cherry and a few other species are frequent exceptions to the first part of this rule, which must be applied rigorously only to peach trees, and for which we will also give the principles of fruiting. Peach trees have two types of branches that are easy to distinguish, but which must be recognized before pruning begins. The first O09 are the woody branches, always the strongest and most vigorous; they are at least the size of a finger, and often reach an inch or more in diameter if the trees are young, and in a suitable exposure and soil; their length, in this case, can reach from three to eight feet. But the best way to recognize them is that, from the autumn of the first year, they take on the uniform grayish hue of old wood. Fruiting branches are to peach trees what twigs are to seed trees, but with the essential difference that they only bear fruit once, and that if they are kept, they only produce other thin fruiting branches. They are rarely less than six inches long, and even more rarely reach two and a half feet. Their ordinary size is equal to that of a small feather-pipe; and, however vigorous a tree may be, it rarely reaches the size of a little finger. On the contrary, they retain a reddish tint on the side facing the sun, and their green color on the side facing the wall. On peach trees, however, there is a kind of fruit-bearing branch that can be compared to lambourdes, but with the difference that it only flowers once; it consists of a small snag no more than one, two or three inches long, trimmed all around with flower buds, and ending at the top with a wood bud (1). In stone trees, the gems destined to form a flower bud, as we have said, can never provide a woody eye; but the result is (1) If we were writing a work on physiology, we would be careful not to establish divisions in the nature of branches; because in reality there are no two species, and all can produce wood and fruit equally well on the peach tree as on all other GOOs O no disadvantage, because, as the flower buds are always placed next to a wood bud, if it were a question of reproducing a new branch, we could always prune on it. To be able to prune with full knowledge of the facts, it is essential to be able to distinguish perfectly between a wood bud and a flower bud, and this is not difficult if you pay a little attention, because by August, the differences are already sufficient to characterize them. Flower buds are rounded and large, and each has a leaf whose function is to attract sap until they are perfectly formed, at which point the sap ceases to be useful and falls off; wood buds are thinner, longer and more pointed. FIFTEENTH PRECEPT. All flowering buds on stone fruit trees remain sterile unless accompanied by a woody bud. Here's a principle of recognized accuracy, published long ago, but which few gardeners are aware of, even though it is essential to know in order to direct the proper pruning of peach trees. The fruiting branches of the peach tree do not all have the same arrangement of flowering buds. On some, a single flower bud is placed next to a wood bud; on others, two flower buds are placed, one on one side and the other on the other side of a wood bud; on a third type of branch, the flower bud is not accompanied by its wood eye, and so the branch, being arbrea. However, as the weakness of one branch means that it is more likely to bear fruit than another, we have adopted a special name for it, and consequently an artificial division into fruiting branches and woody branches. We have done this to present the pruning operation in a clearer and easier way. If the fruit is sterile, it must be removed, because if it were to ripen, as it sometimes does, it would be of mediocre quality. Finally, in the species of lambourdes we mentioned earlier, the flowers gathered in bouquets have no wood-eyes next to their buds; but they are no less fertile, because the bud, placed at the top, is sufficient to provide them with the necessary nourishment: so they should never be pruned, since this could not be done without removing the wood-eye. It's easy to see that nature has only placed a bud next to each flower to attract sap and supply the young fruit. If the bud is missing, the blossom falls without tying the knot; if by chance the fruit forms, it languishes for a while, turns yellow, dries out, and usually perishes before reaching a third or half of its size. Peach trees should therefore not be pruned until the wood-eyes have begun to develop; for, as winter very often exposes them to death, if we were unable to recognize those that are alive and intact, it would very often happen that we would keep as good fruit branches whose wood-eyes had been destroyed by frost or cold, and the result would be that we would have kept many sterile flowers, while we would have cut down good ones. In addition, these useless woody branches would remain bare, without flowers, leaves or wood, for the whole year, forming unpleasant and pernicious snags. However, we must not wait to prune until the tree is in full bloom, because then the operation would be very-difficult so as not to knock off a large part of it, especially when palming the branches. 100 SIXTEENTH PRECEPT. Once a peach tree branch has borne fruit, it no longer bears fruit. It is therefore necessary to renew it, i.e. to cut it down in order to have another one develop on one of its lower eyes, which will have been preserved for this purpose. It is the knowledge of this principle that has led to the discovery of the pruning operation in the peach tree, which we call replacement, and which we will deal with in greater detail, because it is as essential as it is little practised by routine gardeners. It is perhaps only in a few gardens around Paris and in the village of Montreuil that all the important consequences of this rule have been drawn. SEVENTEENTH PRECEPT. Leaves are used by plants to breathe; any tree that is partly deprived of them is impaired in its health; if it is entirely deprived of them, it risks perishing. So, in summer pruning, disbudding, etc., only as much foliage should be removed as is strictly necessary to achieve the intended purpose. If a tree - a peach tree, for example - were so full of fruit that a large proportion of its leaves had to be removed in order to allow it to benefit from the sun's fragrant and coloring influences, it would be better to sacrifice the quality of some of the fruit than risk making the tree very tired. This precept also indicates that, when pruning in spring, we must leave a tree, albeit formed, with a sufficient quantity of wood to provide it with a well-filled foliage, otherwise it will gradually perish from asphyxia. It also follows from this principle that whenever we remove all the leaves from a twig or branch while the sap is flowing, if the season is too late for sub-buds to develop, the branch or twig will be abandoned by the sap and will dry out. EIGHTEENTH PRECEPT. Any branch or twig around which air, light and heat cannot circulate freely will wither, elongate, become thin and flabby, and produce neither fruit nor wood. It was this rule of vegetation, known to ancient French growers, that perhaps gave rise to the first idea of subjecting trees to regular forms, which, by placing each branch and twig in a calculated position, enables all parts of a tree to enjoy the influences of air, light and heat. We see proof of this in the cups hollowed out on the inside, and in the fans so highly recommended by Laquintinie. We draw the consequence from this principle, that a gardener should never allow branches and twigs to pile up in the center of a tree, so as to make a mass of foliage too bushy and thick enough to prevent atmospheric influences from circulating freely through it. Nature herself has laid down this principle, an example of which she gives us in all the individuals abandoned to her care. If we follow the development of an apple tree, for example, throughout its life, we will see that, as its branches lengthen and move away from the trunk, it forms domes of greenery, provided only by the ends of the branches, and placed in such a way as to be able to receive the influences of the air which can circulate with ease in the bare interior of the tree's head. It will not be objected that this is an effect of the principle and not the cause of it, if we consider that the branches refuse to naturally produce wood buds along most of their length, and mainly as they approach their base. NINETEENTH PRECEPT. Old wood only produces buds when forced to do so by pruning or by the alteration of the young wood that ends the branch. It is therefore necessary, especially in espaliers, for the gardener to prune carefully so as to keep branches at the base of the main branches, as they lengthen, in order to always have enough wood to fill the interior of the tree. If it is stripped, it will be impossible to develop wood eyes, and the tree will needlessly occupy a very large space. This is the major flaw in almost all peach trees found in amateur gardens. There is now only one way to repair this defect, and that is to bring a bud from a neighboring branch onto the branch lacking wood, and graft it there using the approach grafting method, page 54, no. 38, or to place a scion graft if the bark is still young enough; but this method will never repair the defect in such a way as to make the shape of the tree as beautiful and regular as if it had been well cared for from the start. 008 TWENTY-FIRST PRECEPT. Any bud developed outside the time of the two sapstocks is usually sterile, thin and incapable of producing wood or fruit. These types of production, which have been given the name of false buds, and which we more appropriately call under-buds, are never found on a tree abandoned to nature's care. They are the result of an accident or of pruning carried out in an untimely season. For example, if the trellising of a peach tree were done too early, i.e. before the buds were sufficiently developed, the removal of useless wood would cause the sap to flow to the eyes that should not have developed until the following year, and these eyes would give only aborted wood, incapable of ripening and withstanding the rigours of winter, hence under-budding. Lastly, any eye that vegetates before perfect maturity, and before a rest between two saps, produces a sub-sprout. They must all be removed as sterile and useless, serving only to exhaust a tree. toviνdddωιυππιυιινπυddυν SECOND SECTION. SOME PRUNING OPERATIONS. The proper management of a fruit tree, or any other, is not limited to spring pruning alone in order to obtain the result one promises. It must also be subjected to various other practices, depending on the circumstances and the goal to be achieved, and it is from the judicious and reasoned execution of all these operations that the art of pruning has been formed. Disbudding. This essential operation for all tree shapes, and indispensable for espaliers, consists in removing all unnecessary or misplaced buds, before or after their development. In the first case, in autumn or spring, as soon as the last sap has run, i.e. as soon as the leaves have fallen, or before the gems have developed, we choose a day of fine weather, or at least without rain, and with our fingernails, or better still with a sharp instrument, we detach and remove all the misplaced buds. As a result, 1° the wounds heal much more easily and, above all, more quickly than if we had waited to disbud until the gems had begun to grow; this is because the wounds are much smaller, and the sap's first efforts can be directed at them; 2° the sap, being diverted from the outset, is more vigorously directed to the useful buds; 3° the second disbudding is easier, less extensive, and therefore less harmful to the tree. With much less suppression, the balance of vegetation will be less disturbed, and there will be few under-budding. The second, and almost the only, disbudding is carried out when the young buds have reached a quarter, a third, or at most half of their growth, which usually happens in May in the Paris area, or earlier or later in warmer or colder climates. We use an instrument to remove all those trees on an espalier that are on the sides of the branches, to 908 keep only those that are on top or underneath, so as to be able to trellis them easily, which would not be possible if they were on the front, unless we made them form a very unpleasant bend. Those placed on the side facing the wall would have an even worse effect, because they would be crumpled in their growth, which would soon lead to gum or canker. It's the terminal buds of the main branches that need the most attention. It must be the most robust to provide a strong, vigorous extension. If this is not the case, either because it has been altered by an accident and has produced only a weak shoot, or because it has failed altogether, the end of the branch should be cut back to the strongest lower bud. This bud must be dominant over all the others, and it is this consideration that would determine the gardener's choice. In stone fruit trees, disbudding is carried out not only on wood branches, but also on fruit branches. These should not be touched until the fruit is knotted and perfectly formed, otherwise irreparable mistakes could be made for the rest of the year. All woody buds not destined to bear fruit should therefore be removed, and even if they have not all set, the branch should be cut back to the last bud to bear fruit; finally, if all the flowers have aborted, the branch should be cut off entirely, leaving only the two buds at its base, to provide new replacement branches, as described in the article bearing this title. It's especially at the time of disbudding that you need to detach with extreme care all the shoots that appear on the stems, and particularly on the root collar.
