Nothing exhausts a tree like the roots; if you delay cutting them off, they will constantly grow back from the nodule they form at their base, and it will be very difficult to destroy them without making a large and dangerous amputation, which can easily be avoided while the buds are still herbaceous. Another essential precaution is to give the buds the shape and inflection they should have when they become true branches; to do this, tie them in the direction they should take and in the place they should occupy, as they increase in length and strength. This is another way of avoiding accidents caused by high winds, which, by breaking them, would deprive the trees of wood that is all the more precious as it should be used to extend the mother branches. Sometimes, during disbudding, it may be useful to remove some of the fruit that a tree is overloaded with. It's up to the gardener to decide how much to remove, according to the nature and vigor of the tree. He should always arrange to leave an almost equal space between the remaining fruits, so that the nourishing juices are evenly distributed. He must also be careful not to injure the attachments or pedicels of the others, and above all to avoid shaking them. Moreover, although we have determined the most favorable time for this operation, it can be carried out all year round, and as often as circumstances require. Trellising. Its purpose is 1° to replace disbudding if it has not taken place; 2° to repair defects or omissions resulting from the first operation; 3° to facilitate fruit coloring and ripening by uncovering them; 4° to 009 allow useful branches to benefit from the sap by removing those that are not; 5° to prepare for spring pruning and make it much less long and difficult. This operation, referred to by some authors as "second pruning", is just as absolutely useful as the first, i.e. spring pruning, especially for stone fruit trees which have been given an espalier shape. The time of year depends entirely on the species, or even the variety, of tree on which it is performed. Clearly, varieties that ripen early should be trellised first, and later varieties last. However, it's not only the earliness of the fruit that should be taken into account in all cases. Here are the reasons why: if trellising were done too early, the large number of buds that would have to be removed would cause sap to carry over to the eyes destined to develop only the following spring, giving rise to a large number of sub-buds, all the more harmful as they would grow at the expense of the following year's wood. What's more, as the trellised buds have not yet reached their full length, the operation would have to be repeated eight days later, doubling the work and wasting a lot of time. The vice would be even greater if you palmed the fruit before it was strong enough to withstand the burning rays of the sun, or the drying influences of the hot winds. Until they have reached a certain size, they need to be covered by the shelter that nature has given them by making them grow under the leaves. We will therefore not say, as most authors do, that trellising should take place in June and July, but rather at the time that presents the least incon4 venience, i.e., as late as possible. Moreover, all buds must be long and strong enough to be easily tied. The moment when the first sap ends, and the interval during which vegetation seems to stop, only to start up again in August, seemed to us the most favorable time to begin. To prevent the fruit from turning brown, it's best to choose a dark or even rainy day, so as to gradually acclimatize them to the influences of air and light. This operation should be continued, if necessary, throughout the year for most fruit trees, and until replacement for peach trees. Young, strong, vigorous trees, especially when they have no fruit, can be trellised before the time we have just determined. Their strong, vigorous buds will more easily take on the shape you want to give them, and as they have not yet been subjected to regular pruning and training, they will be freed of a large quantity of useless branches and wood. As fruit-bearing branches are not expected, and their shoots are always longer than necessary for their formation, there is less fear of under-budding. The trellising operation consists in attaching to the walls and trellises in espaliers, to the hoops and stiles in counter-espaliers, goblets, etc., all the buds that must be kept, either to give wood or to provide fruit-bearing branches; it also consists, as we have said, in removing all the useless wood. The ties used should be soft and flexible, so as not to injure the young bark in any way. Against trellises, hoops or stiles, use rushes, and against walls, use nails and rags; in all cases, tighten the hen as little as possible. All preserved wood and fruit bungholes should be tied at various points along their length, depending on whether it is more or less considerable; they should be spaced, as evenly as possible, at distances calculated to avoid confusion, and above all they should never cross one another. Whatever the shape of the tree, they should be immediately placed in the position they must always maintain to maintain this shape and even give it grace. As for the useless buds that need to be cut off, there are several types, which we will deal with below. The first is to remove all those that are badly placed, i.e. those that would harm the tree's beautiful shape, due to their poor insertion on the mother branches. For example, in a perpendicular espalier, you should scrupulously remove any branches that protrude in front of or behind the branches, as they would tend to thicken a tree that should only be thick in width and height. A fine espalier should have, so to speak, only one surface and no thickness: at least, the latter should not be greater than the greatest diameter of the trunk, or the greatest length of a twig. In horizontal espaliers, on the other hand, we remove all buds above and below the trunk, for the same reasons applied to our species. Excluded from this general rule are twigs and lambourdes, as well as those small flowering branches, with a single terminal bud, often found on stone trees, and which, for peach, we have compared to lambourdes. If they are to bear fruit for several years, they should be kept as they are; if not, they should be removed at the next pruning. In the goblet, keep the necessary lateral buds, and remove the others, especially those growing outwards and inwards. The cattail is disbudded in a different way: as it must be filled in internally, but there must be no confusion, if the nature of the tree were to give it a tendency to fill in too much, only the lower buds are left, i.e. those turned outwards; this is always essential for a pyramid. Any poorly placed bud that can't be trained without forming a basket-handle bend gives the tree an unpleasant appearance, indicating the inexperience of the tree's leader. Buds which cannot be trained without causing confusion are poorly placed, whatever their insertion: they must therefore be cut back. When the eye destined to provide the extension of a branch has grown two or three buds, only the strongest and longest should be kept; the others should be cut off. However, it often happens that this terminal bud has developed several small sub-buds at its tip, giving it an unpleasant, spiky appearance. These cannot harm the shape of the tree, since they will be removed the next time the branch that produced them is pruned; in addition, they can be useful by attracting sap to the main bud, which strengthens it even more, and by amusing it in such a way as to prevent it from developing sub-buds in places where they could be harmful; they should therefore not be cut. At the second or third trellising, they can be removed, because by then the branch, as it lengthens, will have produced others which will replace them. In stone trees, and particularly in peach, remove all buds next to the fruit, but take extreme care 1 not to cut them below their third or fourth leaf; if you cut them off entirely, the fruit will lack nourishment, and may dry out; at the very least, it will rarely reach its natural size, and will lack fragrance. If we cut below the third leaf, the result would be much the same, because the snag left would run the risk of perishing, or would not attract a sufficient quantity of sap to nourish the fruit. The three lower buds, whether or not they accompany fruit, are left for the following reason. In trees of the same type as the previous ones, we cut off all the fruiting branches on which the fruit is aborted, and we cut them down to two or three lower buds, which we keep and trellis to replace them. If the three lower buds are missing, whether or not the branch has fruit, the higher ones should be kept. But it's a matter of principle that the lower ones are always preferable, because they lengthen this precious branch less, which is all the better for being placed closer to the stem of the mother branch. With the exception of those mentioned above, all under-bud trees are to be cut down, unless they are needed in places where, by chance, they are needed to fill a gap. In that case, we run the risk of seeing them perish during the winter, for want of having been sufficiently Augusted; but, if they resist, at least in part of their length, which happens quite frequently, they will become extremely valuable. These under-buds are what the authors no doubt call "rag branches". A branch that is diseased at the tip is often seen to produce, a little before the point where the disease begins, a vigorous bud of extraordinary growth; in a word, what gardeners with few ins2. 11 2 truits call a gourmand. In this case, and even in most other circumstances, we must be careful not to cut it down, because it will replace the sick or weak branch very advantageously; almost always we find strong, well-constituted sub-buds on its sides, especially when a trained eye has recognized and pinched it during disbudding; we keep them to make main branches, when it has become (at the next pruning) a vigorous mother branch. It remains for us to make an observation on this first trellising, which puts us at odds with the authors. They claim that every time a bud is cut at its base, i.e. on the bark of the branch on which it will be placed, the sap or gum will travel to the wound, which the season will not allow to heal, and that incurable cankers will most often result. Consequently, they want us to leave a small heel half an inch long, which we will cut down during the spring pruning. We think that this should be cut very close to the branch, because, contrary to what they say, the season is very favorable for healing, and in all possible cases, it is better to have only one operation, which always causes the tree less suffering. We have said that trellising must be carried out all year round, and this should be the case in a well-cultivated garden; but it is especially for peach espaliers that this work is indispensable. For the second trellising, little work is required if the first has been well done. The whole operation is limited to continuing to tie the buds as they lengthen, removing the under-buds as they are replaced by others capable of entertaining the sap, and trellising those you wish to keep. At the last trellising, when the sap is completely withdrawn, which in our climates takes place in September and October, we can, if we wish, remove all the under-buds from the ends of the branches, because we will no longer have to worry about growing the lower eyes destined to supply the following year's shoots. Breaking. This operation consists in breaking off three or four eyes of the twigs to be used for fruiting. We say "twigs" because we have heard of branches that are already ready to bear fruit, but which do not flower because they have an overabundance of sap that forces them to grow wood. Only long experience can enlighten the gardener as to which buds to break, and we consider this method to be extremely difficult to apply, although bad workers are in the habit of using it without the slightest hesitation. Every time you break a vigorous branch, or even one of mediocre strength, you'll get the exact opposite result to the one you were expecting; and, far from making it bear fruit, you'll make it develop a large number of sub-buds on the very eyes you wanted to turn into laths. Breakage affects fruiting by exhausting the branch through a wound which, unable to heal because of its splinters, causes a great loss of sap for a long time. What remains is carried to the gems, engorging them and preparing them to bear fruit. Care must be taken when performing this operation on peach and apricot trees, as the gum would immediately flow, and the tree would infallibly perish unless promptly remedied. More specifically, it can be used to great advantage on trees with seeded fruit. However, it should only be used on trees that are too vigorous, that lose their wood every year and don't produce fruit; it should be used sparingly on weak trees that are well equipped with joists and twigs. Whatever the majority of authors have said, breaking should usually only be carried out at pruning, and very rarely at the time of trellising. However, there are a few circumstances in which it can be useful during vegetation: when an untamable tree resists all the means used to make it fertile, such as very long pruning, for example; in this case, only the tip of the branch should be broken, and it will soon develop twigs and stalks. When you want to produce

In addition to breaking off the tip, other methods can be used to remove fruit from a vigorous branch which is to be removed later. These consist of cutting the branch transversely, more or less deeply, depending on its size, or cutting a notch in the bark and sapwood, or making an annular incision or arcure. Ring incision. This operation, unknown to ancient farmers, has been, like all modern discoveries, either too much blamed or too much praised. It is certain that, if used blindly in all circumstances, it will exceed its useful purpose; but, done with discernment, it can ensure harvests, increase the yield and size of fruit, and above all hasten maturity. It consists in removing a ring of bark from the branch on which it is applied. Below the OL fruit, a first circular incision is made, penetrating to the sapwood and embracing the entire circumference of the branch. Below this, about a line more or less, depending on the size of the subject, a second similar incision is made, then the strip of bark in between is removed. If you wish, you can operate with a serpette or a grafting tool, but it's easier to use a ring incisor. The operation can be carried out at any time of year, but is particularly effective during sap flow, when the flowers are about to bloom. Authors have written that the annular incision influences fruiting, because, they claim, the descending sap, being interrupted in its course, gives itself entirely to the fruits, to the formation and nourishment of which nature has entirely devoted it. We will ask these physiologists why, if it is only destined for fruit, it moves towards the roots when the annular incision interrupts its course; why, finally, it descends. We'll ask them why the branch that has undergone the annular incision doesn't stop covering itself with leaves and twigs which, according to them, are the product of the ascending sap. We will ask them why a wound made to a branch, but which does not interrupt the course of the descending sap, such as a through-hole, an implanted dowel, the longitudinal incision, or simple twisting, produces the same effect on fruiting as the annular incision they cite as proof of the existence of these two saps. But we won't pursue this line of reasoning and these awkward questions any further, because it was never our intention to turn this manual into a work of controversy or polemic. We will content ourselves with giving these effects the same cause as that which we have attributed to the effects of grafting and pruning: alteration. Only here, it is only local, existing only above the wound; whereas in pruning, it is general, and in grafting, it is general to the whole area above the weld, that is, to all the branches forming the head. If the effects of the annular incision are more marked, it's simply because, the lesion being more essential, the alteration is also greater. In any case, this operation should only be carried out on parts of a tree destined to be removed the following spring; for, even when the bark has reunited and the wound is perfectly healed, the branch is still suffering and exhausted. In addition, a plant on which this is done too often would end up suffering in all its parts, becoming generally exhausted, and perhaps perishing soon, or at least being weakened for the duration of its existence, which would itself be greatly shortened. From what we have just said, it can be seen that the ring incision is of little use on trees whose fruit-bearing branches are to be kept for several years, such as those with pepen fruit. For trees with stone fruit, it can hardly be used, as it would certainly lead to gum disease, and consequently to canker. This is because the fruiting branches are fed by clear sap, and renew themselves every year. For this reason, it is only with regard to this precious plant that the annular incision has been advocated or decried. Whenever a vine is vigorous, and placed in soil rich in nourishing juices, when the wood has developed and the grapes are about to blossom, the operation should be carried out at about the middle of the interval between the bunch and the old wood, i.e. above the fifth or sixth eye, so that the two to be preserved for pruning in the spring are not altered in any way. Perhaps it would also be a good idea to wait until the fruit is knotted, as the annular incision does nothing to prevent germ abortion, commonly known as coulure. Vine flowers, like the flowers of all plants, only abort or drop when the pistils have not been fertilized by the stamens, and there are several causes that can prevent this fertilization. A cold fog one night, followed by a blazing sun the next, is enough to destroy a flower's delicate pistil, or at least its stigma. Heavy rain during flowering can wet the anthers, bursting the pollen vesicles and dispersing the seminal liquor before it reaches the pistil. The annular incision can do nothing about this. We conclude from all this that, with the exception of vines, the gardener should only consider this operation as an extreme means of taming too abundant and vigorous sap in a branch. He should therefore only use it when all other means have been exhausted without success. Longitudinal incision. In some provinces, we were surprised to see this incision used to obtain a result positively opposite to the one it actually produces. Growers have been known to apply it to young chestnut, walnut and other high-growing fruit trees, to increase their vigor and make their trunks bigger. The longitudinal incision is made by splitting the bark of a tree from the birth of the branches to near the root collar. As it has to be done at the moment of sap flow, if the wound is to heal immediately and not cause any decay, it results in a great deal of wastage, which considerably weakens the individual. If this operation is really useful, it is to alter a young tree and force it to bear fruit before the time set by nature. But the gardener must be convinced that, if he obtains premature harvests, it is always at the expense of the tree's longevity. The longitudinal incision can also be made on a branch, in which case it has fewer disadvantages, because the stem still supplies sap, making it easier to repair the damage. Its results, in terms of fruiting, are absolutely the same as those of the annular incision. Arcure. This is the name given to the action of bending a branch into the shape of an arc or half-ring, in order to impede the flow of sap which, as we have said, tends to rise vertically. So, for the arch to fulfil its intended purpose, the end of the branch must be inclined towards the earth, and the supposed bowstring must be at least parallel to the horizon, if it were not inclined more towards the weaker side of the branch. The result of bowing is to make a bud develop twigs, laths and fruit-bearing branches, to the detriment of the wood; it should therefore only be used on all the branches of a tree when it possesses a vigor impossible to tame by elongated pruning, and which renders it sterile. There is one circumstance in which budding becomes extremely useful, and that is when the aim is to obtain a strong, vigorous bud along the length of a main branch, to replace, at the next pruning or later, D0 the branch's languishing tip. When disbudding, select a well-placed bud of good constitution; detach the branch and straighten its lower part (i.e. from its insertion in the stem to the bud), but without greatly affecting the shape of the espalier. The upper part (from the bud to the tip) is tilted as much as possible, forming a sharp bend just below the bud. The angle of this bend should be as acute as possible, without breaking or even overtaxing the woody fibres. The sap will immediately abandon the inclined end, which will nonetheless produce beautiful, good fruit; it will flow abundantly towards the bud, which will lengthen, grow considerably, and become what we call a gourmand, especially if it is pricked vertically. When it is big enough and long enough to replace the end of the branch, the whole branch is removed, the bend is straightened a little, the base is inclined as it was before, and the bud is formed into a vigorous extension, while the old end is used as a secondary branch very suitable for providing a quantity of twigs, lambourdes or fruiting branches. Delimbing. In fruit trees, the purpose of this operation is either to change the shape of a tree that is still young enough to lend itself to a new shape, or to bring together an old and exhausted tree, but valuable for some particular quality. By bringing it closer together, or, to use the gardener's expression, by lowering it right down to its trunk, it is forced to produce very vigorous young shoots, which soon bear 4 fruit, prolonging its existence for several years. These amputations must be carried out with the utmost care, and treated in the same way as described in the article on wood cutting on page 126. When you want to rejuvenate an espalier tree, an operation which is rarely carried out on anything other than pear and apple trees, you should always leave a foot or so at the base of the mother branches; if you cut closer to the root collar, you run the risk of seeing the stump perish. On full-wind trees, we also leave stumps of varying lengths, because the gems will form and pierce the bark more easily than the trunk, which is always much harder, and the new head will be a little less deformed. We even leave a few secondary branches to draw sap into the stumps, thereby hastening both their healing and the development of the new head. Sometimes a tree has to be limbed for reasons of locality, not all of which we can foresee. If, for example, it was to uncover a neighbouring property whose shade was detrimental, the branches would be amputated as close as possible to the trunk, to prevent them from regrowing buds, and to avoid putting ourselves in the position of having to start again soon; the sap, by its propensity to rise, would be carried in the vertical branch placed most perpendicularly to the wound, and would make no effort to form new gems around the scar, which it would abandon as soon as it was covered. But it's mainly amenity and forest trees that are prone to lopping, and it's also for them that this operation, so simple at first glance, requires a great deal of discernment and knowledge of dendrology. To achieve this, trees are delimbed in their youth, until their trunks have reached the desired height. Delimbing should be done with moderation, because it is by means of the sap attracted by the branches to different parts of the trunk that the latter gains strength and diameter. If it were deprived of this before its size was in balance with the volume of its head, it would always remain thin and slender, and would risk being broken by the slightest storm. Nor should a tree be limbed all the way to its tip, which would make it unpleasant to look at, and easier to be broken by the winds, besides which its head would never be proportionnće to the height of the trunk. Any forked tree, rising on two stems, must have one of them cut off, unless it is intended to produce a picturesque effect in a garden; and in that case it is liable to be parted by the wind, if its two main branches are not provided with branches and foliage right up to their enfourchure, so as to present only one mass of resistance to the storm. But it's exotic trees in particular, whose natural habit is unknown to us, that require great precautions when lopping. If their nature is to form a broad, low head, we run the risk of destroying them by lopping them to make them rise vertically. If, on the other hand, their stems are straight and slender, like those of the Italian poplar, and they are lopped to give them a broad, rounded head, they will never develop into anything more than languid, stunted individuals. Finally, there are some for which amputation can be fatal, when the branches have acquired a certain I SIZE. size; the tulip tree and virgilia in particular provide examples of this. If they are lopped, especially when the sap is in motion, the wound usually refuses to heal, and leaves a passage for the sap for several years, which extravasates, gets lost, and decays the bark around the amputation. Resinous trees are also in this situation, and cannot be safely lopped until they have reached twenty or thirty feet in height; and even then, only one of their whorls of branches should be felled each year, starting with the one at the bottom. Pruning. This operation consists of cutting off all diseased branches and dead wood from a tree, or subjecting it to regular shearing, to obtain wood suitable for making faggots; it is particularly performed on willow, poplar, ash, elm, etc., to obtain the latter result. In the first case, pruning in spring can be very useful for maintaining the health of ornamental trees and increasing their vigour; in the second, every two, three, four or five years, a fairly good quality of branches is obtained, but this is always at the expense of the trunk, especially when, to make cutting easier, the trees are topped and kept at a much lower height than they should naturally have been. If you want the amputation to cause as little harm as possible, it should be carried out at the right time, with an extremely sharp serrated blade, so that the clearer wounds can also be covered more easily with bark. No snags should be left, but neither should the cut be made very close to the trunk, otherwise too much sap will be lost. Shearing. This is done with shears or a crescent, on ornamental trees and shrubs that are to be given regular, pleasing shapes, for green fences, cradles, canopies, vases and other figures. It should only be carried out during sap stagnation, and will be repeated several times a year. The first will take place in spring, before the buds develop; the second towards the end of the spring sap, and the third after the August sap, around mid-September. This last one is not necessary if you intend to do the spring one; on the other hand, if it is well done, the spring one is reduced to very little. Trees and shrubs intended to form palisades are prone to receding at the bottom, which is a major defect. To avoid this inconvenience, they must be sheared with great care during their early youth. Before they have reached their intended growth, they should be pruned back only a few inches in length, so as to produce strong branches on the sides, capable of filling out perfectly, and subsequently attracting a sufficient quantity of sap for their nourishment; once they have reached the desired height, they should always be pruned back on the same branch, to maintain them in the given proportions. This principle, applicable to all trees subject to shearing, needs to be explained in greater detail. When the scissors or the crescent always cut a branch in the same place, the result is an unpleasant snag, and nodules which soon blacken and become cankers after a few years; you are then obliged to cut back on a portion of the large branches to rejuvenate the tips. This is a serious operation, the results of which are often dangerous and always unpleasant, because trees treated in this way lack greenery until they have produced new buds and sub-buds, which does not usually happen in the first year, unless they are young and vigorous. The way to avoid this vice is to lengthen the pruning by two to six inches, more or less, depending on the size of the individuals, at each shearing and for three years. At the end of this period, the buds are renewed and the foliage is constantly thick and vigorous, while unpleasant snags are avoided. In any case, only those species whose nature makes them suitable for regular shaping and periodic shearing should ever be subjected to this procedure; such are, for example, hornbeams, privet trees, lime trees, yews, boxwoods, thorns, etc.; those with very long buds and few lateral sub-budding will always refuse to do so, and their mutilation will never be anything but unpleasant. However, with care and discernment, most trees can be subjected to palisade shearing. Pinching. Pinching a bud means cutting off its tip with the nails, or a sharp instrument, before its final development. This operation is sometimes necessary, in fruit trees, to stop the sap flowing too abundantly at the tip, and to make it produce lambourdes, twigs or fruit-bearing branches on the sides. Pinching can be done at any stage of vegetation, and will always be useful when you don't need to extend a branch. Pinching that is well done, and followed exactly during the course of the two sap flows, can save a great deal of work when pruning, and has the added advantage of reducing sap loss to almost nothing. However, it must be done with care, to avoid the development of under-buds. In many circumstances, this operation is particularly useful for greenhouse shrubs. Several species, among those never taken out of the greenhouse, tend to grow out of proportion on thin, slender stems, incapable of supporting the weight of their branches. This unnatural growth, most often the result of a lack of air and light, must be stopped, and there is no better or easier way of doing this than pinching. As soon as their young shoots rise, pinch them at the tip, causing buds to develop on the sides, and diverting the sap to the lower branches. This operation is especially necessary during a plant's youth, to give it an even, regular shape, and above all to establish in all its parts a balance of strength and vegetation that it will retain throughout its life, provided it is well cared for. The lateral buds themselves would be pinched if they had too pronounced a tendency to rise; they would increase in strength, and the plant would be better furnished with them, because of the sub-budding they would produce. The purpose of pinching is not only to improve the habit and shape of greenhouse shrubs, but also to make them produce their flowers earlier and more abundantly, and to ensure that the seeds ripen. It is also useful for plants grown in vases, because it helps to restrict their growth and keep it in balance with 11 their roots. It's easy to see that, if left to their own devices, their heads would take on a great deal of growth, out of proportion to that which the roots can acquire, resulting in an irregular and unhealthy shape, because these heads, no longer receiving sufficiently abundant nourishment from the stem, would lose weight, and the branches would recede near their base, leaving only some thin foliage at their tips. The damage would gradually increase, and the tree would soon die of exhaustion. The only way to bring it back to health would be to cut back all its branches to their trunks; and, trained by experience, the new head would be managed according to the principles we have just given, but its enjoyment would still be delayed by several years. o THIRD SECTION. TRAINING FRUIT TREES. We call forming a fruit tree, giving it a particular shape, more or less distant from the one it would take on its own if left to nature. These shapes vary according to the species of each one, and even sometimes according to the varieties within the same species; so they are only arbitrary up to a certain point. All the shapes applied to fruit trees up to now are limited to those which we will present in a table, in order to give a better understanding of their analogies. C 1 E V. 2 5 b2 6 e E 4 49 chadoi 42 obliq la Aont ier ran nop :ayouvaqyona DE LA TAILLE. o anoz quox,tou NNRAASSONSSSCSOSSES 1° .° ... ... atre two opposite frameworks. ne single mother branch inclined inclined mother ches eaq xn ieur mother branches 3. secondary straight secondary ches arched perpendicular face orizontal face 5 -- 10 12 the 22 3 ent au que 1 tteig 28 no quoV 4 * vault irland development indriq "vale elongated ddopoa; ITe no fac 12P superp i-sphere eur era a ige 2 ut 9. ne . 2 1 Ai 1u, IIILV oogle 1. The natural espalier is a tree leaning against a wall, but to which no attempt has been made to give it a shape other than that which it owes to its nature and to chance; this is almost always the effect of the inexperience of the person who trained it in its youth. Moreover, we sometimes find trees of such indomitable sap, that we are obliged to abandon them in terms of form, but never in terms of fruiting. 2. The frame espalier (pl. 10, fig. 2) is designed to make peach, apricot and other fruit trees produce fruit in cold climates, where they would not ripen without special precautions. It consists of a palmette, or French espalier, supported by a framework inclined in a proportion calculated according to the height of the sun in the climate. In bad weather, the tree and frame are covered by a glass frame. 3. The horizontal espalier (pl. 8, figs. 1 and 2) is perfectly suited to pear and apple trees on franc and doucin, whose overly vigorous buds would be carried away by the wood, to the detriment of the fruit, if they were not inclined horizontally. The branches are trellised on a flat-roofed framework, parallel to the surface of the ground it shades. If the espalier is raised on a parasol-shaped stem, it can be framed with hoops, supported by light stakes, until the tree is formed and can stand on its own. 4. The oblique espalier (pl. 9, fig. 7 ) is suitable for all trees that lend themselves to the ordinary espalier shape. It is used when you want to fill in a wall that has a good exposure and is not very high, quickly and without gaps. It consists of a sloping main branch, topped with secondary branches, especially below. These espaliers overlap one another so that, when they grow too much, one is removed between two, and the wall remains perfectly lined from the second year of planting until the trees are destroyed, an advantage not offered by other methods. 5. The French espalier (pl. 9, fig. 6) is a tree leaning against a wall, and raised on three or five main branches. It is ideal for pear, apple, plum, apricot, cherry and peach trees. 6. The Montreuil espalier (pl. 9, fig. 5) is best suited to peach trees. It consists of two main branches, open in a V shape, at about a 90-degree angle at their origin, and evenly topped and bottomed with secondary and fruiting branches. This is the tree's most popular shape. 7. The palmette with straight branches (pl. 7 fig. 4.) consists of a single straight branch, raised perpendicularly, and throwing from distance to distance, on its sides, secondary branches, horizontally trellised, themselves furnished with tertiary and fruiting branches. This form is suitable for peach trees, plum trees, some species of cherry trees and pear trees. 8. The Chinese palmette (pl. 10, sig. 1) differs from the previous one only in that its secondary branches, instead of being straight, are shaped like a very pronounced arch, or rather a half cradle. It can be applied to the same species as the previous one, but only to the most vigorous and fruit-bearing ones. 9. The counter-espalier is a true natural espalier, either French or Montreuil style, but not leaning against a wall, and planted against stiles. It is only suitable for trees with seeded fruit, unless sheltered from the north by straw mats. 7 10. The goblet (pl. 8, fig. 3) is a French-style counter-pallet, whose sides round out in a circle, and join together to give the tree the shape of a vase hollowed out inside. No longer in common use, it was usually used for fruit trees. It grows on three, four, five, six or seven main branches. 11. The vase (pl. 11, fig. 1) belongs only to apple trees grafted on paradise. It differs from the goblet by its infinitely smaller dimensions, and by its irregularity, as it is not possible to form it regularly on main branches, with symmetrical limbs. It's simply a bush hollowed out inside to facilitate air circulation, with the only difference that it can have a small stem. They are used in quincunxes in small gardens. 12. The trellis (pl. 9, fig. 8 ) is a vine applied against a wall, and trellised on two, four, six, or even eight main and opposite branches, stretched horizontally and parallel to each other when there are more than two. 13. The cradle (pl. 11, fig. 3 ) may consist, if made of vines, of a trellis forming, on a framework, a horizontal curtain, flat or arched; if it is formed by other fruit trees, it is a true horizontal espalier whose plane is more or less arched. 14. The cordon (pl. 11, fig. 4) consists of a branch extending as far as possible over the jamb of a light frame, and trimming it lengthwise with a garland of foliage, flowers and fruit. It's sometimes made from trees with seeded fruit, but more usually from vines. The latter is often garlanded around a pillar, column, etc. (see pl. 11, fig. 5) O6 15. The cattail (pl. 8, fig. 4), of German origin, consists of a straight, vertical stem with secondary and tertiary branches from the bottom to the top, rising along the stem and forming a more or less open angle with it, depending on the species or variety. Suitable for seed trees and some plum and cherry trees. The horizontal espalier, shown in the plate, is only suitable when the overly vigorous sap of a pear or apple tree grafted onto a franc tree needs to be tamed. 16. The prramid (pl. 8, sig. 5) differs from the cattail only in that its more inclined main branches, especially those at the bottom, provide the greater thickness of the tree very close to the ground. It is also suitable for the same species as the cattail. 17. The girandole (pl. 8, fig. 6) differs from the pyramid and the cattail in that its stem is furnished with secondary branches, and consequently foliage, only at regular, calculated distances, so as to form several heads emerging from one another. It is suitable for the same species as the two preceding ones, but is more particularly suited to varieties whose fruits need more air and light to ripen properly. 18. The ball is a special shape for orange trees, especially those grown in boxes for ornamental as well as productive purposes. It consists in making a tree take on the perfect shape of a globe of greenery. 19. The plein-vent is a tree whose head, growing freely, is carried on a trunk as high as the nature of the tree allows. As this is the shape that belongs to nature, it is also the one to which all trees of large species are most inclined. It is therefore suitable for all varieties grafted onto wild or free-run trees, as well as for those grafted from seeds or cores. 20. The half-wind differs from the preceding only in that its less vigorous trunk does not reach the height of the other, nor its head as considerable in breadth. This form is very well suited to trees grafted on stumps, on subjects with less sap than the preceding ones, or which have already undergone some alteration through cultivation. Because of the large space they occupy and the shade they cast far enough away, the plein-vent and mi-vent are only used in orchards or large gardens. 21. The bush (pl. 11, fig. 2) is a tree that art or nature has deprived of a stem. Its main branches, starting from a graft placed near the root collar, take the direction ordered by nature, and are not subject to any particular shape. They are pruned only to remove useless wood and to set fruit. Individuals grafted on paradise and other similar subjects, i.e. those that produce little sap, are formed into bushes. These trees take up very little space and produce very fine fruit, and have become very fashionable in recent years. They are planted in staggered rows and given the name Normandie. We have now enumerated and defined all the usual forms to which fruit trees in general have been subjected; it remains for us to indicate the means by which we most easily succeed in giving them the form we choose, and to guide the amateur in this choice. O08 FIRST DIVISION. TRAINING AND PRUNING STONE FRUIT TREES. The section on stone fruit trees comprises 1° peach trees, 2° apricot trees, 3° almond trees, 4° plum trees and 5° cherry trees. We will deal separately with the pruning of these five species, starting with the peach tree, because not only is it the most important for the beauty and goodness of its fruit, it is also the most delicate and the most difficult to manage properly. Training and pruning peach trees. This precious tree only succeeds perfectly in espalier, in the Montreuil or French style, and in full or half wind in favorable climates. We have vainly tried to make it take shapes other than these, and have never obtained anything but highly unsatisfactory half-success; but, in climates other than Paris, we are convinced that this is due more to the inexperience of those who lead them than to the nature of the tree. We have trained trees into cattails which would always have given us a lot of fruit, if frosts (which are little or nothing to fear below 44 degrees Celsius) had not very often destroyed them. As full success in the training of these delicate trees depends very much on the nature of the subjects on which they are grown, we will indicate the necessary conditions without which they should not be chosen, and those they must meet to merit preference. Peach trees can be grafted onto almond, plum and peach trees; some varieties reproduce free from 008 seed. The most beautiful trees are those grafted onto almond trees, especially hard-shelled and sweet almond trees, but they only do well in light, deep, warm and slightly dry soils; they are also much less prone to gum than the others. Next in line are those grafted on plum trees; they do better than the former in strong, slightly cold and damp soils, or in light soils that are not deep enough for almond trees. Finally, those grafted on peach trees are the least esteemed, because they are very prone to gum and never grow vigorously. Those grown from seed, and therefore free-standing, are the best for full- and half-wind conditions, but unfortunately only a few varieties are available in this way. Once you have decided on one of these three types of trees, based on the nature of the terrain and the shape to be given to the trees, you need to see whether the other conditions are met. To do this, the graft should be placed four to six inches above the root collar for espalier trees, and four to six feet high on the stem for mid- or full-wind trees. A single, straight stem should emerge from the graft, with large, well-formed, vigorous lower eyes. The graft should never be more than one year old, and care should be taken to ensure that it has not been what the Montreuil growers call rebooted, i.e. cut the first year above the first eye, to obtain a more vigorous stem the second year. These subjects, being attacked from the start in their organization, rarely produce good trees and must be rigorously rejected. Finally, the roots should be scrupulously inspected to make sure they have not been broken or crushed when the trees were moved. When the ground is perfectly prepared to receive the young students, we plant them five or six inches from the wall, and bury them so that the graft is two or three inches above the surface of the soil. The roots will remain intact, unless some are spoiled or crumpled, and only these will be cut off. As for the distance to be set between each foot, this should be calculated according to the type of plant chosen, the quality of the soil, and the type of espalier to be used. This, being one of the most essential things, requires further development. If only peach trees are planted against a wall, they should be placed eighteen to twenty feet apart if they are on plum trees, and twenty-five to thirty feet apart if they are on almond trees. These variations in distance depend on the quality of the soil. If the espalier is formed with peach trees and apricot trees in between, the trees grafted on plum will be spaced twenty to twenty-five feet apart, which implies a peach tree first, then an apricot tree ten or twelve feet further on; another peach tree at the same distance, then a second apricot tree, and so on to the end of the wall. The apricot trees should be on stems, at least six feet high, and pruned so that the left side of their fan covers the right side of the fan of one peach tree, while the right side covers the left side of another. This method assumes a wall fifteen or eighteen feet high. If the trees were grafted onto almond trees, the peach trees would be spaced thirty to thirty-five feet apart, which would place them fifteen to seventeen feet apart. In excellent soil, you can also plant a vine between each peach tree, spacing them further apart, and raising them one or two rows of cords above the espaliers, right up to the coping of the wall; this presupposes a wall twenty to twenty-five feet high. But this method 3 should only be used in small gardens where space is at a premium, because it has its drawbacks. As autumn is the most favourable season for planting peach trees, if planting were carried out at this time, care should be taken to cut the stem of the graft; this operation, by making the transplanted individual more tired, would make it more sensitive to the cold, and you would be in danger of seeing it perish in a hard frost. It should therefore only be brought closer together in spring, and this is the first pruning operation. From espalier to Montreuil. The first year. Since this is the year of planting, very little work is required. The stem is cut eight or ten inches above the graft (Pl. 9, fig. 1, a), with the wound facing the wall. Remove any buds that may have sprouted (b), taking care not to damage the bark of the stem, or the small bead at the insertion of these buds, as this is where the young branches most essential to the formation of the tree will emerge. As the buds develop, remove those growing in front of and behind the stem, and trellis those on the sides as they lengthen. In the first year, it may be a good idea to leave all the well-placed buds to grow, to give the plant time to recover fully. This would not delay fruiting. At this point, you should decide which buds are to form the two mother branches (pl. 9, fig. 2, a, a), and trellis them accordingly, i.e. at an angle of around ninety degrees. In this case, remove the others, or incline them further so that they don't attract too much sap, to the detriment of the others. If the graft has produced only one vigorous bud, it is trained vertically, and the following year, it is operated on as we have just said for the stem, of which it becomes an extension by straightening it accordingly. The two buds that are to provide the mother-branches would then come from it, delaying the formation of the tree by a year. During this first year, the gardener's attention must be focused on the balance of vegetation to be established between the two buds. If he notices that one has more strength and vigour than the other, he will hasten to incline it further and straighten the weaker one. The latter will soon grow stronger; and, when equality is perfectly established, he will trellis them to the same height. If this essential operation were neglected for the first two years, all the sap would be carried to one side, and it would be impossible, or at least very difficult, to re-establish equality thereafter. When inclining the two mother branches in a very open V-shape, care must be taken to bend or arch them in the slightest way: this must be strictly observed throughout a tree's life. If you do otherwise, the secondary branches (1) will carry away all the sap and ruin the mother branches, even supposing you manage to form them, which seems highly doubtful to us. 2° Year. If one of the buds trellised the previous year to form the mother branches had died, pen(1) The main branches are indifferently called mother branches or first members. The branches that grow on them are called secondary, tertiary, etc. (second limbs, third limbs, etc.), depending on whether their insertion is directly on a main branch, or whether it is more or less close to it. In the winter, we would straighten the other, and we could still obtain a beautiful tree by treating it as we said for the first year; but, if this accident happened later, the tree would be disfigured forever. This year, the only thing to look for is the lower secondary branch (a, a, pl. 9, fig. 3), and the extension, 6, b, of the main branches. We therefore prune these two main branches very close to the stem (pl. 9, fig. a, b, b,), on two eyes, one of which, below and closest to the stem, will provide the lower secondary branch; and the other, above and closest to the cut, will provide the extension of the mother branch. The end of the stem (pl. 9, fig. 2, c), which was left above the two main buds the previous year, is cut off, and the wound is perfectly joined to allow it to cover easily. As they grow, the two buds provided by each branch should be trellised; the lower one almost horizontally, but raised rather than inclined, and the upper one in the exact direction of the mother branch, of which it forms an extension. Care should be taken not to bend or arch either of them, unless you need to give one of them this disposition to stop over-vigorous vegetation, which would destroy the balance of the sap. When trellising, all buds placed in front or behind should be strictly removed, but some of the weaker ones may be left on the branches, to attract more sap and make them grow in size and length; nevertheless, care should be taken to pinch them at the end. This is especially necessary for weak, delicate trees. The two fruiting branches, c,c, of sig. 3 can be kept if the tree is very vigorous. 3rd year. Spring disbudding can already be done advantageously. The mother branch is pruned to create an upper secondary branch (pl. 9, fig. 4, a, a), and to extend the main branch b, b. To do this, two good upper eyes are brought together (pl. 9, fig. 3, d, d); and if there are not two in a row, the lower one, placed between the other two, is disbudded. The tip bud will provide the extension of the mother branch, and will be trellised in its direction; the other bud will be inclined and trellised to form the first upper secondary branch. The inclination of the upper and lower secondary branches is calculated according to the space that the fruiting branches (fig. 4, c, c, c, etc.) must occupy in order to be trained regularly without overlapping each other. As these are usually eighteen inches to two feet long, this is also the space that must be left between the secondary branches and the main ones. If the gap were any smaller, fruiting branches could not be placed there without being crumpled or cut too short; if it were any larger, there would be a gap that could not be filled, which would be a very serious defect. It's easy to see that, to space these secondary branches gracefully and evenly, they need to be placed at regular intervals on the main branches. This is the stumbling block many gardeners come up against, because not all trees lend themselves equally well to acquiring beautiful shapes. For this, they must grow vigorously enough to provide a long, strong terminal bud each year, from which the desired length can be obtained between the birth of each branch, a length which must never be less than eighteen inches, and which can sometimes exceed two feet. On weak trees, it would be better to wait a year than to form a secondary branch too close to another. It's true that this method would delay the full development of the fan for more or less time, but the tree would be better off, and would bear no less fruit while it was being raised. It would also increase its life span. The secondary branch, formed the previous year, will produce fruiting branches this year, as well as wood buds. To obtain its extension, it should be trained in the same way as the main branch, i.e. pruned to a good wood eye (fig. 4, d, d), and left at a length in harmony with the other branches. As a rule, it should never be as long as the main branch, so as to be more or less level with it on the line formed by the circumference of the fan. This year, disbudding requires a great deal of care, attention and intelligence. Usually in May, when the buds are between ten inches and a foot long, we remove all the useless buds, leaving only those which will provide the wood necessary for the formation of the tree; they will be much more vigorous and produce more fruit-bearing branches. The operation is carried out as described in the article on disbudding, page 154. At the end of June, and even better in July, trellising should be carried out with all possible care, and according to the principles described on page 156. The gardener may be persuaded that this operation is almost as necessary as pruning, and cannot be postponed under any circumstances. In addition to the reasons we have given for delaying trellising, Butret gives another, which seems all the more plausible to us, as it is quite close to the apparent course of nature. He says: "Trellising is usually postponed until July, so as not to do it before the fruit is assured, which can only be known after the solstice, because it is during this time that nature, which only works for the fertility of sprouts, is busy forming the fruit kernel: all those whose kernels are vitiated fall at this time; we see many on the ground, which ignorance of the laws of nature so inappropriately attributes to bad winds and bad weather, whereas this fall only comes from the vice of the reproductive germs; which means that all these infertile fruits, abandoned by nature, which does not want to work for infertility, die and fall. Open all these runts, and you will see the truth of these just observations." From which he concludes that trellising should be delayed until the August shoot, in order to be able to judge which fruits to keep; this could not be done before then, or we would run the risk of removing those that would have held, and leaving those that must fall. 4 Year. The tree is trained as in the previous year, and the extension of the mother branch and the second lower secondary branch are obtained. When a tree reaches this age, it sometimes begins to lose its ability to produce buds as strong and long as in previous years; at other times, on the contrary, it is more vigorous, producing shoots four, five or even six feet long. When the wood is of ordinary strength, it is pruned from the strongest to the weakest point, i.e. where the wood begins to diminish in size. To be sure of the precise point, the bud is bent into a semicircle, if desired, and cut where it forms an appreciable bend. In the second case, if the tree is sufficiently vigorous, this pruning would be much too short; so we lengthen it, but still retain the beautiful forms, and we can even leave the under-buds on top, which sometimes form fruiting branches well furnished with good eyes, and which can be pruned according to their strength. This principle should be applied at all times, from a tree's second year through to full formation. In top-quality soil, and in a well-directed espalier, it is not uncommon to see young trees grow so vigorously that their shoots reach six, seven and even eight feet in length. This is particularly true of species grown from seed. In these fortunate circumstances, pruning can be extended prodigiously, and the mother branches can be lengthened by three to four feet each year; but then two secondary branches must be obtained on each pruning, which is not always easy. If you succeed, after five or six years you can have fans thirty feet long and ten to twelve high. On a sturdy tree, care must be taken not to cut off the vigorous buds growing vertically on the upper secondary branches; this would be the way to abort the lower eyes. When pruning, simply incline them almost horizontally. Disbudding and pruning are carried out in the same way as the previous year, but with greater care as the operation becomes more complicated. This is also the fourth year in which replacement takes on great importance: we'll deal with it in detail at the end of this article. More attention than in previous years will be paid to the fruiting branches. In the fifteenth precept, on page 148, we said that, to be productive, the peach tree fruit bud must be accompanied by a wood eye: so, whether the flower bud is single or double, before pruning on it, make sure it is accompanied by a wood eye. No more than four fruit buds should ever be left on a fruit-bearing branch, whatever its strength, and only two on those of ordinary vigor. The principle is always to prune as short as possible, i.e. on the buds closest to the insertion of the branch: but it sometimes happens that they are not accompanied by wood eyes, and then you are forced to lengthen your pruning on others that are further away and have them. This inconvenience can be avoided when trellising, if care is taken to keep only wood that is well nourished and therefore fruitful. If a fruit-bearing branch has flower buds but no wood buds next to them, it should be cut off without hesitation; however, when its removal creates a gap, it should be cut back onto the wood bud closest to its insertion, in order to obtain a new fruit-bearing branch for the following year. These little bunches of flowers, two or three inches long, topped by a good woody eye, which we have compared to the lambourdes of fruit trees with pepins, must remain intact wherever they are placed, even on the front of the branches; their fruits, sufficiently fed by the terminal bud, rarely fail, and almost always become the most beautiful. Once they have reached maturity, we remove the small branch that bore them. Finally, when two fruit-bearing branches are left next to each other, as described in the article on replacement, they should never be pruned on both, so as not to form an unpleasant fork; instead, the lower one should be pruned back. 2. 13 1 5° Year. Same management and care as for previous years. Prune the mother branches so as to obtain their extension, and the second, upper, secondary branch, according to the same principles as for the first, i.e., on two upper eyes. At all prunings, but particularly when the tree has already reached a certain stage of development, before beginning to cut any buds, it should be stripped of all ties holding it to the wall, and scrupulously inspected in all its parts, to make sure it has no cankers or diseases that can be remedied either by amputating the infected part, or by other means. Once this has been done, the tree is cleared of all the garbage that may have accumulated in the armpits of its large branches; in particular, it is cleaned of the mosses and lichens that form, albeit quite rarely, on the peach tree, either at the base of the main branches or at the collar of the fan; after which it is pruned. When you've finished the work of the serpette, all that's left to do is to repalish it and tie it up with wicker, keeping the branches in their original position or, if necessary, giving them a more or less pronounced inclination, depending on the urgency. We have said that the two mother branches must always be dominant; and it is on this that the gardener must, at all times, focus his solicitude. If, by chance, a superior secondary branch suddenly seizes the sap and manages to equal the size of the main branch, it will soon outstrip it and, if care is not taken, exhaust it and the secondary branches below it. To halt its growth, it should be inclined, even below the horizontal line if necessary, and you shouldn't be afraid to cross it over another, because this position, unpleasant to the eye, should only last as long as it takes for the mother-branch to regain its superiority. In addition, when trellising, place the mother branch on the strongest bud that has grown at the tip, while placing the secondary branch on a weaker bud. If these precautions don't work, leave a few flowering branches at the end of the secondary branch to keep the sap flowing. These can be removed when the tree is trained, or left until the fruit ripens if they have knotted. Finally, in the event of all these means being insufficient, there would remain only the resource of inclining this secondary branch on the same line as the main branch, whose end would become the lower secondary branch; while the gourmand would replace it in this part, and form its extension. 6° Year (pl. 9, fig. 5). Pruning is carried out in exactly the same way as the previous year, with the same care. The third lower secondary branch is obtained, and the tree can now be considered to be formed. In subsequent years, the focus is less on gaining secondary branches than on maintaining existing ones. If you have always chosen buds of the same strength to form the corresponding limbs; if you have trained them all in the same way and pruned them to the same length; if you have pruned the wood buds long and the fruit buds short; finally, if you have not sacrificed elegance of form for premature fruiting, you will have a superb fan, filling eighteen to twenty-five feet of wall, without the slightest gap. Figure 5 shows a trained tree, with each year's pruning marked in a, a, etc.. The Montreuil espalier is not based on an arbitrary shape, dictated only by taste or, if you like, whim, and consecrated because experience has shown its happy results. It has been indicated by the very nature of the tree subjected to it. Look at a peach tree growing freely in the vineyards of the Maconnais, Lyonnais or the south of France: a vigorous, straight stem forms the mother branch, and weaker branches grow around it, rising obliquely to form the secondary branches, which produce abundant fruit-bearing branches. When M. Thouin said that, in a well-pruned tree, each main branch and its branches should have the same disposition as a whole tree, he was giving the best precept to follow for assigning a beautiful shape to an espalier; and in fact, when it is pruned à la Montreuil, it is nothing other than two trees inclined in opposite directions, and welded at their base, or rather emerging from the same collar. Replacement. This operation is specific to peach trees. It consists in cutting off the branches that have borne fruit, to replace them with new buds, and is carried out as soon as the fruit has been picked. This is the result of our twelfth and fifteenth precepts. Many gardeners, lacking knowledge of the true laws of vegetation, prune the fruit-bearing branches of the peach tree very lazily; in addition, the following year, they keep the weak bud that has grown at their tip, prune it long, and thus leave these branches to grow excessively long for several years, until they dry out and die, which happens in the fourth year at the latest. As the fruit of the peach tree comes only from the current year's wood, three quarters of the fruit-bearing branch remain bare, forming a very unpleasant void in the tree; only weak buds grow at the tip, incapable of nourishing the fruit; But the most serious disadvantage is that the peach tree grows back with difficulty on its old wood, and when the branch is dead, the void in the tree can never be repaired. So, whenever a fruit-bearing branch has yielded its harvest, it should be cut from the two or three best buds, chosen as close as possible to its insertion into the mother branch. These buds will already have been preserved for this purpose, when, during disbudding and trellising, the other buds on the fruit-bearing branches are cut off. If we recommend leaving two or three, it's not because they have to stay the following year; they can be cut off at pruning to keep only one: but, as frosts often attack them and destroy their woody eyes, it's wiser to have to choose from three, in spring, in order to triple the chances of success. The replacement operation, carried out first after the fruit has been picked, allows the buds that are to replace the fruiting branches to benefit from the sap, and also makes pruning much easier. From the French Espalier. 1"* Year. Once the subject has been chosen and planted as for the previous form, cut the graft ten or twelve inches above the collar, lower if you are sure of having seven or eight good wood eyes (Pl. 9, fig. 1, a). As the buds develop, remove those at the front and back, but scrupulously retain those at the sides. However, if the top bud should prove strong and vigorous, it can always be kept with some advantage, because, as it is to be trained in a vertical position, it will never have to be bent into a basket handle, either in front of the stem of which it is to become the extension, or behind it. These buds should be trellised with a very slight inclination, and only to establish a perfect balance of sap between them, but only three, four or five should be kept at the time of this operation, to provide the main branches, and the others should be removed. If these buds are very vigorous, they may sprout sub-buds on their lohgueur; these should then be pinched off as they appear, but only at two or three eyes, because they will give the branches body by stopping the sap there, and preventing it from carrying excessively to the extremities. In autumn, small snags resulting from pinching should be cut off very closely, taking extreme care not to attack the bark of the branches. When one of the branches has been broken off by an accident of any kind, it should be swallowed up immediately on the strongest bud below the wound, and trellised vertically to try to give it strength, and bring it back into balance with its corresponding branch, which should be inclined accordingly. If, despite these means, we can't manage to restore sufficient vigor, we'll remove the branch entirely. If the tree had only three branches, we'd give up on making a French espalier, and take it to Montreuil. If it had four or five, the accident would be very easy to repair, by bringing together the two branches between which the removed one would have been placed, had it not been missing. 6 OF FISHING. 199 2nd year. After disbudding before sap, prepare for pruning. To do this, detach the three, four or five main branches, and reposition them. Place them at equal distances from each other, raising the middle one vertically if there are an odd number of branches. In all cases, the two lower branches are trellised in a very open V-shape, forming an angle of just over ninety degrees; the two upper branches are also trellised in a V-shape, but much less open. The distance between the vertical branch and the first upper branch should be equal to that between the first and second branches. Finally, the tree should have the exact shape of a two-thirds developed fan. If the branches are of good quality, they can become much longer when pruned; but if they are weak or mediocre, they are cut from strong to weak, i.e., at the point where they begin to thin out appreciably. They can therefore be from two to four feet long, more or less. Sometimes, in a vigorous tree, they grow a few small fruiting branches, which must be entirely removed, because by diverting the sap they would harm the vegetation of the buds destined to form the second limbs. Disbudding must be carried out with the utmost care, especially in the second year, when the first buds of the secondary branches are selected. We remove all those growing in front and behind, and keep two of the most vigorous on the sides of each branch, if they are four feet long; and only one on each side, if they are only two feet long. In any case, the secondary branches should be spaced two feet apart, and in an alternating position. G00 That is to say, if the first secondary branch on the right side originates one foot from the graft, the first secondary on the left will be two feet from it, the second on the right three feet, the second on the left four feet, the third on the right five feet, and so on. If two secondary branches were opposite each other, they would attract too much sap, and the upper end of the main branch could suffer. So make sure that the buds of the secondary branches have this arrangement, or at least come as close to it as possible, and remove the others. Attention should also be paid to the bud which is to provide the extension of the main branch; it should be chosen from among the most vigorous, and, if necessary, it would be better to reduce the end of this branch to a very strong bud, than to leave a weak one at the end. It's easy to see that, since it has to be trained straight, its distance from the last secondary branch doesn't need to be determined; however, it shouldn't be close enough to fork it, so that they don't have to compete for each other's sap. It doesn't really matter whether it's in front, behind or to the side, but it would be better if it were in front. As all the buds gain strength, they should be tied in the position they should have, i.e. those of the secondary branches should be inclined, and those of the extension should be made to take a straight direction parallel to that of the main branch. When the branches are trellised, they should be detached from the wall, and given their final position by being trellised again; 906 201 to achieve this, the lower main branches should be inclined almost horizontally, but raised enough to form the shape of an extremely open V, and to ensure that the lower secondary branches are perfectly horizontal, and never more inclined. It's by giving them this direction in the second year that they gain enough strength not to be starved by those whose position is more or less vertical. Fruit-bearing branches that have grown during the first sap will be spared, but only in small numbers, so as not to exhaust the tree before it has formed. They should be trained in the same way as for the Montreuil espalier. 3rd year. We prune the mother branches so as to obtain their extension, and to gain second limbs or secondary branches. Follow the same principles as the previous year. As peaches should already be available, we pay particular attention to the fruiting branches, and treat them in autumn by the replacement method. If there is a vertical mother branch, care should be taken to prevent it from taking over all the sap, favoured by its position. To avoid this serious inconvenience, prune on a weak bud, provided it is healthy, and choose the lowest one possible; keep more fruit-bearing branches than on the others, and lengthen their pruning more; when disbudding, leave a few under-buds on the sides, to amuse the sap and prevent it from reaching the tip. If all these precautions are not sufficient, arch the upper bud and straighten the second limbs. Finally, this main branch should be arranged in the same way as a palmette. Coo The arrangement of the secondary branches, and the place they should occupy so as not to harm or intertwine with each other, should be planned in advance. When distributing them between the two lines formed by the first limbs, great care must be taken to ensure that they do not meet; and, to achieve this, their insertions on the main branches must never be perpendicular to one another, but rather alternate. Suppose the second lower limbs of the second main branch are placed as follows: the first at one foot, the second at three, the third at five, etc.; the second upper limbs of the first main branch must be placed as follows: the first at two feet, the second at four, the third at six, and so on. Needless to say, the second lower limbs of the upper branch will be inclined a little below the horizontal line, and the second upper limbs of the lower branch inclined a little above. There must be enough space between them to trellis the fruiting branches in the same way, and above all without confusion. 4° Year. Pruning is carried out as in the previous year, but care must also be taken to extend the secondary branches, so as not to allow them to be stripped of fruit-bearing branches. If any are missing, the peach tree will no longer produce gems on the old wood, and the loss will be irreparable. The tree is then disbudded and pruned; finally, it is managed in the same way as it should be for the rest of its life. If it is not fully formed, it only lacks second limbs, which are obtained each year. However, as it ages and loses vigor, pruning will be shortened, and it will bear less fruit. The most important thing is to maintain as regular a shape as possible, because this is the key to perfect sap balance. We have shown (pl. 9, fig. 6) a French espalier, on which we indicate in a, a, a, etc., the annual pruning of the main branches B, B, etc., and in d, d, d, etc., the pruning of the secondary branches E, E, etc. From Espalier to chd̂ssis. The peach tree, being very sensitive to frost, reuses badly in the north of France, and can hardly be grown in the open ground at colder temperatures, unless special precautions are taken to ensure its preservation. It is also very sensitive to excessive heat, so that it does not do better in the south, in Italy for example, than in the north. In this ancient homeland of the masters of the earth, it produces only small, tasteless fruit, because it can only be grown in the open air; espaliered against a wall, it dries out and dies; or, if placed in a cool spot, the little fruit it yields is of less than mediocre quality. The apricot tree is in much the same situation. We have sought, for these two precious trees, the means to repair, by dint of art, the rigors with which nature has armed itself in certain climates; and we have the experience that, when we want to obtain good quality fruit wherever the temperature (too hot or too cold) seems to oppose it, we will succeed in overcoming its pernicious influences, by means of the espalier with frame, shown (pl. 10, fig. 2). In too hot a country, you need to be able to place. In too hot a country, the aim is to be able to place and manage an espaliered peach tree, without leaning it against a wall, and in such a way as to allow it to enjoy all atmospheric influences, principally air and light, without allowing the sun's rays to burn its blossom or dry out its fruit. To achieve this aim, a framework is established, more or less ineligible, formed by two planes, a, a, a, a, and b, 6, b, b, supported one on the other. These planes will offer their surface, one to the east, the other to the west; and their inclination will form, with the surface of the ground, an angle of 45 degrees, more or less. They will be ten to twelve feet long, from the ground to the top, and their width will be calculated according to the number of trees to be grown. These light frames will be made of planks no more than two or three inches wide, leaving at least the same width of empty space between them. Once these frames are solidly in place, we plant on each side, two or three inches from their base, the plants we have chosen accordingly; and this choice is not as arbitrary as you might think. In warm climates, preference should be given to trees grafted on to almond trees, because they are less afraid of drought, and their development, more considerable than that of plum trees, will be less embarrassing; the reason for this is that the framework, never having to be covered with glass frames, can be established in large proportions without many inconveniences. The tree can be given the form of a French espalier, but the palmette shape is more suitable, as it is more likely to be maintained in a space of limited size. In both cases, the tree should be trained according to the principles we've outlined, with the difference that it should be kept as tightly as possible, and the leaves should be kept in the shade when trellised, so that the wood and especially the fruit are shaded, at least until ripening begins. 4 To maintain freshness, water often, especially under the trellis, because humidity is more easily retained there, and evaporation between the gaps in the trellis and through the foliage brings an invigorating freshness maintained by the air, This air will not only circulate freely on both surfaces of the tree, but will also form currents through the interstices of the boards, balancing out the drying influences of the heat, and maintaining life and health in all parts of the tree. If, in the middle of the day and during the summer, the sun's rays, although striking obliquely on the foliage of the espalier, were to become sufficiently intense to be harmful to it, a cloth should be spread out every day, and at least for a few hours, to shade the tree. But it should be at least a few feet away from the tree, so as not to impede the free circulation of air, the primary agent of vegetation. If you want to grow peach trees in cold climates, you need to base your operations on much more meticulous considerations. The frame will be built in the same way, but without gaps between the boards, and in smaller dimensions, because it will have to be covered with glazed frames for part of the year, and as these frames have to offer a much larger surface than his own, it would be very-difficult to assemble them solidly if they were too long. When it comes to planting the tree, the choice becomes much more important than before. Whether it has been taken from a country not far away, or has been on the road for several days, its roots must be examined with the utmost care, in order to remove any fractured or dried-out parts. If there was the slightest doubt about its health, it would be planted in a basket, which would be immersed in a warm layer until it recovered perfectly. During this time, the season would advance, and we would soon have a degree of warmth in the open air sufficient to place it in its basket. It would be buried at the same distance from the framework as the previous one, but in such a way that the graft was only an inch or two above the surface of the ground; for, in this kind of espalier, you have to be stingy with space.
