Whether the tree is to be taken up in a basket, or placed immediately in the ground, the bud of the graft is cut, at the time of planting, over two, three or four good eyes at most. During its growth, it is trained and cared for in exactly the same way as other espaliers, i.e., by pinching off badly placed buds, and trellising the others according to the positions required by the shape adopted. During the summer months, this is all you need to do, but as soon as the nights start to get colder, you'll need to start putting up the glass frames. In excessively cold countries, where peach trees cannot withstand the winter, we'll build a kind of enclosure in planks two or three inches thick, and eighteen to twenty inches high. This casing will extend in front of and behind the espalier along its entire length, and will be far enough from the trees so that there will be a distance of at least one foot between the glasses and the peach trees, when the frames are placed on the casing, and supported one on top of the other according to the same principles as the framework. The difference, however, will be that they will be supported at the top on brackets attached to the frame, so that one can be opened as required without having to move the other on which it is supported. The sides of this kind of tarpaulin can be closed with boards or glazed frames. It's essential that the whole thing closes hermetically, so that air and consequently the cold can't get in. 1I Underneath the framework, and between the two rows of trees, we'll establish the passage of a furnace or stove pipe. When the cold becomes intense, we'll throw enough manure against the casing and outside to form a bed two feet thick and two feet wide all along the tarpaulin. Heat the stoves to keep the temperature at two or three degrees below zero at all times, so as not to provoke vegetation, and cover the frames with good doormats. During the fine days of winter, when it's not freezing and the sun's rays are showing, we'll provide air by opening the frames from above, as we see fit. Before closing, carefully remove any drops of water that may have adhered to the glass due to humidity. Bear in mind that frames and furnaces are not designed to keep out the cold, but only to ensure that it doesn't get any colder than ten or twelve degrees. So the difficulty lies in preventing the thermometer from falling any lower, without ever rising above freezing; for the heat would do more harm than two or three degrees more cold, unless we wanted to force the trees to bear fruit in a premature season; but then they would require special care that we need not concern ourselves with here. From the first days of spring, if the season is not too rigorous, it will be necessary to give light often by removing the straw mats, and air whenever this can be done without danger. The heat should then be turned up a little, to prepare the trees for vegetation. Once the first sap movement has been noticed, the peach tree should be inspected in all its parts, and any mildewed or even D6-suffering twigs should be removed without hesitation. This is the time to do the first disbudding to prepare the tree for pruning. Pruning is carried out a few days later, following the same principles as for espaliers. However, there is a difference in that, as the tree will only occupy a very limited space, far from trying to extend the branches, we'll try, on the contrary, to keep them in their smallest proportions, and we'll increase the number of fruit-bearing branches. This is not to say that you should leave all the fruiting branches, as the tree would be exhausted even more easily than one growing without care in a favourable climate; but, as there are many more chances for the ovaries to abort at the moment of fertilization, you should wait to cut off the overabundant fruiting branches until the peaches are perfectly knotted and secure. As the season warms up, reduce artificial heat, pinch off useless buds, and give air more often. Finally, when the cold and the slightest chill are no longer to be feared during the nights, and the thermometer remains constantly at six to ten degrees and above during the day, we remove the frames and replace them the following autumn. It's then that the trellising is done, and with all the more care as this is the best time to heal the wounds. For this reason, never leave a snag, and always cut back the bark. The replacement operation should only be carried out in spring, to prevent the wounds from gumming up, which would necessarily happen if the humidity of the frame remained on them over winter. For both apricot and peach trees, pruning should be combined in such a way as to avoid having to cut any branches or buds at any time other than when the trees are often enjoying the benefits of the open air. Finally, general cultivation should be combined according to needs and circumstances. In a region not cold enough to kill a peach tree in the ground, but where spring frosts would destroy its blossoms, we would use glass frames in the same way; but we would replace the heat pipes with a layer of warm manure, two or three feet wide, eighteen inches thick, and placed on the ground between the two planes of the espalier. During the winter, this layer is simply warmed up two or three times with new manure; but as soon as vegetation begins, it is renewed and completely redone every fortnight, and even more often if deemed appropriate. We then take advantage of every moment when the sun comes out to let in some air, and we take great care to wipe the moist steam provided by the manure from the glasses, as well as to remove, as soon as it appears, any mould that might have a tendency to take hold of a few branches. As soon as vegetation begins to appear, all diseased or just suffering branches are scrupulously pruned and removed, and the tree is cleaned of garbage and other foreign matter that may have collected at the branches' bifurcations. In all circumstances, we treat espalier trees with frames, according to the principles we have taught for Montreuil, French or palmette shapes. Palmette with straight branches. Against a very high wall and not very long, this shape is one of the most advantageous for peach trees. We have illustrated it, pl. 7, fig. 4. Year. The tree is selected and planted in the same way as for the Montreuil espalier; but if you wish, you can leave only four or five 2. 14 eyes by turning back the bud of the graft, so as to have two very vigorous lateral shoots to form the first two lower limbs. However, before cutting, make sure that the eyes are healthy and well placed. When they have developed, select the three strongest buds, one on the right, one on the left and one above, to form the first two lateral branches, and the vertical stem that will supply them all. The latter is planted as straight and perpendicular as possible; the other two are attached in a horizontal position, but only when they have acquired enough strength to compete with the first for sap, which sometimes happens only the following spring. All unnecessary buds should be removed. When establishing these two main branches, care should be taken to ensure that they are not placed opposite each other, but a little below each other, even if only a few inches apart. Without this precaution, they would compete for sap and harm each other. You'll also need to equalize their vegetation, by straightening the less vigorous one a little and tilting the other more. 2nd year. If the tree has grown vigorously, we'll take care of the second two main branches, and the secondary branches of the first two. To do this, prune the stem on three strong buds, one for its extension, the other two for branches. If the lower main branches have grown well-placed buds to provide second limbs, these buds should be kept and trellised accordingly, and the branches shortened to two well-placed eyes, to provide their extension and a second lower or upper limb, as required. 908 Keep a few fruiting branches, if you like, or at least bring them closer together over two lower wooded eyes, so as to have good ones the following year. The main and secondary branches should be trained in the same way as for an espalier à la Montreuil, with the difference that the first branches should always be trained horizontally, and the others should be trained a little above the parallel line of the first limbs when above, and a little below when below. 3rd year. New secondary branches are obtained, the extension of the main branches, and on the stem, the two third main branches, as well as the extension of the stem. It is easy to see that this vertical branch or stem, having much more sap than the others, should not be used to obtain limbs in the same way as secondary branches are obtained from the main branches. On the latter, only one is obtained every year, and on the other, two must be formed. However, as this requires the terminal shoot to reach at least three feet, so that the limbs are spaced two feet apart to provide space between them for the secondary and fruiting branches, it sometimes happens that, not reaching this length, we are forced to bring it much closer together and below the desired distance. In this case, we don't require any main branches, and are content to extend it, so as to have two good lateral buds the following year. If chance had provided sufficient stem length to space out and form a single main branch, we would take advantage of this, and prune for its extension and to obtain this branch, just as we prune the other branches to form their extension and a secondary branch... As for the rest of the operations, they are carried out in exactly the same way as for other espaliers. 4° Year. Disbudding is carried out very carefully in spring. Prune in the same way as the previous year, to obtain one or two main branches, and secondary branches. This year, attention should be focused on the fruiting branches, because if the tree has not been delayed by some particular cause, it should already be fruitful. Once the tree has acquired its shape, all that remains to be done in other years is to encourage its regular development, always following the same principles. Cattail. It is generally believed that peach trees do not lend themselves to this form, and this is wrong. Those who have fully understood our pruning principles will easily guess where this error comes from. As we have said, the peach tree only very rarely produces wood eyes on old wood, and never fruit buds; hence, if branches that are to be shortened are allowed to develop in length, they recede at the base, forming an unpleasant gap that is suitable for an espalier, and destructive of the fruit in a cattail. As this form of tree is, in all its beauty, only five to six feet in diameter at its thickest, it follows that the main branches, at their greatest growth, can only lengthen from three to three and a half feet, thanks to their obliquity; for, if they were to move horizontally away from the stem, they could only grow from two and a half to three feet at most. So far, gardeners with little training are not at fault; and, by dint of pruning the limbs, they maintain908 them easily enough in these proportions. Not knowing how to distinguish between the different kinds of branches, they preserve useless wood by damaging the good; and this is still only half a bad thing, because it can be repaired. But here's the reason why the cattail peach tree can't bear fruit in their hands: fruit-bearing branches in all trees, and particularly in the peach tree, rarely tend to grow vertically; more often they assume a horizontal position. The first year, they will grow eighteen inches, and will bear fruit along their entire length, or only at the tip; the ignorant gardener will be careful not to shorten them by the replacement method; on the contrary, he will let them lengthen another foot the following year: then they will be stripped of wood in three-quarters of their length; and we know that no means will be able to grow any more. What happens? They've reached the outer surface of the cattail, which they can't be allowed to extend beyond, and we're forced to cut them down. No new ones form, since the old wood doesn't produce any; and since we can't keep those that might grow at the end of the main branches, because they would exceed the shape of the tree, the result is that the peach tree remains sterile for its entire life. No matter how skilful a grower might be at this moment, he would have few means left to bring it back; he would try to graft it in escutcheon or in approach on all the places where there should be good fruit-bearing branches, if the tree had been formed with discernment; or, by bringing it close to the stem, he might be able to obtain new buds if the bark was not too old. But the tree would be no less spoiled, and it would take as long to restore it as it would to train a young tree. Now that we've explained the simple causes of a prejudice against the cattail peach tree, we'll show you how to bend it into this form, for which nature has prepared it as well, and perhaps better, than any other species of fruit tree, but which is perfectly suited only in countries where it doesn't have to fear the spring frosts which, in Paris, often destroy its flowers. 1 "st year. We plant a subject that has grown a straight, robust vertical shoot. This shoot is cut one foot from the graft, or even higher if it is vigorous. As the buds develop, the best placed and strongest are chosen to form the limbs, and the others are cut off. For these limbs to be flattened, the line that passes over their insertions must turn regularly in a spiral around the stem, starting at the insertion of the first limb, then passing over that of the second, then the third, and so on to the top of the tree. The branches must also be spaced so that the first is perfectly covered by another a foot away, the second by another at the same distance, and so on. If, as they grow, the buds show a tendency to rise vertically and get too close to the stem, they are kept apart by tying them to stilts; if, on the contrary, they move too far away, they are brought closer by the same means. Each must form an angle of about forty-eight degrees with the stem. The terminal bud should rise straight up, and should be maintained in this direction by means of a stake, if necessary. 2° Year. Spring disbudding can be carried out if you wish, and then all the eyes above and below the limbs should be removed, leaving only those on the sides, i.e. only the espaliers should be disbudded in the opposite direction. Here's the reason: all parts of the tree, and especially those closest to the stem, since these are the ones that will bear the fruit, as we'll say in a moment, must enjoy the influences of air and light, if we don't want to see them languish first, and then recede. The only way to let them enjoy this is to give each branch the flattened shape of a horizontal palm, so that the sun's rays and the air can easily penetrate between them. If this were not done, the tree would be nothing more than a thick mass of greenery; the shaded fruit would take on neither color nor fragrance, and the humidity, remaining on the bark of the stem, would soon encourage the growth of mosses, lichens and other parasitic plants. When pruning, if the lower branches are vigorous, leave the terminal bud of a certain length to obtain several limbs, in addition to its extension. If, on the other hand, they are weak, the upper bud of the stem should be cut back over two or three eyes at most, so that they can benefit from the portion of sap it would otherwise have taken. In all cases, the limbs should be pruned to a greater or lesser length, but always in such a way as to force the lower eyes to develop into fruit-bearing branches. We watch them for the first few days, to see whether they are growing or dormant; if they don't start, we force them to do so by pinching the upper buds, or even by lowering the whole branch if necessary. The fertility of the cattail, and therefore the success of pruning, depends on the development of these eyes into fruit-bearing branches. Disbudding is carried out at the same time as trellising of espaliers, and for this year, all care is limited to this. 3rd year. Disbudding is carried out as in the previous year, but without touching the fruit-bearing branches placed laterally on the limbs. Pruning is carried out to obtain an extension of the limbs and stem, as well as a few more branches at the top. All woody branches are cut back, because this type of shape doesn't want secondary limbs. Attention should be paid to fruiting branches in particular, as the future fertility of the tree will depend entirely on this pruning. The branches closest to the stem should be carefully preserved and, regardless of the quantity of fruit, pruned as short as possible, so as to determine the vegetation of their first woody eye, i.e. the one closest to their base. Let's explain: if the four lower flower buds were good, with their wood eye, we'd prune on them; if they were worthless, we wouldn't try to lengthen the branch any further, and we'd cut on the two or three wood eyes at the base, depending on whether they were more or less reliable. It's true, but it's much better for it not to bear fruit for one year, and then to bear fruit for twenty years, than to bear a few today, only to bear none, because it can't be replaced. When trellising, any limbs that deviate from their proper position should be re-attached to stiles, and disbudded as for espaliers. Once the fruit has been harvested, replacement will be carried out as described in the article on page 196, and with as much rigor as for the Montreuil espalier. 4° Year. Same conduct as previous year. 20e If a lot of woody branches have developed along the length of these limbs, those closest to the stem should always be given preference, so that they can be kept for a long time without reaching the outer surface of the tree. The same care as in other years will continue. Later, when the limbs have reached a sufficient length to form a cattail five or six feet in diameter, it will be necessary to maintain them in that proportion which this form of tree hardly allows to be exceeded, unless the subject has reached a considerable height. To achieve this, prune very short every year, removing the previous year's bud from a single eye. If, in spite of this care, the tree becomes thicker, the limbs are cut back on the first fruiting branch, and the latter is pruned on its lower wood eye. This gem, suddenly receiving a great abundance of sap, would develop entirely into a woody branch, and provide a new end to the limb. Conclusion: to force a peach cattail to bear fruit in abundance, all you have to do is keep the fruiting branches as close as possible to the tree trunk, so as to preserve them throughout the plant's life.

or keep them sterile, or cut them down, and there would be no way of obtaining new ones. Peach trees can only be grown as cattails in countries where they thrive in the open air, i.e. below forty-four degrees Celsius. Counterpallet. As the peach tree needs a lot of heat to ripen its fruit, it is never placed in a counter-espalier, 1 unless it is sheltered on the north side, by leaning against permanent straw mats. In this way, it's a real espalier set against a trellis or stiles, instead of against a wall. In Austria, and particularly around Vienna, peach trees are no longer grown against walls, because the sun's rays damage the fruit in summer. Instead, two solid, perpendicular frameworks are set up in the open air, forming two trellises leaning against each other, but separated by a gap of eight or ten inches. One side faces south, and the other north, which is rather awkward, as the espaliered trees would be equally well placed if one faced east and the other west. But even more clumsy is the fact that the trees are planted directly opposite each other, with the result that their roots, touching each other, are forced to compete for ground, which they exhaust in a very short time. It would be much better to alternate them to avoid this serious inconvenience. Despite this, they produce superb peaches. In all cases, sheltered or not, the counter-espalier can be used in the same way as the espalier à la Montreuil or à la française, depending on the taste of the grower. Full wind and half wind. It is particularly important to choose a robust tree, grafted on the subject most capable of providing abundant sap, when raising a high-stemmed or half-stemmed plein-vent tree. If the tree has been grafted on the spot, which is always the best method, the first year we take care of extending the bud of the graft, by disbudding as we shall say; but when it has been transplanted, we remove the graft on a good eye placed more or less high, depending on whether the roots of the tree have suffered during the transplanting and transportation. When the buds have reached part of their growth, choose the strongest one to continue the stem; trellis it as straight as possible, and pinch the others at six inches, to prevent them from seizing the sap. At this length, they will retain enough vegetation to force the nourishing fluids to stop in sufficient quantity in the stem, and encourage its development in size. This operation is called hook pruning. We also pinch off all buds growing on the hooks, and those growing on the extension bud. In autumn or the following spring, all the hooks are brought down to the stem, and if the latter is not yet the desired height, it is continued in the second year as it was the year before. If an accident should break the stem, or weaken its tip through disease, it should be lowered onto an eye or a vigorous bud placed below the ailment. If it bifurcated, we'd choose the strongest shoot, straighten it and keep it upright by means of a stake; we'd pinch the other, even tilt it if necessary, and remove it at the next pruning. As soon as the stem has acquired the desired length, we pinch off its terminal bud, if this is during vegetation; otherwise, we cut off its tip at pruning time. It's essential to keep a close eye on the buds that will develop at the tip to form the main branches, so as to select four or five of the most vigorous, and remove the others. As far as possible, keep those whose position is closest to the whorl, or, if you like, which move away from the axis of the stem in regularly spaced rays. From this moment on, you can leave the management of 208 the tree to nature; however, if you were to sacrifice a few more moments during the following two or three years, it could only gain a lot. In this case, we would treat each branch as we said in the cattail article, but with the difference that we would provide secondary branches, alternating with each other as for a French espalier. For the duration of the tree's life, which can extend to a century or more, depending on the species and the subject on which it is grafted, all that remains to be done is to remove the dead branches, bringing them down to the bare wood so that the wounds can heal, and to cut off those which, if badly placed or too vigorous, could draw all the sap to themselves, to the detriment of the others, which would destroy the balance of vegetation, deform the tree and hasten its old age. As for the branches known as "gourmands", if we manage to stop their vegetation by the means, already mentioned, of arching, etc., we will keep them preciously, because they will not take long to produce a lot of fruit; in the event that they find themselves untamable or ill-disposed to replace weaker ones, we will remove them entirely. Training at La Sieulle. We won't end the history of peach tree pruning without mentioning the training method invented by M. Sieulle, published by him in a small brochure entitled Avis aux Jardiniers, and approved by three reports from the Paris Agricultural Society, one in 1811, the second in 1813, and the third in 1820. M. Sieulle manages an espalier of peach trees at the Duc de Praslin's home in Vaux-Praslin, which we have already mentioned in one of our works as one of the finest in Europe. In twenty-five years, the trees cultivated by this skilful gardener have grown from sixty-five to seventy feet, which proves, whatever the quality of the soil, that his method is good and deserves to be known and used in favorable circumstances. Here's one of the passages from the last report made by the committee of the agricultural society: "The sight" of the espaliers at Vaux-Praslin made an impression on us, and "this impression was all the more striking, as all" these trees, without exception, presented us with "the most beautiful and richest vegetation. Beautiful " limbs, smooth bark, with no appearance " of snags, scars or gum, beautiful shoots, " broad dark-green leaves; such were " the peach trees of Mr. Sieulle. In general, all of them "seemed to us to cover equally the wall against which they are set". We did not notice, as "we had reason to fear from what we had been told", that their lower part was balding "or tending to baldness, etc.". This quotation is undoubtedly worth more than any reasoning in favor of his method. If you want to grow a peach tree à la Sieulle, the first and sometimes the second year, you direct it in the same way as the espalier à la Montreuil, from which it should more or less acquire the same shapes. The two mother branches are obtained by the same procedures (sig. 2, b, b, pl. 9). As soon as these first two limbs have been gained, we select, in spring before vegetation, two well-placed wood eyes to develop the first two lower and upper secondary branches: we leave them intact, and remove all the others with a sharp instrument, even cutting down to the small bead they have at their base and on which others could form. The bud's terminal eye is left to provide the branch's extension, and the operation is limited to this for the first year. As the eyes left grow and extend, they are bent into a V slightly more open than 45 degrees, because, as the tree will grow to very large dimensions, if the V were not open enough, the top of the branches would overtake the wall in very few years. If, by chance, we forgot to disbud a few eyes, we would pop them out as they appeared, because we should never be obliged to disbud either at the time of trellising, or at any other time; and it is this particularity, so to speak, which distinguishes M. Sieulle's method from all others; or at least the one which would seem to make it preferable, because there is no loss of sap and no useless vegetation. The following year, the two secondary branches are pruned to half-length, but the terminal bud of the main branch is never touched, because it is always through its last eye that it should be extended. Cut off all the eyes as in the previous year, leaving two more on the extension of the mother branch to provide two new second limbs. Secondary branches are trimmed in the same way, leaving two eyes for tertiary branches. Here we must point out that the two eyes left on each branch, which are renewed every year, are always accompanied by fruit buds, and are the only ones that should provide peaches, since all the others are removed, no distinction is made between woody and fruit-bearing branches, and consequently no replacement operation is carried out. However, when short branches loaded with flowers are encountered, they are left in their entirety, which increases the tree's yield a little. 08 Every year, the operation is carried out in the same way: 1° that the mother branches, never being pruned, extend rapidly and fill a large space in a short time; 2° that, never having any scars, because the wound made by the removal of the buds heals quickly and leaves no trace, the trees are not subject to gum or canker; 3° that, as no considerable amputations are made, there is almost no loss of organic substance, which maintains the tree's vigor and youthfulness; 4° that, as the subject has a much greater number of branches forming third, fourth, fifth limbs, etc.., the wall is much better furnished; 5° but also that, as there are never more than two flowers on each new branch, the peaches, although a little larger than on individuals otherwise trained, are in smaller quantity compared to the development of the espalier. We have said that secondary branches are cut back to half their original length, but the same cannot be said of tertiary branches and the others they supply. By cutting them back, for example, on the first two eyes with flower buds, we could keep them short for a very long time. We don't even see any problem in treating them by the replacement method, without removing the bud closest to the insertion of these branches. During trellising, we frequently find under-budding that has developed at the end of shoots that have grown since spring; we can leave them if we wish, because they will be removed at pruning time; but, if they deprive the fruit of air and light, they must be removed. In short, Sieulle training differs essentially from Montreuil training in only two respects: the mother branches are never shortened, and useless wood is prevented from growing rather than removed once it has formed. Apricot trees, and even trees with seeded fruit, can be treated in the same way. Apricot training and pruning. This species, being earlier than the peach, should also be pruned, disbudded and trellised first. The only forms to which it has so far been subjected are espalier and plein-vent. Espalier. The apricot differs from the peach in that it grows back easily on old wood, and it is this faculty alone that brings about some difference in its size; moreover, it behaves in the same way, whether trained in espalier à la Montreuil, à la française, en palmette, etc. We will therefore make only one comment on this subject. We will therefore only cover all these pruning methods in a single article, referring the reader to each particular form of peach tree. It would be futile to expect apricot trees to have the regularity and beautiful shapes so easy to give to peach trees; their wood, never growing very long, and rarely straight, naturally opposes them. So, we'll content ourselves with getting as close as possible to this, by turning our main attention to fruiting. The second essential aim of the gardener is to leave no gaps, without causing confusion. This will be easy to achieve, since there's nothing to stop him lengthening or shortening the pruning at will, since he's sure to get wood wherever it's needed. To achieve this, all he needs to do is prune back the old branches, and he'll soon see a host of buds emerge. 900 As the flower buds are grouped by five, six or even fourteen or fifteen together on small fruit-bearing branches, it will be necessary to prune these short, and sometimes even to remove part of the apricots when trellising. This tree is covered with small lambourdes, an inch or two long, which produce a quantity of good fruit; they should be left intact until they have fruited, after which any misplaced ones that might cause confusion should be removed. If an apricot tree has produced a lot of fruit one year, it is obvious that it should be pruned very short the following year, to prevent it from wearing out and becoming gummed, to which it is very prone. Its fruits, to acquire all their qualities, must receive the direct influences of the sun, like those of the sinner; but they are very delicate and, if they were suddenly uncovered, they would be burnt and fall. It is therefore only with great care that we thin out the leaves; and, as far as possible, we choose a rainy day, or at least a day when the sky is covered with clouds. First, lightly uncover, then a little more, two or three days later, and so on for several days, until they are fully exposed to the sun's rays. This is particularly important for espaliers facing south. Apricot trees and other stone fruit trees are not subject, like peach, to the replacement operation. Full wind. It can be raised like the peach tree, but if you want to keep it for a long time, it must be pruned every year according to the same principles as the espalier tree, otherwise it will rapidly lose its foliage in the lower parts, its branches will grow too long and the tips will be only slightly vegetated, and after a few years, you will be obliged to bring them up to the trunk, an operation which will inevitably cause considerable harm to a tree, by altering its health for ever. During the first few years, the pruning of a plein-vent tree can be lengthened a little more than that of an espalier. When the tree is in danger of thinning out, prune shorter, or even cut back to force the sap to flow back into the lower parts of the branches, and take care to avoid confusion so that air can circulate easily and freely inside the tree. Trees for espaliers can be grafted onto plum and almond trees, but the best for full-wind conditions are those grown from seeded pits. Training and pruning the almond tree. Only in very cold countries is this species subjected to espalier training; in this case, if the soil is damp, we choose trees grafted on plum. However, if the soil is dry and sandy, preference is given to those grown from seed, or grafted onto their own species. Espaliered almond trees are grown in the same way as peach trees. If the climate is unfavorable, it can be grown in the open air like an apricot tree. If the climate is unfavorable, it can be grown in the open air, after raising the stem as we have said for other open-air trees, and then left to nature. It grows back on old wood, which modifies its size accordingly. Plum tree training and pruning. Since plum trees only come into leaf after apricot and peach trees, they must also be pruned after them. So far, this tree has only been trained in espalier, D4 counter-espalier, cattail and full-wind. As its pruning differs only slightly from that of the two species we have just mentioned, we will limit ourselves to indicating the slight differences it requires. The choice of subject is rigorous. Grafted from seedlings are preferred for full-wind planting, and should be used for espaliers or counter-espaliers capable of filling a very large space. Those grafted on scions will be more suitable for forming cattails and espaliers in lesser proportions. The gardener's first task is to cut off the buds and shoots that rarely fail to grow, right down to the plum tree's roots. The closer the first buds are cut, the less they will show up, and the more you remove the small crown of bark that usually exists at their insertion, and which develops new gems with the greatest ease. These productions, by capturing a large part of the sap, are very harmful to the subject they exhaust. When pruning a branch, care should be taken to cut only on a bud with a wood eye, otherwise the branch will dry out and form an unpleasant snag. It's best to wait until the eyes start to develop, so as to be able to choose the right length and arrangement of wood. Pruning can be much longer than for peach trees, because they grow back very easily on old wood. Sometimes, plum trees produce far more fruit than they can feed while maintaining their vigor and health, with the result that exhausted branches dry out over the winter. It's better to have prevented them from forming by pruning fruit-bearing branches like those of the peach tree. Cherry training and pruning. This tree is most commonly grown as a full canopy; however, it is sometimes trained as a charming cattail, a girandole or an espalier. It is particularly the early cherry trees from England, etc., that are pruned. The choice of subjects on which to graft should be made as follows: For full-wind grafting, graft from stones; for half-wind grafting, from scions; for cattail grafting, from morello cherry; and for espalier grafting, from mahaleb or Sainte-Lucie. Individuals on offshoots are more delicate and, above all, more prone to gum. Full-wind cherry trees, especially when only grafted at stem height, could be left to nature, with the only precaution being to cut back the dead wood exactly; but it will always be more advantageous, in order to form a regular head, to subject it to pruning after three or four years, in which case it will be raised absolutely as we said on page 218, by lengthening the pruning a little more. As it grows very easily on old wood, when, through old age or an accident, its head becomes bald, its branches are removed, and they soon produce vigorous shoots which form a new head. In espaliers, it is much easier to manage than the previous species, because its long, flexible branches lend themselves to any shape, and bend to any position. Here, the artificial distinction between wood branches and fruit branches, which is essential in the peach tree, is of no use at all, because the cherry tree, even on fairly old wood, is filled with a considerable number of small shoots, which provide more than three-quarters of its harvest. The whole pruning operation should therefore be limited to removing misplaced branches, trellising the others so as to fill the wall without causing confusion, cutting back any buds or twigs that might protrude from the front and tend to increase the thickness of the espalier, and sparing all the small flowering clusters, wherever they may be. If there are any gaps, they can easily be filled by bringing the branches on the sides closer together. If a suffering branch threatens to perish, or at least never reach the vigor of the others, it should be cut back to a stump of a few inches; by this means, robust sprays capable of filling the empty space will soon be produced. Chinese palms. Several species of cherry have a tendency to let their branches hang down; these can be used to form Chinese palmettes (pl. 10, fig. 1), full of grace, with a very picturesque look, and capable of producing varied and piquant effects, if intermingled with other espaliers to destroy monotony. Overly vigorous trees, grafted on cores, can also be very advantageously subjected to this form, when it comes to taming their vegetation. To this end, they should be treated as described in the palmette article on page 209, but with the difference that all branches, buds and twigs should be arched as they develop, and disbudded with great care to prevent the eyes placed in the middle of the branches from attracting all the sap to the detriment of the tips. 008 SECOND DIVISION. PRUNING TREES WITH SEEDED FRUIT. We have seen, on page 143, thirteenth precept, that the fruiting of seeded trees is quite different from that of stone trees: they are not treated in the same way in this respect when pruning. As for their formation, there is little difference, except in the case of those which bear fruit at the tip of the branch, such as quince. Pruning and training the pear tree. The varieties of this precious species have multiplied astonishingly, and cultivation has so modified their nature that each of them prefers a particular exposure and shape in order to be completely successful; there are even a few that absolutely refuse to take forms to which the others submit with the greatest ease. So, before deciding whether to plant a particular garden species in a cattail, goblet, espalier or in the open air, we need to be sure not only that it is suitable, but also, from a production point of view, which of the shapes it can best adapt to. Pear trees can be trained in espalier, cattail, pyramid, girandole, goblet, high-stemmed and low-stemmed full-wind, and each of these forms requires that they be grafted onto suitable subjects. The plein-vent will be grafted on wild or on franc; the espalier, when it will have enough space to acquire a big development, will be also on franc, and in the contrary case on quince tree of Portugal; to cover a very-small extent of wall, it will be on the small quince tree. Cattails, girandoles, goblets and pyramids will be chosen from those grafted on franc, if you want them to be very large; on cognassier de Portugal, if you want them of ordinary proportions, and on petit cognassier, if you want them to be dwarf. However, the quality of the soil and climate should be taken into consideration, since in poor, shallow soil, it is sometimes necessary to graft on to a franc tree, to obtain trees of the same size as those grown on quince in soil rich in nutritive juices. What's more, several species that do not do well on quince can only be grown on franc, whatever the type of pruning to which they are subjected. We note that trees grafted on small quince are better suited to cold climates, because this type of tree is more robust and more resistant to the rigors of winter. For espaliers, exposure is not irrelevant either; in general, fruit grafted on quince trees are most at home in the east and west, and fear the south, because their bark is sensitive to the sun's rays during hot weather, and their roots, which are closer to the earth's surface, are also more prone to drying out. Facing east is particularly suitable for fruit that ripens in summer, but south is better for liver fruit. Before planting a young tree, check its roots as we did for the peach tree, and prepare the soil properly so that it can spread its new hair at ease. The graft should always be out of the ground, so that it cannot emit any roots, which would cause an unpleasant bulge, and even harm the quantity and quality of the fruit. 90 De l'Espalier. Rarely given the form of the Montreuil espalier, but usually that of the French espalier. Sometimes it is left to grow at will, with the only precaution of trellising it and spreading it on the surface, preventing it from becoming thicker; it then forms what we have called the natural espalier. Suppose you want to train it in the French style, here's how you'll do it. "e Year. If the grafted shoot has three, four or five vigorous buds, capable of providing the beginnings of three, four or five first limbs, they can be preserved by cutting them back to four or five good eyes. If not, the bud of the graft is cut back to seven or eight inches in length. During vegetation, we monitor the development and growth of the wood, by disbudding all misplaced shoots, i.e. those growing in front of and behind the branches. When the young shoots have acquired sufficient firmness to be inclined without breaking, they are inclined and palisaded according to the principles we gave in the peach tree article, and that's all there is to it for now. 2° Year. By this time, the main limbs should have formed the shape the espalier will have later on. The tree can then be treated à la Sieulle, i.e. by removing buds before they sprout. When pruning, if the tree has grown sufficiently, you should obtain a long extension of the first limbs, and when trellising, the first upper and lower secondary limbs. If vegetation has been weak, prune short, to obtain vigorous wood. 908 In the third and fourth years, the same principles apply, but always with second, third and fourth limbs, etc., an essential difference that characterizes trees with pepins. It's possible that twigs and shavings will have already shown up, as fruiting on these trees doesn't have a fixed, determined period like that of stone trees; it can be early or late, depending on the greater or lesser vigor of each individual. If no buds have yet developed, pruning is lengthened and, above all, disbudding on the front of the branches is stopped, as this is where the shoots will grow. The buds should be pinched to make them grow fruiting eyes, and when trellising, four or five eyes should be broken, as we said in the article on breaking. See pag. 163. If one of the main limbs seems to want to seize too much sap and dominate over the others, it should be detached from the wall, and its growth stopped by means of inclination and arching, at the same time as the weaker limbs are straightened. Finally, in terms of shape, these trees are trained according to the same principles as stone trees, the only difference being in the way they bear fruit. As a general rule, trees should be pruned short in the first few years, to form vigorous wood, and long in the remaining years, to set fruit. However, pruning length should always be calculated according to the health and condition of the tree. For example, if an extremely vigorous espalier has not fruited for a long time, not only do you lengthen the pruning, but you can also just cut or break off the tips of the branches, and wait until vegetation has begun; this is a sure way to reduce the most rebellious trees. If, on the other hand, the subject is weak, as is very often the case with those grafted on quince, we prune short, i.e. on three, four or five eyes, more or less, depending on circumstances. As there is not an eye on the pear tree that cannot provide, according to the will of the intelligent man who manages it, a lambourde, a twig or a branch for wood, we will always succeed in making fruitful an individual which, in the hands of an uneducated person, would have remained sterile for a large number of years. So any gardener who accuses a tree of sterility when it has been under his direction for two or three years is, in our eyes, merely denouncing his ignorance. When a tree is overloaded with twigs at the front, which increases its thickness and gives it a bad grace, when it has been left overloaded with branches and they cause confusion, finally when the tree is languishing for some particular reason, most usually due to exhaustion or old age, we shorten all its branches to renew them, or even cut them back to the first limbs; or, if the damage is great, we cut back these first limbs themselves more or less close to the collar, depending on whether the individual is more or less altered. A large number of buds will grow back on all parts of the old wood, and these should be managed in the same way as those of a young tree, but with the precaution of taking greater care of them. Finally, we'd like to point out that several varieties of pear trees, including the good summer Christian, which produce most of their fruit buds at or near the tip of the branches, should be trained in such a way that the branches don't have to be cut back until after fruiting. D Palmette pear trees, à la Montreuil, and natural espalier pear trees, can be trained in exactly the same way as above, except for the difference in shape. Du Contre-Espalier. This, being nothing other than an espalier, must, as a general rule, be operated in the same way. We work on both surfaces, as we do on only one in the previous one, and we disbud, according to the same principles, the misplaced wood growing on the sides of the branches, while nevertheless sparing the joists and twigs. As it is impossible to give a counter-espalier the same height as an espalier, because the eye must be able to see the garden over it, it is only ever trained on two main branches, according to the form used for espaliers à la Montreuil (pl. 9, fig. 5); and even then, so that the limbs never exceed four feet in height, the main branches must be in a much more open V-shape. To maintain the inclination of the limbs, they can be staked against stiles; or, if you prefer, they can be pruned short for two or three years, to give the main branches enough strength to stand on their own. In good soils, especially if you have neglected to choose trees grafted on small quince trees, it often happens that you cannot easily obtain fruit if you want to maintain the trees at the height indicated; you are then obliged to let them climb, and the view is obstructed. This disadvantage, combined with the fact that the shape of the espalier makes it very difficult to cultivate garden squares, as it impedes passage and makes flowerbeds awkward, is the reason why counter-espaliers are now rarely seen in well-kept cultivations. De l'Espalier oblique. If you want to enjoy a wall quickly and cover it within three or four years, no form is more suitable than the oblique espalier. Grafted quince trees are selected and planted as for an ordinary espalier, five or six feet apart. They are trained à la Montreuil, but only on a single inclined stem, with the head facing the east and the root facing the west, representing a single side of the espalier (pl. 9, fig. 7). In this way, the trees cover each other and, in a short space of time, cover the entire surface of the wall. As they grow taller and threaten to harm each other by crossing their branches, one is removed between two, leaving the espalier without any gaps. These trees are managed according to the same principles as the ordinary espalier; but, as they are much easier to fill in at the bottom, pruning can be lengthened considerably in the first few years, and consequently fruit can be obtained much sooner. The only thing you have to watch out for is that the upper secondary branch a, a, doesn't draw in too much sap as it gets closer to the vertical line. If, in spite of the means known to stop it, this cannot be achieved, it should be removed entirely, and its place filled by the b, b limbs of the neighbouring tree, which should be lengthened accordingly. All tree species suitable for espaliering lend themselves readily to this form, and are even much easier to manage in this way than in any other. Their yield is also greater, for the simple reason that the espalier is better furnished. In addition, this form is better suited to low walls. Goblet. This is used for trees whose branches extend from the root collar, and for others whose stems rise one, two, three or even four feet. In both cases, they behave in the same way. A goblet (pl. 8, fig. 3 ) is handled in exactly the same way as a counterpallet, with the difference that it is raised on three, four or five mother branches, the second limbs of which are supported on hoops, so that the tree takes the shape of an inverted cone, the inside of which is hollowed out like a vase. It should be no thicker than an ordinary espalier, and should be well lined with joists and twigs, both inside and out. When pruning and trellising, great care should be taken to remove any buds that might fill the vase as they develop inside. Another principle is to space the main branches evenly, so that none dominates the others. Moreover, the goblet, as advocated by ancient authors, is the most vicious shape that can be given to a tree, for several reasons. The first is that pruning it requires a great deal of care and time, to produce only the results of a half-wind left to nature; the second is that two of these trees placed opposite each other in two flowerbeds have, in a very few years, obstructed the passageway in an alley five or six feet long; finally, the fruit that grows in the interior and on the north side, being shaded for most of the day, never has the color and fragrance of that which ripens on the other points of its outer surface; in addition, the time of ripening can be significantly delayed. DC De la Quenouille. This form (pl. 8, fig. 4) is one of today's most fashionable, and also the most advantageous, in that it bears fruit very quickly, occupies very little space and causes no embarrassment in flowerbeds, and consequently offers a much greater variety of enjoyment by enclosing in a small space a far greater number of species and varieties of fruit. Cattails can be planted eight, ten or twelve feet apart, depending on the nature of the soil, either in rows in a flowerbed, in which case a paradise, a vine or a redcurrant is usually placed between two, or in staggered rows, in a chessboard, etc. When my father brought the cattail pruning method from Germany to France, in the royal gardens of Brunoy and Gros-Bois, it had the greatest difficulty in spreading during the first fifteen or twenty years. Routine gardeners argued that these trees would not last long, and that they would produce little. These two errors have been refuted by experience whenever trees have been in the hands of men who had some notion of the laws of vegetation, and they can now only be supported by ignorance and bad faith. It is certain that a tree a few feet high and thick cannot produce as much as a full wind; but if we calculate the shade cast by the latter and the amount of land it takes away from cultivation; if we establish as many cattails as it would take to compensate, we reply that these will produce three times as much as the other; in addition to which the fruit will be much more beautiful, of better quality, and of as many different species as we wish. If you want to grow cattails up to twenty-five feet tall or more, you'll need to choose trees grafted on franc; for medium-sized trees, you'll need to choose those grafted on quince. It must be assumed, however, that they should be placed in good soil; for, in very poor soil, those grafted on franc will only form ordinary trees, and those on quince may never exceed five or six feet in height. It's up to the gardener to study the quality of his land before making a decision; he must pay all the more attention to it, because otherwise he'll never achieve his goal. The subject grafted three or four inches above ground must have a strong, straight stem, well covered with branches, especially at the bottom; for most of the cattails found in all gardens have the great defect of being bare near the ground, and this is an irreparable defect. However, in cold, damp soils, the lower branches of a cattail often suffer considerably from the proximity of the soil surface; the flowers drop off every year, the branches are thin and etiolated, and the bark is covered with moss. In this case, which is an exception to the rule, far from keeping a cattail or a furnished pyramid close to the ground, it is raised on an eighteen-inch or two-foot stem. As it is very rare to have well-directed subjects in nurseries, we are going to put the amateur in the position of supervising his young trees himself until their third year of grafting, at which time they are put in place. 1" Year. The procedure will be absolutely the same as for the peach cattail. The inclination of the limbs should not be calculated so as to provide, as in the latter, an angle of forty-five degrees with the stem, because pear trees form, depending on the variety, an angle open to certain determined degrees, and it would be pointless to antagonize them in this respect. So, never trellis the limbs, and let them develop according to their nature. You should get two or three this first time, and a good terminal bud to form the extension of the stem. It's mainly this year that you need to keep an eye on these limbs, and give them every means of acquiring enough strength to always dominate over the others, and never be abandoned by the sap. It is on them alone that the beauty of the cattail will depend throughout its life; and, if they were to fail, it would be irreparably and forever defective, as we have said. To protect them in their development, we therefore carefully remove all buds which could harm them by attracting part of the sap. 2nd year. If the limbs are vigorous, we keep seven or eight eyes on the terminal bud, in order to obtain three or four new limbs, and we prune short those of the first year, to further increase their vigor. If the tree has only made a weak growth, the terminal bud should be cut back, to obtain this year only an extension, or at most one or two limbs. The same principle as for the previous year should be applied, i.e. in favor of the lower limbs, which should be left with all their buds to attract sap. 3rd year. At this third pruning, we could stop the terminal bud at one foot or eighteen inches, if the tree was vigorous, and by this means obtain four or five new limbs. If its vegetation was weak, we would continue to prune short. We don't need to tell you that limbs should be placed alternately on the stem; that the lower ones, being the oldest, should also be the longest and strongest, and that those obtained from year to year should decrease proportionally in length and strength. Quite often, a limb, thwarted in its growth by some particular circumstance, deviates from its position, which must always move regularly away from the stem like a ray from its axis. If it seems to want to throw itself to the left, it will be pruned on a bud placed on the right side, in order to regain its direction; if, on the contrary, it throws itself to the right, it will be pruned on a bud on the left. When the bud seems to want to leave the natural inclination of the variety, we do the same, i.e. we place it on an upper eye if it needs to be raised, or on a lower eye if it needs to be lowered. Sometimes, to make this procedure more effective, we prune seven or eight lines above the gem, to force the bud to turn away from the straight direction it could take by extending the branch, if it were not prevented from doing so by the small snag that we remove at the next pruning. 4° Year. We assume that the tree is in place. If it has been raised in the same soil, it should be moved and replanted immediately, in autumn, with the greatest care, and pruned in the same way, without cutting back too much, because we can be sure of a good recovery. If, on the other hand, the tree has travelled and its roots have suffered, pruning should be carried out much closer, but in the same shape. Finally, if the tree is already bald at the bottom, it should be cut back to within a foot of the graft, more or less, depending on the strength of the stem, and shaped in the same way as if it were grafted this year. We'll continue describing the operations as if the recovery were assured. We would prune the terminal bud and limbs as in the previous year; but, when disbudding, we would leave eyes to form twigs and 20 16 1 lambourdes, and we would always choose them placed on the sides, so that each limb remained entirely free of those placed above and below; Finally, air and light should be able to circulate easily in the interior, which is the only way to preserve the bark of the stem from the mosses, lichens and cankers that humidity would otherwise attract and maintain. 5th year. This is the second year of planting, and if the recovery has been good, the tree should already be producing fruit. It will be managed according to the same principles as in previous years, but in addition the twigs and joists will have to be watched over, according to the rules we set out earlier. From year to year, we'll act as we've just said, never losing sight of the fact that the tree must always remain well furnished at the bottom; that the stem must always rise perpendicularly and in as straight a line as possible; that the limbs must be regularly spaced, that they must not bear any secondary limbs, but only twigs; Finally, the greatest thickness of the tree should be at the end of the lower limbs; from this point to the top, the tree should decrease regularly and progressively in thickness, so as to form the shape of an elongated cone. A cattail pear tree, trained as we have just said, will remain vigorous for many years, and will produce fruit that is all the better for having received all atmospheric influences with the greatest ease, especially those resulting from the sun's rays. DC Prramid. This is nothing other than a cattail, the greatest thickness of which is close to the ground, and which has the regular shape of a cone, starting from its base. Moreover, the pyramid can be cut and driven like the cattail, without any difference, and offers the same advantageous results. When raised, it is a little more difficult to fill in the bottom, due to the inclination of the branches; but this is achieved by lengthening the pruning of the lower branches during the second and third years (pl. 8, fig. 5). This shape is particularly useful for setting fruit on stubborn species. It is also used on varieties whose branches have a natural tendency to form a forty-five degree angle with the stem. De la Girandole. In the Paris climate, we have species which, to ripen perfectly and acquire their full fragrance, need a greater quantity of air and light than the others; and it's because we can't distinguish between these species that it's been said that fruit grown on full wind is more fragrant than that harvested from other subjects. The girandole has the advantage of allowing the fruit to enjoy all atmospheric influences, to the same degree as full-wind trees, and can therefore replace them for the same purpose; but it has one quality over them, which is that, subjected to the same pruning and cultivation as the cattail and espalier, its fruit acquires the same volume as those of the latter. The girandole is pruned and trained in the same way as the cattail and pyramid, but differs in the way its limbs are inserted on the stem. We have said that, on the latter two, the branches should be alternate, and 908 that a line passing over the insertion of all of them would turn in a spiral around the stem; here, on the contrary, we obtain four or five members whose opposite insertions form, as far as possible, the whorl (pl. 8, fig. 6). The stem is then extended, without branches or twigs, up to eighteen inches in height if the limbs are inclined horizontally, and up to two feet if they are left at their natural inclination, as in the cattail. Here another whorl is placed; a third eighteen inches or two feet above, and so on to the end of the tree. We have fixed the distance between the whorls, assuming that the operation is carried out on a pear tree grafted on quince and in ordinary soil; but, in all other circumstances, it is up to the gardener to determine this distance, according to the presumed development of his trees. It can be set closer together for trees grafted onto small quince trees in poor soil, and much further apart for those grafted onto frankincense trees in good soil. Each whorl should also be set back from its neighbor in proportion to its size, and distances will become closer from base to top, following the proportional degradation of the whorls. It is possible, for example, to have three feet between the first whorl and the second; two and a half feet between the second and the third; two feet between the third and the fourth, and so on. Full-wind and half-wind. The full-wind is provided by a subject grafted on wild or on franc; the half-wind is provided by a subject grafted on quince tree of Portugal, in the excellent and hot grounds, and by a subject grafted on franc, in those which are mediocre or bad. The subject can be grafted at the top of the stem for either type, but it is better DOt that it be close to the ground, because if a gust of wind breaks it, you have the resource of reforming its head below the wound, without having to graft it again. The stem is raised and the tree formed in exactly the same way as the peach tree (vor. pag. 218), but the buds are pinched off a little longer, and the under-buds are pruned to no more than two inches in length. Once the tree is formed, it is left to nature, with the sole precaution of removing the dead wood and buds that develop along the stem every year. Should a branch seize the sap and threaten to destroy the balance of the vegetation, it can be prevented by the ordinary means of bowing, ring incision, etc. The tree is then left to its own devices. It would be a good idea to remove some of them when they have reached a quarter or a third of their size: those remaining would be larger, less gravelly and more fragrant, and a harvest for the following year would be assured. Apple tree training and pruning. The apple tree takes any shape you want, without exception, and succeeds equally well with all of them when properly trained; but, in order to adapt to each one, it must be grafted onto its own subject. 1° The longest-lasting and largest full-springs, those usually grown to produce cider apples, should be grafted onto wild cuttings taken from the woods, taking care to choose only those from seed, and not those from the stump: they are easily recognized by their roots. 08 2° To produce full-springs of good knife fruit, graft onto seedpods, i.e. onto subjects from cider apple seedpods sown and grown in the nursery. 3° To obtain mid-sprouts, large cattails, goblets, horizontal espaliers with stems, graft on subjects from good knife fruit seedlings, sown and cultivated as above. 4° If you want to form cattails and pyramids of ordinary size, or perpendicular and horizontal espaliers, graft on doucins. 5° Finally, for dwarf trees of the species, bushes, vases, very small cattails and pyramids, and espaliers three or four feet high, graft on paradise. Whatever the nature of the soil in which you plant, you must choose your subjects according to the indications we have just given, or you will never succeed in establishing, in trees subjected to pruning, a constant balance between fruiting and vegetation. For example, espalier trees grafted on wild or egin trees will never produce anything but wood, and if they do bear fruit, it will only be when old age diminishes their vigor. If, on the other hand, they are placed on paradise, in whatever way the gardener manages and spares his pruning, he will never succeed in giving them more than four or five feet of development; even then, they will only reach this size in top-quality soil. The Espalier. Depending on the wall space to be filled, a tree grafted on franc, doucin or paradis is chosen, and the tree is trained in the same way as the pear tree. There is, however, one difference: since apple trees often bear fruit on young wood, pruning should be done carefully, so as to preserve any buds from the previous year that may be showing signs of lagging; there is always time to shorten or remove them in autumn. It often happens that apple trees on hardwood or doucin have great difficulty setting fruit, especially when they have been pruned short and before vegetation. There's a simple way of reducing this problem: prune only when flowering begins, and prune very long. If that weren't enough, we'd arch all the branches without exception, and only break the tips. Horizontal espalier. This shape is perfectly suited to trees grafted on franc and doucin, to which we wish to give a picturesque appearance by increasing their yield. Several shapes are possible, but the parasol shape (pl. 8, fig. 1) seems to us to be one of the most attractive. According to drawings we have received from China, this type of tree is very fashionable in that country, and perhaps this is a recommendation, as the Chinese are known to be more advanced than we are in the cultivation of fruit trees, of which they make giants or pygmies at will (1). To form a parasol, we raise the stem as we have said for full wind trees, and stop it either by pinching in August, or by pruning in spring, at a height sufficient to allow us to pass easily under its head when it is formed. Four or five buds are obtained as close together as possible (1) The Chinese have tamed the vegetation of fruit trees to the point of keeping them all twelve or eighteen inches high, in vases seven or eight inches wide. These charming miniatures sc place on the table at dessert, and each guest is mat̂tre to pick at his or her choice the fruits with which they are laden. O of the others, so as to have like a whorl. These buds, destined to form the mother branches, are placed on a hoop supported by stakes, until the branches are strong enough to carry it themselves. These first limbs are trained as if to form an espalier, i.e. each year we obtain their extension and second limbs to garnish in between. Disbud carefully, allowing only the buds on the side of the branches to develop during the first two or three years of training, which is the opposite of disbudding in the perpendicular espalier. When the limbs have grown in length, new hoops are placed and supported in the same way as the first; but this one will no longer need a stake, which will already clear the underside of the tree. At this point, we'll stop disbudding the eyes on the upper surface, because we'll need to obtain some short twigs and strips; to do this, we'll pinch the buds at four or five inches, and break them off during pruning if necessary. Repeat this procedure year after year, until the tree has reached the necessary development. The branches will then be strong enough to hold themselves in position, and the stakes can be removed from the hoops, but the hoops should always remain to support the young shoots. One extremely important point is never to let a bud grow in a vertical position, as it would soon starve the others. If you want to stop a cattail growing and force it to set fruit, you can extend some of its lower branches in a horizontal espalier, as shown here (pl. 8, fig. 2). Trees trained in this way produce fruit of superior quality, as they enjoy the full benefit of the open air and sunshine. Cradles, alleys, avenues, etc., can be covered with this type of espalier, whose shape is more or less suitable for all fruit trees, except peach and apricot. In windy situations, simply raising the horizontal espalier two, three or four feet above the surface of the ground will ensure harvests that would be highly doubtful if the trees were given any other shape. Vase. This name is given to very small apple trees grafted on paradise, rarely reaching more than three feet in height. They are pruned and trained according to the same principles as the goblet; but, as they tend to grow very irregularly, it is difficult to give them a symmetrical shape. They are therefore simply trellised on one or two hoops (pl. 11, fig. 1), and hollowed out inside in the same way as the goblet. These small trees often bear fruit in their second year, in which case they can be spared from pruning. But when they start to wood and become untamable, there is no better way than to leave them for a year without pruning, and to simply break off all the woody branches at the end of May. Apple trees on paradise are more likely than any other species to produce flower buds on young wood. We recommend keeping these small strips until at least after the apple harvest. The vases take up very little space, and can be planted three or four feet apart without harming each other. They are planted in rows, often lining flowerbeds with one between each cattail, and even more often forming staggered or chessboard-shaped clumps, in which case the square containing them takes on the name of Normandy. These apple trees, commonly known as paradise, produce little fruit, but of surprising size and premium quality. Du Buisson. This is a tree grafted on paradise that is pruned solely for its fruiting, with no regard for its shape, which is left to chance and nature. Moreover, it is trained like a vase, and sometimes even called a paradise (pl. 11, fig. 2). It is intended to occupy the same places in the garden, and is also used to make normandies. If you wish to give the apple tree a form other than those we have just described, follow the same rules as for the species we have already mentioned, taking into account the differences that its particular mode of vegetation and fruiting necessarily entails. It is not uncommon to see a full-wind apple tree form such a bushy head that the lower branches are inclined to grow and develop their twigs in a more airy atmosphere; the result is that they prevent the air from circulating freely around the stem, causing the mosses, lichens and often cankers to which this species is quite prone. To avoid this inconvenience, low branches are pruned, but with the precautions we recommended in the article on lopping. (See pag. 169.) Cognassier pruning and training. This tree can hardly be pruned, for several reasons: the first is that, as it grows very irregularly, it would be very difficult to subject it to a regular shape; the second and more important reason is that, as it only bears fruit at the end of the branches, it would be impossible to preserve its crop by pruning it. However, it can be espaliered and cattailed; but to do this, it must be trained before it sprouts, the buds left behind must be trellised as they develop, and the pruning knife must only be used every two or three years once it has formed. If you do this, you'll lose most of the fruit every third year. To form a cattail or espalier, it can be obtained from cuttings; but to form a stem, it must be obtained from seedlings. It is not customary to graft it. "Pruning and training of the medlar. This tree, even more misshapen than the quince, like the quince, produces its fruit buds at the end of the branches; hence it is only pruned to put it on a stem, after which it is left to nature. The only care it needs is to clear it of dead wood, and to remove, in spring, the aborted fruit that has dried and remained at the end of a few branches. It absolutely refuses to take any regular shape. Pruning mountain ash. We're talking here only about the domestic mountain ash, whose very acerbic fruit is only edible when it has reached the state known as chard in pears and medlars. This tree is extremely valuable, but only for its wood, so it is always raised on a straight trunk and as high as possible. As it grows very slowly, it will take 908 several years to form a straight stem, which can be maintained in this position by means of a good stake. Pruning should be done in hooks, as for other trees; But as it takes several years to reach a sufficient height, i.e. ten to twelve feet, let three or four buds develop near the tip, to form a kind of dummy head, which we raise each year by cutting off the lowest branches as new ones form along the terminal bud, which we always care for and trellis vertically, so that the stem takes on body in proportion to its height. When it has reached the desired height, stop it as for other species, and leave the tree to grow freely, giving it only the care customary for all full-windows. Pruning and training. We need to consider the orange tree from several different angles: 1° as a fruit tree, in which case we'll show you how to prune it to obtain good fruit in favored climates, where it grows in the open ground; 2° as an ornamental tree, raised in boxes and kept in a greenhouse in winter to protect it from the rigors of frost, which would destroy it in our climates. In this case, it is important to consider whether it should be used solely for pleasure, or whether the harvesting of its flowers should combine the pleasurable with the useful. In each of these circumstances, it must be managed differently. In the south of France, in Italy and Spain, for example, whether you grow it on a stem or keep it in an espalier, it is quite common to graft it onto a lemon tree, or another species with sour fruit, to make it grow more quickly, and onto itself to make it more robust. In espaliers, we are content to let it take its natural shape; but on stems, we usually give it a regular head, and then we prune it as we shall see below. Only, as flowering is more regular in its periods, the time of pruning also varies much less. People who speculate both on its flower and its fruit, spare the winter pruning all the twigs that mark its flower, and only remove them when the fruit is assured, i.e., at disbudding time. They then leave only enough to ensure the following year's harvest: in addition, they prune according to the same principles as for other fruit trees, i.e., shorten the weak individuals, and long those that are very vigorous. In our countries, you mustn't touch the orange tree while it's in the greenhouse, because the humidity that might remain on the wounds would be more dangerous than the cold itself. During the winter, we must be content with scrupulously removing all moldy leaves, and wiping off mold wherever it occurs. In Paris, preference is given to orange trees grafted onto limoniers, as they grow much faster. Let's suppose that the subject is grafted six or eight inches from the ground, and that we want to raise it on a stem. Every year, when you take it out of the greenhouse, you will prune the lateral buds of the graft, but on four or five eyes, so that the stem can take on body; which would not happen if you did not stop the sap by means of hook pruning. When it reaches the desired height, we pinch off the terminal bud to obtain three or four main branches. If you have raised a wild tree in this way, you should wait to graft it until its stem is at least the size of a finger, so as to be able to place two escutcheons, one on one side, the other on the other. Whether you've brought an orange tree to this state yourself, or had it brought in from Genoa, you'd do as we're about to say. You prune the two grafts, each on two eyes, to obtain four buds to form the main branches, and you have a subject in the same state as the one we assumed above, grafted close to the ground. It is trained in the same way. If you want to obtain flowers and fruit quickly, you prune very long from the second year onwards, or even just pinch off the ends of the branches; in subsequent years, you let the tree grow as it pleases, and just pinch off the ends of branches that would tend to move too far away from the stem to form a ball-shaped head. After a very short time, the tree stops growing on its own and starts to bear fruit, especially if its roots are a little hindered. But if you're wiser and prefer a well-formed, healthy tree, you prune very short during the first three or four years, to give the head a regular shape, and the branches sufficient strength to maintain for centuries a balance of sap, without which the orange tree will never be truly beautiful. When disbudding, only useful, well-placed wood is left, and year after year the pruning is lengthened until the tree is finally ready to bear fruit. At this point, the orange tree is managed according to different principles, depending on whether fruit or flowers are to be harvested. In the first case, as soon as the tree is out of the greenhouse and accustomed to the open air, it is pruned without regard for the flowers, according to the same principles as other fruit trees. All weak branches and those that might have vegetated in the orangery are pruned short, and anything that looks etiolated is removed. Over-abundant twigs are cut back, and branches that would deviate from the spherical shape of the head are shortened. The interior is cleaned of rags and other misplaced branches that would intercept air circulation, and only vigorous, well-formed wood is left. When disbudding, only as much fruit as can be produced is left on the tree, and in the autumn, any useless buds that might have developed by misleading the grower are removed. If a few branches were to sap to the detriment of others, we'd have to see whether they could be used to replace languishing ones that we'd remove, or to fill a gap by directing them into the necessary position; in both these cases, we'd prune them very long, or even just pinch them at the end to stop them growing. If it is absolutely necessary to cut them off, care should be taken to cut them off at the bark insertion, to ensure that they do not grow back. If orange trees are to provide abundant harvests of flowers, other principles apply. Don't touch the trees once you've taken them out of the greenhouse, as you won't be able to prune them without removing some of the twigs on which the young flower buds are already appearing.
