Just cut off all the bad wood you can without touching the twigs, and wait until the harvest is over before pruning according to the same principles we have just described. However, there is always a serious disadvantage: as the season is very early when pruning begins, the young buds don't have time to gain strength before winter; they don't ripen their wood, and remain throughout the bad season in a kind of sap hesitation which makes them extremely sensitive to cold and humidity. 4 When an orange tree finds itself hampered in its box, which is easily recognized by the yellowish tint of its foliage and the weakness of its shoots, it must either be given a larger vase, or its roots brought closer together. The latter operation destroys the balance of sap so necessary to vegetation, and only pruning can restore it. In this circumstance, all branches are shortened, more or less according to the urgency of the situation, and the tree is directed in the same way as when it was first formed, until the balance is re-established and its new head begins to bear fruit. This pruning must be repeated each time the tree is repotted in the same vase. Grenadier pruning and training. This tree is in exactly the same situation as the orange tree, and should be pruned according to the same rules. However, there is less need to use a pruning knife, as it can be easily pruned by pinching. To grow it on a stem, you need to obtain it from seed; but as a bush, it works perfectly from cuttings or marcottes. To obtain good fruit, hollow it out internally, cut off all the weak branches in spring, which it multiplies profusely, and pinch it when its buds have reached half or two-thirds of their length, in order to bring it into flower. In countries where the pomegranate can overwinter in the open ground, it lends itself well to shearing, and can form charming palisades. THIRD DIVISION. PRUNING AND TRAINING OF BERRY TREES. This division includes trees and shrubs whose vegetation and fruiting vary greatly from one species to another, and therefore require completely different pruning methods. With the exception of grapevines and currants, none of them have been subjected to regular pruning, and are only pruned for education. Vine pruning. Whether the young tree has been obtained from marcotte or cuttings, or whether it has been grafted or not, when it is planted, only two or three buds should be left, in the first year, whatever shape you want to give the tree. Above the latitude of Paris, perfect ripening of the grapes can only be expected when the vines are espaliered against a wall facing south. Beyond the forty-eighth degree, only vigorous, early-ripening varieties can be grown. In the North, and particularly in England, they are grown only in purpose-built greenhouses; but in all cases, pruning is carried out according to the same principles: for the reason that, in climates that are favorable to the vine, its size varies as it does in greenhouses, according to its greater or lesser vigor. Remember that we have recommended never cutting too close to an eye, whenever we use the pruning knife on new wood, to avoid the mortality that always occurs a few lines below the wound; but to prune to about half an inch above the gem, in a bevel with the base opposite it (pl. 7, fig. 2), so that the 2. 908 flow of sap, which is very abundant in the vine, can easily escape without wetting the eye, which it could drown. The right time to prune is very important to know, although it varies from one climate to another. In countries where there is little or no frost, pruning is carried out at the beginning of winter. This is an excellent method, as the wounds have time to dry out before vegetation starts, so less sap is lost; the plant is therefore more vigorous, and vegetation is much earlier, which ensures the ripening of grapes that usually only ripen at the end of autumn. In countries where the weather is very cold, pruning is delayed to a greater or lesser extent, to ensure that there will be no more frost when the cut is made. If this were not the case, the moisture brought to the cut, either by the sap or by fog or rain, would form an ice cube which would disrupt the germ by the cold, or by causing the shoot to burst. In addition to this disadvantage, the buds, by developing much too early, would be more exposed to the white frosts that often last until the end of April. In the part of France below the latitude of Lyon, pruning can begin as early as the end of January; a little further north, the operation is delayed until the middle of February; it can rarely be carried out in the vicinity of Paris before March; and, if you are further north, it can be postponed until the end of April. It's up to the grower's intelligence to choose the most favorable time, according to the ordinary temperature of the climate he inhabits. De la Treille. This name is generally given to all vines that, like the espalier, have a surface that is not thick909 seur. The trellis is usually laid against a wall or against a cradle-forming framework; and, in both cases, it is directed in several ways, according to the taste of the person pruning; but there are some that are more advantageous than others, and we are going to describe these. To obtain a beautiful trellis (pl. 9, fig. 8) that perfectly covers a large expanse of wall, and provides an abundant harvest, preference should be given to the following method, adopted by the inhabitants of Thomery. Plant the plant against the wall, or better still, if you wish to follow the principles of cultivation of the inhabitants of the village mentioned above, three or four feet away, in the flowerbed, lying on the side of the wall. In the first year, only a single shoot is left, which is then buried again a foot deep, so that the tip only emerges six inches from the wall. Only then do we begin to direct it to form the trellis. "e Year. We prune on three or four eyes, which we leave to develop until the time of disbudding, which should take place in June and July, at least in the Paris climate. At this time, only one bud is kept, the most vigorous of the three, which is pruned vertically to obtain the c stem. 2° Year. Prune very long, so as to give the full height of the stem this very year, and disbud or pinch off all the lateral sarmens. If you wish to form a very tall stem, capable of spreading its lateral branches on espaliers, you will have to neglect the second limbs, and only deal with them the following spring; but, if you have a sufficiently tall stem, you can spare the three upper buds, in order to obtain the two lower lateral branches d, d, and the extension of the stem e. 3rd Year. We prune the vertical shoot to two feet in length, to obtain its extension h, and two new lateral branches f, f. However, if it is not very vigorous, it would be better to bring it closer together on its two lower eyes to obtain a new, more robust shoot, which we would prune the following year, as we have just said. The first two limbs d, d, can be left more or less long, depending on whether they are more or less vigorous; but it is always wiser to keep them at a maximum of one foot for this time. All unnecessary shoots are rigorously disbudded, but two, g, g, can be kept to provide the first two fruiting branches. Needless to say, this disbudding must be carried out every year, and at all times, from the time the fruit is knotted until maturity. In this operation, only the buds intended to provide the extension of the stem, lateral branches and fruiting branches should ever be spared; all the others should be removed without exception, and the same principles should be applied throughout the life of the plant. 4th year. If you want to form the trellis on several rows of cords, continue to extend the vertical shoot, and make it produce new lateral branches. If this is not the case, cut it off at the base of the bark, i.e. below its lower eye, so that it ceases to produce buds, which should be removed with great care. The extension of limbs d, d, f, f is obtained, and fruiting branches k, K, etc. are placed on top. Those e, e, obtained the previous year, are pruned over two eyes, which will form two new fruiting branches, which are trained almost vertically to fill the empty space, two feet wide, between the lateral branches. As a rule, fruiting branches should always be pruned over two eyes, so as to have only two branches, the taller of which is removed each year, and cut back to the lowest, in order to lengthen as little as possible the snag or spur o, o, etc., which supplies them. These spurs themselves are shortened when they provide a good bud capable of replacing them, and inserted close to the mother branch. The fruiting sarmen will be spaced more or less apart on the side branches, depending on whether they are more or less vigorous; but they will never be less than six inches apart, nor more than eighteen. They should be trained regularly, so as not to confuse or cross each other. All those that are weak or badly placed will be cut back. 5° Year. As with previous prunings, the main focus is on extending the lateral branches. To achieve this, select the most vigorous bud closest to the tip p, p; incline it horizontally, and prune it short or long according to its vigor, since it must be spared all the more as it is destined to provide wood to preserve, and fruit. If it is too short, it will produce a lot of superfluous wood and no grapes; if it is too long, it will produce many bunches, but they will be small and of inferior quality, and there will be no wood and the vine will soon be exhausted. This rule applies to all branches and all vines. 2 Finally, every year, we continue to manage the trellis as we have just said; and, when we want to stop it, at every pruning we bring the terminal shoot of the lateral branches p, p, closer together on a spur, as we have said for the fruit-bearing branches. Vine training should never begin until the fruit is well knotted, unless some of the sarmens are in danger of breaking, in which case they need to be supported. If the operation were carried out before this time, the sap from the buds that are removed would be carried into the bunches, lengthening them considerably into tendrils, and causing the grapes to abort. The effect is the same when pruning too short on very vigorous wood. It is also very important not to tie the vine shoots too tightly, so as not to hinder vegetation, which would allow sub-buds to develop before winter below the ligature, depriving the shoot of good eyes for the following year. Some time before the fruit ripens, a general disbudding is carried out, and the leaves that prevented the grapes from enjoying the sun and fresh air are removed. For vines, as for all other trees, we only remove as many leaves as are necessary to achieve our goal, and never more, as this would be dangerous for the plant, as we have already said. The buds growing at the bottom of the stems and on the roots will be removed right to the roots. A vine, trained in this way on two or three rows of cordons, can line a considerable length of wall, if the soil is perfectly suited to it. One vine in Holland, for example, is said to extend one hundred and twenty feet in length, while another in England forms a cordon one hundred and fifty feet long. Although we have said that this method was the one adopted in Thomery, some inhabitants modify it as follows: instead of forming four or five cords on the same stem, they form only one, which they extend to the right and left, and other vines provide the upper cords. In this way, they plant as many plants as they want to establish cords, and space them accordingly. The first vine 4 supplies the lower cordon at eight or ten inches high; the second supplies the second cordon eighteen inches above the first; the third vine supplies the third cordon as far from the second as this one is from the first, and so on, until they have covered the entire height of the wall. This type of trellis is very well suited to areas of mediocre depth, where a single vine would not be able to sink its roots far enough to provide vegetation for four or five rows of branches. Du Rerceau. The way in which vines are trained to cover cradles and other green rooms is rather arbitrary; however, you should always try to give them a regular shape (pl. 11, fig. 3), and prune them according to established principles. However, as there are fewer main limbs to be obtained, the pruning can be lengthened each year, so that the framework supporting it is covered earlier. This method is less advantageous than the others for the quality of the fruit, because the bunches, always hanging inside, have little air, heat and no sun at all. If we were to attempt to give them some by thinning out the leaves, we would fail entirely in our aim of providing shade when establishing these small arrangements. Du Cordon. This method of vine training is extremely advantageous for lining the coping of a wall against which an espalier is grown, and even better for forming a curtain in front of a flowerbed where you might be afraid of casting harmful shade on the trees in the espalier. - To establish a cordon under the coping of a wall, we plant as we said in the article on trellises; but if the land on the other side depended on the garden, it would be better, for the cordon as for the trellis, to plant on the other side and pass the stem through an oblique hole drilled in the wall for this purpose. The result would be that the roots of the vine and espalier could not harm each other. Even if the wall separated the garden from a paved courtyard, the same would still apply, since vines thrive equally well under paving stones and even flagstones. For the first few years, we'd raise the stem as we've said; then, when it reached the height of the coping, we'd stop it, leaving it to grow only one limb on the right and one on the left. These branches would be managed in the same way as the cords of the trellis, so as to extend them and obtain fruit; but, as there would only be one row, the pruning would be much longer, without fear of exhausting the stock. In our pl. 11, sig. 3, a, we have illustrated, against a cradle, a vine branch developed into a cordon; there is this difference: instead of trellising the fruit-bearing branches on a framework, as in the figure, they will be inclined more so that they do not overhang the wall, and they will be attached to the rag, on the coping below. If you wish to establish the cordon in the open air, or, to explain more clearly, to form a counter-pallet, you plant stakes in a line along a bed, spaced eight or ten feet apart, and support them with a crossbeam, which should only be three and a half or four feet high at most. Next to each stake is planted a tree which is raised to the height of the crossbeam, and trained on it; it can be trained straight (pl. 11, fig. 4), in which case the fruiting branches are bent around the crossbeam; or it can be trained spirally around the crossbeam, in which case the fruiting branches are trained straight, but always inclined horizontally. If you wish to have a better supplied counter espalier, establish two rows of cords, one two feet high on a first crossbeam, the other four feet high on a second crossbeam. In both cases, the vine is pruned and governed in the same way as the trellis, and the length of the training pruning is calculated according to the vigor of the stock and the quality of the soil. This method is used for large-scale cultivation in some cantons of the Mâconnais, Beaujolais and Lyonnais regions. Each row of vines is spaced twenty or twenty-five feet apart, with cereals grown in between. Only white wine is harvested from the cordons called hautains by the inhabitants of these countries. Pillars, columns and the like (pl. 11, fig. 5) may need to be trimmed. The same principles apply as for the cords we have just described, with the difference that the stem must always embrace the body around which it is to extend its branches. It must therefore be driven in a spiral. But, if the column were very large, two or more vines would be needed to fill it everywhere; then the stems could be extended in opposite spirals, as we have illustrated, and at each turn they would cross each other; or they could be spiralized in the same direction, in which case care should be taken to space the two stems always in the same proportion from the bottom to the top. In any case, fruiting branches should be trained vertically. We'd now like to talk about the pruning of vines grown singly in gardens, or in open fields to produce wine harvests. Pruning varies considerably from place to place and from custom to custom; but, as its principles are always based on the laws of vegetation, you can't go wrong if you've fully understood what we've said above, and taken into consideration the few general rules we're going to add here. 1° As the vine can only produce wood of a certain size in a year, never or at least very rarely exceeding that of an inch, it can be kept in small proportions, or made to cover a large space, at will, by bringing the pruning closer together or lengthening it. It produces equally well in either case, if its roots can be balanced with its head. This means that it can be kept very short in all soils, but cannot be made to grow very large in shallow soils. 2° Since vines are always more vigorous in good soils than in mediocre ones, pruning can be made longer in the former than in the latter, regardless of soil depth. 3° Since vines grow more vigorously in hot climates than in cold ones, pruning should be lengthened the closer you get to the south, and shortened a lot in the north. 4° Too much heat dries out the fruit, making it difficult to ripen; too little heat prevents it from ripening. Shade its branches in very hot climates, and don't blame the inhabitants of the south, who mingle its branches through the foliage of the elms around which they grow their vines. In temperate climates, i.e. below 44 degrees Celsius, they can be grown at any exposure; closer to the north, they can be grown at midday, planted on slopes that are sufficiently inclined to receive the sun's rays more or less perpendicularly. The consequences of this principle for pruning are very important. We know that the earth reflects the sun's rays and heat; that the closer an object is to its surface (when it is not damp), the more heat it experiences. Thus, in very hot climates, vines are cultivated at a high elevation, as they are in the south of France; in temperate climates, they are raised only a few feet, and in colder climates, such as around Paris, they are kept very low. 5° The lower the vine, the fewer buds are left on it; and this is the natural consequence of the principles we have just established. We have illustrated (pl. 11, fig. 6) a vine stock pruned as it should be in temperate countries, from 44 degrees to 46 degrees and beyond; but modifying the pruning according to exposure, resulting temperature and quality of soil: a, is the stem raised only a few inches; b, b, b, are three limbs that are lengthened as little as possible: in a very-favorable temperature and in excellent soil, four limbs can be kept; in cold climates and in mediocre soil, it would be better to have only two. c, c, c, etc., are fruiting branches pruned over two eyes; they are carried on very short spurs, which are often renewed. Each of these horns will provide two fruiting branches, the upper of which will be cut off at the next pruning, so as not to increase the number of spurs, which would otherwise be doubled every year. A vine stock such as the one shown here, if pruned and de-budded as described above, will produce twelve fruiting branches, each of which can easily provide two beautiful bunches; in total, twenty-four grapes, which would provide an enormous harvest of wine, if each vine stock in a vineyard produced as many. When trellising, follow the established principles, tying the sarmens to stiles, and thin out the leaves in season. II Pruning the Olive Tree. This precious tree can only be grown in the open air, and pruning is only customary for the first three or four years, after which it is left to nature to prune out dead, defective or misplaced branches. The result is that it often bears a large quantity of small, poorly nourished fruit, becomes exhausted, and remains sterile the following year. We believe that if it were subjected to regular, reasoned pruning, based on the laws of its vegetation, the balance would be restored in its harvests, which would be all the better for it, because the size and quality of the fruit would compensate sufficiently for the quantity, especially if we took the decision to multiply it by sowing and grafting. It can be treated in the same way as other stone trees. Fig tree pruning. We never try to give this tree a regular shape, because the large quantity of sap it loses every time we make an amputation makes pruning dangerous. In spring, just before sap flow, we simply remove dead and sterile branches. You should never cut more than half an inch above an eye, because mortality would overtake it, as in the vine, if you cut above it. For the same reason, a branch should never be felled by cutting it back to the trunk. When you want to raise it to a stem, you manage it like any other tree; but it's better to pinch it than to cut it, and even better to prune it. By subjecting it to the latter practice, we can train it without exhausting it too much. 008 Mulberry pruning. It is rarely pruned regularly, unless it is to form avenues, covered walkways, etc., in which case it is sheared (see page 173) as with other ornamental trees. If pruning is carried out, it is only to remove dead wood and useless branches, or to rejuvenate the tree when it is too old or its fruit has degenerated. In this case, the procedure is as described in the lopping article on page 169. Brusetrees can be pruned in the same way. Pruning and training currant bushes. This charming shrub (the species with bunches) lends itself with the greatest ease to any shape you wish to give it. Placed in a flowerbed between cattails, it can be trained into bushes, cattails, swirls and balls. Along a wall, it forms very pretty espaliers; finally, it can be used to make very pleasant palisades to divide flowerbeds and other small arrangements. Pruning takes place in February, and regardless of the regular shape you wish to give it, dead wood should be removed, and old branches that will soon cease to bear fruit should be cut back. Other pruning operations should be carried out according to the general principles of vegetation. Raspberry pruning. The raspberry tree only ever bears fruit on the previous year's wood, and this is what the gardener should focus on when pruning. When planted in suitable soil, it produces vigorous shoots every year, three or four feet long or more, which are invaluable for renewal. We select 009 the most vigorous of these young shoots, prune them by the end, and bend them into a half-bucket at the tip. In the upper third of their length, they will soon produce a number of small twigs covered with flowers, and later with fruit. When you have enough of these offshoots to line the palisade you're making with the raspberry bush, you remove to the ground the stems that bore fruit the previous year; if there weren't enough of them, you'd bring the old stems closer to the most vigorous bud, which you'd arch and trellis like the offshoots. Both are bent in the same direction and in a single line, to form the symmetrical figure of a hoop trellis. The offshoots, too small to bear fruit within the year, are pruned to fifteen or eighteen inches in length, in order to obtain fruitful buds for the following year. Pruning the Vinettier. Barberry is rarely pruned other than to form hedges that become impenetrable in a very short space of time. This is done with shears, as mentioned in the article on shearing. However, with a little care, and by simply removing branches that cause confusion, or those that are too vigorous and draw a large portion of sap to themselves, thereby destroying the balance of the vegetation, we can always succeed in increasing its yield. p08 PRUNING OF ENVELOPED FRUIT TREES. This division includes walnut, hazelnut and chestnut trees. As none of these trees have been subjected to a regular form, we will only deal with them in one article. Walnut is grown for both its fruit and its wood. To obtain the first result in the most advantageous way, it must be grafted, in order to increase the number, size and quality of the nuts. In the second case, we simply raise it from seed. If we wanted to obtain both wood and fruit quality, we would raise it from seed until its stem was formed, and then graft it at the top to form its head. Whichever way you decide, you raise the young tree as you would other fruit trees, that is, by pruning in hooks until the stem has reached the desired length; then you stop it: and, whether you graft it or not, you leave the tree to its nature. All that remains is to remove the dead branches, and to bring it closer together, by means of delimbing, when it becomes old and the stems are too long.

its branches begin to dry out at the tips. If you want top-quality wood, it's best to cut it down at this time. Chestnuts grow like walnuts, but as they form their head, they are prone to growing greedy branches, which must be cut back to preserve some regularity, as well as twigs and weak branches. As it begins to age, it often happens that the vegetation abandons the ends of the branches to a certain extent, in order to move to their insertion and develop suckers. The three branches 271 bend and become very good fruiting branches, with much larger chestnuts than before, though fewer in number. This operation can be repeated two or three times or more, until the tree dies of old age and exhaustion. Hazel remains a bush if left to grow freely, but if pruned for the first three or four years, it can easily be raised to a stem, and become quite a pretty tree. It can be treated in the same way as the two preceding trees, the only difference being that it needs to be cleaned of offshoots, which grow at the base much more easily than with the others. There are also some fruit trees, such as jujube, pistachio, etc., which we have not included in this treatise on pruning, because, at least in France, they belong more to the ornamental tree section than to the fruit tree section. However, if you were to care for a few of them, of whatever species, by directing them according to the general principles we have given under the name of precepts, you would be sure to succeed in bringing them to fruit, and to bend them into the shape you would like them to take if their nature did not oppose it. O soccece:cosceccccoceccc--e--c.e--o----e.c--.cee.c."-cecec-e--.-..c.caoDU JARDIN- POTAGER. IN dealing with this important branch of horticulture, we have had to consider it from all its points of usefulness; we have not, therefore, confined ourselves to teaching the cultivation of only those plants used in cooking, but of all those that enter into the planting of a vegetable garden... Aromatic plants, which do not belong exclusively to the field of medicine, but are of daily use in the domestic economy, naturally had to be included in our framework. Some trees and shrubs seem to belong more particularly to this division than to fruit trees: for example, the caper, whose flowers are preserved in vinegar before they blossom; the laurel, whose leaf is used to flavour stews; the cherry-cherry, which cooks so carelessly use to impart an almond-like flavour to boiled milk, etc. However, we have included them neither in our list of aromatic plants, nor in that of fruit trees. However, we haven't included them in either the vegetable garden or the orchard, because we would be repeating ourselves if we were obliged to describe them and teach their cultivation in the family histories that make up the final volumes of this work. Some of our readers may be astonished to find our vegetable plants arranged in alphabetical order, whereas most authors who have written before us on this subject have endeavoured to classify them methodically in the order of their asfinities, either in use or in cultivation. Our naive reply to this is that the task has been beyond our powers, and that we even believe that a rigorously analytical2. 18 208 rigorously analytical classification is impossible; our readers will perhaps be convinced of this, as we are, when they learn that the Jardin des Plantes in Paris has been looking for such a classification for over fifty years, without yet having been able to find a satisfactory one. Let us cite an example of the difficulties encountered at every step. Let's suppose that cabbage, the most widespread and useful plant in our vegetable gardens, has to keep us busy trying to find a classification for it. Have you adopted a method based on the use to which each plant is put? You'll find that 1° in kohlrabi, only the root collar is eaten; 2° in cabbage, only the leaves; 3° in cauliflower, only the stem and flowers; 4° in colza, only the seed is used to make oil. Will you include cabbage among plants with edible roots? edible leaves? edible flowers? etc.? Most vegetable plants fall into this category. But if you want to classify plants according to their cultivation analogies, the thing becomes even more impracticable; for the cultivation of the same plant varies according to the use you want to make of it, the time when you want to harvest it, the climates where you grow it, the quality of the soil where you tend it, and the variety to which it belongs. It has been suggested that vegetable plants should be grouped in Jussieu's order of natural families; but when this classification is attempted, such bizarre comparisons are made in terms of use and cultivation, that we are forced to abandon it. Besides, let's agree on one thing: a scientific classification of the vegetable garden is completely useless, and serves only to satisfy the mind without teaching the grower anything. What's more, it has the serious disadvantage of making research difficult in a book, and is impractical in the field. However, to satisfy I all classes of amateurs, and to show students the road on which all growers have been shipwrecked, without excepting my late friend the learned professor M. Thouin, we will give the table of its classification by order of use, then another table in which we have endeavoured to arrange vegetable plants in an order based on their cultivation analogies, and finally a third in which they are classified, month by month, according to the times at which they are planted or sown. CLASSIFICATION OF VEGETABLE PLANTS IN ORDER OF USE. FIRST DIVISION. PLANTS WHOSE LEAVES, RIBS OR STEMS ARE EDIBLE. FIRST SECTION. THOSE TO BE EATEN COOKED. 1°. Alone, or with seasonings. Mushroom, asparagus, hops, purslane, cardoon, celeriac, periwinkle, rhubarb, kohlrabi, cabbage, lettuce, chicory, sorrel, spinach, baselle, tetragone; 2°. Aszaisonnement. Mushroom, arrowroot, celery, chicory, tarragon, lettuce, cabbage, sorrel, parsley, periwinkle, purslane, fennel, savory, sage, chervil, leek. SECOND SECTION. THOSE TO BE EATEN RAW. 1°. In salads. Celery, chicory, lettuce, cabbage, watercress, lamb's lettuce, picridie; 2°. As a garnish. Celery, chervil, cornucopia, watercress, tarragon, mustard, trique-madame, stonewort, parsley, salad burnet, purslane, rocket, spilanthe, hallelujah, basil, cochlearia, mint, tansy-balsam, fennel (its young stalks blanched). SECOND DIVISION. THOSE WHOSE FLOWERS ARE EDIBLE IN WHOLE OR IN PART. 1°. Cooked. Artichoke, cauliflower, broccoli; 2°. Raw and used as a salad. Nasturtium, borage, bugloss. THIRD DIVISION. Squash, cucumber, tomato, pepper, melon; 3°. Those to be preserved in vinegar. Cucumber, melon, pepper. 1°. Those with floury seeds. Peanut, cowpea, broad bean, chickling pea, bean, lentil, mace, corn, pea, white vetch. 2°. Dry and aromatic seeds. Anise, coriander, fennel, mustard. FOURTH DIVISION. PLANTS WITH BULBS, ONIONS OR EDIBLE ROOTS. 1°. Those whose bulbs and onions are eaten. Onions, garlic, spring onions, chives, shallots, leeks; 2°. Those whose tubers are eaten. Orchis, Jerusalem artichoke, potato, nutsedge, truffle; 3°. Those whose roots are eaten. Potato, beet, carrot, parsnip, celeriac, cabbage, chervil, turnip, horseradish, raiponce, turnip, salsify, scorzonera. FIFTH DIVISION. AROMATIC PLANTS. 1°. Those taken as theiform infusions. Wormwood, ragweed, sage, lemon balm, angelica, hyssop; 2°. Those candied in sugar, or used to make liqueurs. Absinthe, angelica, anise, coriander, peppermint, fennel; 3°. Those used as spices or seasoning. Basil, coriander, cochlearia de Bretagne, fennel, savory, thyme, sage, anise, mint; 277 DU JARDIN 6°. Those distilled to prepare health-giving waters. FIRST SECTION. PLANTS THAT ARE PROPAGATED ANNUALLY FROM SEED. 1°. Those sown in water. Floating macer; 2°. Those sown and cultivated on soil. Mushroom, okra, melongene, melon; 3°. Cucumbers, pumpkins, tomatoes, hot peppers; (B. Ordinary exposure; plants that are mounded or tied to blanch, and from which only the leaves are harvested). Cardoon, celery, chicory, lettuce; (C. Ordinary exposure; plants that are not blanched.) Cabbage, basil; 4°. Plants sown in the open ground in furrows or broadcast. (A. Furrow sowing in very loose, very deep soil to facilitate root development). Turnip, rabe, beet, carrot, parsnip, chervis; (B. Broadcast sowing. Plant cultivated for seed only.) Mustard; (C. Regularly spaced sowing in furrows. Plants with a tendency to climb, most of which must be rowed). Bean, pea, lentil, chickling vetch, white vetch, broad bean, nasturtium; (D. Sow in furrows, lightly covered. Non-climbing plants, so do not row). In the vegetable garden, as throughout this book, we will indicate annual plants by this sign Q; biennials, by this one o; perennials, 209 by this other Z, and finally those that are woody, by this one D. ABSINTHE (GRANDE), ABSINTHE ROMAINE, ALUINE. (Artemisia absinthium; L.) 2. Indigenous. This aromatic plant is used every day; it is used to make liqueurs and is taken as an infusion in many circumstances. It thrives in all soils and withstands the harshest winters; but to preserve its full aroma, it must be placed in a warm location, where it lasts for fifteen to twenty years. It can be propagated from seed, but more easily from splinters or rooted shoots, which are separated and planted in autumn or spring. PETITE ABSINTHE or ABSINTHE PONTIQUE (artemisia pontica, L.) is native to Italy and the shores of the Black Sea. It is suitable for the same uses, and is cultivated like the other. GARLIC. (Allium sativum; L.) 2. this bulbous plant, like the following one, belongs to the Liliaceae family, originating in Sicily and southern France, and has been cultivated since antiquity. Little used in Paris and northern France, it is widely consumed in the south, in Italy, Spain and even Germany. However, the strong odor it imparts to the breath of those who have eaten it makes many people reject it, and almost everywhere it is relegated to the kitchen of the poor and the country dweller. Garlic grows well in all soils, but prefers those that are light and a little sandy, substantial and warm. It very rarely bears seed; more often than not, the pistil metamorphoses into soboles or bulbils, which are planted and treated like cloves, but only produce beautiful heads in the second year. If you were to collect seed by chance, sowing it would undoubtedly produce a more robust race, but you'd have to wait longer. The usual way to multiply this plant is to separate the cloves and treat them as we shall see. In the cold, damp lands of northern France, we plant towards the end of March and even the beginning of April, and we barely cover with soil to avoid rotting; in the south, we plant in November, and even better in February; in the vicinity of Paris, and in most of France, at the end of February or in March. You plant them in beds and rays, four or five inches apart in all directions, and one, two or three inches deep, or in borders, but in soil that has been loosened and amended, if necessary, with a vegetable fertilizer or at least one that is very well-consumed. They are left to grow in this way without any care other than weeding and destroying the weeds, and are removed when their leaves turn yellow and begin to dry out. Before putting them into botillons, they are left on the ground for some time: the soil separates from them, and the air and heat complete their ripening by removing the moisture which might otherwise cause the head to rot, especially if the botillons were not placed in a dry place to keep them until spring. SPANISH GARLIC, ROCAMBOLE. (Allium scorodoprasum; L.) 7. This species, less widespread than the preceding one, differs mainly in its less flat, almost cylindrical leaves, its tortuous stem and its stronger odor. Few gardeners can claim to have seen its seed; it is almost always replaced by bulblets, which serve to multiply it. It can be grown in exactly the same way as its predecessor, and is used for the same purposes. In medicine, the ancients attributed a large number of virtues to this plant, almost all of which have since fallen into disuse. ALLELUIA, OXALID, LITTLE CUCKLE, CUCKLOO (oxalis acetosella L.). 2 Found in shady woods throughout most of France. In the kitchen, this little plant is used only to make salad supplies; but in the arts, it is more important, because it is from it that sorrel salt is extracted. However, poor country folk eat it in the same way as sorrel, although it is much more acidic. It grows in good, light, shady soil, amended with leaf compost, and is propagated from seed in spring, or by bursting the roots. The roots are bulbous and much sought-after by mice, which must be protected. This plant also thrives in warm, open locations, but requires a lot of watering. AMBROISIE, CHENOPODE AMBROISIE ( chenopodium ambrosoïdes L.). O From Mexico. Its sweet scent makes it ideal for infusions. Sow in spring in any soil, but in a warm location. When the plant is strong enough, it can be transplanted if it has not been sown in place. All other care consists of weeding and watering as needed. CURNOW ANANAS ( bromelia ananas L.). 2 Found in India, Africa and South America. The fruit of this beautiful plant is one of the best known, and none can compare with it in terms of fragrance, not even the strawberry. As it is still little cultivated in the provinces, we feel we should give a brief description. The pineapple is a fat plant, that is, with a fleshy substance that is more or less succulent in all its parts, which means that humidity is extremely unfavorable to it, as it rots very easily. A thick, short stem is surrounded at its base by narrow, gutter-folded leaves, one to two feet long or even longer, with short, sharp spines at their edges; their color is a more or less glaucous green. The spike at the end of the stem is tightly packed and ovoid, with a large number of bluish flowers. These flowers are followed by red, yellow, purple or black berries, so tightly packed together that they appear to form a single fruit, roughly the shape and regularity of a pine cone. The stem extends into the interior of the fruit, ending at the top in a rosette or crown of short leaves. The pineapple has a strong but pleasant odor, unique to it and unrelated to any other fruit; its flavor is acidic and sweet, and the juice of the berries is so caustic that it partially oxidizes the blade of a knife implanted in it in a very short time. If eaten a lot, it can scald, but we've never seen it ulcerate the tongue or palate, as it's said to do. Several varieties are grown, differing in shape, color, size and fragrance. Here is a list of those that are the most successful in our gardens. 1° RED LEAF ANANAS (ananas rubra). Oval fruit, very elongated, same color as the foliage, turning a pleasant yellow when ripe; its berries are of average size, and we have seen as many as sixteen and eighteen on each spiral row, giving the fruit fifteen to sixteen inches in length. It has been cultivated for a very long time, but has remained rare, because it is more delicate and has fewer eyelets than the others. 2° AvANAS PITTE or GREEN (ananas viridis). Small fruit, 909 but much more fragrant than the larger species. Its ripeness can only be recognized by its very strong odor, and a slight yellowish tint. Still rare. 3° PINEAPPINES WITH LARGE PURPLE FRUITS (ananas macrocama violacea). Very large berries, few in number, which gives the fruit a rounded shape, acidic, not very fragrant, sometimes developing into seedlings of which we have noticed up to seven on a single apple, which greatly reduces its size. This very vigorous plant is not very hardy, because it can be multiplied by its seedlings; moreover, its beauty alone would be enough to make it cultivated as a pleasure plant. 4° ANANAS NOUVEAU A CROS FRUITS (ananas nova macrocarpa). We have recently been growing this new variety, whose fruit is said to grow so large that it sometimes weighs eighteen to twenty pounds. As ours have not yet borne fruit, we cannot guarantee this. Its leaves resemble those of the apple pineapple, but are larger and wider. 5° ANANAS GÉANT (pineapple gigantea). Its leaves, reaching four to six feet in length, are soft green, not covered with glaucous dust, deeply canaliculated, and with teeth spaced far apart. A stem two and a half to three feet long bears a fruit that is lemon-yellow when ripe, with probing flesh, sweet, but less fragrant than in other species; the berries, about an inch in diameter, sometimes number up to ten in each row, making the fruit nine to ten inches high. We recommend this beautiful plant to pineapple lovers, as it is less delicate and easier to grow than the others. 6° ANANAS DE LA PROVIDENCE (ananas providentialis ). This variety, rare in France and very common in England, has leaves slightly tinted violet when young, and broader than those of the other species. The fruit is very large, yellow when ripe, often weighing five to six pounds, and the flesh is not as fine as the others. 7° ANANAS EN PAIN DE SUCRE (ananas prramidalis). One of the *OCI most cultivated in France, because of the beauty and fragrance of its excellent fruit, elongated, yellow, with berries smaller than those of the reinette apple pineapple. 8° PYRAMIDAL ANANAS WITH PANCHED LEAVES (ananas prramidalis variegata). A sub-variety of the preceding one, to which it resembles the fruit, but its leaves are alternately striped red, green and yellowish. It is very rare. 9° -ANANANAS WITHOUT SPINES (ananas inermis ). Rounded fruit, f a pale saffron color, washed with pale violet, good, but a little stringy and tart. Rare. 10° ANANAS PEU ÉPINEUX (pineapple vix spinosa). Rounded fruit, whose berries are larger than the previous one, but have the defect of sometimes splitting when ripe; they are lemon-yellow. This pineapple is not very fragrant, and its flesh is a little stringy. It is still rare. 11° ANANAS PONIME DE REINETTE (ananas rotunda). Its fruit, one of the best, is rounded, with fairly large, yellow berries. Its leaves are whitish, gutter-shaped and not very spiny. It is generally cultivated. 12° PANCHED LEAF ANANAS (ananas variegata). The fruit of this sub-variety of the preceding one is slightly smaller, less fragrant, and never acquires such a brilliant yellow color. Leaves are variegated with white or yellow. Rare. 13° ANANAS NEGRE (pineapple nigra). Its fruit, initially very black, turns a little yellow as it ripens. It has the same shape as the pineapple pomine de reinette, but its berries are smaller and a little less fragrant. This variety is very rare. 14° ANANAS A FAUIT BLANC (ananas alba). Mentioned by Dumont de Courset and all his copyists, but we have never seen it. 15° ANANAS DE MONSERRAT (ananas flava). We have never seen its fruit. The compilers have still indicated a YELLOW ANANAS AFRUIT, pineapple aurea, because the above-mentioned author mentioned it; but as its name suits 2. 10 2000 almost all pineapples, we think that this variety duplicates one of those we have described; perhaps with the pineapple pomme de reinette, whose berries are golden yellow. Some gardeners in Paris claim that the pineapple has become somewhat acclimatized since it was first cultivated in France, i.e. in the last century or so. This is an error that no doubt stems from the fact that they began growing them with delicate varieties, which they have since replaced with more robust ones. As for me, having been brought up in the vegetable gardens of the Royal Gardens at Brunoy, where my father, who ran them for nearly 40 years, raised six thousand pineapple plants every year, I can confirm, from his experience and my own, that this plant is as delicate and as difficult to grow today as it was when it was first introduced to France. The first thing to take care of when growing pineapples is to obtain: 1° suitable soil; 2° pots of the right proportions; 3° frames; 4° a tarpaulin if you don't have a greenhouse; 5° layers; 6° a barrel, split on one side, which you place in one of the corners of the greenhouse or tarpaulin, and in which you leave the water to warm up for three or four days before using it. When water is used, it should always be at the temperature of the greenhouse, or at least at eight or ten degrees on a thermometer immersed in it. The most suitable soils for this type of cultivation are: 1° free soil; 2° amended garden soil; 3° heather soil. They are prepared a year in advance by placing them in piles, which are stirred and remade three or four times a year. The older they are, the better they are. The fertilizers you mix into them must be highly consumed; they consist of potting soils, 1° from 00 horses; 2° from cows; 3° from sheep; 4° from leaves; 5° in colombine; 6° in poudrette. These soils and potting soils should be placed in a shed, sheltered from the rain but exposed to all atmospheric influences. Mixtures should be made as described above, and never used as trophumides. However, if you don't have access to all these materials, you shouldn't give up on this interesting culture, as you could still succeed with a mixture, in equal portions, of good garden soil, horse potting soil and heather soil. If you didn't have the latter, you could replace it almost as advantageously with leaf compost. Pineapple pots, see pl. 12, fig. 1 and a, should be of a more elongated shape than the pots usually used for other plants; they should be no wider at the opening than at the bottom, which should be pierced with five round holes, and five slits around the perimeter, as shown in fig. 2. They should come in three sizes: the first will be from three to five inches in diameter; they will be used to hold eyelets, seedlings and crowns. The second will be seven to eight inches in diameter, to hold plants. The third will be eight to nine inches in diameter, and will be used for plants in good standing. For circumstances that may arise, it's a good idea to have some in between. A tarpaulin may suffice, at a pinch, to cultivate these precious plants and guide them from their first planting to the ripening of the fruit; but the result is that, as the fruit all ripens at the same time, there is a lot of it in one season, and a shortage during the rest of the year. So, as we've said, it's better to have frames, a tarpaulin and a greenhouse. 9O6 Here's how to build the tarpaulin. Choose a spot in the part of the garden where the soil will be the least humid, and open a trench four feet deep and seven and a half feet wide. Its length, from east to west, is indeterminate: it will depend on the number of plants you wish to grow. A wall one foot thick will be erected to support the walls of the trench, coy. pl. 12, fig. 3, A,A; it will be four feet high on the front, which will face south, B, and six and a half feet high on the back, i.e., on the north side, C; necessarily, the inside of the tarpaulin will be five and a half feet wide, which is the most advantageous proportion. On one of the sides facing east or west, there will be a small door, b, eighteen inches wide, reached by a staircase that will need to be covered with a roof, to divert rainwater, which would otherwise carry moisture into the tarpaulin. If we wanted to build a stove, which is essential for people who don't have a greenhouse, we'd build it according to the principles we taught in our first volume, and the pipe, H, would pass under the pathway. The tarpaulin is completed by covering it with glass panels, D, like the greenhouse. On the inside, an eighteen-inch-wide pathway, E, is cut in the front, leaving only four feet for the bed, F, which is separated from the pathway by a field brick, G, L, or by a three-foot-high plank partition. All that remains is to prepare the layers. These are made with warm donkey, horse or mule manure, mixed with old manure or hornbeam or oak leaves, depending on the circumstances. - 00 Beds are made from this, which are trodden and trampled on as much as possible to give them strength, and are raised, as indicated in I, I, to two-thirds or three-quarters of the height of the bed. We cover these beds with enough tan, K, K, to be able to push the pots into them, a, a, a, without their bottoms touching the manure. Sometimes new tan is used, i.e. tan that has not yet been used as a bed; sometimes a mixture of new and old is used. This depends on the circumstances described below. The frame will be six feet long and four feet wide. Its trunk will be proportionate to the height of the plants, and the inclination of the stained-glass windows will be the same as that of the tarpaulin we have illustrated. As for the hot greenhouse, as its shape is nothing special, we refer to its article in our first volume. MULTIPLICATION. Pineapples multiply by the offshoots that grow at the foot of old stems, by the crown that tops the fruit, and sometimes by the seedlings that the berries provide. To obtain sturdy offshoots, as soon as the fruit has been cut from old plants, the base should be cleaned of any heavy leaves that might shade the offshoots or drain them of sap; they should be left attached to the stump, and instead of removing it, it should be plunged back into the bed, which should be warmed by adding a third of new tan well mixed with the old. These old stumps should be managed in the same way as other plants, so that the eyelets are not separated until spring. However, this method, which increases cultivation costs, should only be used for shoots that are too young to be detached immediately; those that are strong enough should be removed with care; look to see if they have any 908 damaged parts, and if so, remove anything that is diseased, cutting to the quick; also remove the leaves closest to the collar, but only when they may interfere with planting. To detach the crowns, twist them as close as possible to the fruit, unite the wound by cutting away any tears the twisting may have caused, and then treat them in the same way as the eyelets. Place them in a dry, shady spot and leave them there for eight or fifteen days, more or less, until the wounds are perfectly dry; without this precaution, the plant would rot. PLANTING. Pineapples need to be planted twice: once to get them going again, and again to prepare them for fruiting. We assume that it will bear fruit in the second year; otherwise, if it is kept longer, it must be transplanted every year. It is never the age of a plant that determines whether or not it will bear fruit, but rather its strength. However, it is usually ready to bear fruit in the third year. Planting eyelets, crowns and seedlings. This is done at the time when the fruit is eaten, usually from June to October, or in spring for carnations that were too weak the previous year, i.e. from February to May. Pots are available from three inches in diameter up to seven, and are chosen in proportion to the size of the individuals. A bed of coarse sand or coarsely crushed oyster shells, from four lines to an inch thick, depending on the size of the pot, is placed at the bottom; a handful of cow potting soil mixed with a third of poudrette is thrown on top, then the pot is filled with a compound soil in proportion to the strength of the plants. For weak crowns, seedlings and O0 carnations, mix equal portions of soil and fertilizer as follows: 1° one-third frank soil, one-third garden soil and one-third heather soil; 2° for the fertilizer, half horse potting soil, one-quarter cow potting soil, one-quarter sheep potting soil, or leaf potting soil, or pigeon potting soil. For stronger grommets, two-thirds of the mixture will be frank soil, one-sixth garden soil and the same amount of heather soil. Fertilizer remains the same. Using all five fingers, we make a hole in the middle of the pot into which we throw very fine, very dry sand; into this sand we press the eyelets or crowns up to the birth of the first leaves, i.e. to a depth of one inch or fifteen lines, and we press the earth around the collar so that the plant is firmly held. The pots of young pineapples are placed in this layer, with the precaution of spacing them far enough apart so that they don't wither, and of placing them as close as possible to the stained-glass windows, which greatly helps their recovery; However, care must be taken to ensure that their leaves do not touch the glass, as this would cause them to receive the moisture that the vapour from the coating deposits against the glass during the night; the drops of water would run into the heart of the plant and cause it to rot. With the help of cloths and mats, which are removed or placed according to how hot the sun is, a temperature of eighteen to twenty-four degrees is constantly maintained until the plants recover. It's only twenty days after planting that we give them their first moderate watering; but before doing so, we need to make sure that the plants already have roots, because we never wet them before, unless the soil is absolutely dry. Watering is increased as the roots become stronger. In other words, once they have recovered, they should be given air, gradually at first, and then, once they have become accustomed to it, whenever the weather permits. When the sun is too hot, cover the frame with canvas or straw mats, otherwise the leaves will turn red; and once a plant has taken on this color, which is a sign of disease, it is very difficult to make it green again. Young plants should be protected from the sun until they recover. They should be treated as described above until mid-November, when they are taken out of the frame and transported to the tarpaulin or greenhouse. Planting trained plants. This is nothing more than a kind of repotting. It can be done month by month, from January 15th to April 15th, and is the best way to avoid bringing all the plants forward at the same time, and thus extend the fruiting season. Weak carnations from the previous year are also planted during this period. Formed plants can be planted in one of three ways: 1° bare-bottomed; 2° with roots; 3° with a root ball. For bare-bottom planting, the pineapple is unfolded, all the soil is shaken from the roots, and the roots are cut off at the collar, removing any parts that are rotten or too long or too woody. The plant is then exposed to the sun in a dry place to heal the wounds, where it is left for twenty-four hours to five or six days, depending on the degree of desiccation of the atmosphere. Pineapples that are to be planted with roots are removed and cleaned of all soil like the previous ones, but instead of cutting the roots, we simply remove those that are dead or diseased, and take care not to touch the others: they must be replanted immediately. Those planted in clods are removed from their pots, taking care not to break the clod of earth surrounding their roots, which must also be left intact. The top of the soil is peeled off, and the soil removed is replaced by a more substantial one. Rooted and mounded planting can only be used on pineapples that are already marking. The first of these three methods delays them considerably, the second less, and the third little. In all cases, before replanting, the leaves must be scrupulously cleaned of any garbage they may contain, and peeled to remove any that are rotten or dry, as well as those which, because of their poor position, would interfere with the planting. Lice and bedbugs are removed with a small flat stick, dropped into a vase and burned. Peelings containing any of these insects should also be burned, or at least removed from the garden. The cleaning operation should be carried out in a place far enough away from the tarpaulin so that the insects can't get inside. The leaves should not be washed with a sponge, as some authors say, as this would remove the glaucous dust that covers them, and the plants would suffer as a result. Instead, a fine rain should be sprayed on them, using a sprayer riddled with a very large number of small holes. The best specific measures we have used to prevent the damage caused by these extremely harmful animals are frequent basins, and a mixture of ash and sulphur flower sprinkled on the plant, which the humidity fixes on the leaves. O6 Pots are then chosen in suitable proportions, i.e. from seven inches to nine; they are filled as we have already said, and the pineapples are placed in them in the same way. If planting early, it is essential to sow the pots two or three days in advance in the tannin of the bed, in order to warm the soil with which they have been filled. Rooted and clodded plants are planted in the same way as other delicate plants. The latter should be watered moderately, if the soil is dry; if it is damp, watering should be dispensed with; but those planted with a bare root ball should be watered only after the roots have formed, as we have already said. A fortnight or three weeks in advance, a layer of one third new manure, one third old manure and one third dry leaves, well mixed and crushed, is prepared in the tarpaulin. We wait until the layer has lost its excessive heat before covering it with tan, in which we place the pots when it has reached the required degree of heat. To be sure, we probe the layer from time to time; if it is too hot, we run the risk of burning the young roots of pineapples that have not been planted bare-bottomed. So probe with a thermometer sunk a foot deep into the tan, and don't put the pots in until the heat has dropped to thirty or thirty-five degrees. The temperature of the tarpaulin must be maintained at twelve or fifteen degrees; and recovery will take place in twenty days. Stained-glass windows are covered with canvas while the sun is blazing, to avoid reddening. Every evening, until June, doormats are laid over the windows to prevent the cool night air from penetrating the interior, and light sprinklings are given from time to time without wetting the leaves. When the recovery is perfect, i.e. during June, July and August, we use the fine-hole sheaf fitted to the end of a syringe: and we water the whole plant to clean and refresh its leaves; we continue this operation until the first days of September, when we must begin to stop wetting the foliage. PINEAPPLE MANAGEMENT. This consists in giving them the care they need: 1° to keep them healthy and vigorous; 2° to bring them to fruit. To preserve them, they require two different types of management: summer and winter. Summer management. We have already described how to manage crowns, seedlings and carnations, from their planting to the first frosts, but only when they have been planted from July to August; those planted since spring can be managed in the same way as we have described for trained plants. Here's how to care for them in general. Whenever the weather permits, air is given. From time to time, we probe the layers to see if they retain sufficient heat. Moisture is prevented as far as possible from penetrating the tarpaulin, and if it does, plenty of air is given during the heat of the day to wipe it out. We keep an eye on the plants to destroy parasitic insects as they appear, and to promptly remedy those that are suffering. As soon as a pineapple is found to be diseased, it is stripped, its roots and neck scrupulously inspected, and any parts attacked by rot are removed to the bone. The wounds are left to dry, and the plants are replanted bare-assed. In August, a little earlier or a little later, depending on whether the layers have retained their warmth, all the pineapples are lifted to warm the layers. If this operation is carried out at the beginning of August or even earlier, half of the new tan is added, which is mixed perfectly with the old, and the heat is maintained for two and a half to three months; if they are renewed in the middle of the month or later, only a third or a quarter of the new tan is added, which keeps them warm for one and a half to two months. These changes must be made very quickly, so that the plants don't have time to suffer. If you fear that the layers will close too much, which could burn the roots, you should only push the pots in halfway until they have regained the degree of heat we have indicated. Winter management. At the end of September, or the beginning of October, choose a day when the sun's rays still have some warmth, and transport the pineapples from the frames and tarpaulin to a warm greenhouse, where they are given the same care as other delicate succulents: when you bring them in, take extreme care to drain them well. If you don't have a greenhouse, you can replace them in the tarpaulin on a new layer made as follows, and fifteen to twenty days in advance. Take new, dry, soft manure, mix in a third or half of dry leaves to capture the moisture, and prepare the layer very high, because it will sag quite a bit during the winter. Cover with a layer of new and old tan, half by half, which has been left to dry for some time in the sun, or by laying it out in a ventilated shed if bad weather has prevented it from being put outside. Water in such a way as never to wet the leaves, especially the heart of the plant, which would inevitably cause it to rot. To do this, use a watering can with a very long, very narrow neck, or a small, portable runner that you fit over each pot as you want to direct the water into it. As soon as the frosts start to make themselves felt and you can no longer give air to the plants on a daily basis, you stop watering them, and only give them water, in very small quantities, when the soil threatens to dry out completely. Two or three sprinklings are usually enough for one winter. Keep a thermometer in the tarpaulin, which should be consulted regularly twice every twenty-four hours, and which should be kept at eight or ten degrees for mature plants, and twelve or fifteen degrees for fruiting plants. If the warmth of the beds is not enough to maintain this temperature, turn on the furnace. In the Paris climate, there are often days during the winter when the thermometer rises to six, seven or eight degrees for a few hours. It's a good idea to take advantage of this to renew the air in the pineapples. Needless to say, during the coldest weather, the stained-glass windows will remain covered with a thick bed of litter, which will be removed and replaced whenever necessary. Every evening, until the spring planting, and even beyond, we spread this litter or straw mats over the glass. Training for fruiting. Any trained plant, whether one or three years old, can bear fruit within the year if cultivated accordingly. In March, a bed is prepared, either in a tarpaulin or in a frame, in the same way as we said for winter management, but the warmest manure is used, and it is covered absolutely with new tan. In this way, we keep it at a temperature of thirty to thirty-five degrees; and, if the layer were not sufficient for this, we would heat it by means of the furnace. It doesn't take long for the plants to start flowering. During the flowering period, we water from the neck up, scrupulously maintaining the same temperature level at twenty-five or thirty degrees; but when the flowering period is over and the fruiting is assured, we resume the sheaf watering that we were able to start in mid-May, with the precaution, however, of wetting the bed as little as possible so as not to cool it. When the season gets hot, and you can give air more often, you increase the number of sprinklings. Especially when you want to bring pineapples to fruit, it's essential to use lukewarm water in the greenhouse. When you see that the fruit is approaching maturity, i.e. a fortnight or three weeks ahead of time, you need to take care with the watering so as not to make them too watery and rob them of some of their fragrance. If you were afraid of having too many fruits at once, despite the precautions taken when transplanting, you could bring some forward and delay others with the greatest of ease. All you need to do is give fifteen degrees of heat to the ones you want to delay, shade them with straw mats during very hot spells, give them air more often, and place a few suction cups in the layer to make it evaporate some of its heat. If you want to bring them forward, do the opposite of what we've just said, and keep the temperature at eighteen degrees. You can tell when a pineapple is perfectly ripe by its color, and even more by its smell. ANGELICA (Angelica archangelica, L.) g. Grows spontaneously in the Alps, Austria and Lapland. It sometimes lives for three years. Its young stems and leaf ribs are preserved in sugar, and its seeds and leaves are used to make sugared almonds and liqueurs. It does well in all types of soil; however, it prefers free, substantial, moist soil, where it often reaches five or six feet in height. It is usually self-sown, but when brought into a garden, its seeds are thrown into the ground in March or September, with the precaution of covering them very lightly. If it has been sown in a nursery, it is transplanted into very loose, loamy soil as soon as the plant is strong enough; it is weeded a few times, especially when young, and watered in dry summers; if it has been sown in situ, it requires less care. As winter approaches, when its stems have dried out, we cover it with a little compost. In May or June of the second year, we begin to cut it back to the ground and bevel it, taking care to leave a stem to provide seed. ANISE, BOUcAGE. (Pimpinella anisum, L.) g. Native to the Orient. Only the seed is used, which confectioners use to make dragées called Anis in the trade; it is also used to make liqueurs. This plant likes light, warm, well-disturbed soil, in which to sow it in March or April, in beds or borders. Wet the soil to help germination, thin out the plant when it is strong enough, weed as needed and water in dry weather. The seed is collected in August from the stems, which are cut to the ground, left to dry in the sun for a few days and then beaten to detach them. The following spring, the plant sprouts new shoots that produce a new crop, and then perishes. The seed keeps well for three years. PEANUT, PISTÁCHE DE FERRE. (Arachys hypogœa, L.) O. of Mexico. Its seed, resembling a large bean, is eaten cooked in water or under hot ashes; it yields an excellent oil, comparable to the best olive oil. This plant was brought over about twenty-five years ago from the Spanish colonies, where it is widely cultivated; it was very successful in the Landes department, but the difficulty of disposing of its products, which are unknown in the trade, has almost generally led to its abandonment. - Peanuts do very well in light, sandy, warm soils; but the peculiar way in which they ripen their seeds will always prevent them from being grown in strong, compact or stony soils. After flowering, the pods incline towards the ground, sink into it, grow and reach their half-mature point. As it grows quite rapidly, we think it could be grown advantageously in all parts of France where corn ripens. In spring, when the soil is warm enough to germinate the seeds entrusted to it, we prepare and loosen the soil as well as possible, and at a good exposure; we amend it, if necessary, with well-consumed potting soil, or even better with plant debris, well decomposed and matured in piles for several years. Sow by broadcasting if you're growing in a large area; but in a garden, it's better to make furrows two inches deep and one foot apart. Throw the seeds in one by one; at fifteen-inch intervals, cover with soil and compost, and water lightly. To hasten germination, soak the seed in water for forty-eight hours before sowing. To make it easier for the pod to sink in, the soil should be kept very loose, and hoeing carried out a little before flowering. Weeds are weeded and removed. Harvesting is very easy; all you have to do is pull the kernels out of the ground, with or without the pod. ARROCHE DES JARDINS, IRIBLE, BONNE-DAME, BELLEDAME, FOLLETTE, CHOU D'AMOUR ou D'ARMOU. (Atriplex hortensis; L.) Q. Native to Tartary. This plant is rarely grown in gardens, except to sweeten the acidity of sorrel with its insipidity. There are three varieties; the first has yellowish 00-green leaves, the second pale red and the third dark red. All three grow to three or four feet, or more in good soil, and have broad, toothed, triangular leaves. As it is usually used in the kitchen as a substitute for pear, it should be sown in early spring, to be enjoyed before the latter produces its leaves. It thrives in all types of soil, but requires some watering in dry, light soils. In any case, it requires no further care, and usually reseeds itself. Its seed is very light, and would be easily scattered by the winds if we waited until it was ripe before picking it; we must therefore cut the stems as soon as the first seeds ripen, dry them in a sheltered place, and shake them on a cloth to collect the seeds. Some authors, struck by the speed with which arroche grows, have suggested that it can provide green fodder at the start of winter and in spring. It remains to be seen whether livestock would do well with its stalks, which harden quickly. COMMON ARTICHOKE. (Cynara scolymus, L.) 7. From Barbary. Everyone knows the use of this beautiful plant, of which several varieties are cultivated. The most widespread and the best are: 1° The GREEN one, of medium size, a little flattened at the top, with very open leaves or scales (or leaflets of the involucre). It is quite tender, but its cultivation has been almost entirely abandoned in favor of the following variety. 2° GROS VERT DE LAON, the most widely grown around Paris, and the best of all. It resembles the previous variety in shape, but with proper cultivation in good, well-watered soil, it becomes very large, reaching up to five inches in diameter. 3° GROS CAmUS DE BRETACNE, earlier, flatter and 2. 20 009 a few months wider than the previous one, but paler green. We owe this variety to M. Féburier of Versailles. 4° Le BLANC, even earlier than the previous one, quite tender, but very small. Not only is it flattened, but the middle of its head is even sunken; its scales end in a sharp, prickly point. This variety has become very rare. 5° The VIOLET, of mediocre size, early, with green scales, washed with violet-red at their tips, and fitted with a spine. Its shape is more elongated. It is most commonly grown in the provinces. It is excellent raw, eaten with pepper. 6° ROUCE, thought to be a sub-variety of the previous one. It is purple-red on the outside, but has a yellow heart. Its shape is the same as that of the violet, but it is much smaller. Eaten raw, it is the most delicate of all when young; if left to grow, its flesh becomes hard and indigestible. 7° GENESE SUGAR. Like the red, it is only good raw, but is even more delicate. It is very small, bristling with spines; its flesh is yellow, and its scales pale green. We don't grow it much, because it degenerates quickly, and we have to bring in carnations from Genoa every two years if we want to have it at its best. Artichokes like open, deep soil. They can be propagated from seed, but as the varieties do not keep well by sowing, it is customary to propagate them by eyelet. We will describe how to operate and cultivate in both cases. Propagation by sowing. To obtain seed, the best artichoke heads are left to flower, with care taken to prevent moisture from penetrating the scales and rotting the seeds; to do this, they are sheltered by some means during the rain, or they are simply tilted little by little and secured in this position, so that water runs down the sides without penetrating to the receptacle. When you see them about to detach themselves, you cut off the heads and squeeze them in a dry place; thus preserved on their receptacle, the seeds remain good for seven or eight years. In the climate of Paris, good seeds are only available in warm years, but in more southerly countries they are available every year. In February or March, prepare a warm bed; sow in pots or on compost, and cover with a frame; in May or June, transplant the young plant in the open ground, as we shall say for carnations. If sowing in the ground, wait until late April or early May. After ploughing and loosening the soil, and giving it the fertilizer it needs, line up the furrows and throw the seeds in fairly close together, as it would be better to have to thin out the plant than to run out if not all the seeds sprouted. We cover the furrows with half an inch of soil, and finish filling them with an inch of compost. Water, weed if necessary and, when the young plants are strong enough to withstand the trausplantation, carefully lift them and treat them like eyelets. If you want to sow in place, work the soil accordingly, i.e. break it up and amend it with good fertilizer in autumn, and plough and loosen it in spring before sowing. Three feet apart in all directions, make four or five small holes one inch deep and two or three inches apart, place a seed in each one, cover with potting soil and water; when the plant has risen, leave only one in each place, and pull out the others to transplant where they are missing. Plants sown in pots almost always bear fruit in autumn, and this happens quite often to those sown in the ground, especially when the year has been 1 hot and they have not been given enough water. This method of propagation would therefore be advantageous, because the artichokes are more vigorous; but unfortunately one runs the risk of having only mediocre or even bad fruit, after having chosen the best seed. The safest thing to do is to obtain carnations of a good variety, and plant them as we shall say. Multiplication by eyelets. As soon as the artichokes have grown sufficiently to be easily distinguished from the mothers destined to bear fruit, which usually happens in April, we dig up the stalks a little, but without penetrating to the roots; then, passing the hand or a wooden pallet between the stump and the sucker, we detach the latter without injuring it; we remove all those which are there, good and bad, to rid the mother and prevent a loss of sap detrimental to the size of its fruit. We choose from among these carnations those whose neck is formed by a kind of bead called a nut, and especially, by preference, those which have already put down some roots. The others are discarded as being of dubious quality. This operation, as well as planting, can also be carried out in September; but artichokes that are too young are more sensitive to frost, and rot more easily in wet winters. If you don't already have artichokes, you can buy carnations at the market or elsewhere, but be careful to choose them as we have said, as those without nuts don't produce until four or five months later, when you manage to get them to recover. Once the soil has been prepared, as for sowing in place, choose a rainy day and plant the eyelets two and a half feet apart in slightly thin soils, and three feet apart in those where vigorous vegetation is expected. Place two of them six inches apart, so as to always have a well-stocked square in case some of them die. Care should be taken not to bury them too deeply, to avoid rotting, and to press the soil tightly around the nut, to fix them firmly in the ground. Only cut off the tips of the leaves when the plants have suffered, and water as necessary, sheltering them for a few days from the sun's scorching rays. If spring frosts are still to be feared, they should be protected with a large bedding plant. Cultivated, weeded and watered sufficiently, a good proportion of these carnations will bear fruit in autumn, and all will bear abundant fruit the following spring. Artichokes fear cold and rot to about the same degree, which makes them very difficult to store over winter. In dry soils, it's enough to butter the plants and cover the leaves with dry leaves or litter, but this isn't enough in wet soils. In wet soils, you need to open gutters between each row of artichokes, and use the soil removed to butter the plants. Cover the entire square, with the exception of the gutters, with a layer of dry manure, which is made thicker around each plant; taking care not to put the manure in contact with the plants, which are placed as if at the bottom of a funnel: cover this funnel with dry litter during frosts, and comb the straw from the litter so as to keep rainwater away from the plants. Whenever the weather gets milder and the sun comes out, we remove the litter and replace it before dark. No matter how you manage the artichokes during the winter, it's rare that they haven't turned a little white. To prevent them from being caught by the sun's rays, they should be gradually uncovered and sheltered from the sun until they have regained their normal color: the covers should not be removed entirely until the slightest frost is no longer to be feared. In the south of France, particularly around Montpellier, when an artichoke head has reached a quarter or a third of its size, the stalk is split five or six inches below the head, and a piece of wood is inserted from one end to the other, holding the two halves of the stem apart. It is claimed that this method considerably enlarges the apple. Artichoke leaf ribs can be used in place of cardoons, and are even more delicate; thus, when you want to destroy a square, you can still obtain this product by delaying its destruction for three weeks, which is enough time to blanch the ribs by treating them absolutely like cardoons. See this article. COMMON ASPARAGUS (Asparagus officinalis; L.) g. Native. Everyone knows how this plant is used. In the past, there were three varieties: large, medium and wild, but these distinctions are completely arbitrary, and all the more ill-founded in that only the more or less careful cultivation of this excellent plant can produce these differences, which are not transmitted by the seed, or even by the roots. The same is true of the Dutch or white asparagus, the violet and the green, which some people still mistakenly regard as three varieties. Asparagus can be propagated in two ways: by sowing and by planting their roots, known as pates or claws. They like light, substantial soil. Sowing. They can be sown in nurseries or in the field. For the former, prepare the soil by ploughing, mixing fertilizer, hoeing and loosening.
