In October or November in countries where winter is not severe, and when sowing in dry, light soil, in March or April in the Paris area and in heavy, damp soil, we broadcast the seeds and cover them with half an inch of compost. The young plant is raised after forty days, weeded and watered as needed, and thinned out if too thick. In autumn, cut off all stems and cover the seedling with three inches of compost or light soil. The following year, the same care is taken, if the plant has not already been uprooted in spring and replanted in situ. Sowing in place is done in a completely different way. The ground is prepared in the same way as for planting, and in the places marked for the claws a small hole one inch deep is made, in which four or five seeds are sown and covered with compost. As soon as the plant has sprouted, we pull out part of it, so as to leave only one plant in each spot. The first year is treated like a seedling in a nursery, and the following years like a plantation. This method is advantageous, because it produces stronger asparagus; but you can't start cutting them until a year later than those planted. Planting. This is done in March for the Paris climate, earlier or later in warmer or colder countries, in beds or squares, prepared the previous autumn. The first method is more advantageous when you have a large plot of land, and the second is more suitable for small gardens. We'll describe them both below. Before planting, if you haven't sown your own seedlings, it's a good idea to get some claws. They should be selected as freshly plucked as possible, and preference should be given to those that are two years old. Younger plants suffer more from replanting, especially when they have to travel; older plants do not do well. The best ones have long, loose, white roots. Before planting, they should be carefully inspected, and any damaged or fractured roots cut off. For planting in beds, four-foot-wide beds are drawn along the length of the land. The first will be dug into a pit, and the soil from the excavation thrown over the second; the third will also be dug, the fourth will form an ados like the second; and so on. Each pit is given a depth of two feet in dry land, and three in wet; this excess is filled with rubble, plaster, gravel and other materials capable of sanitizing the soil and allowing water to run off easily. A foot-thick bed of cow manure, or other good fertilizer, is thrown into the bottom of the pit, and well tamped down so that it sinks as little as possible. We prepare the soil from the excavation by passing it over a rack, and mixing it with much-consumed fertilizers, of which we will use a greater or lesser proportion, depending on whether the soil is stronger or weaker, as the essential thing is to make it light and substantial. A three-inch-thick bed is spread out and raked together. Four or five months in advance, you draw three equally-spaced lines with a chalk line, and place the claws in staggered rows, fifteen or eighteen inches apart, with the precaution of placing them on a small mound of potting soil, so as to elevate the eye and spread the roots by tilting them a little. The whole bed is then covered with three inches of soil prepared in the same way as the one below, and taken from ados that can be grown as salads, supplies, etc. We water, pull out the weeds and, in November, after cutting the stems, we cover with a new layer of soil three inches thick. The following spring, hoe carefully to avoid cutting the roots, weed and water. When autumn arrives, we cover with three or four fingers of good, well-consumed manure, which we bury the following year by hoeing in spring and reloading in autumn with two or three inches of soil. Each asparagus claw lasts only three years, but a new one forms during this interval, and always above the old one it is intended to replace; so that, if you don't take the precaution of replenishing the soil each year, it will soon be at the surface, unable to form any more, and the plant will be lost. If you want the asparagus to be constantly beautiful for the duration of the asparagus plantation, i.e. for fifteen or twenty years, take the precaution of giving it a layer of good, well-consumed manure every two years, buried as we have said. In cold, compact soils, horse manure mixed with a little colombine or poudrette is ideal; in hot, dry soils, cow manure is preferable. Leached ashes work very well in all soils. An asparagus plant begins to produce in the third year; but this first time, only the largest asparagus should be cut, leaving all the others so as not to exhaust the plants. In the fourth year, more asparagus is cut, and by the fifth they are in full production. Always cut with great care, so as not to injure the young shoots still hidden underground. 10 Use a knife with a blade curved at the end and a saw-toothed edge. Once the asparagus pits are level with the ground, they should not be filled with new soil each year; simply remove a three- or four-inch layer every year, and replace it with another mixed with suitable fertilizers. Planting in squares has, as we have said, the advantage of producing a greater number of asparagus plants, because, as there are no ados, all the land can be put to good use. As early as August, prepare a square of indeterminate size, using manures suited to the soil type. If the soil is hard or compact, we try to make it light by mixing in leached ashes, or sand, or even better, if available, heather soil. In the following month of March, the soil is divided into planks five feet wide, and a spade-head of earth is removed from the top of the first plank and thrown on top of the second plank; by this means, the second plank has already been dug eight to nine inches; the trench continues to be opened until it is two feet deep in dry ground, and two and a half feet deep in wet ground. The soil from this excavation is taken to a storage area. If two and a half feet have been dug in wet soil, a six-inch bed of gravel or coarse sand is placed at the bottom of the pit; in both cases, a layer of consumed manure, or any other fertilizer suitable for growing asparagus, is placed so as to replace the soil from the second dig, i.e. the pit is filled to within eight inches. The remaining eight inches will be used for planting in the same way as for the planks, i.e. first put four inches of the soil from the second plank, place the claws, and cover with the remaining four inches of the same soil. Even though it was prepared the previous autumn, we'll still add some new fertilizer. The first bed thus planted is level with the ground. We move on to the second bed, which we treat in the same way, throwing the soil on top of the third. When it's planted, you move on to the third, then the fourth, and so on to the end of the square. Treatment and cultivation are absolutely the same as for bedded asparagus. In the first year, the fertilizer settles sufficiently for the square to be reloaded with three inches of soil without heightening it; but this will not be the case in subsequent years, so the soil should be loaded as little as possible, i.e. by two or three fingers each time. The soil to be used for this purpose should be excavated and mixed with the potting soils indicated. It sometimes happens that a well-exposed asparagus plant begins to show young shoots as early as April, and that late frosts can surprise them. You can avoid this inconvenience by throwing down a large amount of bedding when you realize that the atmosphere is getting cooler. Asparagus is cut as soon as it begins to appear, and continues to be harvested until July, when cutting is stopped to avoid exhausting the plants and causing them to perish. However, from the first days of May, the most beautiful stems should be left if you want to harvest seeds; as they develop, you keep an eye on them to protect them from accidents that could harm them. The asparagus beetle, for example, is an insect that will eat them away completely if they are not protected from it. This is why we look for it and its black eggs, symmetrically arranged on the stems, and crush them. It is said that a few feet of hemp sown near asparagus plants keep these insects away, because they fear the smell. The larvae of the beetle, commonly known as the white worm, attack their roots: as soon as this is noticed, a few lettuces are sown across the beds; the larvae, which are very fond of them, leave the asparagus to attack it; and as soon as the leaves are seen to wither, a search is made at the foot of the plant, the worm is found and killed. When the asparagus seed is ripe, it turns a beautiful red; then the stalks are cut and the berries removed, which are left in a heap for two weeks to ripen. They are then crushed with the hands, and washed in a vase filled with water; the seeds sink to the bottom, where they are collected to dry in the shade and air. The asparagus with the best reputation are those from Besançon, Marchiennes, Mons, Vendôme, Gravelines, Ghent, Saar-Louis, Strasbourg, Darmstadt, Ulm, etc. These are the cities where asparagus is grown. These are the cities from which the best seeds and plants are obtained. PRIMEUR. Forced asparagus, i.e. asparagus grown at a time other than that indicated by nature, has little flavor and cannot be compared in any way to spring asparagus. However, it is sought-after because it is rare, and especially in winter, when little greenery is eaten. There are several ways of forcing asparagus; we're going to teach those that are both the safest and the least expensive. Asparagus can be forced on the spot or on a bed, with the same results; but those obtained on the spot cost less than the others, and the plants can be used several times; we therefore advise giving preference to this method. Forcing in situ. Whether you want to sow or plant, you divide your land into plots four feet wide, or only three feet wide in cold countries. A pathway two to two and a half feet wide is left between each plank. We plant with two-year-old claws, or sow exactly as we said, but space the plants less; we put them in three or four rows a foot apart, which leaves six inches on the sides, and ten inches apart along the length of the rows. For the first three years, the plantation is managed in the same way as any other. It's only when the asparagus are in full production that you can start heating them, if you don't want to risk losing your asparagus plant in a short time, which would happen if you exhausted the plants in their early youth. From November to March, depending on whether you want to have asparagus earlier or later, you dig the paths around the beds, eighteen to twenty inches deep and across their entire width. If you have portable frames, you remove the soil from the excavation; but if you don't have one, you throw it on the boards, so as to cover them by at least eight to ten inches. The earth around the edges is beaten to hold it in place, and the trenches are filled with warm horse manure, well trodden and trampled, raised five or six inches above the level of the plank, which is itself raised by the earth from the path. Six inches of hot, dry manure are placed on top of the plank, and a bed of dry litter is placed on top. The soil soon warms up, and after a fortnight or three weeks the asparagus appears. The manure is lifted and cut, then the trail stoves are reshaped, or even remade with new manure if the first has lost its heat. Every two - days we visit and cut, and every two weeks we remake the stoves. If it's a rainy winter, we change the manure covering the bed whenever it gets wet; but if it's only snowing, we can be satisfied with replacing only the bedding cover. When the weather permits, the manure cover is lifted to give the plants air; but when, on the contrary, the cold is very intense, its thickness must be increased. A bed treated in this way can produce asparagus for a month and a half or two months, after which time all the manure must be removed, the soil put back into the paths, and the asparagus left to nature's care. When spring arrives, they will grow back weakly; the following year they will recover, if not cut back, and you can start heating them again in November. In this way, a board can be heated every two years, and therefore produce alternately every other year, for eight to ten years. Those who have frames will modify their cultivation as follows: when digging paths, soil will be removed instead of being thrown onto the beds. Stoves will first be raised to ground level, then the frames will be placed, and then the stoves will continue to be raised against their chests. Only a light bed of very dry manure should be placed on the ground beneath the frames. Whenever the weather permits, air should be provided; and at night, or when it's freezing, the glasses should be covered with thick straw mats or heavy bedding. Forced bedding. At the same times as for the other on-site cultivation, we prepare layers of warm manure, eighteen to twenty inches high and four or five inches wide. When they are at their hottest, they are covered with ten inches of compost 1, to which one third of light soil has been mixed. On top of this, six inches apart, we place claws aged four years or more, on top of which we spread a two-inch bed of the same compost, and on top of this a little hot manure. Leave the bed in this state for a few days, and when you think the asparagus are about to start growing, remove the manure and replace it with three inches of new compost, after which the frames are placed. The heat of the bed is renewed by means of stoves, which are changed every time they are needed, and the frames are governed as described above. After twelve or fifteen days, the asparagus begins to produce, and we cut for as long as it lasts, i.e. for about a month, more or less. When the asparagus stops producing, the layers are destroyed and the claws discarded, as they are burnt and useless. Old claws are used for this purpose, when they can be obtained by destroying used asparagus plants. Asparagus obtained in this way lacks not only fragrance, but also color. The latter is restored once they have been cut, by tying them into bundles and burying half of them in the manure of the stoves, and covering them with a transparent cloche. Light turns them green in two or three days, especially if it's sunny. BASELLE ROUGE, BASELLE BLANCHE, ÉPINARD DU MAlabar (Basella rubra and Basella alba; L.) g. Indies. These two climbing plants, with thick, large, oval leaves, are excellent substitutes for spinach, because they have the same qualities and produce more. They are grown as annuals, as they do not like the cold. In March, they are sown on a warm bed, under a bell or frame, in light soil amended with 11 compost, either in small pots or on the bed itself. In May, or when late frosts are no longer a concern, they are transplanted to the ground, at the foot of a south-facing wall, against a trellis where their long white or red stems can cling. Weed them and give them plenty of water during hot spells. When the leaves are harvested, those of the most vigorous plant are left to provide the seeds that will ripen perfectly during the year. In the space of a summer, each plant can provide three or four dishes. COMMON or KITCHEN BASIL. (Ocymum basilicum; L.)O. Native to Persia. It and its large-leafed, curly-leafed or purple varieties are used in seasonings, and rarely as a salad ingredient, because its strong, aromatic flavor is not to everyone's taste. Some people cut it before flowering, dry it in an airy place, grind it to a powder and store it in a tin for use with spices. In the Paris climate, basil is sown on a bed in February and March. When the young plant has six leaves, it is planted out in soft, terra-cotta soil, in a warm spot; it is protected from the sun's rays until it is fully established, and is given copious watering throughout. In the south of France, it can be sown in the open ground, but only in April or May. The seed ripens from August to October, and the first to ripen should be harvested first, as autumn rains frequently abort those of September and October. BETTERAVE COMMUNE (Beta vulgaris; L.)g. This plant, native to southern Europe, is all the more valuable today as the sugar produced by the factories it supplies competes favorably for price and quality with that imported from the colonies. Everyone knows about its nutritional properties. It has produced several varieties, the main ones being: 1° GROSSE ROUGE, the most widespread and the oldest cultivated. It is blood-red both inside and out, and quite elongated in shape. 2° PETiTE ROUCE, formerly known as Castelnaudari; smaller than the previous variety, with the same color and milder flavor. It is also much earlier, and can be harvested as early as August. 3° ROUCE RONDE; of the same quality and color as the previous one, also early, but rounded. 4° JAUNE; same shape and color as the large red, but much sweeter in flavor. 5° La BLaNCHE (large and small); more tender, more delicate than the others, but a little less decisive in taste. 6° CHAMPÈTRE or starvation root; now grown almost exclusively as fodder. Beets like deep, well-developed, warm, light soil, and smoked the previous year, or at least with well-consumed compost. However, they do well in all types of soil, provided they are well loosened and amended. After two good ploughings, to a depth of at least one foot, they are broadcast, in rays or in borders, in March and early April in warm soils, in April and May in other soils; they germinate in ten or twelve days, if the season is favorable. However, it's wise to sow a little late, so that they don't have time to bolt before autumn. When the plant has six leaves, thin it out and replant in places where it is lacking, taking care to leave the roots intact. Arrange the plants so that they are spaced from one foot to eighteen inches apart, depending on the 2. 21 ★08 quality of the soil. Weed, hoe two or three times, and water deeply if necessary. We should point out that this plant fears transplanting, and that consequently, when transplanting, it should be kept out of the ground for as little time as possible, taking advantage of rainy weather to do so. Towards the end of October, or at the beginning of November, we pull them out of the ground; we cut off the leaves that can be fed to livestock, we clean them of the soil attached to them, we leave them to dry out in the air for twenty-four or forty-eight hours; and, when their surface has lost all its moisture, we clamp them in a dry place away from the slightest frost. Although they fear rotting, they keep quite well in a healthy cellar. We leave some of the most vigorous ones in the ground to obtain seeds, taking care to protect them from the cold by means of dry leaves covered with litter to prevent rain from penetrating and rotting them. Despite these precautions, we don't always succeed in preserving them. In this case, in February or March, we replant a few of those removed in the autumn. As the stems develop, they are supported with stakes; and when the seed begins to ripen, the stems are cut and hung in a dry, airy place. When they are completely dry, we collect the seeds, which are good for two or three years. BOURRACHE COMMUNE (Borrago officinalis, L.) O. It is more widely used for cooking in Italy than in France. Here, only its blue, white or purplish flowers are used to dress salads. It grows well in all types of soil, and can be sown at any time. Its fine seed doesn't need to be covered much with soil, and if you want the plant to rise 08 quickly, all you have to do is give it a few sprinklings. In any case, it is quite self-sowing. To collect its seeds, cut the stems a little before maturity, dry them on a cloth and then collect the seeds, which will detach themselves as they ripen. BUGLOSA OR BUGLOSA OFFICINALE. (Anchusa ossi cinalis; L.) 2. native to France, this plant is cultivated more for the medicinal virtues attributed to it, than for cooking, where only its flowers are used to garnish salads. It can be grown in any soil, provided it has been well tilled. It is propagated from seed, which is sown in March, or from offshoots, which are separated and planted in spring and autumn. Its seeds are collected like those of borage. CAPUCINE (GRANDE), CESSON OF THE INDIES, MEXICO, PERU (Tropœolum majus; L.) 7. From Peru like the following, PeTiTe CAPUCINE ( tropœolum minus; L.) Z, which differs only in its smaller size. The flowers of both are used to pear salads. The flower buds and seeds, picked long before maturity, can be preserved in vinegar and used in place of capers. Although these two plants are perennials in their own countries, they are grown here as annuals, because their seeds ripen well during the year. They can often be resown on their own, but it's safest to sow them in April, in clean, light, substantial soil with a warm exposure. If they don't climb a trellis, the first seed is raked out, and frequent watering is given during hot spells. The seed is good for two or three years. CARDON, CARDE (Crnara cardunculus, L.) 2. native to Barbary, this plant has long been cultivated in France, although it is rather delicate and fears the cold. Its leaf ribs and root are eaten cooked. Its flowers, like those of the artichoke, are used to curdle milk. We have obtained several varieties, which we will list in the order of their age. 1° CARDON COMMON or Spanish; thornless, with thin, slightly hard, white ribs. It climbs very quickly. 2° CARDON DE TOURS; extremely thorny, with wider, more delicate, slightly reddish ribs. Rises less quickly. 3° FULL AND SPINELESS CARDON; has the same qualities as the preceding one, but its white ribs are not armed with prickles. 4° CARDON WITH ARTICHAL LEAVES; an excellent variety with very smooth, thornless ribs. Cardoons multiply only from seed; they like light, deep soil, and frequent watering during hot spells; moreover, their cultivation is very similar to that of artichokes. In April and May, in perfectly cleared, amended and loosened soil, dig holes eighteen inches wide and twelve or fifteen deep, three feet apart, and keep the soil around the holes, filling them with well-consumed horse potting soil mixed with a little poudrette or colombine. Three or four seeds are sown an inch deep, leaving only one plant when they germinate, and water frequently until the plant has developed enough to be blanched, which usually happens in October. Then we mound it, bringing the soil closer to the diggings, and bind it with wicker or rushes, so that we can cover it with a kind of thick cap of long litter, which we tighten around the leaves, taking care not to injure them. 1008 After a fortnight, the rib is white and must be eaten, or it will rot. This is a good indication that you should only butter and cover plants as needed. When frost approaches, remove the rootballs and place them either in a dry, well-ventilated greenhouse, or in trenches if you don't have a greenhouse. In the first case, all the leaves are lifted and bound, but loosely, so that air can circulate inside the plants, which are then buried in fresh sand. Whenever possible, air is given, and it's not uncommon to keep them this way until spring. If you have little space to devote to them, simply lean them against a wall; but then you must take care to visit them often, to remove any rotting leaves. Whichever way you place them, they will blanch without covering, and the most advanced ones will be chosen for consumption. People who don't have a greenhouse open a trench in dry ground, three feet deep, four wide and of indeterminate length. They place a bed of straw against the sides of the trench, i.e., a good thickness of long straw laid upright; they lean one or two rows of cardoons against this bed, then put a new bed of straw against them, a second row of plants, and so on until the trench is full. Note that the ends of the leaves must be left exposed to the air. The pit is rigorously protected from the slightest rain, and the plants are covered with a large layer of dry litter, which is removed whenever it doesn't freeze. To obtain seed, we leave a few plants in the ground; when the cold comes, we cut off all the leaves a few inches from the ground, and we gap0e rantitate the plautes from frost, as we said for artichokes, but with much more care, because they are more sensitive to it. The seed is collected in the same way, and retains its germinative powers for more than ten years. Plants that have produced seed are no longer good for blanching, but they are no less valuable because, being less sensitive to cold, they can produce seed for six or eight years without requiring much care. Primeur. From January onwards, seed is sown in pots in a warm layer under a frame. When the plant has two leaves, it is placed in a larger pot and on a new layer; finally, when it is strong enough, it is placed on a dull layer, covered with a foot of compost, mixed with a quarter of good soil. We cover with the frame, and in May we can already blanch for consumption. As they are usually quick to bolt, in March we transplant some of the plants sown in January into the open ground, shelter them from the cool nights with cloches, and manage them like those sown in the open ground. They start to bind well in June and July, but many bolt. CARROT, PASTONADE, PASTENADE. (Daucus carotta; L.) g. This southern European plant has been cultivated since antiquity. Its uses in cooking are well known. It has produced several varieties. 1° JAunE; the most widespread of all. It is elongated in shape, or rounded in the yellow-round sub-variety. It is still perhaps the best of all, despite the new acquisitions made in recent years. 2° The WHITE; less cultivated and less good. Its shape is elongated, and like the previous variety, it has a white-round sub-variety. d 3° COURTE DE HOLLANDE; rounded in shape. It is highly prized. 4° ROUGE; round

elongated, and rounded in the red-round sub-variety. It is very good and highly cultivated in Germany, but has been little accepted in France, because it turns the broth and stews in which it is used red. 5° The YELLOW ORANGE; which appears to be a sub-variety of the yellow variety, of which it has the same qualities. 6° E SPANISH VIOLET; very large, very sweet, but prone to bolting if sown early, it has the same disadvantage as the red variety. Carrots like deep soil, rather light than strong, well loosened by ploughing in autumn and spring. They like fertilizer, but it must be applied at least three or four months in advance, otherwise the roots fork. From the end of February to June, after the soil has been thoroughly loosened, seed is broadcast or sown in rays spaced ten inches apart. Only lightly cover with a rake, then cover with a finger of compost to prevent the soil from being beaten up by frequent watering and weeding. As they emerge, we visit them and destroy the slugs and insects that are so eager to eat them. If there are any missing in a few places, we lift them in clods without touching the roots, and transplant them with the greatest care. Choose rainy weather for this operation, and take advantage of the moment when the carrot has only two leaves in addition to its cotyledons. Thinning is carried out wherever the plant is too thick, and in such a way as to leave, for this first time, four or five inches between each plant. Continue to weed and keep the soil moist, but not too wet, and when the carrots are finger-sized, begin to pull out 2008 for use. We take one between two, leaving eight to ten inches between each plant. Before the frosts, we harvest the carrots, pulling them out with a fork to cut less. Their heads are cut off at the collar to prevent them from growing back, and they are fed to the livestock. They are washed, left to dry out, and kept in an airy greenhouse, impenetrable to frost, where they are placed side by side, heads out. It goes without saying that we don't cut off the collar of those we intend to set seed the following year. If you don't have a greenhouse, open a trench as we said for cardoons; spread a good bed of sablesec at the bottom of the pit, and place heads of long straw against the sides of the hole; once this is done, spread a bed of carrots, roots turned against the sides of the pit, then cover with a layer of sand; new bed of carrots, new bed of sand, and so on until the trench is filled. Then we cover the whole with straw, and throw back the earth from the pit, which we raise into a cone to keep out the rainwater. But preserved carrots never have the flavor of those eaten fresh and young. They should therefore be sown in September and treated, as we have said, with the precaution of covering them with litter during the winter to protect them from the rigors of frost. They will produce until the spring sowings begin to give. To obtain seed, you can keep plants in the ground, protecting them from the cold; but it's safer to replant them in spring, fifteen or eighteen inches apart, in good, loose, well-soiled soil, from roots kept in the greenhouse or simply in a cellar. They come up in May, and the seed is harvested in August, with the stems cut off and left to dry. The seed is not removed from its umbels until it's time to sow, and is cleaned of hairs by rubbing it in the hands with a little sand or ash. It's good for two years. CULTIVATED CELERY, API, ACHE DOUX. (Apium graveolens, L.) g. This plant grows spontaneously in the marshes of southern France, where it has a pungent, aromatic and bitter flavor, which cultivation has corrected. Its ribs and roots are used both cooked and raw. Several varieties have been obtained. 1° CELERI-RAVE BLANC; very large, elongated root, or round in a sub-variety. 2° RED CELERY; similar to the previous one, but with red or purple veins on the root. 3° HOLLOW CELERY, or small celery; its short, almost creeping leaves are used as salad greens. 4° GROS VIOLET DE TOURS; the largest of all, with purplish-pink ribs. 5° PLEIN BLANC; one of the most widely grown, with very fleshy ribs. 6° TURKISH OR PRUSSIAN CELERY; a sub-variety of the above, but larger. 7° FULL RED CELERY; another sub-variety with 8° FRESH DARK CELERY; very tender, brittle, used for supplies. Celery is sown on bedding from January to March, and on soil in the open ground from April to June. With these different sowings, celery is available all year round. On a layer of six inches of compost, sow fairly lightly, water and cover with cloches or a frame. Once the plant has emerged, air it as often as possible. As soon as it 3008 has three leaves, we transplant it onto a new layer, and place the plants two or three fingers apart, so that they don't wilt. It is treated and secured in the same way, and left there until it is strong enough to be put in the ground, which is done at the beginning of April. In light, loose soil, a bed can be prepared in different ways, depending on how dry the soil is, as celery is very water-loving; the more the soil retains its moisture, the less need there is for repeated watering. Celery is planted in rays spaced eight inches apart; this gives the bed more or less width, depending on whether you want to plant more or fewer rows. In very hot soils, the bed is dug six inches deep, and the soil is thrown back onto the paths, which are left with a bottom, smoked, and the celery is planted there, mounded when necessary with the soil from the paths. On ordinary land, we set up four- or five-foot planks, leaving an equal space between each one where we grow lettuce or other small plants so as not to lose space. The celery is then suitably loosened and fined, and planted in rows and staggered. When it comes to buttering, we use soil taken from between the beds. In any case, the plants should be placed eight to nine inches apart, and watered immediately to help them take root. If the weather is dry, they should be watered every other day, and weeded if necessary. When the plant is strong enough, first tie it with straw or rushes near the root, then with straw or rushes near the middle of the plant, and finally with straw or rushes near the tip. Then wrap the plant entirely in straw or litter, or bury it near the tip of its longest leaves. Within a fortnight, it turns white and can be used for cooking. Celery treated in this way starts to yield in June if sown in January, in July if sown in February, and in August if sown in March. Celery sown in the open ground in April, May and June provides yield for all the other months of the year, and even all winter, if not left to rot. It is sown very lightly, the plant is thinned out, and it is only transplanted into the ground, where it is treated as we have said. As it yields, we bleach it by pulling it out, tying it up and burying it in old layers or potting soil, taking care to leave the tips of its leaves in the air. With a large planting tool, you make a hole, push it in along its entire length, and don't bind it, i.e. don't squeeze the soil around it. Cover it with bedding, and it quickly turns white; it can be kept for a month at most, after which it rots. If you prefer, you can mound it; but this applies to celery that supplies until October, as late-season celery must be mounded before the hard frosts. Celery can also be kept in greenhouses, in which case it is treated in much the same way as cardoons, by burying it in sand. A few plants are left in the ground to provide seed. In March, they are removed from the ground, and soon start to grow. The seed is harvested in September and keeps well for three or four years; the newest is always the best. Celeriac is grown in the same way as the other varieties, but does not need to be tied or ridged. Just pull it up like the turnip, and bury it a few inches above the crown in the greenhouse sand. The stalks, kept in the ground to set seed, are protected from the cold by a blanket of litter. CERFEUIL. (Scandix cerefolium; L.)O. Native aromatic plant, cultivated for salad and seasoning. It has given rise to a pretty variety, CREEPED CERFEUIL, which can be grown in the same way and used for the same purposes. It adapts well to all soils and can be sown in all seasons; you even need to sow it every fortnight if you want to keep it tender, as it grows very quickly. From March to May, it is sown at the foot of a wall with good exposure; during hot weather, it is placed in the north and in the shade, and in autumn it is sown again with southern exposure. The one sown in October is the last to grow, and is ready to supply in spring. Seeds are collected in June and keep for two or three years, but the newest are the best. CERFEUIL MUSQUÉ, d'ESPAGNE, FOUGÈRE MUSQUÉ, (scandix odorata; L.) 2, native to the country whose name it bears, is used like the previous one; but it has a particular flavor that not everyone finds pleasant. Its root is very good in soup and gives it a very good taste. It is sown as soon as the seeds are collected, otherwise they take a very long time, even several months, to sprout; they are only good for one year. MUSHROOMS. Everyone knows the harmful qualities of most mushrooms, and how difficult it is to distinguish between poisonous and non-poisonous species. They should therefore only be used with great circumspection, and we should never eat those found in the fields unless we know them inside out. M. Boitard, my former collaborator in the Bon jar1 dinier, is preparing an interesting work on this subject which will be truly useful, because all the innocent and edible species will not only be described and compared with the harmful ones which have some analogy with them, but also drawn and colored with care. He indicates that the best counterpoison is an emetic taken in a dose of three or four grains if a large quantity has been eaten, and if the accidents are serious. If the pain is not very severe, drinking a quantity of very strong vinegar, or sulphuric acid diluted in water, will suffice to halt the progress of the disease. We feel it necessary to describe cultivated mushrooms, in order to avoid mistakes as far as possible, while warning our readers that all species, without exception, become dangerous as they age. The cultivated mushroom is Linnea's edible agaric (Agaricus edulis). It is round, a little flattened at the top, with a short, thick pedicel. As the edges of its cap are attached to the stem when it emerges from the ground, they tear during its development, making them look fringed; a few fragments also remain on the stem. The top of the cap is never perfectly smooth, and its color is more or less dirty white towards the center, sometimes gray or grayish red. It is covered with a film that is easily removed by lifting it from the edges to the center. The underside is lined with a large number of very thin leaflets, first white when the mushroom is very young, then a beautiful pink, and finally gradually turning dark black. When this last color is reached, the mushroom becomes suspect and must be discarded. The flesh is spongy, opaque white, never turning blue in the air, nor green when rubbed; its own juice is limpid and never milky. Numerous experiments carried out by the aforementioned author show that all species whose flesh turns from white to blue or green when exposed to the air, those whose own juices are milky, and those whose odor is virulent and nauseating, are lethal to most animals and to man. All mushrooms are indigestible to anyone with a delicate stomach, or who eats a certain quantity. Let's move on to how to propagate this singular plant, which has only been available at will for a hundred years at most, thanks to ingenious methods. For a long time, mushroom beds were created without any certainty as to the results they would produce, for two reasons: firstly, the preparation and selection of the manure from which they were built was neglected; secondly, the beds were not seeded, and their production was left to chance. Today, if we act as we're about to say, we can be sure of success. Mushroom spawn are small white filaments, resembling mold at first glance, found on soil, manure or compost on which mushrooms have grown, particularly around their base. The first thing to do is to see if you can get hold of some, as otherwise the operation would be of dubious success. This white fungus can be found by dismantling old millstones, as we shall see below, or by building layers for it, or by looking for it around the mushrooms that grow spontaneously in the fields, and transporting it with the soil in which it is found. First, we'll assume that you have some, and deal with the grinding stone where you'll put it to obtain certain harvests, after which we'll come back to the bed to obtain some if you didn't have any, and if you were too far from the gardeners engaged in this culture to be able to obtain some from them. The mushroom wheel. Its success depends largely on the choice and quality of the manure. Market gardeners even give preference to manure from horses fed on oats, and reject it when it comes from horses fed on bran, or from brewers. The most important thing is that it should be short, well soaked with urine and mixed with dung, and that it should not be taken out of the stable until it has been used as bedding for at least a week. Take a quantity calculated according to the number of millstones you wish to establish, and place it on dry ground, away from farmyard animals. It is then placed in a sort of toisé, as wide or as long as you like, but never more or less than two feet thick. While it's being forked in this way, great care is taken to remove hay, dry straw, and generally any foreign matter that might be mixed in. As it is placed in beds, it is walked on and trampled to make the pile as compact as possible. If the weather is very dry and hot, we water it, but not too abundantly, to encourage fermentation rather than prevent it, which would happen if it were too wet. In ordinary weather, we don't water it, and even if it rains too heavily, it would be very good to be able to shelter it from it. After eight to ten days, we'll look inside to see if it has fermented properly, which can be recognized by the whitish color it will have taken on inside. Then the whole heap is taken out and stirred again, after which it is reshuffled, with the precaution of placing in the middle of the heap the manure that was on 009 the edges and on the surface, to bring it to the same degree of fermentation. Leave it in this state for a further eight to ten days, then see if it has acquired the necessary qualities to be used straight away to form the grindstone. This point is so essential that, if we fail to obtain good mushroom harvests, we shouldn't attribute it to other causes. For it to be as good as one could wish, it must be brownish in color, neither too dry nor too wet, but soft and unctuous, well bound and soft to the touch. If it were too dry, we'd put it back in a heap, and bring it to the desired point by means of a few sprinklings; but if, on the contrary, it were too damp, if we drained the water by pressing it into our hands, its heat would be gone forever, and we'd have no chance of success. In that case, we'd have to start all over again with new manure, or give up on making millstones; and this decision would be even better than wasting a lot of time and care to achieve nothing. All that's left is to prepare the millstone, which can be done at any time of year, but most advantageously in spring and autumn. It can be set up outside, in a greenhouse, or in a cellar, and all three locations bring with them a few minor details of their own. 1° When it comes to setting up outside, you need to choose the healthiest location, i.e. the one with the least humidity and where water cannot get in. It must be far from any gutters or other obstructions that could collect water during a rainstorm. If the site is chosen in spring or summer, it should be in the shade, but in autumn or winter, the millstone should be placed at midday. If the ground is not of a nature to absorb moisture quickly, it should be broken up to a foot or eighteen inches deep and two to two and a half feet wide, and the earth removed and replaced by gravel, plaster or rubble. A layer of earth should be placed on top and trampled down a little, just to give it solidity. Once the ground has been prepared to receive the millstone, lay a bed of manure twenty inches to two feet wide, and trample it down; on top of the first bed, lay a second, a little less wide, which is trampled down in the same way to unite it perfectly with the first; on top of this, lay a third, then a fourth, always reducing their width, so as to give the layer the shape of a humpback, with a height in the middle equal to its width. After consolidating the layer by beating on the sides with a palette knife or spade, the surface is evened out by combing it from top to bottom with a large rake, and removing any excessively long strands of straw. Then place a shirt over it, i.e. a large, dry bedding cover about three fingers thick. Let the grindstone warm up in this state for a few days, and if you fear that dryness will harm fermentation, increase its activity with a few moderate sprinklings. To find out how hot it is, stick a stick into it, and after an hour or two, you can easily tell by touching it with your hand whether it's at the right temperature. Now it's time to larder, in other words, to place the mushroom spawn. The earthen cakes that contain it are cut into three- or four-inch pieces, and inserted into small openings of the same size, made with the fingers on the sides of the grinding wheel. These pieces of white are placed at a height of four inches from the base of the layer, and ten or twelve inches apart in the 2. 22 8 longitudinal direction of the grinding wheel. Make two or three rows, depending on the width of the sides, placing them five or six inches from the first row. The white must be flush with the manure, and perfectly bonded with it. Once the millstone has been larded, it is covered with its liner; the white cakes are left to soak up the humidity of the manure for two or three days; then, using a palette knife, they are gently pressed down to incorporate them well with the manure, and covered. Eight days later, remove the jacket and check whether the spawn has set. This can be easily recognized by a kind of mold, with the appearance of small white threads, which spread over the manure around the lardons. If these fillets don't form within a fortnight, the spawn is worthless, and you need to replace them with better ones, which you push into the holes between the first ones. When the white has set, the grinding wheel is gopted. If necessary, firm it up, as mentioned above, or even beat it lightly with a palette knife or the back of a wooden shovel. If the weather is dry, a light sprinkling is given; then a very light layer of soil is applied over the whole surface, often mixed with fine sand, or even pure potting soil, but much consumed and reduced to dust. Some gardeners even sift the soil and potting compost together. The whole is covered with three inches of new manure, except towards the top of the millstone, which is left almost uncovered. Eight days later, a new layer of new manure is applied in the same way as the first, and a further eight days later, the soil is completely uncovered. We clean the millstone of any garbage the manure may have left behind, and arrange a new layer of long, finger-thick bedding on top, the straw of which should be inclined to facilitate the drainage of rainwater. If the operation is carried out in autumn and cold weather, three good inches of warm manure prepared eight days in advance, as we said for making the grinding stone, are thrown on top of the shirt, and covered again with the manure just removed before putting on the shirt. Fifteen days later, harvesting begins. If the season is fine, as the millstone is only covered by the shirt, we uncover the layer in front of us, remove the mushrooms with the precaution of filling in any holes we might make in the layer, give it a light watering and cover it immediately; but if the weather is cold, we are obliged to uncover the whole layer at once, and immediately cover it, placing more or less new manure on the shirt, depending on how cold it is. Picking is repeated every three days, and can last for more than three months. 2° When you place a millstone in a sheltered place, such as a greenhouse, you don't need to dig the soil at its base to sanitize it, because there's no presumption of humidity. The layer is made in exactly the same way as above, with the difference that no manure is placed on it after it has been gopted, and it is simply covered with the liner. 3° In a cellar, the grindstone can be made into a square or a humpback with the same advantage; and, after gopting it, you don't need to cover it with either manure or a shirt. However, the cellar's window wells must be tightly sealed, so that neither air nor light can penetrate. If you set up a mushroom bed in a small cellar, you'd have to be wary of the carbon the manure would release through fermentation. To do this, you'd enter with caution and a lit candle; if you suddenly saw the flame diminish in volume, flicker or go out, you'd have to withdraw immediately, or run the risk of being asphyxiated. Leave the door open for a few moments, and after a very short time there would be no danger. Mushroom beds. These are always done outdoors, and can be set up as early as September. The main thing is to place them in dry soil, which absorbs moisture quickly so as not to cool or rot the base of the beds, which are intended to last a long time. We start by digging a pit two feet wide and five or six inches deep, because the base of the layer must be buried, which is not necessary for the grinding wheel. Horse, mule or donkey manure is prepared as described above; the bed is raised in the same way, and immediately gopted with the earth from the hole. Without further precaution, it is left in this state until April, when it is covered with a shirt of large litter, three fingers thick. If the season is favorable, the following month, or June at the latest, it is covered with mushrooms, which are collected every two days by lifting the liner, which is then replaced immediately after a light watering. If these beds are on dry, sandy soil, and if they have been made with good, well-prepared manure, they will last four months or more. If, on the other hand, they are set in strong, greasy soil, they will take longer to establish themselves, and will last much less: in the latter case, you are obliged to put gravel in the pit, which you dig deeper; and, to gopter it, you mix fine sand or potting soil with the soil from the excavation. 2 When a layer has finished producing, break it up and set aside the mushroom spawn, which is removed in the form of small cakes and placed in a dry place for storage. It can be kept for up to two years if stored in a place protected from frost and the slightest humidity. When old millstones are dismantled, the white they once produced is also preserved. In England and Russia, champi gnons are grown in greenhouses made in this way: the ground is dug eighteen inches deep, and a vault eight feet high is built of thick masonry. The interior of this type of cellar must be seven feet wide by an indeterminate length. No windows or other openings are left, and the door is hermetically sealed. Shelves or shelves two and a half feet wide, in three rows, are built all around the walls, so that they are spaced two feet apart, one above the other. We make a millstone on the ground, in the same way as we said for a cellar; see page 339, n° 3, and we drive it absolutely in the same way. A second grinding wheel is made on the first spoke, a third on the second, and a fourth on the third. They are raised in such a way that they can produce mushrooms on the side of the wall where there is an empty space, as well as on the side of the road. In our climates, where the temperature is warmer and the air more desiccating, it might be necessary to bury the cellar, cover its vault with a certain thickness of earth, or at least place it under the shade of a few trees. We also think that this construction could be improved by making it wider, which would make it easier to leave a path behind the shelves. We should also make two doors separated by a small cabinet, as we do for our hot greenhouses. TRUFFLE (Lycoperdon tuber; L.) is a species of mushroom that grows underground. It consists of an irregularly rounded, spindly tuber with a chapped, chagrined surface, having no leaves, stems, roots or gems. Its color is black on the outside, mottled black or grayish, or even white on the inside. Its fragrance and flavor are generally pleasing, and it is used extensively in delicate cuisine. Truffles are preserved in olive oil after cooking in a court-bouillon, or simply dried and powdered. They are found in almost every province of France, but those from Périgord are the most highly prized. As pigs are extremely fond of them, they are quite often used to discover them; however, these animals can be replaced with great advantage by dogs that have been trained for this kind of search. In light, black soil, at the foot of oak and hornbeam trees, and always facing north, they are found buried from two inches to six inches deep, and never more. We've noticed that the soil above them remains barren, and is almost constantly covered with small winged insects, no doubt attracted by the scent of truffles. We believe, however, that it is possible, and we urge amateurs to try this experiment, which we ourselves would have tried if work of another kind had given us the time. We believe that by analyzing the soil of a natural truffle field, in order to create an analogous one, by mixing with it roots of oaks and hornbeams whose truffles are perhaps parasites, by watering with water in which the truffles would have been washed when collected, or even using the soil removed from around their tubers, and combining all these means with others derived from our knowledge of cryptogam vegetation, we believe we can achieve satisfactory results. CHERVIS, GIROLLES, CHIRUIS, CHIROUIS. (Sium sisarum; L.) 2. The roots of this native plant are tuberous, fleshy and sweet; they are rarely eaten except fried. Sow in March or September in clean, well-disturbed, fresh or even slightly damp soil; hoe, weed and, above all, give frequent watering if you want the roots to have any delicacy. From November onwards, we start to dig up the plants for use, and the harvest continues throughout the winter. It goes to seed in the first year, but it has been observed that its first seeds are rarely fertile, so we wait until a few plants are two years old before harvesting seed; it is ripe in September, and keeps well for two or three years. This plant can also be propagated by root bursts, but these are less delicate in this case than when taken from a seedling of the same year. CATERPILLARS. (Scorpiurus vermiculata; L.) Q. From southern France. Its spiral-rolled pods, bristling with small tubencules and hairs, look rather like caterpillars, hence their name and the use made of them on salads to surprise people who see them for the first time. They are also preserved in vinegar. This plant can be sown in March or April, at the foot of a wall in the south, and is not fussy about the quality of the soil. When you want to preserve its seeds, pick them and throw them into the vinegar as they form, or at least before they are hard. CHICORY SAUVACE. (Cichorium intybus; L.)2. Found everywhere along roadsides, it can be eaten as a salad when very young, or after blanching. It has three varieties: 1° COMMON, which can be grown as it is found in the wild. 2° PANACHÉE, whose leaves have a few reddish veins, which turn bright red when blanched. 3° CHICORÉE A CArÉ, whose leaves are used in the same way as those above, and whose fleshy roots are calcined and pulverized to imitate or lengthen coffee. Chicory can be sown on a bed as early as January, but as autumn chicory lasts until spring, it is more usual to sow it in light soil at the end of April, and later in stronger soil every two weeks, so as to always have some good to cut. Sow thickly if you want to eat the young leaves, and more lightly in rays if you want to blanch it. The only care it requires is to be weeded as needed and sometimes watered. When large, the leaves are fed to livestock. From October to January, we pull it up to bleach it. To do this, a layer of sandy earth or potting soil is laid down in a cellar or other warm, dark place; first, a bed of earth two feet wide and two inches thick is made; its length is indeterminate. This is then covered with a second bed of two inches of soil and a new row of roots, and so on up to a suitable height; if the soil is too dry, a light sprinkling is given, and soon long, thin, etiolated leaves sprout from each collar, which are cut off, or pulled out with the root, so that they can be tied into bunches. This is the type of salad that market gardeners sell under the 009 name of peasant's hair or capuchin's beard. Another method is used to obtain the same result: a barrel is knocked down, and rows of holes are made in it with a crankshaft, two or three inches apart; a first bed of sand is placed at the bottom, followed by a row of roots whose necks emerge through the holes, a second bed of sand and roots, and so on; the chicories grow in the same way, and are cut several times until they are exhausted and no longer produce leaves. Sand, like topsoil, needs to be watered lightly if it threatens to dry out. Other people, mainly market gardeners, don't take such a close look; they are content to bind chicory in bundles as they pull it out, and bury it in hot manure, or even leave it on the ground, covering it with dry manure; they remove the bundles as they turn white. To collect seeds, simply leave a few plants in the ground, where they overwinter, come up in spring and ripen their seeds in September. They keep for ten years or more. WHITE CHICORY or ERDIVE. (Cichorium endivia, L.)O. From India. This plant, eaten raw in salads or cooked in a variety of ways, has given rise to several varieties and sub-varieties. 1° CRICORÉE FRISÉE or large endive; the most common, grown almost exclusively in the départemens. 2° CHICORÉE DE MEAUX or endive; almost as widely grown, although it has the disadvantage of rising if over-watered; it rises less if sown a little late. It is a sub-variety of the previous one. 3° CHICORÉE FINE D'ITALIE, CHICORÉE D'ÉTÉ; rises less 000 easily than the other, which means it can be sown early. It is more delicate and garnishes more quickly. 4° CHICORÉE ALWAYS WHITE; as it doesn't garnish much, it is cut young. 5° CHICORÉE SCAROLE, scarolle, scariole, or small lettuce; same cultivation and use as large endive. Resembles lettuce somewhat in its broad leaves. 6° CHICORÉE GRANDE SCAROLE, or escarole de Hollande; a sub-variety of the preceding one, differing only in its longer leaves. 7° CHICORÉE SCAROLE RONDE; another sub-variety whose smaller, rounded leaves tend to peel. 8° CHICORÉE SCAROLE BLONDE; a third sub-variety that differs from the others in that its leaves are yellowish in color at birth. 9° CHICORÉE COURTE ou célestine; the earliest of all. It doesn't climb much, is very tender, but very small. All these varieties degenerate rapidly if not cultivated with care. From January to March, seed is sown on beds, under cloches or in trays. January seedlings are transplanted to a new bed when the plant has four leaves, to wait for the moment when they can be planted in the ground; the others are transplanted when the plants are strong enough. Around the middle of March, we start sowing in loose soil, well mulched to prevent rain or watering from beating it, and we can continue sowing all year round. Transplant to a bed and stagger the plants, ten inches or a foot apart, and give them a good watering. Weeds are weeded and removed, and the plants are wetted if necessary, especially in hot weather, to prevent them from bolting. Once the chicory has reached its full strength, it is time to blanch. When the weather is dry, raise the plant and bind it close to the ground with a first 1008 straw tie; a few days later, when the heart leaves have elongated, place a second tie, then a third. From this point onwards, you should only water at the neck, to avoid wetting the plant's leaves, which would cause it to rot. You can cover the plant with straw or a flowerpot, if you want to speed it up; but in any case, it takes no more than a fortnight or three weeks to blanch. A few plants of the first sown are kept to collect seed, which ripens in autumn and keeps well for six or seven years. The oldest is the best, because the plants it supplies grow more slowly. CABBAGE. (Brassica oleracea, L.) g. Native. Everyone knows its many uses. This plant has given rise to a large number of varieties and sub-varieties, all of which can be divided into five main types: Head cabbage, savoy cabbage, green cabbage, cauliflower and kohlrabi. CABBAGES. These form hard, tightly packed heads, sometimes with a musky odor that is not to everyone's liking. Cabbages from this division are used to make sauerkraut. 1° CHOUX D'YORCK; highly prized, small, elongated. a. Cabage, or superfin hátif; very -small and very -early. b. Nain hatif; also early, with lower stem and less elongated head. c. Gros d'Yorck; less early, larger. 2° HATIF EN PAIN DE SUCRE cabbage; very good; elongated, hooded, sometimes in the shape of an inverted cone, a little blond, not very prone to splitting. 3° BOEUr cabbage; excellent: fairly widespread. a. Small; early. 008 b. Medium; larger and less early. c. Large; more rounded. 4° CHOUX POMMÉS ou GROS CABUS BLANCS; larger, more rounded and more widely grown than the preceding varieties. a. Milan des vertus, or large German curly cabbage; apple the size of a large white cabbage; leaves slightly blistered, slightly curled, sometimes glaucous: hardy and not afraid of the cold. j. Second-season German milfoil; smaller apple, white inside; fairly thick stem; dark green leaves: tender and very good. L. Milan d'automne, large Saint-Denis kale; very large, less round head; slightly blistered, pale-green, broad, rounded leaves; large, fairly high stem: hardy and good. l. Rosette cabbage, Brussels sprouts; two- or three-foot stem, producing small curly apples in the leaf axils. CHOUXVERTS. These do not pomace, are more resistant to cold, and are good to eat only when frost has softened them. We eat the leaves, which we detach in winter without tearing up the plant, and the new shoots, which we break off in spring before flowering. They are grown more as fodder than in the kitchen garden. O08 a. Chou cavalier, chou à vache, chou en arbre; stems five to six feet long; large, plain leaves. b.
