Cabbage with broad ribs and fringed edges; its name indicates how it differs from the two preceding varieties. CAULIFLOWER. This variety, whose use is fairly well known, can be divided into two breeds: 1° CROUX-TLEURS proper; they grow less tall than 1008 broccoli, are earlier, and have a white color tending more or less to yellowish. a. The tender or há̂tif; plant less strong; apple less tight; thresholds more united, straighter, less wide than in the others. b. The hard; thicker, shorter stem; later, fatter, tighter apple; larger, folded, wavy leaves. c. Semi-hard; in shape and quality, it occupies the middle ground between the two preceding varieties. 2° BROCOLIS; they differ from cauliflower in what we have said, and in their more wavy leaves. a. White; apple: preferred with purple over all other varieties. b. Purple; like the previous one. c. Dwarf purple; apples like the previous two. d. Green; does not head, but produces sprays like the following. e. Yellow. f. Red. CAULIES. These varieties are grown for their leaves and neck, which is large and fleshy, and is used in cooking like turnips. There are three main varieties. 1° SIAM CROUX-RAVES; swollen stem above the ground, forming a ball from which the leaves emerge. a. White; the latest of the three. b. Violet; purple stem; ribs and veins of thresholds tinted this color. c. Hátif dwarf; small, few leaves. 2° CHOUX - NAVETS, TURNEP DE LAPONIE; oblong, large, fleshy root, same flavor as kohlrabi: more resistant to hard frosts. a. Ordinary white kohlrabi. c.Red-necked; ribs and veins dyed this color. 3° RUTABACA or SWEDISH KALE; similar to the above, but yellowish, quicker to produce and infinitely better for cooking. GROWING. Cabbages like clean, slightly strong, well-smoked and naturally fresh soil. They grow to enormous size in newly cleared soil. They can be propagated from seed in all seasons, in loose, light soil, with a little shade when sown in summer. During their youth and after transplanting, until they have fully recovered, they are given frequent watering, which is continued if the season requires, but less abundantly. As far as possible, choose rainy weather for transplanting, as this is more reliable and faster, and aphids wreak less havoc. When planting a young cabbage, the soil should be perfectly loosened so that the planter doesn't compress it too much; the root should be buried without crumpling, and so that the eye or collar is slightly buried rather than raised. The plant must be neither too weak nor too strong; in the former case, it languishes and more easily falls prey to insects; in the latter, it is prone to climbing, knotting and hardening. Stakes are the scourge of young plants; they can be removed from seedlings by sprinkling them with ashes. Later on, cabbages are devoured by caterpillars, if you don't visit them night and morning, and crush them with a small wooden spatula. Slugs and red bugs attack them at all times; the only way to keep them at bay is to look for these pests and crush them. When transplanting, we visit the neck of the plant: if we find a small tumour, we open it by cutting off half of it, and find a larva or worm, which we remove. To obtain good seed, choose the strongest plants, with the most straightforward and best characterized varieties, and let them overwinter, taking care to protect delicate plants from frost. Care should also be taken to ensure that seedbeds of different varieties are not too close together, to prevent the mixing of fertilizing dust, which would produce hybrid and degenerate individuals. In spring, if the apples don't open up to allow the stems to pass through, they are split slightly in four; or, if they are a little spoiled by rain or frost, the rotten parts are removed by cutting right through. The seed obtained from the sprouts of a cabbage from which the head has been cut off is also good. When ripe, cut off the stalks, dry them on a cloth, thresh them and, after cleaning, store them in a dry place. It retains its vegetative quality for seven or eight years, with the exception of cauliflower seed, which largely ceases to sprout when over five years old. Cabbages are sown: 1° from August 15 to September 15; Yorck cabbages are sown at the end of August and transplanted in October. The others are also transplanted in situ, or in nurseries, to be planted permanently in February or March. The distance between plants varies from fifteen inches to three feet, depending on their size. They form their buds from the end of April to August. 2° They are sown on beds in February, to be transplanted in late March or early April. 3° At the end of February, they can be sown in the ground, but well potted and at the foot of a wall with a southern exposure. 4° In the open ground, in March and at the beginning of April, to be transplanted, like the previous ones, as soon as 2. 23 008 they are strong enough. The last three seedlings start to bolt in the first days of September, and produce until December. Savoy cabbages can be sown in August and September, but are best grown from the end of February to May. Milan des Vertus can be sown in April at the latest. The latest sprout in June and the others at the beginning of winter, to be eaten until March. Those whose heads are not fully formed, the pancaliers and ordinary kites, are left outside and only gain from frost, unless the cold is excessive. It is quite common to protect cabbages and kites from the effects of frost by burying them in place. The cabbage is dug at the foot, on the north side, tilted, and the soil from the hole is placed on its roots. That's all there is to it. Green cabbages are extremely easy to grow. They are sown in nurseries in July and August, or in March and April, and transplanted in the field, the former from September to November, the latter from April to May. They produce leaves throughout the winter and into their second year. Palmetto and Naples cabbages, originally from Italy, are less hardy than the others, fear the cold more, and are more winter hardy when sown in July and August. Broad-ribbed cabbages are sown from May 15 to the end of June, and transplanted in July and August. Cauliflowers, which are much more delicate than all the other species, therefore require lighter, better smoked soil, and much more watering. Hard cabbages are very sensitive to drought, so they are more difficult to grow in summer than other varieties. They are sown: 1° Every day in September, in potting soil or, better still, in old potting soil. 906 Twenty days after the young plant has emerged, it is planted out in a bed against a wall facing south, in four inches of good potting soil that has been thrown on top of the soil. When the frosts arrive, cloches are placed on the plants, spaced so that they can hold fifteen to twenty under each one. At first, these bells are only put on in the evening, and as the season advances, air is given whenever the weather permits. If the cabbages continue to grow rapidly, they should be stopped by lifting them up and replanting them immediately in the same place. When the frost becomes heavy, and you fear that it will penetrate under the glass, surround and cover them with litter, and if necessary, throw mats over them. The most important thing is to remember to give them air whenever you can. In March, earlier or later, depending on the temperature, we transplant into the ground, and the cauliflowers produce from the sin of May to the beginning of July. Hard and semi-hard varieties are particularly suitable for this sowing. 2° Sow at the end of August or beginning of September, and overwinter the plant in a frame, spacing it four inches apart. At the beginning of February, we prepare a dull layer, made of half hot manure and half dry leaves, placed bed by bed, fifteen to eighteen inches thick, and covered with nine to ten inches of potting soil and earth mixed in half. When the soil is at its hottest, the cabbages are planted twenty inches apart, covered with bells, and bedded, as we said, if necessary. After transplanting, air is gradually added, and the cloches are removed in March. These cauliflowers produce in April. 3° From the end of January to February 15th, sow on a warm bed under a frame or cloches. Transplant to a new bed a fortnight after emergence, and protect from cold as above. In March or April, cauliflowers are replanted in the ground, and produce in June and July. 4° Sow in the first fortnight of March, and work as for the previous sowing. If you had sown very lightly, you could leave the plant in place and only transplant it into the ground in April. It produces in July. 5° Finally, from April 15 to May 15, you can sow in well-soiled soil and only lift the young plant to put it in place. These cauliflowers do not do as well as the others, because they have to fear drought, especially in hot summers. Semi-hard cabbages are more suitable for this season. 6° Sow in the shade from June 10 to 25 and transplant in July. They are grown in the same way as ordinary cabbages, but with much more watering, as the soil must always be moist if you want to be sure of success. Cauliflowers produce from the end of August to November. The last cauliflowers sown can last until February if you know how to store them. All you have to do is cut them three inches below the head, clean them of all their small leaves and bracts, and hang them in a dry, airy place where frost cannot penetrate. As for those that have not made their apples when the cold arrives, strip them of most of their leaves, remove them with the root ball, and bury them either in a cellar, or in a trench where bedding debris has been thrown, and which is covered with a frame; they make small apples, but quite good, and sometimes succeed one another until March. Broccoli is sown in May and June, and dwarf violet 1 in July. They are treated in exactly the same way as cauliflower, crop no. 6, but are planted two and a half feet apart, and mounded when the cold comes. A small pit is dug at the base, the plant is tilted, or rather laid down, and covered with soil, leaving the tip of the head exposed to the air. When the frosts become severe, we cover it with dry litter, which we remove whenever the weather is fine. You can also raise it in a clump, make a hole in the ground and stick it in, protecting it from the cold as we said. They produce their apples at the end of winter and the beginning of spring. Cauliflower seeds are harvested in the same way as ordinary cabbages, from sturdy plants sown in autumn and sheltered over winter under cloches or a frame. SEA CABBAGE, CRAMBE MARITIMA. (Crambe maritima; L.) 2. native. This plant, whose young stalks are eaten cooked and seasoned in various ways after blanching, is all the more valuable for its availability in February and March, when no other vegetables are available. In March or August, loosen a bed, draw lines two feet apart, and make small holes equally spaced on each; throw in a handful of potting soil, and sow three or four crambe seeds, leaving only the most vigorous plant when they have germinated. Water, weed and keep the ticks away. If you already have this plant, it is more expedient to propagate it by eyelets or by sections of its roots, which you plant in spring; either way, you leave the plants to grow strong for the first two years, and it's not until the third shoot that you start cutting: to do this, you cover the young shoots as they appear, i.e. from February to March, with a pot or a straw hive. These shoots, consisting of the young leaves pressed together, and roughly shaped like very long pine cones, quickly turn white and are cut off near the collar when they are six or eight inches long. The plant lasts a very long time, but for this reason it must not be too exhausted, and a few stems are left to develop. When the stump perishes, it is naturally replaced by the eyelets. If you want to obtain early crambe, from December onwards, place pots and hives on the plants, and fill the gaps with a good thickness of large litter; in very cold weather, cover the whole with straw or dry leaves. This plant is still not very widespread in France, but is widely consumed in England. CIBOULE ORDINAIRE, ONIONETTE. (Allium fissile, L.) g. in one variety, and Z in the other. It is thought to be a degenerate onion, or, if you prefer, a variety of onion; whatever the case, it has the same taste and is used for much the same purposes, and as a salad garnish. The first variety, being an annual, is sown in light, substantial, earthy soil. Sowing times vary according to the time of year you want to harvest, and the climate in which you grow it. In the jatdins of Paris, we sow 1° in February and March, for replanting in April and May; 2° from the fifteenth to the thirtieth of July, for replanting in September and harvesting in April and May of the following spring. In more temperate climates, you can start sowing as early as the sin of January and the beginning of February, and if these initial sowings are unsuccessful, start again in March. As chives are more delicate the newer they are, some gardeners are in the habit of sowing them every month, and growing them across their salad beds. The seed is broadcast, fairly thickly; it is covered with a rake and, if the soil is strong, a more or less thick bed of compost is spread over it; it is weeded and wetted if necessary. Whatever the time of sowing, the plants should be transplanted, two or three at a time, in borders or rays, six inches apart and four deep. They soon form thick clumps that will produce until the frosts, and even until spring if the winter is not too harsh. Moreover, cultivation is not difficult, as the plant only needs to be purged of weeds. The best seeds are harvested from old, replanted plants, whose stems are supported by sticks and light hoops. The umbels are not cut until they are fully ripe, which can be recognized when they are ready to fall off; they are gathered into botillons, left to dry in the sun, and then hung in a dry, airy place. Seed kept in its capsules is good for three years; if it is removed, it must be sown the following year. The sub-varieties of ordinary spring onions are white spring onions and early spring onions, which can be grown in the same way. Perennial chives almost never produce seeds, which makes it necessary to propagate them only by bursting their clumps in spring or autumn, and replanting them seven or eight inches apart, as they expand greatly. It can also be propagated from cloves, but this method is inefficient. This variety, hardier than the previous one, requires some watering during hot spells; it is more spring-like. If you want to have chives during the rigors of winter, pull up a certain quantity as frost approaches; and, if you don't have a tarpaulin or greenhouse, just place it, close by, in an open trench twelve to eighteen inches deep; cover it with a sufficient quantity of dry litter to prevent frost from penetrating; this precaution alone is sufficient to have some when needed. CIBOULETTE, CIVE, CIVETTE, APPÉTIT. (Allium schœnoprasum; L.) 2. originates from Siberia, and is used for salad supplies, sometimes in seasonings. Its flavor is similar to that of the spring onion. As it varies quite easily in size, depending on the suitability of the soil, it was thought to provide three varieties to which the names civette, cive d'Angleterre and cive de Portugal were given. This error has now been corrected. This plant could multiply from seed, but to avoid a long wait, it is customary to separate its large clumps every three years: in February or March, break off three or four cloves and leave them together, then plant them seven to nine inches apart, in beds or borders, in loose, substantial, light soil with a warm exposure. In a short space of time, they form large tufts of leaves somewhat similar to those of the chive, but smaller; they are all the more tender and grow back all the better for being cut more often. Chives can be kept in the same place for four or five years, with the precaution of weeding if necessary, purging it of weeds, and watering it sufficiently during hot spells. It's a good idea to give it two or three hoeings, especially in spring. As soon as winter approaches, cut its leaves back to the ground, let their bases dry out for a while, and cover with a good inch of perfectly-consumed compost. COCHLEARIA OFFICINAL, CRANSON, HERBE A LA CUILLER (Cochlearia officinalis, L.) g. Native. This plant is grown 1 in every garden for its pungent flavor and anti-scorbutic properties. It grows well in all soils, but prefers fresh, light, substantial ones: it is sown in spring, and watered a lot during droughts. COCHLEARIA DE BRETAGNE, CRANSON RUSTIQUE, CRAN, RAIFORT SAUVAGE, GRAND RAIFORT (Cochlearia armoracia; L.) Z. Native. Its scraped root is used to sprinkle on boiled dishes; it has a very pungent flavor, approaching that of mustard. This plant is propagated by bursting the roots in autumn, or from seed in spring; it grows well in all soils, with ordinary exposure. Its seed, like that of the previous plant, is good for two years. CULTIVATED CUCUMBER, CORNICRON, (Cucumis sativus; L.) Q. From Africa. The fruits of this plant are eaten cooked in various ways; before they have reached their full size, they are candied in vinegar, when they are called gherkins. Several varieties are grown. 1° BLANC LONG; one of the most esteemed in Paris; widely grown, as are the following two varieties; elongated and round at both ends. 2° BLANC HATIF; same shape: grown in trays. 3° CROS BLANC DE BONNEUIL; same shape, but larger. 4° HATIr DE HOLLANDE; same shape; first white, then yellow; grown under glass. 5° Jaune LoNG; often bends into a bow. 6° PeTiT VERT; most commonly used for gherkins. 7° VERT LoNC; more elongated than the previous one; same use. 8° NoIR; white cucumber shape; but brown rind, sometimes striped with yellowish white. 9° CONCOMBREA BOUQUET, Russian migvon; the earliest 000 of all; fruits are small, almost round, gathered in bunches of three or four. 10° CONCOMBRE ARADA; rarely reaching more than two inches in length; delicate, but producing much when successful. Only suitable for preserving. Cucumbers are grown in the same way as melons, but some varieties can be sown later, especially those that need to be picked before ripening for pickling. 1° Early varieties are sown in pots, on a warm bed and under a bell or frame, in December and January. Air is let in whenever the weather permits, and the soil is covered with litter or straw mats only when the cold is so intense that frost is likely to penetrate even the young plants. A month later, or rather if the layer has lost its warmth, we put them in larger pots, and place them on a new layer. A fortnight later, we place them on a third layer, on which we've spread seven or eight inches of compost mixed with a third of light soil. We cover them with frames, and keep them warm with heaters. 2° From February to March, sow on a bed like melons, and replant in April on a soft bed. 3° From April 15 to May 15, sow in eighteen-inch-deep holes filled with manure and covered with six inches of compost. Cucumbers intended for gherkins are not sown until this time, as it would be pointless to do so earlier. Cucumbers are pruned like melons; they like warmth and watering. To collect the seed, leave a standing fruit to rot, collect the seeds, wash them and dry them in the shade. They keep well for ten to twelve years, or even longer. OC4 The snake cucumber or LUFFA (cucumis flexuosus, L.) Q, from the Indies, is hardly cultivated except as an object of curiosity; however, it is candied in vinegar. It is very long, smooth and pale green. It is grown in the same way as the previous varieties. CULTIVATED CORIANDER. (Coriandrum sativum, L.) O. From the Orient. This aromatic plant is used to make white beer, in ratafias and sweets, and its seeds are used to spice stews. The Egyptians use it extensively as a seasoning in almost all their foods. It prefers light, warm soil, although it grows well everywhere. It is sown in March, and the seeds are harvested in August and September. They keep well for two years. CORNE-DE-CERF, PLANTAIN. (Plantago coronopus, L.) O. Indigenous. This rather insignificant-tasting plant is used only for salad supplies. Only the leaves are used, which are cut as needed and grow back very quickly, provided that copious watering is given, especially in the plant's early stages. Sow in light, well-amended soil in spring, and harvest from September to October. SQUASH, PUMPKIN, PEPON. (Cuourbita pepo) Q. From India. This plant, the largest of its kind, covers a very large area of land. Its fruits, sometimes reaching an enormous size, can be eaten in a variety of ways; but they are even more valuable in large-scale cultivation than in the kitchen garden, because they provide abundant, healthy food for livestock. Two varieties and several sub-varieties are grown. 1° JAUnE (luteus); the most widely grown around Paris. 2° VeRr (viridis); almost as widespread. D09 3° NoiR; highly cultivated in Bresse. 4° BLaNC; rare around Paris. 5° VERRUQUEUX or brodé, believed to have firmer flesh. 6° POTIRON D'ESPACNE, said to be the best of all. These plants like warmth and watering, but not damp soil. In the North, in March, their seeds are set to germinate in pots which are placed in a warm layer and covered with cloches. When frosts are no longer to be feared, a hole is dug with good exposure; a little fertilizer is thrown in, and the plant is placed in it after having been removed from the soil. In the Paris area, it is sometimes sufficient to make a hole at the beginning of April, fill the bottom with manure, throw eight or ten inches of compost over it, and plant three seeds, leaving only the strongest foot when they have sprouted. In either case, water often. To obtain good seeds, choose the first-knotted and best-formed fruit, keep it for as long as possible, and only extract the seeds when it begins to rot. Those sown on the bed bear fruit in August; the others from the beginning of September until winter. When the frosts start to set in, they are picked and stored in a dry place away from the cold, where they can be kept for varying lengths of time. The MELOPEPON PUMPKIN (cucurbita melopepo, L.) O, native to the same country, provides several edible varieties, some of which are preferred for their firmer, less bland flesh. 1° CIRAUMON TURBAN; firmer, sweeter flesh than pumpkin. 2008 2° BONNET D'ÉLECTEUR, pâtisson, (melopepo clypeatus); same quality. 3° L'ARTICHAUT DE JÉRUSALEX (melopepo radiatus); same quality. 4° MELONATED OR MUSKED CURRANT; highly prized in the south of France. 5° CIRAUMON NOIR. 6° CIRAUMON LONG DE BARBARIE or longuc squash. 7° COURGE A LA MOELLE, whose flesh is very sweet. 8° MELOPEPON TUBERCULEUX (melopepo verrucosus). 9° MELOPEPON ORANGE (melopepo aurantiiformie). 10° MELOPEPON PYRIFORME (melopepo prriformis). All can be grown like pumpkins. The WHITE-FLOWERED PUMPKIN (cucurbita leucantha, L.), O of South America, produces unusually shaped fruit with a hard, limp rind and peregrine gourds. Its most remarkable varieties are: 1° COUGOURDE or bottle (leucantha lagenaria). 2° GOURDE (leucantha latior). 3° POiRE A POUDRE (leucantha prrotheca). 4° CaLEbaSsE or club (leucantha longa). These are grown like the others, but as their fruits are lighter, it is customary to row the plants to make their stems climb. CRESSON DEFONTAINE (Sisymbrium nasturtium, L.) 2. native. This plant, used medicinally as an anti-scorbutic and in cooking for salads and supplies, grows only in clear water. If you have a piece of water in your garden that is always transparent and pure, or even better, a stream, by throwing a few cress roots on its banks 008, they will soon spread and grow stems every year that can be cut tender until the worst frosts, provided they are not allowed to go to seed. If you don't have a pond, take a large tub, dump six or eight inches of soil in the bottom and fill with water. The watercress roots planted in it will produce quite well if you take care to renew the water from time to time, so that it doesn't corrode or even heat up. If you wish, you can multiply it by its seeds, but this is a long and risky process when cultivating in a tub. CRESS ALÉNOIS, NASITOR, PASSERAGE CULTIVÉ. (Lepidium sativum; L.) Q. From Persia. Its young, tender shoots are eaten in salads and as a garnish. Three varieties are grown: ordinary, broad-leaved or golden, and curly-leaved. As this little plant goes to seed very quickly, it has to be sown every two weeks, in spring on old bedding soil, and in summer in the open ground and in the shade. Sometimes, autumn-sown plants can withstand the cold until the spring sowings begin to produce cuttings. Seedlings are sown in rays and a little thickly. They need watering, especially during hot spells. EARTH CESSON, VIVACE CESSON, BARBARED VEGETABLE, LYRUS LEAF ERYSIMUM (Erysimum barbarea, L.) 2. native. Used to replace watercress, from which it takes on some of the flavor. It is sown lighter than watercress, in rays, in light, slightly moist, open soil. Meadow cress (Cardamine pratensis, L.) 2. native. Like the above, it has the same uses as watercress, with its pungent flavor. It is grown like watercress. It is sown in spring, and lasts for several years if grown in open, damp or even marshy soil. PARA CESSON (Spilanthus oleracea; L.) O. Indies. This creeping plant is used in supplies and seasoning because of its pungent flavor. In February or March, sow on beds and under frames or cloches, and avoid letting the coldest weather reach the young plant. When the season is assured and the seedlings are strong enough, transplant them into light, warm soil in a south-facing position. They are watered to keep the soil constantly moist. You can also sow in the open ground at the beginning of May, with good exposure and only lightly covering the seeds with Gn potting soil. BRAZIL CRESSON (spilanthus brasiliana, L.) O. is cultivated in the same way and used for the same purposes as the preceding plant. Both sometimes self-sow, when the soil and exposure are favorable. DANDELION. (Taraxacum dens leonis; Lam, leontodon taraxacum, L.) 2. native. It is very common in meadows, where it is collected in the first days of spring to be eaten as a salad. Cultivation is highly advantageous, as it causes it to lose some of its bitterness. In spring, on any type of soil, but best on fresh, open ground, it is sown a little thickly, watered and harvested. As it occupies the soil for a year, it can be sown as a border plant, if desired. In the following spring, as soon as it begins to appear, it needs to be covered to whiten it; for this, you can use flower pots that are tipped over, dry litter, etc.; but the best is to cover it with four fingers of loam or even well loosened 0 earth. It is cut off at the root collar when it has pierced the layer of compost, and the tips of its leaves can be seen poking out. Its seeds are collected as soon as they ripen, so that the wind doesn't scatter them. DOLIC ONGUICULÉ, MONGETTE, BANNETTE. (Dolichos unguiculatus; L.) Q. West Indies. A species of bean widely grown in southern France, with pods much longer than those of our ordinary beans. Other species are also grown: 1° DOliC Lablar or Egrpte bean (dolichos lablab, L.) O, from Egypt, and its white-flowered variety; this is grown in ornamental gardens. 2° DOLIC A LONGUES GOUSSES, or asparagus bean (dolichos sesquipedalis, L.) O, from the West Indies, whose fleshy, narrow and very long pods are, like those of the preceding varieties, excellent in green. Dolics are very sensitive to cold; they like open, light, warm soil. In April, they are sown in pots in a warm layer under a bell or under a frame; in May, they are planted with the rootball, in a well-exposed spot. In May, they can also be sown in the open ground at the foot of a wall facing south, but if the seasons are not right, the fruit will not ripen. In any case, they are rowed and cultivated like beans. ECHALOTTE. (Allium as calonicum, L.) 4. This garlic, native to Palestine, is more widely used than the others, because of its milder flavor and less strong odor. It is a little more delicate, and has a greater fear of rotting. For this reason, it is only grown with great success in light, substantial, warm soils. It multiplies from cloves, which are planted in February and March, and sometimes before winter, in countries where frost is unlikely. They are planted in beds or borders, three or four inches apart, with the extreme precaution of covering them very lightly with soil to avoid the dampness that would inevitably rot them; they should even be dethatched if the season is wet, and especially some time before ripening. From the end of May onwards, they can be used for cooking, but they should not be harvested until the leaves have dried out, at which point they should be plucked, left to dry in the sun for two or three days, and stored in a dry place away from frost. My colleague and friend, Mr. Vilmorin, believes that the variety known as GROSSE ÉCHALOTE is simply the result of cultivation in better soil. If you didn't have suitable soil, you could lighten it by mixing in a sufficient quantity of sand. Few gardeners have ever seen shallots go to seed. The thinnest and longest cloves produce the most beautiful bulbs. SPINACH (Spinacia oleracea, L.) Q. From northern Asia. Two varieties of this plant are grown, and everyone knows its uses. One has bristly seeds, the other (S. oleracea lœvis) smooth. Each has a sub-variety with broad leaves. Spinach is sown broadcast, or in rays six inches apart, every month from March to the end of October; as it remains in the ground for only a short time, it can be planted in beds to be used for another crop six weeks or two months later. To produce high yields, it requires very loose, well-smoked soil and copious watering. Heat makes it rise very quickly, so place it in cool, shady soil during the summer. The seed is collected from spring seedlings, and to get the best of it, we pull out the male plants as soon as fertilization has taken place; the females benefit from greater space, and the seed is better nourished; we support them with sticks to prevent the seed from escaping. It keeps well for two or three years. 2. 24 ESTRAGON, SERPENTINE, DRAGONNE (Artemisia dracunculus; L.) 2. From Tartary. Its young shoots are used for supplies and seasoning, and are pickled in vinegar with gherkins. It is seldom propagated other than by sprouting in spring, rarely by cuttings taken in spring and summer, and even more rarely by seed, as these are difficult to obtain. It likes clean, light, loose soil that's not too damp. Its shoots can be picked every two weeks, but care must be taken not to tear them off, as they don't hold up well in the ground. FENNEL. (Anethum fœniculum; L.) 2. native. The leaves are sometimes used as seasoning; the seeds are used to make sweets and flavor liqueurs. Sow in March in light, warm soil with good exposure; water and weed while the plant is young, then leave it to nature; it is hardy and usually resows itself. SWEET FENNEL, or PARIS ANISE (A. fœniculum dulce) is a much lower, softer and milder variety, whose roots and stalks are used in cooking like celery. It is highly prized in Spain and Italy. It is grown in exactly the same way as celery, and blanched in the same way. MARSH BEAN (Faba vulgaris; Moencn, Vicia faba; L.) Q. From Persia. Its floury seeds are considered less indigestible than those of other legumes. Several varieties are grown. 1° Rève, de Marais or grosse seve ordinaire (faba vulgaris major); its stems are two feet high, and its seeds very large. A smaller sub-variety is called seve picarde. 2° FÈVE DE WINOSOR, or large round sap; its stems are large and strong; its pods are larger and wider than those of the other varieties, but usually contain only two beans. 3° The HATIVE NAINE BEAN, brought from the coasts of Africa by the English, who gave it the name mazagane. Its branching stem, forming a bush, rises to a maximum of one foot. It is very productive and can be grown as an early plant under glass. As it degenerates easily, it is best to choose seed-bearing plants with the most prominent characteristics of the variety. 4° PETTTE FÈVE, julienne, séverole (faba vulgaris minor); very early and high yielding. 5° FÈVE D'HÉLIGOLAND; also productive, but less early. 6° rÉveRolle D'HIVER; hardier and better able to withstand the cold. These last two seem to be sub-varieties of the petite feve. All three are grown extensively for livestock feed, mainly for horses. -Faba viridis is native to China and produces a lot, but is a little late. It remains green as it dries. 8° FÈVE A LONGUES cossts; its long pods contain more seeds than the other varieties; it is quite stodgy. All fava beans like substantial, soft, well-smoked soil. They are sown broadcast in fields, in rays, in clumps, and in beds in gardens, in February, March or April at the latest, because they fear heat and especially drought. However, if you want to enjoy them all year round, sow in May, June and even early July, but in cool, shady spots, and in December and January for spring. When sowing in clumps, make shallow holes a foot apart, and throw in three or four beans. When they are a few inches high, we hoe them, and put soil back into the holes, so that the young plant is stopped by an inch or two; a fortnight later we put soil back against its foot; and we hoe it again a third time when it 4009 has grown a little taller. The result is that the base of the stem emits new roots, which increase the plant's vigor. If the seed has been sown in rays, the soil from the edges of the furrow is hoeed in the same way; but if the seed has been sown broadcast and in the open field to provide fodder, simply weed without hoeing. Once the beans have passed the flower, the ends of the branches and stems are pinched to force the sap to concentrate on the fruit, which then becomes larger and ripens more quickly. If you want to eat green beans, you pick them when they are about a quarter of the size; then you can cut the stem down to the ground, and if the season is right, it will grow new shoots, which will produce a second harvest. The seed is harvested when it is perfectly ripe, from plants of very strong varieties, chosen from among the sturdiest and first sown. If kept in their pods, they can remain good for five or six years. When the beans begin to degenerate, it is absolutely necessary to change the soil, and even the climate if possible. ~ (Fragaria) 2. native. This plant, highly prized for the sweet fragrance and delicate flavor of its fruit, has given rise to such a large number of varieties that it would be impossible today to recognize the type, indigenous or exotic, to which each may belong; hybrid fertilization has made this impossible. 1° COMMON STRAWBERRY (fragaria vesca, L.), too well known to describe. In the field, it naturally provides the following variety; large-flowered woodland strawberry (fragaria vesca grandiflora.) Petals larger than the calyx, flowers often dioecious. All have red, fragrant fruit, but give only once, in spring. a. White-fruited (f. vesca alba); similar to the above, but with white fruit. b. Threadless, or bush strawberry (f. vesca eflagellis); less productive, but very convenient for borders. c. Large wood strawberry (f. vesca magna); high stems; flowers often dioecious; fruit similar to common wood strawberry. d. One-leaf strawberry (f. vesca monophrlla); fruit of the same quality as the preceding strawberry, from which it differs only in its single-lobed leaf; it regains three lobes if cultivation is neglected. 2° FRAISIER DES ALPES (f. vesca semperflorens. ) Less productive, but supplies until frost; its smaller, more elongated fruits are the most fragrant of the genus. a. Alpine strawberry with white fruit; fruit of same size, but rounded; plant also productive. b. Alpine strawberry sana filets or Gaillon strawberry; fruit slightly smaller. Plant does not produce coulans. c. Strawberry from the Alps without nets and with white fruit; superior to the preceding one for the abundance and quality of its fruit. 3° FRAISIER DES JARDINS, DE MONTREUIL (f. vesca hortensis); its fruit is fairly large, elongated, often compressed and terminated by two nipples. It produces only once. a. De Bargemont or majaufe (f. vesca cœesalpina, aut bifera); smaller than the preceding one and with two seasons. Its fruit is very good, but its musky flavor means it is not to everyone's taste. We found it growing naturally near Coulange-sur-Yonne. 4° FRAISIER VERT D'ANGLETERRE, FRAISIER DE CHAMPAGNE (f. vesca viridis); its fruit is very watery, brownish on the sunny side and whitish on the other; mediocre quality. 5° CAPERON, CAPITON, CHAPERON (f.vesca moschata); large fruit, of very mediocre quality. a. Caperon with elongated fruit; better than the previous one. b. Apricot caperon; very good. c. Raspberry caperon; good, but a little watery. d. Male caperon; few hermaphroditic flowers; produces few fruits. 2008 e. Prolific Hautboy; very large, sun-colored, very smoky fruit. This variety comes from England, where we brought it back this year. We have kept its English name, along with two other new varieties. 6° ANANAS (f. ananassa); very large, attractive fruit, not very fragrant and watery. -° ÉCARLATE OR VIRCINIA STRAWBERRY (f. virginiana); early, crimson-red fruit, grows well under frame; it is good, fragrant, and fruits twice a year. a. Strawberry with pink fruit; fruit is very pale red, of medium size and pleasant flavor. 8° FRAISIER DE LA CAROLINE (f. caroliniana); fruit is shiny, colorful, fairly large and very good. This species is the most widely grown in England. a. Bath strawberry (f. caloliniana milleri); fruit very large, not very colorful and not very fragrant. b. Imperial hean's strawberry; fruit almost as large as the Chilean strawberry, quite bright red on the sunny side, and very pleasant flavor. c. Pine scarlet strawberry; fruit of medium size, bright red, very smoky. d. Strawberry prince noir; fairly large, dark purple fruit; very pleasant flavor. 9° FRAISIER DU CHILI, FRUTILLER (f. chiloensis); this species is dioecious and therefore only produces when both male and female are present. Its fruit is very large, but not very pleasant and not very fragrant. a. Fertile Chilean strawberry; flowers all hermaphrodite; fruit smaller than the preceding ones, more abundant, with a more pleasant flavor. 10° FRAISIER DES INDES (f. indica); yellow flowers; oval, rounded, brilliant-red fruit of very poor quality; seeds spaced and protruding, revealing the smooth, shiny pulpy body between them; calyx borne on an involucre with five very large, crenellated lobes, forming a collar around the fruit, and contrasting pleasantly with the strawberry's color by its beautiful green 3008. This plant loves humidity. We still have many new varieties sent to us from various countries, but we refrain from talking about them because we have not yet seen their fruit. Five-threshold, double-flowered, Swedish dwarf and single-leaved Versailles strawberries are grown as ornamental plants. All strawberry plants multiply by seed, splinters or by their creeping, rooted stems, known as coulans or nets. They do well in all soils and at all exposures, but prefer light, warm, half-shaded soil. As soon as the strawberries have reached perfect ripeness, they are picked and placed on a plank in a draught to dry out quickly. The seeds are removed by rubbing and loosening with the hand. You can also crush the strawberries, wash them in various waters, and collect the seeds that precipitate to the bottom. Immediately sow them in a terrine full of heather soil, mixed with a third of highly consumed potting compost. The seed is not buried, but simply pressed against the soil with a wooden pallet or your hand. Some people cover the seedling with chopped moss, and water over it with a very fine sheaf. If you wish, you can leave it uncovered and water from underneath, dipping the base of the bowl into a tub of water. Seedlings of any kind should always be kept in the shade, until the little plants have grown strong enough to brave the sun's rays. You can also sow in light, loose soil, or wait until spring to sow in a terrine; but in the first case, very few plants germinate, and in the second, the seed takes a very long time to germinate, and much of it is lost. The seedling will germinate in less than a month, and when the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted into well-tilled, top-soiled soil, spacing the plants six or eight inches apart, a little more if you have sown the broad-leaved varieties. We hoe and water often, especially when the plant is young, and we scrupulously remove its runners, so as not to let it become sluggish, especially in the early stages. When you have a bed of strawberry plants, whether you have propagated them from seed or by sprouting, every year at the start of winter, cover the bed with a fingerful of consumed manure, just as it is when you undo a layer; In spring, we bury it by hoeing and replace it with finely chopped straw, covering the soil to prevent it from being beaten up by rain and watering, to preserve its humidity, and to prevent the strawberries from getting dirty by touching the soil. This precaution also keeps slugs away. Every two or three years, in spring, or better still in autumn, the strawberry plants are removed, the rooted eyelets are separated, the stumps are broken up, and, after spading and smoking the bed where they were, they are carefully replanted; they are watered and sheltered from the sun by mats supported on poles and stakes, until they recover perfectly. Autumn planting is more prone to melting than spring planting, but it also produces as early as the following spring. If the multiplication of a precious plant were more important than its fruit, we'd leave it its coulans, and each rosette of leaves it provided would put down roots and form a new plant. To obtain early strawberries, we take 1 plants from the previous sowing, or, if we haven't sown them, early varieties such as the Alpine strawberry, n° 2, and the Virginia strawberry, n° 7; we put three of them in a pot in August, and let them recover in the shade, with moderate watering so as not to etiolate them. In September, place them in a temperate layer under a frame, and keep them warm with heaters. If they are given adequate air and watering, they will start to produce fruit in January, and continue to do so until you have strawberries in the ground. They can also be planted in the open ground with good exposure, and covered with a frame in February to produce strawberries in April. In England, the Horticultural Society is working hard to perfect the cultivation of this delicious fruit, of which it has obtained a large number of new varieties. They plant from one to two feet apart, depending on the size of the plant. Precious species are lightly buttressed once flowering has passed; they are mulched as we have said, or slates or tiles are laid under each foot to support or keep clean the fruit, which their weight drags to the ground. These different methods avoid the inconvenience of washing strawberries, which takes away some of their fragrance. strawberry, spanish lentil. (Lathyrus sativus; L.) O. Indigenous. This plant has been given almost as many names as there are provinces where it is grown: poisgesse, pois carré, pois breton, lentille carrée, garoute, garousse, gesse-blanche, etc. Its seeds are eaten while still green at the end of the day. The seeds are eaten while still green, like peas, and when dry, they make quite good purées. But it's especially when grown in large quantities as fodder that this plant really comes into its own. It does fairly well in all soils, provided they are not very2 damp. In southern France, it can be sown in autumn, and in temperate climates in March and April. In gardens, it is grown in exactly the same way as peas. OKRA, EDIBLE KETMIA. (Hibiscus esculentus; L.) Q. From India. Its flowers are quite similar to those of the marshmallow. This plant, widely used in America, is beginning to be cultivated advantageously in France, especially in the south, where its seeds ripen every year. Its young shoots and still-green fruit are used in cooking. In February, we sow it in pots placed on a warm layer under a frame; in March, we put it on a new layer, and in May, we unpack it and plant it with the rootball at the foot of a wall in the south, in a very warm spot. Take care of it, and above all, water it abundantly. COMMON BEAN. (Phaseolus vulgaris, L.) O. From India. We know enough about the qualities and uses of beans to make it unnecessary to talk about them here. An infinite number of varieties are grown, but most of these are due to differences in soil and climate. We will therefore only mention the selection made by our colleague M. Vilmorin (1), adding a few varieties we think are of interest. 1° HARICOTS WITH RAMES; these are climbers, and we are obliged to support their stems with poles, which is called rowing. A. Those with white seeds: a. From Soissons; large and flat, very good dry, but not acquiring in Paris the qualities it has in Soissons. (1) We invite amateurs and gardeners who wish to be sure of the quality of their seeds, and especially to receive the varieties requested, to apply for all vegetable plants, cereals and forages, to Maison Vilmorin, quai de la Mégisserie, Paris. 1008 b. Sabre d'Allemagne, podded; medium size, flattened; its broad pods are excellent when green, and still good when semi-dry; the seed, dry or green, is the best of all bean varieties; its stems rise very high. c. Prédome, prudhomme, white prodommet without parchment; its pod is excellent when green, good when semi-dry; the grain is valued when dry. d. Sophie; medium size; good in green, flat, thick-skinned and hard, of mediocre quality. e. Riz; very small and oblong; good in green, newly shelled, and dry. f. De Lima; thick, large, dirty white; it produces a lot, but is late and delicate, and rarely ripens in the Paris climate unless it has been advanced in pots and on layers. g. Hatif; of medium size, the earliest of all; very good in green, but a little hard-skinned when dry. h. De Picardie or de Liancourt; very large, flattened; high yield, but a little hard-skinned when dry; resembles Soissons, but larger and taller. i. Round or pea bean; soft and tasty; numerous pods, good when green; very small, rounded, russet-white beans, very good when dry. j. Gigantic; larger than the Picardy bean, resembling it but producing less; needs oars of eight to nine feet. B. Colored grains: k. Prudhomme jaune; similar to white except for color, but less prized. l. De Prague, or red pea; round, violet-red; late, but high yielding; good in green; dry, it has

The skin is a little thick, but it is floury and has a pleasant flavor similar to that of chestnuts. m. Prague bicolor; round and variegated; same qualities. n. Belly of doe; tawny yellow; less rounded; good in green and dry. o. Grivelé; flax grey with black speckles; good in green, very008 productive; dyes the water in which it is cooked black. p. Purpurin marbled; reddish with brown speckles; good in green. q. Pink; good in green and dry: but low yield. r. Red; good green and dry; must be paddled high to produce much. s. Cardinal; white with a broad purple crown around the germ; good green; very large, flattened kernels; ripens late and with difficulty. 2° DARK BEANS; self-supporting and therefore do not need to be branched. In wet soils, in free soil, and during rainy years, dwarf trees tend to climb like those with oars. To keep them free, they should be sown as much as possible in dry, light or sandy soil. A. Those with white grains: a. Hatif de Hollande; its long, narrow pod is excellent for green cultivation; it is one of the earliest of the dwarfs, and does well under sowing frames. b. Hátif de Laon or flageolet; long, cylindrical, very hasty, very widespread, preferred for sowing under sowing frames; excellent for green cultivation and quite good for dry cultivation. c. De Soissons, or gros-pied; very hasty, flattened, of medium size; excellent freshly shelled, and for dry cultivation. la d. White without parchment; flattened, small, very good green and dry. e. Sabre dwarf; similar to the previous one, but with a wider pod; it has the same qualities. These two varieties form large, low, branched clumps, whose very long pods sometimes drag on the ground and rot if they come into contact with moisture. Two or three seeds per clump should therefore be sown in dry soil. f. Blanc d'Amérique; small, slightly elongated, very good dry, productive; its pod, large and slightly arched, is colored reddish-brown at both ends. g. Red Swiss; same shape as above, but with red grain, mottled with different colors depending on the soil; good for green and dry growing. k. Grey Swiss; same shape; blackish-red grain, inlaid with white; excellent in fresh or dried pods for winter storage. l. Suisse gris de Bagnolet; grayish background, spotted with black and brown; same qualities as above. Both are widely grown around Paris. m. Swiss belly, same shape as above; more prone to bolting; good in green; mediocre in dry, but makes excellent purée. n. Black or negro; small, similar to Swiss; early, high yielding; excellent in green. o. Rouge d'Orléans; red, small, flattened; excellent dry. p. Jaune du Canada; very small plant; rounded, pale yellow kernel with a small brown crown around the umbilicus; very early, no parchment; good green. q. Brown-yellow; round, medium size; productive; good dry. r. From China; fairly large, rounded, pale sulfur-colored; very productive; excellent dry and freshly shelled. s. Yellow without parchment; small, oval, very early, arched pod; excellent green and dry. It is very prone to rising and consequently to degeneration. When it rises, the grain turns from yellow to black. t. Yellow with parchment; even earlier than the previous variety; high yielding; good dry. u. Red without parchment; large clump, very productive; good green and dry; excellent for purée. We have indicated the qualities of the varieties we have just mentioned, but amateurs will do well to stick to the species recognized as the best in their 2009 province, as the change of climate has such an effect on beans that those we have indicated as excellent could become mediocre, and vice versa. However, this does not always happen. The SPANISH BEAN (phaseolus coccineus, ENCYCL; multiflorus, willp.) O, from the West Indies, is a species that was first cultivated for ornamental purposes, but which was transferred to the vegetable garden as soon as its qualities were known. It has two varieties, grown like pole beans. 1° With scarlet flowers; of mediocre quality; large, flax-gray speckled with black. 2° With white flowers; same shape and size; mealy, bark a little thick, but good dry. Produces a lot. In general, beans like light, slightly cool, very loose soil, amended with consumed fertilizers. In clay and oily soils, sow later, smoke more and cover the seed less. Sow from April to May for dry harvesting, and to July for green eating. As they are very sensitive to cold, this is the plant to choose for each country and terrain. In the same climate, it is sometimes necessary to delay sowing by fifteen or twenty days in cold, damp soils, and to bring it forward by the same amount in warm, dry soils with good exposure. Sow in clumps in light soils, to shade the plants and keep them moist, or in rays in heavy soils. In the first case, holes two or three inches deep are made in a chessboard pattern, eighteen inches apart. Five or six beans are thrown in, and covered with an inch of very fine soil, or better still, potting compost. If humidity or cold weather (4000°C) made you fear that not all the beans would rise, you would throw more into each hole, but with the precaution, when they do rise, of leaving only the number we have mentioned. To sow in rows or rays, twelve or fifteen inches apart, open furrows with the hoe, and throw grains in one by one, spaced about three or four inches apart. Cover as described. If rain or sprinkling beats the soil, the crust is broken with a two-pronged trowel, to facilitate bean emergence, otherwise the seed will perish between two plots. Fifteen days after sowing, they should all have emerged. When the plants are a month old, they have already been hoeed once, and we give them a second hoeing by ridging them; for this last operation, we pull the soil left at the edge of the holes or on the sides of the sowing furrows together around the feet, we row them, and everything is kept there until harvest, unless rain or other circumstances have caused weeds to grow, which we pull out by hand as they appear. To obtain seeds, choose the most honest and first-sown plants. If they are pole beans, we pick the pods as they ripen; but if they are bush beans, we leave them all to ripen on the plant, then pull off the stalks, which we bundle together and hang in a sheltered spot. Seeds kept in their pods are good for four years; shelled, they stop germinating in the third year. Early market. Green beans can be purchased from May until the full beans are ready to bear, and without much care. All you need to do is choose the earliest dwarf species, sow them in February in pots on a warm layer and under a frame; at the end of April, carefully remove the seeds and plant them at the foot of a wall with good exposure. They are quick to produce, especially if covered at night with glass cages or straw hives, etc. If you want green beans over winter, sow in the same way, but in December and January; a month after sowing, put the plants back in larger pots and on a new bed. When the beans are ready to bloom, they are carried to a deaf layer, planted in eight or ten inches of well-consumed compost mixed with half light earth, covered with trays and given air whenever possible. At the same time, beans are sown in pots large enough for the plants to develop fully, and placed on the shelves of a greenhouse near the stained-glass windows, where they are left until harvest. Finally, the method most commonly used in Paris consists of sowing them in tarpaulin, in eight or ten inches of potting soil spread on a layer prepared with one third old manure and two thirds new, and cultivating them in the same way as those grown in the open ground. Sow in January and February, and heat when necessary. CULTIVATED HOPS. (Humulus lupulus, L.) 2. native. This plant is widely cultivated, because its flowers are used to make beer; in cooking, only its young shoots are used, which are prepared in the same way as asparagus. We mention it here only because it is found in many gardens, where it has been brought to cover arbors, cradles, palisades, etc., and we could make use of the surplus of its shoots. It can be propagated with root flakes in spring or autumn, and planted three or four inches deep in light, loose soil amended with fertilizer. HYSSOP OFFICINALIS. (Hyssopus officinalis; L.) 6. This plant from southern France has several varieties, with white, pink, red or blue flowers and variegated leaves. Its sweet, aromatic scent has long been used to form borders in the kitchen garden. It likes substantial, open, light soil and sunny exposure. It can be propagated from seed sown in March, from cuttings taken in summer, or even better from cuttings taken in autumn. When planted in borders, it must be replanted every two years, or it will spread and form unpleasant gaps. LETTUCE. (Lactuca sativa; L.) Q. From Asia. It has given rise to two garden species, the PUMPKIN LETTUCE and the ROMAN LETTUCE, from which we have obtained more than fifty varieties, but which can be reduced to an infinitely smaller number if we consider this plant only in terms of its usefulness in the vegetable garden. We could have expanded the list of lettuces by many more varieties, but as most of them fall within those we have described in terms of use, quality and the times at which they can be cut, we felt that more quotations would be as tedious as they would be useless. We must also point out that each variety has its own particular terrain, where it succeeds very well, while in the others it loses its qualities, shape and color. So, to make a choice, you should base your decision less on what we have said here than on your experience of your soil, and stick to the varieties that do best there, after having tried them out. CULTIVATION. Cultivation varies according to variety and period, but can be generalized as follows: 1° Spring lettuces. Sow in February and March, on a bed or in the compost of a flowerbed facing south. In April, if the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted into a bed of soft, well-smoked soil. The rows are spaced more or less apart, depending on the size of the variety, but the interval between each lettuce can be no less than seven to eight inches. When planting, care should be taken not to press the heart of the plant into the ground, and to press the soil only moderately around the stalks. Water immediately afterwards, and moisten whenever necessary. When the lettuces have recovered, they are given facons with a serfouette, and the pores of the soil are kept open by weeding until they are ready to be cut. 2° Summer lettuces. They should be sown in the same way as spring lettuces, starting in the first days of April and sowing again every two weeks, so that the lettuces follow one another throughout the season. We don't stop sowing until July. Give them the care we mentioned above, and don't spare the watering if you want them to be tender and sweet. 3° Winter lettuces. We start sowing them immediately after summer lettuces, and continue until mid-September. At the end of October, they are transplanted into a bed at the foot of a south-facing wall, and protected from frost and snow by covering with straw mats or dry litter, which is removed whenever the weather permits. Romaine lettuces can be sown and cultivated in exactly the same way as head lettuces, with the only difference being that to blanch them, the species that don't cover themselves are tied with one or two bundles of straw. Bind only when the weather is dry and the leaves have no moisture. From then on, you should refrain from watering the leaves. To obtain good lettuce seed, you need to choose the least degenerate plants as carriers, offering all the characteristics of the variety. As soon as the seed is seen to be crowned with its aigrette and the leaves of the plants turn yellow, the stalks are removed and exposed to the sun, standing upright against a wall, for a few days, which completes the ripening of the seeds. The stems are beaten up, the seed is winnowed and stored for three or four years. Two-year-old lettuces are always the best to sow if you want plants that pump easily. A number of lettuces require special care, which we shall now explain. Dolphin will grow well in any soil, but it needs a lot of water, and the suckers it grows around its first leaves must be removed. The 1 batavia only works well in light, free soil, and needs frequent watering, but in the evening and morning, because if you wet it during the day it will stain and rot. Flagellates and black seeded bloodroot do well only in soft soils, and only flower in spring. Green Genoa lettuce needs watering only when it's badly needed, but wants to be hoeing often. Big red lettuce acquires all its qualities only in oily soil, where it apples in all seasons. Italy, on the other hand, requires thin, light soil and little water. La royale requires a lot of watering. Cocasse should be grown in soils that are neither too strong nor too light, unless it is given a lot of fertilizer and watering; by planting it, it sinks a little deeper than the others, which causes it to fade. We won't go into further details, which must be learned from experience. PRIMEUR. By means of special cultivation, lettuces can be grown all winter long, choosing for each seedling the varieties best suited to the season. If you just want lettuce for cutting, sow pancakes, gotte and other small early lettuces, as well as chicory and spinach, all winter long on a bed under a frame or cloches. Small crepes and small black lettuces are preferred for peeling. From the end of August, we sow them on teens in a south-facing position, then transplant them on beds, where they head in December. The second sowing is done on a bed at the beginning of November, transplanting once when the plant has two leaves, then a third time in place on another bed; these lettuces head in January. Finally, the third sowing is done in December and continues until March, but with special care. The layers should be covered with four inches of potting soil, into which the seed is only placed when the most intense heat has passed, and with the precaution of covering the seed very little with soil and placing cloches or frames over it. A few days later, the seedlings sprout, and as soon as the first leaf (in addition to the cotyledons) begins to develop, transplant under other cloches, spacing the seedlings an inch or two apart, and reheat the bed if it has lost heat. As far as possible, lettuce beds should be kept in a state of tepidness, never more and never less, as excess heat and the slightest cold melt plants equally. Cover the frames or cloches with bedding and straw mats, so as never to let the frost penetrate to the plants, and provide air whenever the temperature allows. When the plant has four or five leaves, it is transplanted again onto another layer filled with six inches of potting soil, and spaced out sufficiently to allow it to grow unhindered. Give it the same care as before, and place heaters around the bed to keep it warm. When the apple begins to form, remove any low yellowing leaves, and press the soil against the base, making it grow more quickly. By sowing every two weeks and cultivating as described above, you can obtain head lettuces from December to February, and even later if you wish. Gotte can be grown in the same way, but with the precaution of giving it frequent airing when it is three-quarters done and ready to head, which crepe and petite noire can do without. COMMON LAVENDER (Lavendula spica; L. )b. From southern France. It is grown as a border plant in all gardens because of its aromatic scent: it is used in cleansing baths, along with a related species, LAVENDULA A LARGES FEUILLES, NARD, ou Aspic (lavendula latifolia, HoRT. PaRIS.) B, evergreen. Both like light, warm soil, and rarely multiply from seed sown in spring, but more usually from seedlings sown in spring or September. They are planted fairly deeply in the ground to ensure a good start, and the borders are re-done every three years. These plants are easily propagated from cuttings on a warm bed and planted in the ground once they have fully recovered. LENTIL, CULTIVATED, COMMON, LARGE, BLOND. (Ervum lens, L.) Q. From southern France. Highly cultivated, especially near Paris, either in open fields or in gardens. Its variety LENTILLE A LA REINE, LENTILLE ROUCE (ervum lens minor), much smaller, russet and rounded, is more highly esteemed, as is its sub-variety, which is slightly smaller and less russet. All three like light, dry, sandy soil; they thrive in strong soils, but produce few seeds. At the end of March or beginning of April, or in autumn in countries where frosts are not very severe, prepare the soil with a good ploughing if it is light, with two or three ploughings if it is strong, and sow in clumps, or in rays, or broadcast. If in rays, space them twelve to eighteen inches apart; if in clumps, place them ten or fifteen inches apart in all directions. If broadcast sowing, thirty pounds is enough for one acre. Simply cover the seeds with a harrow or rake, and weed them once or twice until it's time to harvest. When the stems turn yellow and the pods take on a dark color, the plant is cut or pulled up and left to dry for two or three days in the sun before being bundled and tightened. It is beaten to ex96 milk the seed as it is consumed, and in this way it can be kept very well for two years, either to eat or to sow. MACK, DOUCETTE, BOURSETTE, BLANCHETTE, SALADE DES BLÉS (Valerianella locusta olitoria, L.) Q. Indigenous. This small plant is eaten all winter long as a salad, along with its larger variety, mâche ronde, and the one known as grande mâche, grosse mâche, mâche d'Italie, whose leaves are covered with a short fuzz that is quite unpleasant for many people. Lamb's lettuce likes soft, loose, well-amended soil. As it bolts quickly, sow it every eight to ten days, from August 15th to the end of October. Don't be afraid to sow it thickly, as the most advanced can be picked as they are consumed, thus thinning out the plant sufficiently. Cover the seed lightly with a rake, and water if the soil is dry. That's all the care you need. The one sown in October will be very good in spring, and the others will provide for winter. As the seeds detach themselves as soon as they ripen, to avoid losing them, we occasionally shake the seed holders on paper or a cloth, and the seeds obtained in this way will always be the best. Then, when the leaves turn yellow, carefully pull off the stalks and pile them up in a cool, poorly ventilated place, to ferment them for two weeks, which is useful for the seed, which detaches itself by shaking them with a fork. It is then collected, air-dried for a few days, winnowed and stored for seven to eight years. It should be noted that this year's crop is difficult and late to raise, so it is better to sow the two- or three-year-old variety. MACREFLOTTANTE, CORNUE, CORNUELLE, TRUFFE D'EAU, CHATAIGNE D'EAU. (Trapa natans, L.) 7. From France, 1 where it is found in stagnant waters with rosette-shaped leaves covering the surface. In autumn, it produces a black, angular fruit with four hard, prickly points, whose excessively hard shell encloses a white pulp, quite pleasant to the taste and approaching the flavor of a chestnut. This fruit is eaten cooked in water or under hot ashes. As soon as it's ripe, it's thrown into the basins where you want to propagate the plant; and the only care left is to pick up the fruit as soon as it's ripe, otherwise it will fall to the bottom. If you want to keep them over winter and only sow them in spring, you'll need to keep them in a vase filled with water. corn, turkish wheat, spanish wheat, indian wheat, large millet (zea corn; L.)Q). From Peru. The benefits of corn seeds for domestic use are well known, but their use in cooking is less widespread. Corn is picked off the cob while the kernels are still soft and milky, and roasted over coals. In America, it is cooked whole in water and served hot on the table; fresh butter is spread over it, and the kernels are removed with a spoon. Several varieties are grown. 1° ORDINARY CORN; stems from five to seven feet long; very large ears, yellow, white, red, purple, or variegated with two of these colors. It is grown extensively throughout France below the 45th degree of latitude; further north, it ripens only rarely, and only in gardens. 2° MAÏS QUARANTAIN; much smaller and less productive than the previous variety, but with the advantage of being earlier, which means it can be grown a little further north. 3° MAÏS A POULET; only 15 to 18 inches tall, it produces even less, but is also earlier. Its ears are the size of thumbs, and its seeds are perfect for fattening poultry. (d In gardens, corn is sown in rays, from the fifteenth of April to the fifteenth of May. Ordinary corn should be planted two and a half to three feet apart, quarantine corn eighteen inches to two feet apart, and chicken corn one foot to fifteen inches apart. The seeds are placed closer together, but when they have risen, the excess seeds are removed. As the plants gain strength, they are stubbed out, and any buds that might grow at the bottom of the stem are cut off. Weed often. When the pistil-like hair on top of the spike begins to dry out, cut off the end of the stem where the male flowers are located. When the husk of the ear turns yellow and dries out, we take advantage of dry weather to harvest it; we break the pedicel to detach it from the stem; we bundle the ears after uncovering the seeds, and store them hanging from poles in an airy attic. Shear only when necessary. Corn likes strong, warm, calcareous soil, and especially well-smoked soil. LEMONGRASS, LEMONWOOD, LEMON HERB (Melissa officinalis, L.) 7. indigenous. These last two names derive from its aromatic scent. Some people include its young shoots in salad supplies and omelettes, to replace parsley, but it is not generally used in cooking. It is used to make eau de mélissse, formerly eau des carmes, and is taken as an infusion for certain illnesses. It thrives in light, warm soil, and multiplies by seed in spring, or by sprouting in autumn. Every year, its stems are cut back to the ground at the first frost, and this simple method keeps it vigorous in the same place for a very long time. There are several varieties for the ornamental garden. MELON (Cucumis melo, L.) Q. From Asia. We won't repeat 4 DU JARDIN what has been said a thousand times about the excellence of this fruit, because there isn't one of our readers who hasn't been able to judge it. Palladius, who lived in the fifth century, left us some fairly good details on its cultivation; but this should be understood as referring only to the market garden or broccoli melon, for it was only in the fifteenth century that the cantaloupe was brought from Armenia to Italy, from where Charles VIII brought it in 1495. The first green-fleshed melon grown in France was brought from Africa in 1777 by a monk from Grammont, near Rouen. The other varieties were obtained by cultivation and by mixing their pollen. The number of melon varieties is so considerable that it would be extremely difficult to unravel their synonymy. For the past three years, we have been collecting and cultivating them all in our establishment, with the intention of publishing a monograph in which each variety will be described and illustrated. In the meantime, we're going to tell our readers which are the most widespread. The best are marked with an asterisk (*). I. BRODEN MELONS (C. M. Saccharinus) a. MELON MARAÎCHER, French, morin, common. It is round, or sometimes depressed from the umbilicus to the stalk, very crinkled, of medium size and without ribs; mediocre and febrile in the late season. Only a few years ago, it was the most widely grown variety in the Paris area. b. TOURS SUGAR; very crinkled, with red, firm and fairly sweet flesh. It varies in shape and size, resulting in three sub-varieties. 1° Small, round, with green rind and very sweet flesh. Sometimes no bigger than an orange. 2° The large, round, with faint but regular embroidery, 908 and fairly pronounced ribs; pleasant, vinous flesh. The long one, similar to the previous one, but elongated. C. SUCRIN WITH SMALL SEEDS; round, small, with red flesh; it is early and does very well under frame. * d. SUCRIN A UHAIR BLANCHE ; quite similar to the previous one, except for the color of the flesh; it is very melting and sweet. * e. DES CARMES; two varieties: one long, larger than the other, which is round. Their flesh is pale, but melting and vinous. The ribs are quite visible. * f. DE HONTLEUR: very large, elongated, with wide, pronounced ribs. Although its flesh is not very melting, it is vinous and tasty. g. DE LANGEAIS; elongated, of medium size, with ribs that are not very prominent and only slightly embroidered; its flesh is red, vinous and sweet. It has a smaller, slightly earlier variety. h. D'ANGERS; mediocre embroidery, variable shape and medium size; sweet and vinous. j. DE LA GRAVE; long, green, finely embroidered, evenly ribbed, medium size; flesh not very melting, very sweet. L. D'AVIGNON; sub-variety of the above; less long, larger, also sweet. * I. DE COULOMMIERS; very large; green, irregularly rounded shape; good quality. m. DÉSART; bright yellow, finely embroidered, with pronounced ribs; sweet flesh. n. DE RUGONNANT; two varieties, the long, the round, with blackish-green, well embroidered rind; fairly melting and sweet. To. DE MINORQUE; medium size, fine embroidery. II. MELORS CANTALOUPS. (C. M. Cantaloup.) ★ a. ORANCE, small, round, ribbed, light green or brown; orange-red flesh, hence its name, a little firm, but good; the earliest of all. * b. It has two sub-varieties: the small rosé and the small renegade. * f.
