DE MALTE A CHAIR ROUGE; differs only in flesh color, but is earlier. 1008 c. MUSCADE DES ÉTATS-Unis; very -small with melting green flesh. d. DE MORÉE, de Candie, de Malte d'hiver; fairly large and elongated; smooth rind; melting, fragrant green flesh. It is stored over winter on straw in a dry, sheltered place. e. SMYANE; small, oval, brownish-green dotted with yellowish; flesh white to greenish, mediocre. f. CRYPRE MELON; small, elongated, hairy until ripe, very thin rind, not embroidered, marbled; flesh very bright red, full, to the point that its seeds, small and elongated, are embedded in it: this makes it the heaviest of melons, of which it is also the best. It ripens in thirty or forty days, and wants to be eaten as soon as the sun hits it. It is the only species that never produces bad ones. It was sent from Cyprus in 1786 to the royal gardens at Brunoy. My father was entrusted by the governor, Mr. Cromot de Fougy, to care for it specially for the king's table. g. FROM PERU; medium size; thin, blackish-green rind; very white, melting, sweet flesh. b. DE PERSE or Odessa; very long; green rind with yellow stripes; green flesh: can be kept in winter. The authors also mention several varieties of melon, but they do not describe them, and we suspect that they are used twice. Melons de Fleuray, de la Caille, cramoisi (we believe Descombe mistook a watermelon, cucurbita citrullus, for a melon, and Génes, de Naples, de Florence ou Zatta; d'Espagne, monstrueux de Portugal, de Minorque, Succado, cantaloup noir hatif, cantaloup de Sèvres, id. du Querci, id. de Castelnaudari, id. de Pézénas, id. de Côte-Rôtie, id. de Bouvert, id. ananas, id. plat, id. turbiné, id. bossu, id. long; melons vert, d'eau (obviously a watermelon), d'Angoury, de Saint-Domingue, brodé, grand Mogol, etc. CULTIVATION. Cultivation varies according to the climate and the time of year you want the fruit, and we'll look at it from these different angles. 2. 26 O08 1° Cultivation in the open ground. It is only perfectly successful below latitude 43°. In warm, light soil with good exposure, small pits are dug ten or twelve feet apart, eighteen inches deep and as wide; at the bottom, a foot of consumed manure is placed, covered with six inches of soil, into which five or six melon seeds are sown, planted one to one-and-a-half inches apart. This operation should be carried out in March, April or the first few days of May, depending on the climate. When the seeds have risen, only two plants are left in each hole, and they are driven as described in the following article. 2° Growing around Honfleur. We dig comine pits for cultivation in the open ground, but give them a depth of two feet by two and a half feet, and make a sort of muted layer by filling them with hot manure, over which we spread nine inches of light, substantial soil. The beds are covered with cloches, and left to warm up for two weeks: then, around the middle of April, a few seeds are sown at a distance of four inches from each other; when the plants have three or four leaves, only two are left in each pit, and we continue to grow them under glass, as we will teach you for those sown on beds. 3° Growing around Paris. In March, good hot manure is used to prepare beds two and a half feet thick, four or five wide, and of indeterminate length. These are covered with eight to nine inches of potting soil, into which one third of light earth has been mixed, or two thirds of light and free earth for large species, and sown as we have said. Water lightly, and cover the seedling with frames, cloches, or verrines, over which litter and straw mats are thrown to prevent the layers from getting wet from rain or frost, which would chill them. As soon as the seedlings have risen, they are accustomed to the light by removing the mats; but the cloches and verrines must remain surrounded by litter until the temperature has become milder, and there is no inconvenience in giving them air, which is done by lifting the cloches a little on the opposite side to where the wind comes from. Every evening, we put everything back in place to avoid the night's chill. It's also important to protect young plants from the rays of too much hot sun. When the plant has four leaves, not including the cotyledons, we pinch the top of the stem, cutting it cleanly below the armpits of the last two leaves, and force it to grow two or three main branches. You then need to give the plant a little more air by raising the bells two or three inches, water it sufficiently, and continue to shelter it with litter and straw mats. The main branches soon overtake the cloches, which are then lifted and kept suspended on the plants by means of racks. It's important to note that sap doesn't get carried away in one branch more than in the other; if it does, we try to re-establish the balance as soon as we realize it, either by cutting the cotyledon on the more vigorous side, or by giving the greedy branch a tortuous position capable of hindering the circulation of sap; the weak branches, on the other hand, rest in a straight direction. If the whole plant seems to want to burst into useless branches, it can be stopped by removing the two cotyledons; but this extreme method should only be used to the utmost rigor, as it considerably weakens the vegetation. The main branches soon give 1 secondary branches and flower. Melon is a monoecious plant, meaning it produces both male and female flowers on the same plant. The former can be recognized by the presence of stamens without pistils, and by the base of the flower resting directly on a peduncle without ovaries or young fruit. Females have only pistils and are placed on a small, highly recognizable melon. Care must be taken to remove male or sterile flowers before fertilization has taken place, and to ensure this, they are not cut until they have wilted. A fruit is certain to be knotted when, with its flower dried out, it continues to grow. Only well-placed secondary branches should be kept, which can grow without tangling or crossing over each other. When the first fruits have set, prune the main branches, keeping them short or long, depending on their vigor. Only one fruit is ever left on the weakest ones, and two on the strongest; but for this to happen, the plant must be extraordinarily vigorous. The end of the branch is cut off two knots above the last fruit, taking care to keep the most beautiful ones, especially those closest to the stump. When these wounds have healed, i.e. five or six days after this operation, we repeat it on the secondary branches, and oh remove those that have no fruit: as for the others, we leave them with only one. In all cases, the best fruit should always be left: but if there were only one on a branch, and it was rounded on one side but not on the other, we would make two or three longitudinal incisions in the bark on the flat side only, and it would soon become rounded. Eight days later, the tips of the fruit-bearing branches are cut off, and almost all the others removed; this operation should be repeated whenever necessary. When the melons have reached a certain size, and the pruning begins, the season is usually warm enough to keep the plants uncovered for most of the day. If this were not the case, they should be given air as often as possible, in order to facilitate the healing of wounds and the development of fruit. We water often and little at a time, taking care not to wet the leaves, and we use pond water or well water, deposited forty-eight hours in advance in barrels, to give it the warmth of the atmosphere. As the fruit approaches maturity, only the essential watering is given, so as not to diminish its fragrance, and it is placed on a tile or piece of board to protect it from humidity. If you wish to hasten their maturity, you can cover them with a bell. 4° Growing around Lyon. Sow at the end of April or beginning of May, in the open ground and in capots. Capots are holes ten inches wide and as deep, filled with a mixture of two-thirds light, very substantial earth and one-third compost. Sow three or four seeds, cover with a bell, and surround with hot manure until the plants have gained strength. Then remove the cloches, some time after the manure, and cultivate according to the rules taught. PRIMERS. At the end of December, we prepare beds in tarpaulins or frames, and wait for the right heat to bury small pots filled with very lightly trodden potting soil. Two seeds are placed in each pot, and the frames are covered with straw mats to concentrate the heat and hasten germination. When the seeds have sprouted, leave just one seedling in each pot, and gradually accustom them to the light by lifting the mats, which are then removed and put back only at night and when it freezes. If you're growing under canvas, you keep the layers warm by reshuffling them in the usual way, and loading them with new tan; if you're growing under stretchers, you put in heaters from time to time. When the temperature permits, the panels are lifted an inch or two to give them a little air, but only for a few hours, and with extreme care to prevent any moisture from penetrating; this time is used to wipe off any moisture attached to the glass. When the plant has two leaves, not including the cotyledons, we pinch the stem above the second leaf, to obtain the lateral branches. Prepare new layers with good new manure, incline them slightly to the south, and cover them with compost mixed with one-sixth light earth; place the frames on top, if you haven't made them in a tarpaulin. As soon as the wounds have healed, make two holes in the potting soil for each frame panel, unstack the plants without breaking the rootballs, and push one into each hole, taking care to bury the stem a little and put on shoes to encourage it to grow new roots. From this moment on, treat the melons as we said above, taking care to keep the layers constantly warm, and to let in air whenever it's safe to do so, especially during flowering. For early cultivation, choose small, early species such as sucrin à petites graines, cantalouprouge, cantaloup sin hâtif, id. brûlot hâtif, id. noir des carmes, id. petit orange, id. petit prescott, which is one of the most successful, etc. Whether you're growing early or naturally, you need to keep melon varieties strictly separated from one another, so that their fertile dusts don't mix, causing them to degenerate. Even more scrupulously, they should be kept away from other plants belonging to the Cucurbitaceae family, especially cucumbers. However, when these plants do not flower at the same time as melons, there is nothing to fear. Melon ripeness is very difficult to recognize, but here are the signs you can most rely on. 1° Most of them ripen a few days early, which means they change color or hue. 2° The quene rims and tears as if it were about to fall off. 3° The fruit gives off a pleasant smell, especially in the morning. In spite of all these signs, we are often mistaken, because the quality of a melon depends on a precise point of ripeness, which for some types lasts only a few hours. A melon is judged to be of good quality when the characteristics of its variety are well indicated, when it is heavy, firm under the finger, well shaped, and when its stem is short and thick. Seed selection. Throughout the cultivation process, choose the melons that are the most straightforward in their varieties and the most beautiful. Let them ripen without picking, or even let them rot on the spot; collect the seeds, clean them, and dry them in the shade and in a draught. You can even collect the seeds of an excellent melon as you eat it. They retain their germinative properties for many years, but are usually kept for only twelve or fifteen years. You don't sow the previous year's seed, as it is more likely than the old one to produce over-vigorous plants that take on branches to the detriment of the fruit. Some people soak the seed twenty-four hours before sowing. The choice of seeds and how to prepare them have always given rise to ridiculous prejudices. Fifteen hundred years ago, Palladius (1) said: Odorati autem fiunt, si eorum seminamultis diebus inter rose folia sicca mergantur; and today, Lord Nynn's old gardener never sows his melon seed without first wearing it for a year in his trouser belt, which is hardly the same thing. WATERMELON, PASTEQUE, PASTEQUE CITROUILLE, (Cucurbita citrullus; L.) Q. Oriental. Two main varieties of this groovy fruit are grown, with smooth rind and sweet, melting, bland flesh that does not please everyone. 1° PASTEQUE D'ITALIE or Provence; round, very large, with green rind; red flesh and black seeds. 2° PASTEQUE D'AMÉRIQUE; smaller, slightly oval, with bark striped or spotted with greenish yellow, so as to represent ribs; whitish flesh, red seeds. These plants can be easily distinguished from melons by their rough, sharply-cut leaves. They are grown in the same way, the only difference being that they are no longer pruned once they have sufficient branches, and no fruit is removed. MELONGENE, EGGPLANT, MERANGENE, MAYENNE, VIÉDAZE. (Solanum esculentum, Dunal. )C). From South America. The fruit of this plant is eaten cooked between two dishes or on the grill, stuffed or with herbs, fried or in stews; its taste is weak and bland. Several varieties are known, not to be confused with the egg plant (Solanum ovigerum), which is unhealthy. 1° MELONGÈNE ROUGE A FRUIT LONG; fruit about the shape and size of a cucumber, dirty purple. 2° MELONGÈNE ROUGE A PRUIT ROUOND; very-small, round, compressed fruit, about the size of a finger; same color. 3° MELONGÈNE ROUGE A RUITOVALE; fruit less large than the first, paler, large at the base. (1) De Re rusticd, lib.4, cap. 9. 4° MELONCÈNE JAUNE A FRUIT OVALE; fruit pale yellow, oval, shortened. 5° MELONGÈNE JAUNE A FRUIT LONG; fruit of medium size, elongated, green when young, pulsing white, and yellow when ripe. All like heat and water. In the south of France, melongene is sown in March in light, warm soil, then transplanted to good, loose soil and planting beds, where it requires only watering to succeed. In Paris gardens, however, it requires more care. As early as February or the beginning of March, it is sown on warm soil under a frame or cloche. When the plant has emerged, it is transplanted into pots, which are immersed in warm layers until the temperature allows it to be placed in the ground, in a warm bed, at the foot of a wall facing south. Scrupulously remove weeds and water frequently. When the plant has emerged, it can also be pricked out in staggered rows, eighteen inches apart, on top of a muted layer of compost. This will ensure that you get good seed, which does not always ripen when cultivated using the first method. PEPPERMINT (Mentha sativa, L.) 4. native. PEPPERMINT (mentha piperita; L.) 2. English. These are the two species most commonly grown in vegetable gardens. The young stems of the former are used as salad greens, while the latter is used to flavor liqueurs and pastilles. They can be propagated by sowing in the shade and in light soil in March or April, or by suckering in spring, or by sprouting in autumn. They do very well in all types of soil, and are harder to destroy than to preserve. BLACK MUSTARD, MUSTARD SEED, ASH MUSTARD. (Sinapis nit.4 gra; L.) Q. Native. Its seeds are ground into flour to make mustard for our tables, and its young shoots are eaten in salads. It is sown in March, very lightly, in smoked soil loosened by two good ploughings. It is particularly successful in sandy soils and woodland clearings, in places where coal has been made. The plants should be uprooted as they turn yellow, which usually happens in September, packed in a dry place, and crushed only with a rod, so as not to crush the seeds, which keep well for four years. Oil of mediocre quality can be extracted. WHITE MUSTARD, WHITE SEED (Sinapis alba; L.) Q. Indigenous. Cultivated in the same way and used for the same purposes. NAVET. (Brassica napus; L.) g. Native. This plant, whose uses are well known to all, offers almost as many varieties as there are villages where it is grown. We will therefore indicate only a few, warning readers that, if their land is not suitable for those they choose, they will degenerate entirely within two or three years at the latest. We will adopt M. Vilmorin's divisions here. I. DRY NAVETS, with fine flesh that does not delaminate when cookedThey only succeed in lean, sandy, soft soils. a. Petit de Freneuse; half-long. b. Gros de Freneuse; longer. Le gros de Freneuse; larger and less good. c. Le Meaux; very long, tapered. d. Saulieu; same strength, blackish bark. e. Petit Berlin or Tellau. f. Baubry; the size of a feather pipe, long, brown. g. Cherouble, similar to the previous one, slightly larger. The last two are highly prized in the Lyon area. = II. NAvers TeNDars (called raves in most of our provinces). Less firm than the preceding ones, of mediocre quality, but less difficult on the quality of the soil. a. Des vertus; very-white, oblong, early and good. b. Des sablons; white, half-white, oblong. Des sablons; white, half-round, very good. c. Rose du Palatinat, pink collar; tender, sweet flesh. d. Gros long d'Alsace, enormous size; mediocre. e. De Clair-Fontaine; very long, half out of the ground. f. Blanc-plat hatif; compressed, mediocre, but early. g. Rouge-plat hátif, mediocre; similar to the preceding one. h. Gris-plat; very large; fairly good. i. From Limousin, rave, rabiole, turneps; large, mediocre, grown for livestock. j. Rave blanche de Bresse; longuc, large, mediocre. L. Rave longue de Bresse; very long, red at the neck, very swollen at the root, mediocre; grown for livestock. All these turnips do better than others in strong soils. III. half-tenor turnips. They are less firm than the first, more so than the second, and thrive in light, soft soil. a. Jaune de Hollande; round; yellow rind and flesh. b. Jaune d'Ecosse; similar to the preceding, but more resistant to frost. c. Noir d'Alsace; long, sweet and good. d. Gris de Morigny; obrond. e. Jaune long de Lyon; large, elongated, with yellow flesh and rind: good. CULTIVATION. All turnips like sandy soil. In fine, substantial, soft sands, they become large and lose some of their quality, which on the contrary increases in quartz and granitic soils, where the soil seems to be composed of nothing but debris from primitive rocks: but then they diminish in size. In free-flowing and very substantial soils, they take on enormous proportions, but are only good for livestock. 008 If sown in spring, they rarely succeed, despite the precaution of sowing only old seeds; but from the fifteenth of June to the fifteenth of August, sowings are excellent, and can even be extended into September for early species. The soil is prepared just before sowing, and broadcast as much as possible in rainy or overcast weather. The plants are thinned out to leave four to eight inches between each one, depending on the size of the variety; weeds are weeded and removed. When the turnips have reached their full size, they are hand-picked, the tops removed and fed to the livestock, and placed in a dry place to be used as needed. In large-scale cultivation, we simply make a deep hole in the ground, pile them in, and cover them with thatch to prevent rain and frost from penetrating. In March, the best turnips are replanted to produce seeds, which are harvested in August. Turnips keep well for two years. OGNON. (Allium cepa; L.) g. This plant, one of the oldest cultivated, is native to Africa. It was almost the main staple of the diet of the peoples of ancient Egypt, who esteemed it to the point of deifying it and using it as currency. Everyone knows its use. Onions, whatever their variety, like a substantial free soil, rather strong than light, amended a year in advance, because fresh manure causes them to rot: a well-consumed compost cannot harm them, even if it is spread only a short time before sowing. The soil should be well loosened by two or three ploughings, but the last one should be done at least fifteen or twenty days before sowing, as they fear freshly disturbed soil. As a general rule, onions are sown in January and February in the south of France, from January 15th to March 15th around Paris, and from March 1st to April 1st in the north. In the latter climates, you should always wait until the soil begins to warm up, and the fine weather is perfectly assured. In light soils, we walk or roll the soil to firm it up, then broadcast the seed a little thickly; we harrow with the fork, rake, or, which is better when we can do it, cover the seed with a good finger of compost. If you wish, you can use the roller only when the seed has been thrown. As the seedling rises, which happens after three weeks, you water it to help germination, especially if there's the slightest drought. If, on the other hand, there's a threat of snow or severe cold, we cover the young plant with litter or dry leaves. As it grows, thin it out in places where it is too thick, and transplant it in places where it is lacking, at least three inches apart. Some time before maturity, i.e. when the plant has more or less reached its full size, we cut and twist the stalk a little above the ground, either with our hands or by rolling, in order to interrupt the course of the sap, and to fix in the onion what would have been carried in the leaves. However, this operation is not essential, especially in good soils. As soon as the leaves turn yellow, a little before they dry out, the onions are dug up and left on the ground for eight to ten days to ripen, but in a place sheltered from the rain, although exposed to the open air and the sun's rays. In the Saône et Loire department, and particularly in the commune of Senozan, where this crop is grown on a large scale, the tops are preserved and, when they are dry, they are braided together to form chains of varying length and width, whose two ends are joined together. They are then hung from poles placed horizontally in the attic, a simple way of ensuring their preservation. Those to be used as seedbeds are chosen from among the finest and sharpest in their variety. In spring, in wet soils, and in November and December for other soils, they are planted seven or eight inches apart, two inches deep, and in August they set seed at the end of long stems supported by stakes. The seeds are collected as for the porreau, and keep for the same length of time. Onions come in several varieties that are grown in much the same way. In listing them, we will describe the slight differences in care required. 1° DARK RED ONION. It is grown in gardens as we have just said; but in the open ground, it is sown after the wheat harvest on a single ploughing, and left to nature, so to speak, with the only precaution of weeding to destroy weeds. Four pounds of seed are sufficient for one acre. 2° PALE RED OGNON. Same cultivation. These two varieties have given rise to the sub-varieties known as prriform or pear-shaped onions, red or white, depending on whether they come from the former or the latter. The latter is sweeter. 3° L'OGNON D'ESPACNE or de Catalogne; red, very large, pointed at the root and neck. In the south of France, it is very sweet, but loses its quality around Paris. Same cultivation. White sub-varieties. 4° WHITE ONION. Two sub-varieties. The coarse white, and the hátif white. The first is sown in February and March 1009; its shape is much coarser than that of the second, which is sown from July to mid-September and transplanted a month later, or better still the following March. It is covered in winter to protect it from extreme cold. It is weeded and watered a lot. These onions are very popular in Paris kitchens, where they are preferred to reds because they are milder. It has been noted that those sown in July never do as well as those sown in August. The early white onion has two origins: in one case, it is considered to be produced by the large white onion; in the other, it is taken from Cairo, and bears its name, but it is nonetheless the same onion, apart from very slight differences. 5° YELLOW ONIONS. Cultivation of the former. 6° L'oCNOX BLANC D'ITALIE ou de Provence. The smallest of all, hardly larger than a hazelnut. It is very soft and tender, and can be eaten green or preserved in vinegar. We sow it thickly and at different times of the year, so as to have it available at all times. Same cultivation, but more watering. 7° L'OGNON D'ÉGXPTE or bulbifere, differs from the others in that it more often produces bulbs than flowers on its stem, resulting in a different mode of reproduction. In other respects, it is cultivated in the same way. 8° OGNON PaTaTE or potato, rarely produces seeds or bulbils; it is propagated by planting, in May, an inch deep and eight or ten centimetres apart, the round and fairly numerous cloves that grow around its root. ORCHIS MALE, SATYRION, SALEP, SALAT. (Orchis morio, L.) 2. Native plant, which Dr. Marillac's experiments have led us to classify as a vegetable plant, along with ORCHIS FEMELLE; (orchis femina; L.). These two species grow abundantly in all meadows and damp places in the mountainous regions of France. Their spikes of white or purplish flowers are more or less tightly packed, and terminate on a straight, round, fluted stem five to eight inches high. When the plant has set seed, the two fleshy tubers that form the root are pulled out and left to dry in a heap on the ground. We then remove the film that envelops them, and wash them in various waters until they are perfectly clean. They are then boiled for five minutes in river water, and dried in a curry house or oven after the bread has been extracted. Stir them often, and put them back into the oven several times until they are perfectly dry. In this state, they can be kept for several years without spoiling. When you're ready to use them, grind them to the finest powder possible, pass the flour through a sieve, and use it as you would potato starch. The weight of one kilo is enough to prepare eight ounces of soup, giving it the consistency of ordinary rice cream. To date, we have not succeeded in cultivating orchises perfectly, and it is above all their multiplication that presents difficulties. However, by placing them in light, fresh, substantial and deep soil, similar to that in which they are found, and by using the same precautions to sow their very fine seeds as are recommended for those of auricula primroses and other plants that are delicate to raise, especially by sowing them as soon as they are collected, we may perhaps achieve more satisfactory success than we have so far. ORIGAN MARJOLAINE (Origanum majoranoïdes; Willd. Sp.) 6. From the Orient. This aromatic plant is used as a seasoning in some stews, and another species is cultivated for the same purpose: ORIGAN A COQUILLE or MARJOLAINE GENTILLE. (Origanum œegyptiacum; L.) From the East. This is an orangery plant. Marjoram does well in all soils; 08 is usually planted in borders, which are renewed every two or three years. Both species are propagated from seeds sown in spring, from cuttings taken in the shade in summer, and from splinters of the stems. ORPIN BLANC, TRIQUE ou TRIPE-MADAME, GROS-RAZINÉ. (Sedum album; L.) Q. Native. This greasy, rather tasteless plant is used as a salad ingredient. It can be propagated from seeds sown in spring in sandy soil with a warm exposure; it should be watered often if you wish to obtain a more tender plant. It can also be propagated from offshoots and cuttings. We grow three species: 1° Des Prés, which we have just named and which has given us the Belleville sorrel variety, with wider, less acidic leaves, and the larger, blister-leaf sorrel. 2° L'OSEILLE VIERGE, DE MONTAGNE, (Rumex arifolius; ALL.) 2. From the Alps. It rarely goes to seed, and its leaves are broader and less acidic. 3° ROUND SORREL. (Rumex scutatus; HoRT. PARIS.) 2. From Provence. Its rounded or heart-shaped, glaucous-green leaves are more acidic than those of the preceding varieties. All are cultivated in the same way, and do well in all types of soil; however, they prefer light, deep soil that is cooler than dry. They are sown in spring, in rays, borders or beds, in very loose soil, as their fine seeds would not germinate if they encountered the slightest obstacle. The seed is covered with half an inch of earth, on top of which a little potting soil is thrown to prevent it from fighting. You can cut them, or better still, pick their leaves, always taking the ones that are on the outside, six weeks after they have been sown. Those that don't 2. 2 D6 bear seeds multiply in spring by splintering. As heat greatly increases their acidity, we grow a few plants in a cool, shady spot, for use in summer. They can be stored for ten to twelve years. PRIMEUR. In the first days of November, prepare a warm layer of soil, on which you spread eight to ten inches of potting soil, in which you plant vigorous sonches that you remove with a little clod; or cover the plant with cloches, and give it air as often as possible. For forcing, choose virgin sorrel or Belleville sorrel. If they are completely deprived of air and light, their leaves wither and lose their acidity and taste. Seeds are collected from July to August in the same way as spinach seeds, and keep well for three or four years if left in their husks. CULTIVATED PARSNIP. (Pastinaca sativa; L.) g. Indigenous. The grossc, sweet, aromatic root of this plant is used to flavour soups. It is also sometimes eaten fried. There are three varieties. 1° LoNG; a long, white root of almost equal size throughout its length. 2° ROND or ROYAL; similar to a round turnip; earlier, and does well in shallow soil. 3° Le BATARD or DE SIAM; a little yellowish, holding, qoant the shape ¿ the middle between the round and the long. It is the most highly esteemed. Parsnips like deep, substantial, cool soil, especially well-developed, so that their roots can rotate at ease. They are grown in the same way as carrots, but the plant is thinned out more because the tops are much wider. As they have no fear of frost, they are left in the ground over winter, to be removed only as needed. In March, roots destined to provide seed are immediately removed and replanted in other soil, eighteen inches apart. It ripens at the end of August, and is good for only one year. SWEET POTATO, BATATE. (Convolvulus batatas; L.) 2. From the West Indies. This creeping plant produces large, long, soft, very sweet roots, with a slight pink taste, especially when they begin to fade; they are eaten cooked in butter or prepared like scorsonères. Their cultivation, very easy and widespread in America, is beginning to penetrate the south of France, where they can be planted in the ground. Paris has three varieties. I° A RACINES BLANCHES; small and mediocre. 2° A RACINES JAUNES; a little larger, but also little appreciated. 3° A RACINES ROUGES; very large, excellent, providing tubers weighing from five to twenty ounces. In Santo Domingo, they weigh up to ten pounds. CULTIVATION. In early spring, a warm bed is prepared in the same way as for melons, over which a six-inch-thick bed of light soil is spread. In mid-April, when the soil is only moderately warm, cut potato roots into one-inch-long sections and plant them on the bed two inches deep and eight inches apart. When the sprays have reached eight to ten inches in length, carefully unfold them, cut off all their leaves except those at the end of the stem; then plant them as we shall say. In the south, break up a plank four feet wide, and loosen the 008 soil perfectly. Draw two lines two feet apart with a chalk line, and open them into furrows, in which you lay the young plants obliquely, so that their roots are three or four inches deep, and only the end of the stem emerges from the ground. Space them two feet apart, so that they form a chessboard. If the soil is dry, water, weed and pull out weeds. No further care is required until harvest, which takes place in mid-October, except to water the plants generously whenever necessary. In the Paris area, to obtain a greater yield, they are planted in the same way, but on a muted layer covered with ten inches of good, light soil, mixed in equal portions with well-consumed compost. Finally, there's a more advantageous planting method for temperate climates. A sort of mound, two and a half feet high and as wide, is raised in the shape of a humpback. The potato roots are planted at the top, and the whole is surrounded with good warm manure stoves, which are renewed as often as necessary. When the young shoots are a foot long, cut off the leaves as we've said, tilt them and cover them with six inches of good prepared soil, but don't move them, and place new stoves, which are maintained until harvest. When you dig up the potatoes, you must do so with great care, as those that are a little crumpled will rot on the spot. The potatoes are then placed in a dry place, neither too hot nor too cold, to be eaten when they begin to give off a sweet pink odor, a sure sign that decomposition is imminent. The great difficulty with this crop is to keep the tubers to be replanted intact until spring. However, this can be achieved by selecting from among the most beautiful those that won't have the slightest scratch, and by burying them in beds, but isolated from each other, in fine, very dry sand, in a box that is protected from the slightest frost, the slightest humidity, and heat capable of making them grow. To do this, wrap the first box in very dry straw, place it in a second, and place the whole in a pile of broken straw or dry litter, which maintains a suitable temperature at the same level at all times. PIERCE-PIERRE, PASSE-PIERRE, BACILE, CRISTE MARINE, CRÊTE MARINE, FENOUIL MARIN, HERBE SAINT-PIERRE. (Crithmum maritimum, L.) 7. native. Little use is made of this plant, which grows naturally by the sea. Its leaves are preserved in vinegar and used as a seasoning. It is sown on a bed in March, or, as soon as the seed is ripe, in light soil, which is kept constantly moist and well exposed. Those sown on a bed can be planted at the foot of a wall facing south or east, or, better still, in holes in old walls or stone cracks, with the same exposure. It is covered with dry litter during winter, as it is delicate and sensitive to frost. Its leaves are collected in late summer. Its seeds do not germinate after the following spring. PERSIL. (Apium petroselinum; L.) g. From Sardinia. This is one of the most widely used plants in cooking for seasoning and garnishing. There are several varieties. 1° COmUN (vulgare), which everyone knows. 2° FRISÉ, or CRÉPU (crispum), whose seeds often produce ordinary parsley. 3° MaiN TRèS-FRISÉ; very slow to bolt. t 4° Panaché (variegatum); freezes easily; yellowish-white leaves. 6° WITH LARGE LEAVES (latifolium); prone to abortion. 6° WITH BIG ROOTS (tuberosum); the root is eaten. 7° OF NAPLES OR PERSIL-CÉLERI; much larger than the other varieties, and the ribs are eaten when blanched like celery. Parsley can be sown from March to August, in any kind of soil, provided it has been well loosened, in rays, beds, borders or broadcast. Its seeds will sprout in three weeks if watered, which should continue to be done until it is strong enough to be left to nature. The Naples and large-rooted varieties should be thinned out so that they are about a foot apart. The first should be ridged as winter approaches, and the other pulled out to keep in the greenhouse. Seeds are good for two years. PICRIDIE COMMUNE, CULTIVATED, PLASTERED EARTH). Picridium vulgare, H. P.) Q. This native plant, frequently used in Italy, has been naturalized in our vegetable gardens by M. Vilmorin. It is sown in April in light, warm soil, and given frequent watering as soon as it begins to gain strength. While young and green, its leaves can be cut daily and eaten as a salad. ANNUAL PEPPER, CHERRY PEPPER, LONG PEPPER, SWEET BELL PEPPER, GARDEN CORAL, INDIAN PEPPER, GUINEA PEPPER, BRAZIL PEPPER. (Capsicum annuum, L.) (S. The fruits of this plant native to India can be preserved in vinegar, with or like gherkins, before they are ripe. When ripe, they are dried in the sun or oven, pulverized and used to replace pepper. For this last use, if you want to give it the appearance of real pepper, here's how it's done. When the fruit is still soft, we open it and scrupulously remove the seeds, cut it into slices and knead it in rye paste; we leave this paste to ferment for twenty-four hours, then put it in an oven, not to cook it, but only to dry it out. When it is hard and dry, it is ground to dust in a mortar, and served in salt shakers on the table. Pepper prepared in this way is known in the trade as indigenous pepper. Several varieties of this plant are grown, and are more widely used in the south of France than in the north; the main ones are the ordinary, round and large Spanish sweet peppers, all three with red fruit, and the tomato pepper, with yellow, sweet, tomato-like fruit. In the Paris climate, they are sown on beds in February or March, for transplanting in May, in good soil with a southern exposure, at a distance of one foot from each other. In the south of France, they are sown in beds from the first days of February, and put in the ground when the cold is no longer to be feared. Seeds keep well for several years. LITTLE PIMPRENELLE (Poterium sanguisorba; L.) 2. Grows spontaneously in almost all meadows in France, and is used only as a salad ingredient. In spring or autumn, it can be sown in rows or broadcast, in all soils, but best in light, dry soil; it can be weeded if necessary, and watered a little during hot spells. It can also be propagated by splintering old plants and replanting them six or eight inches apart, but this method is not as good as the first. It requires no care, and grows back the more often it is cut. Its seed is good for two or three years. PEAR, CHARD, RLETE. (Beta vulgaris; L.) g. From southern Europe. Two varieties are grown: the ordinary 9, whose insipid leaves are mixed with sorrel to correct its acidity, and the cardoon variety, whose thick, white, succulent ribs are used in cooking like those of cardoons. Poirée grows well in all types of loose, suitably prepared soil. The ordinary variety can be sown in rows or broadcast from March to August, and can already be cut six weeks after sowing, if it has been weeded and sufficiently watered. We note that the March plant does best in light soils, and the April plant in strong soils. As for chard, while not more difficult to grow, it does require some care. It is sown in March and April for autumn and winter production, and from late June to early August for spring production. Sowing is done very lightly, and is even thinned out if necessary, so that the plants are fifteen inches apart. Sometimes, plants sown in June and July are transplanted at this distance. The main thing is to water a lot if you want big, tender ribs. During severe frosts, we cover the plant with dry litter, and in spring we uncover it little by little so as not to let the sun overtake it; it usually starts to produce in mid-May. The seedbeds must be supported by stilts to prevent the wind from knocking them over; and when the seed has been harvested at its perfect point of maturity, i.e. when it turns from green to an ashen or russet hue, which usually happens in September, it can be kept for nine or ten years. PEAS. (Pisum sativum; L.) Q. Indigenous. A large number of varieties are grown, divided into: 1° parchment peas, i.e. peas whose pods cannot be eaten; 2° peas without parchment, whose pods can be eaten. Each of these divisions is subdivided into two sections, the first containing dwarf peas, the second those that must be rowed. We will limit ourselves to citing the varieties indicated by M. Vilmorin. I°. PARCHMENT PEAS. A. Dwarf peas, which therefore do not need to be rowed. a. The hative dwarf; eighteen inches to two feet high; the earliest of those in its section. Its flowers appear from the second or third node, and are followed by a small pod of fairly good quality. It can be sown from January for the first season. It does well under chassis. b. Nain de Hollande; smaller, producing as much, but less early: good for chassis, because it never rises. c. Nain de Bretagne; it rises only five or six inches. d. Large, sweet seeds; excellent; very productive, but late. e. Small green dwarf; very small seeds. f. Green Prussian dwarf; similar to the above, but very productive. g. Small Blois pea; rises from fifteen to eighteen inches. Its grain is good, small and smooth; early. B. Climbing peas, to be rowed. a. Pois michaux, Paris peas; excellent; very early. Sow before winter at the foot of a wall in the south; pinch at three or four blossoms. b. Michaux de Ruelle, also good; earlier, and slightly larger pods: same cultivation. c. Michaux de Hollande, Frankfurt pea; earlier still than the preceding ones, but more difficult to cultivate. Fears cold and damp soils. Sow in February or early March. If pinched, it can be grown without oars, like the two previous varieties. O08 d. Black-eyed michaux; very good, a little more hasty than ordinary michaux, and its grain is a little larger. e. Hátif à la moelle, English pea; very good; higher and with larger pods than michaux, to which it succeeds. f. Dominé; similar to the above, but with less rounded pods. g. De Marly; very large, but late. h. De Clamart, fine square; large, very productive, sweet; suitable for sowing in the late season. i. Carré blanc; tender, soft, unevenly shaped, more square than round; a little late and rising very high. In good soils, it produces only wood. j. Carré à œil noir; same qualities and defects as the preceding one; its foliage is a little less vigorous, but its flowers fear fog. L. Fèvo; very large, with mediocre quality seeds, though large; it is late. l. Géant; larger than the previous variety; very large seeds, not very sweet. m. Gros vert normand; late and large; very good dry. n. Ridé ou de Knight. This variety, named after Mr. Knight, President of the London Horticultural Society, is excellent but late. The pod is large and long, and the grain large, square and wrinkled. o. Sans-pareil; late, but yields long and abundantly; soft, very sweet kernels, good in green. p. Suisse; very large, very long pod, but few kernels; good. q. Laurent; less early than the dominé which it resembles. r. Grosse cosse hdtif; similar to the above, but stronger stem and more rounded pod; productive and of good quality. s. Baron; quite similar to michaux de Hollande, but higher. 2°. PEAS WITHOUT PARCHMENT, or EAT-ALL. A. Dwarfs. a. Hatif; a little high, but still cu chassis. 008 b. Ordinary; pods smaller, but more numerous than in the preceding one, very tender and esteemed. c. Fan-shaped; stems not rising more than a foot; late, not very productive. B. Sickle peas. .a. White with large pods, belier's horn, sickle pea; curved pods, very large and very tender; late, very productive in good soils; rises very high. b. Without parchment with half-stalks; as productive as the preceding one, less late and rising less high; pods less large, with tighter grains. c. With red flowers or eye peas. Red-flowered or pea with a wart-eye; grows to seven or eight feet, and produces a lot, but is very late; its pod is large and hooked, tender and sweet; its grain is greenish-red, speckled with violet, round, large and even. d. Turc or crowned; very tall; its numerous pods are tender and sweet. e. Turc with purple flowers; very attractive; same qualities as the previous one. It should be noted that our classification of peas into dwarf and row peas is not rigorous, as the lowest varieties can become climbers if grown in excellent soil, just as in poor soil and by pinching the ends of the stems, most of the other varieties can be maintained at a very low height. CULTIVATION. Peas do fairly well in all types of soil, provided they are not sown in the same place for two years running. It is even a good idea to let the soil that has produced them rest for two or three years. Fertilizers are harmful in that they make them produce a lot of foliage to the detriment of the fruit. They can be sown in clumps or in rays, like beans. In the first case, hollows are made a foot apart, and five or six seeds are thrown in; in the second, a furrow is opened with a hoe, and the seeds are thrown in two by two, about eight inches apart. Cover with two inches of 2009 soil, and eight or ten days later the seed sprouts. Water and remove weeds whenever necessary. When the plants are five to six inches high, they are re-shod, i.e. a little earth is brought up against their roots, and they are rowed. Early peas are sown in November in free soils, and at the beginning of December in light soils. Place them on a hillside facing south, or at the foot of a wall with a similar exposure. If the soil is dry and light, they are walked on, then covered with soil, and a little colombine is spread over them, making them stronger against frosts. From December 25th to 30th, you need to start sheltering them, at least during the nights. To do this, we use large pieces of bedding or straw mats, which we support at the right height using poles and stakes. As the cold increases, we thicken the blankets, but still provide air and light whenever the weather permits. If the peas are frozen, we have to be careful not to expose them to the sun's rays, which would kill them immediately; we have to wait for them to thaw. They are uncovered when the temperature warms up, but always leave the blankets close by to be used if necessary. In February, remove all the bedding or straw mats, crimp them, shoe them and, when they flower, pinch them above the third flower if you want to hasten their maturity. If the season is favorable, the same varieties can be sown in January, February and March; then continue with others, sowing for the second and third seasons. Finally, using Clamart peas, you can extend sowing into July, and even into mid-August; but these are likely to be surprised p by October frosts, at which time they begin to bear fruit. PRIMEUR. Sow in the first days of November, earlier or later depending on when you want to harvest, in baskets seven to eight inches high and ten to twelve inches in diameter, filled with a mixture of equal parts of soil and well-consumed loam. Twenty to twenty-five grains are placed in each basket, and after covering them with the soil indicated, an inch of crumbled croutin is spread over them. In this state, they are left to germinate and grow outside, in a warm spot, with the precaution of leaving the baskets buried, or at least keeping the soil constantly moist. When the frosts become severe, remove the baskets from the ground and bring them into a cold greenhouse, where they can be easily ventilated. As far as possible, keep the temperature of the greenhouse at zero, because it's enough if it doesn't freeze. More heat would make the peas grow and wither. Once the season has warmed up, we take them out as often as possible. In February, we place them on muffled beds prepared for good exposure, and in the manure of which we have mixed a little tannin to increase and maintain heat. The baskets should be buried in the soil of the bed, and spaced six inches apart. Needless to say, before placing them in the baskets, let the high heat pass through, as this could burn the roots. It won't be long before they bloom, and to hasten them even more, pinch them at two or three blossoms. In the event of frost, they can be covered with straw mats, which can be vaulted using barrel hoops and a few laths. If managed with care, they will bear fruit as early as the first days of April. For late-season peas, michaux should be sown in baskets at the end of August or in the first days of September, and trained as we have said, taking care to water them every other day if the season is dry. When they are five to six inches old, we'll bring them in, and when the frosts start, we'll take them out of the greenhouse whenever the weather permits. They will bear fruit from the first days of November to the end of December. Everything we have just said is in favor of people who don't own either a tarpaulin or a frame; as for those who do, they will find it much less difficult to obtain early fruit in all seasons. If you have a tarpaulin, prepare a warm layer which you cover with light earth and potting soil, into which you mix a small quantity of poudrette. Sow in November, and later pinch out three or four flowers. If you only have trays, sow in September in baskets as described above. When the cold arrives, or when you want to bring the plants forward, you transport the baskets in a warm layer of potting soil, and cover with trays. You can still sow thickly in December and January on the warm layer, then transplant to a new warm layer when the plants are three or four inches old. Plant them in rows six to seven inches apart, put two plants together, and space the holes four inches apart on the rows. Peas are grown under tarpaulin and frame in the same way as other early crops, i.e. they are given air whenever it is safe to do so. When picking peas for seed, choose the best varieties if you don't want them to degenerate quickly. Kept in the pod, they retain their germinative qualities for four or five years. CHICHE, CORNU or POINTU, CISERON, GARVANCHE, SEIRON. (Cicer arietinum; L.) Q. Oriental. In southern France, Spain and Italy, it is widely used in purées, soups and lentils. Three varieties are grown. I° YELLOW SEEDS; white flowers; fairly large seeds. 2° WITH WHITE SEEDS; white flowers; small seeds. 3° WITH RED SEEDS: pink flowers; medium-sized seeds. Sow broadcast or in rows from May to June or July, in perfectly loosened, well-smoked soil. They are covered and cultivated like regular peas, but the plants must be spaced further apart, as they spread out more. They are harvested when the pods are yellowish and almost

Then they are threshed, but the grain must be perfectly dry, otherwise it will crush under the flail. It retains its germinative qualities for two or three years. This plant could be grown in large quantities to provide fodder much appreciated by livestock. TER'RE APPLE, PARMENTIÈRE, TRUFFLE. (Solanum tuberosum; L.) 2. From Peru. A large number of varieties have been obtained. 1°. REDS. a. Truffe d'aotlt; round, red, pale, good and early. b. Ronde de Lalle; late, but very productive. c. Tardive d'Irlande or Américaine, or pomme de terre zuisse; round, excellent, keeping, without growing, until the next harvest. d. Corne de bique; long, very productive, but growing easily in the greenhouse. e. Descroizille; long, pink, keeping very well; excellent. 1008 f. Vitelotte; long, good, keeps fairly well. g. Rouge longue de Hollande; very good, but not very productive. h. Pelure d'ognon, langue de bœuf; the earliest of all, although it only flowers when the others do: good and hardy. i. Red long marbled; very similar to cow's white, but marbled with white and red; its tubers form a large cluster at the base of the stem. j. Red oblong; from Long Island; very fine, excellent flavor; does well in strong soils. 2°. YELLOW. a. Yellow gherkin, yellow holland; long, floury and very good. b. Chave or shaw; obround, large and very early; productive. c. Early dwarf; round, small, but even earlier than the preceding one. d. Yellow patraque; pale yellow, very large, mediocre; produces fairly well. e. Round-flat; sent from New-Yorck; floury and very good. 3°. VIOLETES. a. Round; very productive, fairly good. b. Black; round, not very productive, with a fairly strong odor. c. Dutch violet; oblong, with purple and yellowish spots on the surface: good, but not very productive. 4°. WHITES. a. Gros du Charollais; very productive; mealy and good. b. Patraque blanche; very large, productive, mediocre. c. For cows, wild, hardy, large white; the most commonly grown in the départemens. It is the hardiest and most fertile, and therefore belongs more particularly to large-scale cultivation. d. BLANCHE-LONGUE or Irish; not as large, but very productive and excellent. e. Souris or corne de vache; long, pointed at one end, rounded at the other, a little flattened; very white flesh, excellent: a little early. f. PETTTE BLANCHE, petite chinoise, sucrée d'Hanovre; small, irregularly round; not very productive, but very good. As for quality, these varieties are nothing if not consistent, as they depend largely on the nature of the soil. In sandy or light soils, and especially in dry soils, potatoes grow less large, but they degenerate less quickly and are much better; in strong, damp soils, their volume is greater, but they are more watery and pungent. CULTIVATION. At the end of March or beginning of April, sow in very light rays spaced fifteen to eighteen inches apart and dug in deep gullies. Cover with half an inch of compost and water. When the plant has risen, it is thinned out and the weeds pulled out by hand; then, when it is eight to ten inches high, the soil left on the edges of the gullies is poured around its stem; it is thinned out again if necessary, so that the plants are spaced about a foot apart. They are then ridged again. When their stems have acquired a foot to fifteen inches, they are ridged a third time. If the soil is substantial and light, the harvest will provide many tubers of ordinary size, and the others will be the size of walnuts. More generally, potatoes are propagated by planting whole tubers, or only cut pieces with eyes; large whole tubers are the most productive: at the same time as for sowing, holes or gullies are made, into which the potatoes are planted, either whole or in sections, on manure spread at the bottom of the holes; they are lightly covered with soil; and, when they have sprouted, they are cultivated and ridged like seed potatoes. It is essential to pull them up as soon as they are ripe, because if it rains, they will turn green again and lose all their qualities. PRIMEUR. From the first days of February, plant whole tubers of early varieties in a plant bed facing south, and cover with three or four inches of earth, on which five or six fingers of manure are spread. Once the plant has risen, it is protected from frosts that may occur, by throwing large dry litter over it every night, and sheltering it, if necessary during the day, with straw mats. If you want to have them in winter, in autumn you prepare a soil with equal parts of well-consumed loam and pure sand. Make a bed six inches thick in a warm cellar, and lay whole tubers on top. Oh cover with a new bed of sand, then place more tubers, and so on, until the layer is two feet high. It doesn't take long for the potatoes to sprout long, etiolated stems, which cover the bed and need to be cared for. Make a hole in the bed, and check whether the tubers are large enough to be used in cooking; then start pulling them out, attacking the bed on one side, and continuing each day to pull them out towards the other end. If planted in December, new potatoes will be available in February, and will follow one another until the harvest of those grown in the open ground, but the same qualities should not be expected. PORREAU, LEEK. (Allium porrum; L.) g. Native to the mountains of France and Switzerland. It is used only to give flavor to broths, purées, etc. Two varieties are known: the long leek and the short leek. Two varieties are known: the long variety, which is more widely cultivated because it yields more; and the short variety, which is more resistant to heavy frosts. This garlic likes light, substantial, well loosened soil that has not been smoked for two or three years. In February and March, sow thickly and broadcast, then walk if the soil is very light; rake and water from time to time, if necessary, to facilitate germination. When the plant has grown to the size of a feather-pipe, which usually happens at the end of June, take advantage of a rainy day to carefully remove it, or in the absence of rain, water it heavily and transplant it into beds six inches apart. The time of sowing, and therefore transplanting, can vary according to climate. For example, in the south of France, you can sow as early as January, while in the north, sowing is sometimes delayed until April 15th. When transplanting, which is done in six-inch-deep holes, roots and leaves are left intact if the young plant has not suffered; if the plant has suffered, only what is necessary to re-establish the balance of sap and vegetation is removed from one or the other; i.e., if the roots have suffered, the diseased tips are removed, and the leaves are shortened accordingly. Weed and water often, especially during the summer; this is the best way to hasten vegetation, and to have fairly large plants by July. As winter approaches, if you've grown a long porreau, pull it out and put it safely in the greenhouse, or place it in an open trench at the foot of a south-facing wall, and plant its feet in dry sand close together, but without letting them touch. Cover with dry litter to prevent the cold from penetrating to the feet. The short porreau can be left in place for use, but the plants intended to supply the seed should be carefully sheltered. In March, we carefully replant the seed-bearing plants, chosen from among the healthiest and most vigorous; they go up in May; and, when the seeds are ripe and threaten to fall, we cut off the umbels and let them ripen by placing them on a cloth, where we shake them from time to time. The seeds that fall off first are always the best and keep well for two years; those that remain in the capsules, if you only take them out to sow them, can retain their germinative virtue for three or four years; but they need to be sown much more densely, because they don't all germinate. As porreau wears out the soil a great deal, and perhaps also because the soil in which it is planted must not have been fertilized for a long time, it must be properly fertilized before any other crop is planted. CURED PURSLANE. (Portulaca oleracea, L.) O. From southern France. This bland, fleshy plant has given rise to a variety known as golden purslane, but which often reverts to its type, regaining its green color. Both can be used as salad greens when young; when a little older, they can be eaten cooked; sometimes they can be preserved in vinegar. At the end of April, in warm years, or in May, loosen light soil along a wall facing south, and sow lightly, barely burying the seed with a rake, or even better, just covering it with a light layer of compost. Water abundantly, especially when cutting, and always at the height of heat to preserve its golden color; this is also the best way to obtain more tender stems. For early plants, we sow in layers, under cloches or frames, from January to March, taking every precaution to avoid the slightest cold which would kill the young plants. In this case, the seed is thrown very lightly onto the soil of the bed, and is not buried: simply press it down with the back of the spade or a board. The seed should be collected from the most vigorous and first-sown plants. As soon as the seed capsules begin to open, carefully cut off the stems and dry them on a cloth for a few days, to allow the seeds to mature. They retain their germinative power for seven or eight years. RAIFORT SAUVACE, CRANSON RUSTIQUE, CRAN, GRAND RAIFORT, CRAN DE BRETAGNE. (Cochlearia armoracia; L.) 7. From France. The roots of this plant, grated and soaked in vinegar, are used to replace mustard; they have an extremely pungent flavor and contain starch. Horseradish grows well in all types of soil, but prefers slightly damp ones. It can be sown in spring, or its roots removed in autumn. It's only good in its first and second years; as it ages, the roots harden and become almost woody. Its seed keeps for two years. RAIPONCE. (Campanula rapunculus, L.) 2. This bellflower, which grows spontaneously in meadows and hedges, has been accepted in the kitchen garden because of its fleshy, tender root, with a very mild flavor. It is usually eaten as a salad, and sometimes cooked. Two varieties are grown, one hairy and the other hairless. This plant likes fresh, light, slightly shaded soil; it does well in soil amended with leaf compost. Sow in June, in a well loosened bed, then cover lightly with fine compost and water often until the plant has emerged. Weed it and give it a few more sprinklings during droughts. It overwinters without any care, and is used from February until it goes to seed, which happens in May. The seeds ripen in July and are good for three years. CAmPANUlE MIROIR DE VÉNUS (prismatocarpus speculum, LuéR.) O, is used for the same purposes and is grown in the same way, but in sandy soil, which is less cool and less shaded; it is best suited to potting soils made from animal matter. CAMPANULE GANTELÉE (campanula trachelium, L.) 7, is grown and used in the same way as the first; shade and coolness are favorable; and, if you want it to be very good, cover its seedling with moss and damp leaves instead of compost. RABE, RADISH, PETITE RAVE. (Raphanus sativus, L.) O. From China. Several varieties are grown, divided as follows: 1° RADISH. Round roots. a. The small gray radish; sown light, it holds up well all summer. b. Yellow; same cultivation, same qualities. c. Ordinary white. d. Petit noir; excellent for summer and autumn. e. Gros noir, or horseradish; can be sown from June to August, and will keep all winter if buried in sand or earth and protected from frost. Its flesh is dry and hard, with a very pungent flavor. f. Large white Augsburg; sown very lightly from May to the end of August. It requires a lot of watering. Except for the color, it resembles the other, but is less pungent. g. The hdtif white, good for sowing on bedding; on soil it is less delicate. POTAGER. i. Small pink or salmon. Id. j. Violet. Id. k. Red-pd̂le. Id. 1. soft red. Id. m. Red inside or red-fleshed. These last six varieties have many similarities, and are of excellent quality. 2° RAVES. Long roots. a. Long red or coral rabe; sow outdoors. b. Petite hd̂tive; particularly suitable for sowing in beds; red. c. White; not as sweet as the above. d. La tortillée du Mans; sow lighter than the others in soft, deep soil. e. La rave couleur de rose ou saumonée. Turnips can be sown and harvested every month of the year, but spring turnips are infinitely better than the others. As early as October, layers of hot manure are prepared, over which eight to nine inches of compost are spread. When the heat has passed, the early varieties are sown on top, taking care to walk on the compost if you want them to be round. When they have emerged, we thin them out. If it's freezing, cover the young plant with cloches, and throw litter over it as the cold deepens. It's essential to provide air whenever possible, because if radishes lack it, they wither and fail to form roots. Continue to sow in beds throughout the winter, using heaters if the cold makes this necessary. In February, you can dispense with covering with cloches, and in March sow in light, well loosened soil, covered with an inch of compost. When the summer heat begins to make itself felt, turnips usually become pi000 quantes or hollow; to prevent this, sow them in the shade, with a northern exposure, and water them a lot. If you don't care about raising beds for this little crop, you can sow radishes at the end of September, pull them out in November and replant them very close together on an ados of potting soil facing south; bury them up to the collar, and cover them with litter during frosts. Rabe seed can be harvested from the best varieties, and can last up to six years. WAVY RHUBARB. (Rheum undulatum, L.) 2. From China; and RHUBARBE RInEs ou GROSEILLE, (Rheum ribes) 2. From Asia. The ribs of these two plants, peeled and cut into sections, are used in cooking to replace the fruit sometimes used in pastries. The second species in particular is highly prized in England. They thrive in light, deep, slightly dry soil with a sunny exposure. They are propagated from seed, sown as soon as they reach maturity, or, more easily, from suckers separated in spring and planted three or four feet apart. Weed, hoe and care for the crop the first year, but in subsequent years, just give it a light ploughing in spring. If you want to harvest the root for medicinal use, wait until it is four or five years old; then pull it out in autumn, cut it into two-inch trunks, and dry it out in an oven at moderate heat. All that remains to be done is to join the pieces with a wood rasp, round off their angles and roll them in a barrel with their rasp. ROSEMARY OFFICINALIS. ( Rosmarinus officinalis, L.) b. Indigenous. Although this shrub is not used in cooking, it is found in all vegetable gardens, either because of its pleasant, aromatic scent, or because of the uses to which it is put. It is used, for example, to prepare water for the Queen of Hungary. We grow a variegated variety in white and another in yellow, both sensitive to heavy frosts. It thrives in light, warm, sheltered soil. It is propagated by cuttings, marcottes and splinters, rarely by seed, as this method is Iong. It is often planted in borders, which can be maintained for a long time in the same place, especially if mowed and watered. In countries above the latitude of Paris, it is wise to stuff it in winter. ROQUETTE. (Brassica eruca, L.) O. Indigenous. The young shoots of this small plant are used in salad supplies; but as it grows, its leaves become so pungent that few people can use them. It is first sown in spring, and then continued each month to ensure a year-round supply; summer sowing is done in damp soil and in the shade: moreover, it adapts to any soil and only requires weeding, thinning and watering as needed; watering in particular greatly reduces its pungency. The seed is harvested in July and August from plants sown in spring; it keeps for three or four years, and can be used to make oil. SALSIFIS, PORRIFLEAF CERCIFIS, WHITE or COMMON SALSIFIS. (Tragopogon porrifolius; L.) &. Indigenous. Its long root, white on the outside and inside, is larger than that of scorsonera and less prone to forking; moreover, it serves the same purposes. In substantial soil, well loosened and loosened, but not smoked, it is broadcast in March, April, May and even early June, and watered abundantly to help germination. A few hoeings are given, and in November we start to pull up roots for use. You can pull them up all winter long; if you're worried about the ground freezing too deep, you can pull them up at the end of December, place them in a greenhouse in fresh sand, or bury them in an open trench in the garden, covering them well during frosts. Those left in place can still be good in spring, until they go to seed. Seeds ripen in July, and germinate well only in the first year. GARDEN SAVORY, SAVORY, SAVORY, SADREA. (Satureia hortensis, L.) Q. From Italy. This plant is only used in cooking as a seasoning for marsh beans, peas and sauerkraut. The Dutch pluck it before flowering, bind it in bundles and dry them in the shade, thus preserving it for winter use. It does very well in all soils, where there is no need to sow any more once it has been sown, thanks to its ability to resow itself. It requires no care. It is cut and dried on the floor as soon as the seeds begin to ripen; preserved in their capsules, they retain their germinative qualities for four or five years. Mountain savory (satureia montana, L.), for the same purposes, and is propagated from good seeds for two years, or by sprouting in spring. SAGE OFFICINALE, FRANCHE. (Salvia officinalis; L.) 7. native. This plant, O in Paris kitchens, is used only to flavor hams and fresh pork; but in northern France and Holland, it is used in almost all seasonings. It thrives in all types of soil and exposure, although it prefers light, warm, slightly dry soil. It can be propagated from seed or cuttings, but it is generally preferable to burst the plants in spring and autumn, as this is the safest and easiest way. Care should be taken to replant them in a trough seven to eight inches deep, and to firm the plants by pressing the soil around the root collar. Seedlings are sown on a bed in spring, and when the young plants are strong enough, they are removed with the rootball and placed, usually in a border. Cuttings are taken in the same way, in spring and on a bed, with the precaution of watering them copiously until they are fully established; then they are planted with the root ball, in the same way as the cuttings. Sage stays in the same place for a very long time, but as it is easily stripped and forms unpleasant gaps in borders, it is usually replanted every three or four years. The PETITE SAUGE OU SAUGE DE PROVENCE (salvia tenuior, L..), grows in the same way and is used for the same purposes. These two species have given us the tricolored, variegated, small-leaved, narrow-leaved, waffled and curly varieties. To maintain their unique characteristics, grow them in lean, light soil. spanish scorzonera, black salsifis, spanish salsifis. (Scorzonera hispanica; L.) g. Its root is black on the outside, which distinguishes it from salsify; it can be eaten with white sauce, fried, etc. Scorzonera is sown in February, March and April, 9 or in late July and August, and is grown in the same way as salsify. It goes to seed quickly, but its root is no less tender. In very soft soils, it may grow large enough to be eaten in the year of sowing, but is usually only pulled up in the second year. Once the plant has come up and its seeds are ripe, the stems are cut back to the ground, and the collars immediately produce new leaves; the following year, the plant can still be preserved by the same method, and its roots, just as delicate, will be much larger. The best seed is harvested from two-year-old individuals; it is only good for one year. EDIBLE NUTSEDGE, GROUND ALMOND. (Cyperus esculentus; L.) 2 This plant, native to the marshy meadows of Spain, produces numerous tubers at its roots which, in many countries, provide a healthy food, a pleasant liqueur and oil. Its cultivation is somewhat similar to that of the potato. In March, in light, damp soil, furrows are dug six inches deep; the tubers are planted, having been soaked in water since the previous day, to swell them and hasten their growth. Cover with soil, and all the care is limited to a few weedings to destroy weeds. In October and November, the new tubers are removed from the ground, and those destined for replanting the following year are kept like potatoes. SPILANTHE CRESSON DE PARA, ABECEDARY (Spilanthus oleracea; S. VEG.) Q. Des Indes; and SPiLanTI CRESSON DU BRÉSIL. (Spilanthus brasiliana) Q. From South America. These two small, creeping plants have a pungent flavor that makes them useful in cooking as a seasoning. They are sown in beds in spring, and when the plant has reached a certain strength, it is transplanted into light, warm soil with a southern exposure. They require a lot of watering. They can also be sown in very loose soil, covered with half an inch of compost, against a wall facing south; but if the season is not favorable, they risk being surprised by frost before their seeds have ripened. TANAISIE-BAUME, MENTHE-cOQ. (Tanacetum balsamita; L.) 2. From southern France. The young stems of this aromatic plant are rarely used for salad dressing. It multiplies from seed in spring, more easily through its numerous suckers. It is planted in open soil in a warm location. TANAISIE COMNUNE (tanacetum vulgare, L.) 2, indigenous and used medicinally, can be grown in the same way, as can its curly variety. STALLED TETRAGON or CORN. (Tetragonia aut cornutia expansa; DESF. et Banx.) QQ. We owe this plant to Cook's voyages to the South Sea islands, where it originated. Joseph Banks brought it to Europe in 1772. It is used in cooking in the same way as spinach. In February or March, it is sown on a bed and under a frame; and when the frosts, to which tetragonia is very sensitive, are no longer feared, it is transplanted into good, light, fresh soil, against a wall facing south, spacing the plants eighteen inches apart. It also works well if sown in mid-April in open soil and good exposure. Maintain the soil in a constant state of moisture with daily watering. It can be cut up to four times during the summer, taking care to spare the hardiest plants intended to supply grainc. Seeds ripen in autumn. O8 DU JARDIV COMMON THYME. (Thymus vulgaris; L.) 2. this miniature shrub is native to Spain; it is used in cooking to flavor various dishes. Three varieties are grown: The common or narrow-leaved, the broad-leaved, and the variegated, which is cut at the time of flowering and dried for use as needed. Thyme from seed and its varieties from cuttings could be multiplied, but as it is much easier and quicker to do so by sprouting plants in spring, only the latter method is used. It can be planted in clumps, or used to form borders that are easy to clump, and renewed every three or four years. It likes light, warm soil and requires no care. LEMON SMELL SERPOLET, LEMON THYME (thymus serpillum citratum) is a closely related species, differing essentially only in scent. It is used for the same purposes and cultivated in the same way. TOMATO, LOVE APPLE, (Solanum Iycopersicum, L.) Q. From South America. Today, everyone knows the use of its red fruit, round or oval, plain or furrowed, depending on the variety. In February, it is sown in warm soil, and when the plant is strong enough, it is transplanted into light soil at midday, and watered abundantly during the heat. Plants of the large variety are planted two feet apart; those of the small variety, fifteen or eighteen inches apart. When the stems have reached fifteen or twenty inches in height, they are stopped by pinching. As a result, the fruit ripens earlier and better, and the plant needs less support. A few days later, we also pinch off the secondary branches above the flowers. When the fruits are about half their size, we remove the new buds that are developing, and remove a few leaves to let in the air and sunshine. Later, as the tomatoes begin to turn red, we remove all the leaves from the fruit. We note that, in the Paris climate, the large variety ripens with difficulty unless sown very early, whereas the small variety always succeeds, even when sown in the ground. JERUSALEM ARTICHOKE, EARTH PEAR, TUFTED EARTH, TARTOUFFE. (Helianthus tuberosus; L.) 2. From Brazil. The root of this plant produces large red or yellow tubers, depending on the variety, which are eaten boiled and prepared in a variety of ways. The stems, leaves and flowers resemble those of a sunflower. It is multiplied by its tubers, which are planted in March in the same way as potatoes. It does well in all types of soil, but prefers free-flowing, substantial soil, in which its roots can be left all winter to be pulled up as and when needed. The Jerusalem artichoke spreads very quickly, and is very difficult to destroy in the place where it is planted. If you want to collect seeds, you need to obtain tubers that have recently arrived from America, as the plants formerly cultivated in France no longer bear them. nigella sativa, pear or cultivated. (Nigella sativa, L.) Q. From the Orient. Its very aromatic seeds are used in cooking like spices, particularly in Italy and southern France. This plant requires light, warm soil and southern exposure. It is sown in April, thinned out so that each plant is eight inches apart, and watered frequently until flowering has passed. Its seeds keep well for several years. NIGELLE DE DAMAS, OU CHEVEUX DE PAYSAN, Ou PATE D'ARAIGNÉE, (nigella damascena, L.) O, from southern France, is still grown in the same way and for the same purpose, but its seeds are less aromatic. WHITE VETCH, LENTIL. (Vicia sativa; L. var. alba) Q. Indigenous. Its white grain is larger than that of the ordinary species; it is used to make purées, and its flour is mixed with wheat flour to make bread. It is grown like lentils, but is less sensitive to cold. It is sown in rows or broadcast at the end of February or beginning of March, and in southern France at the end of August. In all cases, hoe and weed, ridge young stems, especially when sown in autumn, and water if necessary. The seed is not collected until the pods turn yellow, and is dried in bunches before threshing. Seed collected from overwintered plants is always the best. setocococobecoecececececececeoteccccecececececececececesccceceso. OF THE FRUIT GARDEN. Nature has covered every part of the earth with a large number of species of fruit trees; each climate has its own, which can only succeed in the places where they originate. For example, date palms, coconut palms, litchis and banana trees will never ripen their delicious fruit under French skies, while our apple, pear and plum trees will not succeed in the hot zones where the former grow. A work dealing with the natural history and cultivation of all the trees whose fruit is eaten in every country on the globe would be extremely interesting in many respects, but it would go beyond the framework we have drawn up for ourselves whenever it deals with a species that can only thrive in Europe in a greenhouse. Considering trees here from the point of view of their usefulness, we have only to deal with those that succeed in the open ground in most European orchards.
