Berries. . ........... Fig tree. Eugenia. FIRST CLASS. FIRST DIVISION. Fruits with non-pulpy shells. COMMON CARUBEAN, SILICON CARUBEAN (Ceratonia siliqua; L.) 5. From southern France. This third-generation tree produces small, dark purple flowers in axillary spikes in August. These are followed by a foot-long, flat, fleshy, multi-located silique containing flat seeds. The seeds are encased in a sweet, reddish pulp that is good to eat when dry, but slightly laxative. GROWING. The carob tree likes open, warm, amended soil, and a south-facing exposure to a wall, where it must also be sheltered during winter by covering it with litter and long straw arranged in a hood. Despite these precautions, it sometimes freezes during harsh winters, so it's a good idea to have a few plants in orangeries in the Paris climate. It is propagated from seed, which is sown in spring in pots sunk in a warm layer and covered with a bell or frame. When the seedling is strong enough to withstand being planted in the ground, it is removed from the soil and planted without breaking up the root ball or touching the roots. Water moderately, unless the heat is very strong and the soil absolutely dry. It can still be propagated by strangling marcottes, but these are long and difficult to regrow. The fruit rarely ripens in Paris, so this tree is only widely cultivated in the south of France. Its extremely hard wood is considered incorruptible. PINYON PINE, CULTIVATED PINE, PINE-PINE, FRANC-PIN. (Pinus pinea, L.) 7. From southern France. The trunk of this very tall tree divides at the top into many horizontally spread, diffuse branches. Its cones are solitary, sometimes reaching four inches in diameter by four and a half inches in length. Their kernels are large, eight to ten lines long, and enclosed in large, hard pine nuts. They are very good to eat, either raw or as pralines or sweets; but they quickly go rancid, and in this state they are harmful. They yield a 1 very mild oil, comparable to almond oil, and are said to have several medicinal virtues. In some countries, they are served on the table with dessert. To remove them from the cones, the latter are exposed for some time to the heat of an oven, which causes the scales to crack; but it should be noted that once treated in this way, they are worthless for sowing. If you want to use them for sowing, wait until the scales have opened by themselves due to the temperature of the atmosphere, or better still, place them under glass cloches in the sun. The wood of this species of pine is fairly white, not very resinous, light and suitable only for shipbuilding. CULTIVATION. This tree, with its attractive evergreen foliage, is one of the most delicate of its kind; however, it does well in all soils, although it prefers light, sandy or slightly damp soils. Like the others, it does not like high, cold places. In the south of France, we sow its seeds as soon as they are harvested, in the ground, barely scraped, in beds, or in a sunny spot, so that the summer heat doesn't harm the young plants. It's even a good idea to shade them by sowing vegetable plants and annuals next to them, whose high foliage will protect them from the sun's rays. If the sowing is too thick, it can be thinned out the following year in April and May, and any surplus seedlings can be transplanted in places where they are lacking. Weed and water. In the second year, it is planted in a nursery or in the field, but always with the extreme precaution of cutting neither its branches nor its roots, and removing it with the root ball. It can be cared for in the same way as other trees, except that it does not suffer from pruning. In the climate of Paris, it is a little more difficult to grow, as it fears the cold for the first two or three years. It is sown in heather soil and terrines, which are constantly kept slightly moist and in a shady spot. The first year, it overwinters in the orangery, and the second year, it is de-potted, the soil divided into as many clods as there are plants, but with the precaution of breaking rather than cutting it. Litter the plant to protect it from extreme cold, and when it's strong enough, plant it out. It then becomes hardier and less afraid of frosts, and can be left to nature. PEA BEECH, FAYARD, FAU, FOUTEAU. (Fagus rrlvatica; L.) b. Indigenous. We only mention this superb tree, eighty to one hundred feet tall and so common in our woods, because of the great services its fruit renders to the inhabitants of the countryside where it is found. It ripens in October, and its flavor is similar to that of the filbert. It produces a very sweet oil, very similar to hazelnut oil, but with the singularity that it acquires quality as it ages, instead of losing it and going rancid like the others. It is used in cooking for lighting, and in perfumery. In addition to its oil, beech is also used to feed and fatten domestic animals, especially pigs. Beech wood is light and fairly hard, and is used for a variety of purposes. CULTIVATION. This tree grows well in all soils, but acquires enormous size in granitic, light, deep and slightly damp soils; it even succeeds in chalky soils and pure sand. It is propagated from seeds sown as soon as they ripen, either broadcast or in furrows, in soil loosened by good ploughing. It is soaked for twenty-four hours in manure water, to give it an unpleasant taste that will deter field mice, who are very fond of it. It is mixed with oats or barley, so that these plants keep the young plant in the shade during its early youth. This should be understood only for large, on-site sowings. If you have sown small seedlings, in order to obtain subjects for grafting ornamental varieties, weed and water them as needed; then transplant them to the nursery when they are a foot tall. Varieties are grafted using procedures nos. 13, 14, 5, 3 and 1; then, when recovery is certain, they are planted. Young beech trees can also be obtained by finding seedlings in the forest, always choosing the smallest, as they recover much more easily. CHESTNUT CONMUN. (Fagus castanea, L. Castanea vesca, WILD.) b. Native. This large, tap-rooted tree produces a fruit that is too well-known for us to waste time describing it. In some provinces, country dwellers use it as their main source of nourishment. Its wood is used for carpentry and joinery; better than any other, it can be preserved in water. Its young stems are used to make hoops and stiles. Several varieties are known, many of which seem to be solely the result of the terroir. A. LES MARRONS; rounder fruit, much larger and more fragrant than chestnuts. a. DE LYON, d'Aubray, d'Agen, de Luc, the largest and best of all. It has several sub-varieties, differing in size. B. CHATAIGNES. a. L'ÉGALADE; very large, flattened, excellent. D. POURTALONNE; large, benne; productive tree. 008 e. La VERTE DU LIMOUSIN; large, good; keeps very well. d. La PRINTANIÈRE; mediocre in size and quality, but early. e. La ROYALE HÉLÈNE; large, smooth, viscous on the outside. f. La ROYALE BLANCHE; large, good; almost as early as the preceding one, yellowish. g. La CAMaudE; very large and very good. It is remarkable for the fuzz it retains at the tip. h. GRANDE ÉPINE; whose shell is covered with very long spines. There are still several varieties, but all are inferior in quality to those just mentioned. CULTIVATION. To succeed, this tree requires clean, light soil, either granitic or sandy, but a little dry. In greasy, cool or even chalky soils, it perishes or vegetates thinly without bearing fruit. The species is multiplied by sowing, and the varieties by pollination. When you want to sow, you keep the best chestnuts in their shells until they come out themselves by shaking them a little; or, better still, you choose the biggest ones that have fallen naturally from the trees. Let them dry a little in the shade, then stratify them in sand, or simply in an eighteen-inch-deep hole dug in the driest part of the garden. If you don't have to worry about field mice and rats, it may be better to plant them in autumn, even though fewer of them will come up. In February or March, loosen the soil, but don't smoke it, unless you're using well-consumed street mud that's been ripening in a heap for at least a year. Furrows are made at two-foot intervals, and chestnuts are planted three inches deep and eighteen inches apart. If you're planting in a warm location, be sure to align the rows from north to south, so that the young plants can shade each other. Hoe the first year, then lightly plough in subsequent years. If you don't want to stratify, you can sow in autumn in the same way as above, but place two or three chestnuts in each hole, two inches apart, so that you'll always have a well-stocked nursery in case any are missing; once they've grown, carefully remove any that are too many, and transplant them where there are none. The plants are raised on a stem of varying height, as required, and are not put in place until they have reached wrist size. In the Bugey region, which supplies Lyon's famous chestnuts, holes five feet wide and four feet deep are dug one or two years in advance, and rainwater is channeled into them, depositing silt that is excellent for these trees. In autumn or spring, the tree is planted in these holes; a little mounding is done to help it withstand the forces of the wind, and the lateral branches are cut back. To ensure good growth, it's a good idea in the first year to keep the soil moist by covering it with straw, fern or thatch. The year after planting, it is grafted onto the top of the stem, or onto the main branches, using the budding bud grafting method (n° 2) and the flute grafting method (n° 63, 64 and 65). It is then treated as described in the article on pruning: see page 271. The distance at which chestnut trees should be placed from one another depends on the quality of the soil in which they are planted, and is calculated on the presumed size they will acquire one day; it is sufficient that their branches cannot overlap, as those underneath would not produce fruit. Chestnuts are usually harvested a little before they reach maturity, because the trees are often 9 far from dwellings, and the fruit falls by itself as soon as it is ripe, exposing it to theft. To cut them down, long gaules are used, which cause a great deal of damage to the branches, breaking their young shoots and destroying a good part of the next harvest. But this is a necessary evil that can only be partly remedied by sparing the trees as much as possible. Chestnuts are kept in their husks until they have fully ripened, and are thus kept for two months in a place exposed to a conrant of air. They are then separated from their shells and dried in the sun on racks. In some provinces, to ensure their preservation, smoke is passed between the racks on which they are laid, or they are stripped of their skin and dried in an oven. The CHINCAPIN, or AMERICAN CHESTNUT ( castanea, pumila, MiCH.), produces a very small fruit that is highly prized in its country. It can be grown in the same way, but is not well suited to the cold climate of Paris. It is usually propagated from seed, but by grafting onto the common chestnut tree, more satisfactory results could be obtained in the south of France. SWEET PAVIER, LARGE SPIKES, DWARF. (Æsculus macrostachya, Micn. Pavia dulcis, Poir.) 4. From North America. This shrub, which is very similar to the horse chestnut, produces fragrant white flowers in clusters in July and August. These are followed by small chestnuts, which are delicious eaten raw or roasted. The pavier likes fresh, light soil, although it does well in all types of soil. If possible, give it a mid-sun exposure, and its more numerous fruits are also better. This shrub can be propagated from seedlings, root offshoots and seed. The first are made in spring by incision or strangulation; they recover quite easily; the second are detached in autumn or spring. If propagated by root, a section about the size of a little finger and seven to eight inches long is cut in spring; it is planted vertically in light, cool, shady soil, covered with a little litter, and soon starts to sprout. Sowing is done in the same way as for ordinary chestnuts, i.e. the chestnuts are stratified in autumn, and planted in a nursery in spring. The following year, we separate the young plants, leaving a small clod of earth for each, and put them in place, where they require no more than ordinary care. However, it is wise to protect them from frost by covering them with litter for at least the first two winters. As sweet pavier produces its flowers at the end of the branches, it cannot be pruned regularly without reducing its yield. CULTIVATED PISTACHIO. (Pistacia vera aut trifolia; L.) b. From Syria. This tree, about twenty feet high, is naturalized in France and could be more widespread in the Paris climate, where it is fairly frost-resistant. It flowers in May; its male flowers are on one individual and its female flowers on another, so that both must be present to obtain a harvest. The fruit consists of a small nut the size and shape of an olive, with a greenish, oily kernel that is quite pleasant to the taste; it is known commercially as a pistachio and is used to make sweets, sugared almonds, etc. CULTIVATION. The ungrafted pistachio likes open, light soil and a warm, south-facing wall, where it can be trained in espaliers without being pruned. Grafted on terebinth (pistacia terebinthus, L.), it is much more vigorous, its fruit is larger and it can grow in any kind of soil. It can also be propagated from marcottes, and much more advantageously from seed. As soon as the fruit is ripe, sow it in pots or trays, bury in a warm layer and cover with a frame if you want to hasten germination. When the young plants have reached a certain strength, separate them with the rootball, and plant them either in the open ground if you're growing in the south of France, or in larger pots if you're growing in the Paris climate; in this case, keep them in an orangery for the first three or four years, after which they can be planted in the open ground, but take care to cover them with straw during very cold weather. A single male can fertilize many females, but he must be close enough to them to allow the wind to carry the dust from his stamens. In Sicily, farmers use artificial fertilization to obtain more abundant harvests. When the flowers of the male pistachio tree are about to open, they pick them and prick their stalks in damp earth, filling a vase with them; they hang this apparatus from the branches of the female pistachio trees, and leave it until the flowers have dried out completely. Or they fill small, light-colored canvas bags with male flowers, and shake them over the female flowers as they bloom. We have often compared the pistachios harvested in our plant with those from the south of France, and have never seen the slightest difference in quality or size. It would therefore be advantageous in the Paris area to multiply this tree further. C00 SECOND DIVISION. Fruits with a pulpy but tough half-shell. 1° CHÉNEA GLANDS DOUX Ou BALLOTTE. (Quercus ballota; DESF. ). B. Native to the Algiers region. In the Pyrenees and in Spain, acorns of this species are served for dessert; they are sweet, mucilaginous, and taste rather like hazelnuts. However, other species could be cultivated, whose fruit would not be inferior in flavor; here are the main ones: 2° YEAR OAK OR GREEN OAK (quercus ilex; L. ); from southern France; sweet acorns, but of mediocre quality. Around Paris, this tree sometimes suffers from frosts. 3° WHITE OAK (quercus alba; L. ); from America. Very large tree; soft, fairly large acorns. Performs well in the poorest soils. 4° STAR OAK (quercus stellata; Willp.); American. Medium-sized tree; mediocre acorn size, sweet and good. 5° CHESTNUT OAK (quercus castanea; WILLD.); American. Very large tree; acorns sweet and good, but small. 6° BICOLOR OAK (quercus bicolor; Willp.); American. Slightly smaller than the preceding tree; fairly large, brownish, sweet, fairly good acorns. Grows in damp soils. 7° PRINCE OAK (quercus prinus; L.); American. It likes damp soils, but sometimes fears frost in the Paris region; acorns of mediocre size, quite good. There are still a few species whose acorns are sweet, but of such mediocre flavor that they can scarcely be included in the nomenclature of food substances. In any case, they are all listed in the article "Chene" in the fourth volume of this book. CULTIVATION. Oaks are tap-rooted trees; they therefore like deep soil. They can be propagated from seed, and by approach and cleft pruning; but these latter methods are only used for rare species, whose fruit is difficult to obtain. We choose the most beautiful and ripest acorns, and give preference to those that have fallen naturally, especially when they are heavy and colorful. Sow in place or in a nursery. In the first case, after the ground has been well excavated or even mined, furrows are made into which the acorns are thrown in autumn, or only in February if they have been stratified, which is more advantageous for rare species, because less is lost to rot or field mice. If you want a thick coppice, space the furrows four or five feet apart, and place the acorns two or three feet apart on each line; you are then free to thin out and replant where they have been missed, using the transplanting method we'll teach below. Once sown, cover with three inches of soil. If you sow in a nursery, the operation is quite different. Beds are dug about a foot deep, and a bed of planks or anything else that roots can't penetrate is established. Eight to ten inches of good, loose soil is thrown on top of this bed, and sown in it in spring, covering the acorns as we said, and spacing them a foot apart, so that they can be left there for three or four years, the age at which they are best able to withstand transplanting. In this way, when the young oaks are moved to another location, all their roots can be easily removed, without having to break the pivot, which is usually the reason why the tree never develops well. Goc In all cases, whenever you want to transplant a young oak tree, the hole in which it is to be placed should be made in advance, so that you don't have to leave its roots exposed to the air for as little time as possible; you should move it with great care so as not to injure or break them, and above all you shouldn't cut any of them under any pretext whatsoever, unless they are diseased or fractured. If you can remove the plants with the root ball, you'll be much more certain of success. Place in the holes, and slide very loose soil around the roots, pressing them down a little with your feet to leave no gaps, but carefully, so as to break as little of the hair as possible; then water abundantly. Planting should take place in autumn; however, in greasy, damp soils, especially where there is a risk of water pooling in the holes, delay planting until February or even March. Choose a rainy day, or at least one that's dark and overcast, and avoid the cold. The recovery of these trees is difficult in the Paris area, but in Flanders and some other northern French provinces, we have seen them planted in avenues and recovered with the greatest ease. If you have sown holm oaks, particularly American ones, you will do well to protect them from heavy frosts during the first three or four winters. This can be done with dry bedding or straw. In any case, you can cultivate an oak nursery like any other, and give it the same care. As you can see from what we've just said, the oak, so robust when it reaches a certain age, is a delicate tree in its youth. It should therefore be given all the care it deserves, even more for the usefulness of its wood than for its fruit. hazelnut, currant (corrlus.) b. All species of this genus produce a fruit whose sweet, pleasant kernel yields an oil that is good for cooking, though little used, and useful in medicine. Confectioners use hazelnuts to make sugared almonds. Several species and a few varieties are grown. 1 1° common hazelnut (corrlus avellana; L.); native. It has produced the following varieties: a. White-fruited hazelnut (avellana sativa vulgaris); long, early, excellent, soft-shelled fruit. b. Red-fruited hazelnut (avellana fructu rubente); long fruit, similar to the above. c. Nettle-leaved hazelnut, which has been made into a species under the name corylus urticœfolia. We saw it at the home of Count de Montmorenci in Montgeron. Its foliage resembles that of the oak-leaved hornbeam, but its leaves are slightly larger and more deeply toothed. Moreover, it is a very curious variety. d. Aveline des bois; small, round fruit, quite good. e. Aveline de Provence; round fruit, with white skin and thick, hard shell: very good. f. Grosse aveline de Provence; same shape and quality, but much larger. g. Noisetier à feuille pourpre; long fruit, very good. 2° RED HAWTHORN (corylus tubulosa; WiLLD.); native. Gardeners make it a variety of the previous one, and call it corylus avellana fructu rubente maximo, because of the red color and size of its fruit. The oval-fruited hazelnut (cory lus grandis) used by gardeners is just one variety; early fruit, very good, oval, with white skin; tender shell. 3° GRAPEBERRY (corylus glomerata); very good, early, round, very large fruit. 4° CORNU NOISETIER (corylus rostrata; H. K.); from North America. It has a very thick shell and a small but good kernel: it is round. 5° NOISTIER CLABRE (cory lus colurna; L.); from Oriènt. Fruit small, of mediocre quality, round. 4006 6° NOISETIER D'AMÉRIQUE (cory lus americana; MiCn.); fruit round, small and quite good. 7° NOISETIER DE BXZANCE (corylus bizantina; L.); leaves very slender, fruit very small, inferior in quality if compared to the first and second species; its kernel is oily. The tree grows very fast, reaching a height of forty to fifty feet. CULTIVATION. Hazelnuts are plants that can form bushes or trees at will. See page 272. They do well in all soils and at all exposures, so it is customary to place them in cold, shady places, where no other fruit tree would succeed: however, they prefer light soil, or even a little sandy, cool and moist, as well as sunny and sunny exposures. They can be propagated from seed, but must then be grafted, as they rarely produce fruit as good as that sown. The safest and quickest ways to propagate them are by marcotte, which roots quite easily when strangled in autumn; rooted offshoots, which are separated in autumn and recover very well; and cuttings. They hardly bear fruit until the second or third year. Hazel wood has only minor uses, and the best we can do with it is to make hoops and ties. It has been observed that, cut precisely when the leaves are falling, it lasts three times longer than felled in winter and spring. According to Haller and Daubenton, this tree lives to be very old, and is capable of great growth. The former says he saw one in the garden of Leyden, in 1727, which had been planted by Charles de l'Écluse; it was over forty feet high and ten inches dia2. 30,200 meters. The second said he had seen one of this size, which was not yet dying. THIRD DIVISION. Fruits with pulpy, dry or bitter husks. WALNUT. (Juglans.) D. If the most general utility served to classify individuals, the walnut tree would be at the head of all nomenclatures; its fruit, wood, leaves, flowers and all its parts are used advantageously in domestic economy, medicine and the arts. The kernel contained in the nut is edible when new: dried, it provides an oil used in cooking and painting; its husk, as well as the leaves, roots and bark, are used by dyers to make a very solid color; the shells and the rest of the fruit are sudorific; the wood is one of the finest and one of the best that can be used by carpenters, cabinet-makers, armorers and so on. Several species and varieties of walnut trees are grown. 1° CULTIVATED WALNUT (juglans regia; L.); from Asia. Superb tree, with excellent wood, and different fruits depending on the variety. a. Common walnut; oval, rounded fruit of mediocre size, but full and yielding a lot of oil. Very productive tree, giving very good wood. b. Tender walnut or walnut tit; fruit earlier, more elongated than the preceding one, less full; wood softer, but good. c. Late walnut (juglans serotina; H. PAR.). The real merit of this variety is that it only flowers at the end of June, which protects it from spring frosts. The fruit doesn't ripen until early October, is good for eating as kernels, and yields a fair amount of oil. d. Large-fruited walnut. Produces what are known as "gauge nuts"; very large fruit, but the kernel shrinks by half as it dries, and yields little oil. Quite a pro1000 ductive tree, but in some years many fruits fall before maturity. e. Walnut with angular fruit; angular nuts, very hard shell and very good kernel, but very difficult to extract. Produces excellent oil. The tree is one of the largest of its kind; its hard, well-veined wood is the most highly esteemed of all. f. Walnut with large, long fruit; full fruit, soft shell, excellent kernel. Quite productive, with good-quality wood. g. Walnut with jewelled nuts; huge, almost square fruit, with good kernels when new, but diminishing in volume when dry, and turning rancid easily. This variety is used to hide gloves or small jewelry inside the shell. Soft wood. h. Cluster walnut; fruits five or six together, in the shape of an elongated cluster, of medium size, soft-shelled, of the same quality as tit-nut. i. Walnut with small round fruits; hard shell; full nut, yielding a lot of oil. A large tree with very hard wood, widely grown around Joigny. L Walnut with mucronate fruit, elongated nut, medium size, rough, with a point at the top, full and good. Hard wood. 2° PACAN WALNUT (Juglans olivæformis; H. K. aut crlindrica; ENCYCL. ). From North America. Fruits small, oblong, almost cylindrical, the size and shape of an olive, excellent for eating. The tree only produces at twenty years of age, and is very sensitive to cold. Its cultivation can therefore only be advantageous in the south of France. 3° BLACK WALNUT (Juglans nigra; L.). From North America. This tree is very large, grows very quickly, does not fear frosts, and provides excellent wood for joinery. Its fruit is small, round, with a very hard shell and woody partitions. We have obtained from seed a variety whose fruit is once as large, and which grows much faster. We have named it jaglans nigra macrocarpa. 4° BUTTERNUT. (Juglans cinerea; MiCh.). From America. Its fruit is very large, elongated and very rough, but the 2009 thickness of its shell accounts for most of its size. The tree is almost as tall as the previous one, and its wood is used for the same purposes. We still know some American walnut trees, such as alba, porcina, squamosa and others; but, as their fruit and wood are inferior to ours, we have deemed it unnecessary to describe them in the fruit garden. CULTIVATION. Walnut trees are pivotal trees whose lateral roots nevertheless extend a long way. They thrive in deep, rocky, sandy or light soils, and even better in red, chalky soils, where they grow to enormous size. They tolerate ten to twelve degrees of cold fairly well, but can be damaged when the thermometer falls lower; the winter of 1709 and 1789 killed a large number of them. They like northern and eastern exposure, and do better in mountainous or hilly areas than in flat ones. They are propagated from seed, and grafted using the flute grafting method (n° 61, 62), the slit grafting method (n° 47) and the escutcheon grafting method (n° 75). To sow, we choose the most beautiful fruits of the common walnut or even of the one with angular fruits if we have to graft, and if not, the biggest nuts of the varieties we want to propagate. Leave them in their husks until they naturally detach, and stratify them at the beginning of winter. A very good method, which would produce strong, vigorous trees, would be to sow them in place; but, as walnut trees are usually planted quite far from dwellings, it would be very difficult to keep an eye on them during their first age, and to prevent cattle from trampling them underfoot. It is therefore customary to form nurseries. A plot of land is dug to a depth of eighteen or twenty inches, and no smoking is allowed, unless 9 it's with a highly-consumed potting soil. In February or March, rays are traced two and a half feet apart, and the nuts are placed two feet apart; they are sunk two or two and a half inches deep, depending on the size of the fruit. The usual care is given to a nursery, and when the plants are strong enough to get in each other's way, we think about thinning them out; but, as those that are removed are suitable for replanting, we choose a season that is favorable to replanting, i.e. spring. One plant is removed between each one, so that the plants are spaced four feet apart; some are put back where they are missing, and those that are misshapen or of poor quality are replaced. From this moment on, you begin to direct the young plants as we said on page 271, in order to form them into beautiful stems. Some people are in the habit, in the second or third year, of cutting off the roots of all plants that spread laterally; to do this, they drive a spade vertically between each plant, about fifteen or eighteen inches from its collar. The result is that the shortened roots produce more hair. This operation is hard on the trees, but the inconvenience is offset by the much safer recovery process. When young walnut trees have reached an inch to an inch and a half in diameter, they can be grafted at the top of the stem, unless you prefer to wait until they are in place and well established. Gresses are more prone to detachment on this tree than on others, especially those in escutcheon; their shoots are tied to small stakes, which are themselves attached to the stem. If you wanted to swell an old tree, all you'd have to do is pull its branches back onto its trunk, and swell the vigorous shoots it produces. These trees are quite resistant to transplanting, when done with care. As far as possible, they should be raised in rootballs, and the soil in which they are planted should be broken up by three to four pledges. To do this, choose the right season, as mentioned above, and take into account the quality of the soil, planting them at varying distances from one another. Six, seven or eight toises are enough space for ordinary soil and for grafted plants; ten to twelve are not too much for those that are not. We note that the roots crumble and exhaust the soil, and that they extend as far as the branches; that harvests are very mediocre under the shade of this tree; that it needs fresh air, and that in clumps it produces very little fruit. It's on the basis of these observations that we'll determine where to place it. Walnuts have to be harvested in the same way as chestnuts, i.e., with gaules, and the same disadvantages result; so only light blows should be used, taking care not to break the fruit and wood buds. Walnuts should be kept in a dry, airy place, until they are easy to remove from their husks; then they should be dried, and kept in a place where the temperature is neither too hot nor too cold, as these two extremes cause them to go rancid or spoil. AMANDIER. (Amygdalus communis, L.) p. From Asia. Medium-sized, tap-rooted tree. Blooms in February and March before covering itself with leaves. Its fruit, known as almond, is covered with a dry or fleshy husk; the shell is hard or easy to break, and the kernel is sweet or bitter, depending on the variety. Its wood is very hard, sometimes with a beautiful reddish color, but it splits fairly easily when heated. Sweet almonds are used for dessert, either green or dry. With sugar, they are used to make marzipan, macaroons, nougats, sugared almonds, etc. Orgeat is prepared from it, and a very mild oil is extracted, used in medicine and the arts, but which goes rancid easily. Whatever the use to which almonds are put, the yellowish film covering them must always be removed, as it contains a resinous, acrid dust that is very irritating. They go rancid within a year, and are no longer edible. Bitter almonds, which are harmless to humans, are a deadly poison for birds and some other animals. They do, however, provide an oil as sweet as the other varieties. A. SWEET ALMONDS. I. Hard-shelled. 1. AMANDIER FRANC; very productive; fruit small, almost round, with a thick, very hard shell; ripe at the beginning of October. Very tall tree, with narrow, ash-green leaves. 2. AMANDIER COMMUN A GROS PRUIT; very productive; large, very full fruit, with very thick, very hard shell; ripe in September. Less tall than the preceding tree, with broader, more pronounced green foliage. 3. AMANDIER COMMUN A PETIT FRUIT; very productive, fruit fourteen to fifteen lines long, with a point at the top. Tree straight, not very spreading, very large. 4. COMMON AMANDIER WITH LARGE WHITE FLOWERS; fruit of medium size, compressed; ripe at the end of September. Medium-sized tree, with fairly straight branches. 5. AMANDIER WITH LARGE LEAVES; fruit slightly larger than the preceding one, compressed, elongated; ripe at the end of September. Medium-sized tree, with spreading branches. 6. WILLOW LEAF AMANDIER; small, rounded fruit; ripe in October. Elegant tree with upright branches. 7. TOURS AMANDIER; largest fruit of all, elongated, uncompressed, almost soft-shelled. Medium-sized tree with loose branches. 3008 JARDIN FRUITIER, 8. PERSE AMANDIER; small, rounded fruit. Shrub hardly rising above three or four feet, not very fertile. 9. SATINY AMANDIER; small, almost round fruit. Very small tree, with bright red flowers. As it yields little and is delicate, it is cultivated only for ornamental purposes; the brightness of its flowers and the whiteness of its foliage produce a charming effect. II. Soft-shelled. 10. AMANDE PRINCESSE or DES DAMES; highly prized, elongated fruit with a shell that breaks easily between the fingers; ripe in August. Very productive tree. 11. AMANDE SULTANE; rounded fruit, also esteemed, but less abundant and smaller. More delicate tree. 12. AMANDE PISTACHE; fruit even smaller than the preceding one, approaching the shape of a pistachio. Delicate tree. B. BITTER ALMONDS. 13. AMANDIER A GROS FRUITS; hard shell; rounded fruit. Fertile tree, rising very high; its whitish stem gives it a resemblance to the birch. 14. MEDIUM FRUIT AMANDIER; hard shell; rounded fruit. Productive tree, with bark like the previous one. 15. AMANDIER WITH SMALL FRUIT; differs from the two preceding trees only in the smaller size of the fruit. 16. TENDER-shelled almond; medium-sized, slightly compressed fruit. Tree of mediocre size, not very fertile. 17. AMANDIER-PEACHER; this appears to be a hybrid of almond and peach. In hot summers, peaches and almonds are often found on the same branch. The former are large, round and very fleshy, but their pulp retains a bitter flavor that makes them suitable only for eating as compote; the latter are large, but elongated and covered with a dry husk. Sometimes the kernel is quite sweet. A very fertile tree with a beautiful habit. 18. ALMOND TREE - DWARF PEACH TREE. This one, which we have 000 obtained by sowing the previous one, differs only in its very small size. We have also obtained several varieties, differing in fruit and height, but too slightly for us to mention them here. CULTIVATION. The almond tree likes light, deep, warm soil; it does very well in calcareous soils. Above the 46th degree of latitude, it becomes very sensitive to spring frosts, and requires a warm, sheltered exposure to set and ripen its fruit reliably. Farther north, its almonds are rarely good to keep dry, although they are excellent green. It is best suited to the climates of Barbary, Provence and Languedoc. It is propagated from seed and by grafting. To sow, we choose the largest, ripest almonds, i.e. those that have fallen naturally when shedding their shells. They are then stratified to promote germination, and planted in the spring. If sown in autumn, they should be planted in well-disturbed soil, two or three inches deep. As it is not certain that they will all germinate, it is a good idea to put two or three of them in the same hole, two inches apart: if they all succeed, the weaker ones will have to be pulled out the following spring. Sow in place or in nurseries: for the latter, place them in rows eighteen inches apart, so that you can easily pass between each one to cultivate or graft. If they are to form peach or almond trees, they are placed twenty to twenty-four inches apart in the rows; if they are to form tall stems, the intervals should be two to two and a half feet. If they are properly hoeed and cultivated, and if the soil has been well O6 amended, a large number of those that have been stratified can be grafted by the end of August. When transplanting them into place, cut off the pivot of those intended for espaliers, leaving it intact for those to be raised in the open air. Varieties are grafted onto the common species and the peach almond tree, for faster growth and greater vigor. Like some authors, we do not recommend grafting almond trees onto plum trees, as the experiments we have carried out on this subject have always been very unsuccessful. The grafts used for this tree are the escutcheon grafts, n° 1, 2, 3, 74, 108. It is grown in espaliers or in the open air, and managed as described on page 226. When espaliered, it is protected from spring frosts, like apricots and peaches. It is subject to the same diseases as these two trees, and is treated in the same way. FOURTH DIVISION. Norau fruit, with sweet, succulent pulp. PEACH (Amygdalus persica; L.) 6. From Persia. Everyone knows the superiority of its fruit over that of all other fruit trees; and yet, if we are to believe the accounts of a few travelers, this delicious peach, which so pleases the eye and the palate, is a dangerous poison in its native land. As this assertion struck us as peculiar, we took exact information from people who had been in a position to observe this fruit in Persia. The result is that in this country it has a bitter taste that it has lost in ours; that the natives eat it with impunity; but that it causes Europeans an inconvenience that is limited to constipation. 9 The peach tree flowers in March before developing its leaves. It has given rise to a large number of varieties that have been classified more methodically since M. Desprez noticed globular or kidney-shaped glands on the leaves, which are fairly constant in each variety. Nora. The best varieties are marked with an asterisk". I. FRUITS COVERED WITH DOWN. A. CULTIVATION. This interesting tree thrives in soils suited to the subjects on which it is grafted; thus its cultivation during its first two or three years is the same as that of the almond or plum tree. See these articles. If you wish to obtain free-standing trees, from varieties that do not need to be grafted, select the pits of the best fruit, stratify them and plant them in the nursery or in the field, as described in the article on almond trees. Peach trees are grafted onto almond and plum trees (see page 183), using the following grafting techniques: 1, 2, 3, 74, 108. Dormant-eye grafts are preferred because, as they only grow in spring, the young shoots of the graft have time to gain strength and harden off, and can more easily withstand the rigors of winter. We have already mentioned the circumstances under which grafting should be carried out on hard-shelled almond trees with sweet almonds: royal, large and small violets, bourdine and red madeleine all do well on bitter-fruited almond trees. If the peach almond tree were less rare, it would also provide robust trees suitable for full-wind planting. As for those that are grafted onto plum trees, they are placed on subjects grown from seed and never from offspring; the most suitable species for peach trees are: gros and petit St.-Julien, gros and petit Damas; however, we note that smooth peaches and chevreuses do not do well on the latter. Whether you're planting a plein-vent or an espalier, the ground must be prepared to receive it. If the soil is good, we simply pass it through a rack and mix in some used fertilizer, then throw it back in; but if it has been exhausted, either by plum, peach or apricot trees, or by other trees, we must renew it entirely with soil prepared in advance and ripened in piles. Once this has been done, plant as described on page 185, noting that autumn plantings are more likely to recover than spring plantings. The bed is covered with two or three inches of manure, which is left on all winter, and then ploughed under in spring. This operation is repeated every autumn. If you want to have superb trees, you'll need to establish a bed at least six feet wide along the espalier, which you'll keep free of weeds at all times, and in which you won't grow anything, not even the least voracious vegetables. In addition, every three or four years, a good manuring is given. Only one ploughing is done per year, and it must take place immediately after pruning; it is done lightly and with a fork, so as not to damage the tree roots; then the bed is covered with mulch, to keep the soil moist and prevent the rains from beating it. For the rest of the year, only a few rakes are needed. During the heat of the year, we water the branches and leaves of the trees with a small suction pump or watering cans; it's essential that the water reaches them in the form of a fine rain, which is done with a sheaf pierced with three small holes, and only in the evening, so that the foliage has time to dry out during the night. If the ground is dry, a small pit is dug at the foot of each tree, and two, three or even four watering cans are thrown in if the peach tree is in full ratio and covers a large expanse of wall; one is enough for young trees.

Once the water has been absorbed, the pit is filled with the soil removed. In all cases, stop watering a few days before the fruit ripens, to avoid losing its fragrance. If the peach tree bears too much fruit, you must be scrupulously careful to remove some of it, if you don't want it to run out quickly. For the rest, you should manage it as we taught you in the article on pruning, page 183. Shelter is one of the most important issues for the grower. The walls against which you plant should face east, west and south; never north in temperate climates. If the garden were oriented so that its four walls faced northeast, southeast, northwest and southwest, peach trees could be grown on all their surfaces. Each variety is placed at the most suitable exposure, with early and late species facing south, so that the former bear fruit earlier and the latter ripen. If the trees are trellised "à la loque", i.e. directly against the wall with nails and rags, the coping should extend four or five inches beyond the wall, to protect the branches from rain and frost, which could damage the eyes. For those trellised against a trellis, the coping should extend out six or seven inches. Under the coping, thin pieces of wood or, better still, iron bars, projecting eighteen inches horizontally, will be sealed two and a half feet apart, so that they can easily support straw mats, which will be laid horizontally over them in the form of a roof or awning. The doormats are laid from February onwards, before the flowers bloom, and are left in place until mid-May, when frosts are no longer to be expected. We visit the trees every day; some people even do it in winter, to remove any frost and snow that may have accumulated on the branches; to do this, we use a broom of fine, loosely-packed heather, so as not to damage the eyes. This will prevent the ravages of frost, which seizes buds and even wood much more easily when they are wet. In summer, we kill snails and other insects, and consider the most effective ways of preventing diseases, or stopping them in their tracks as soon as they appear. During severe droughts, it's a good idea to shade for a few hours the branches and even the entire foliage of espaliers facing south, to prevent the sun's scorching rays from drying them out. This precaution is especially useful for plum trees. To do this, use canvas or straw mats, and spread them out in front of the trees, but a few feet apart so as not to impede air circulation; leave them there only during the hottest part of the day. Peach trees are very delicate trees, and they are all the more so when they are poorly trained and badly managed. The diseases that most commonly attack them are blister, gum, rust and canker. On this subject, see our chapter on Plant diseases. The peach harvest begins in July and continues uninterrupted until October. Their ripeness is recognized by their color, not by their tact, as some clumsy people do, since the slightest pressure is enough to bruise them and cause them to rot within twenty-four hours. On the contrary, they should be picked with great care: grasp the peach with all five fingers, pull gently, and it comes off if it's ripe; if it's not, and you want to keep it for a few days, give it half a twist, and it comes off. But before they can be harvested, the fruits require a little care. When they are two-thirds full, we begin to remove the leaves that hide them, so that they can enjoy the influences of air and light. Fifteen days before ripening, the red peaches are fully uncovered, but this must be done in stages, so as not to expose them to burning. The others, most of which ripen later, are uncovered longer before ripening, because the sun does not color them, but ripens them; they are stripped in September, regardless of when they are to be picked. The leaves should never be torn off for fear of injuring the buds they accompany; they should be cut with scissors or a pruning shears, above the petiole. ABRICOTIER (Prunus armeniaca; L.), from the Orient. It is said to have been first brought from Armenia to Rome, and from there transplanted to the rest of Europe. This medium-sized, tap-rooted tree flowers in February and March before showing its leaves, making it susceptible to spring frosts. Its excellent fruit can be eaten raw, candied, in compote, etc. Those produced by plein-vents are more highly esteemed than those produced by espaliers, although they are smaller. Its wood is not very useful, but is used for lathe work. 1. COMMON ABRiCOT; fruit quite good when in full wind and sun, and in this case, large, reddish and scabby; in shade it remains pale and has almost no flavor. Ripe by mid-July. Large, vigorous tree when grafted, but, like all apricot trees, remains shrubby when grown from stone. Full-wind, more reliable, but less good in espaliers. 2. ABRICOT DE HOLLANDE, or AMANDE AVELINE; small, rounded fruit, red speckled with purple on the sunny side, with melting, vinous, spicy, very pleasant flesh; sweet, hazelnut-flavored kernel. Ripe at the end of July. The tree is more fertile when grafted on cherry; but the fruit is larger on Saint-Julien, especially if grown in espaliers. 3. ABRICOT DE HOLLAN DE A FEUILLES PANACHÉES DE JAUNE; fruit similar to the previous one, and ripening at the same time; leaves variegated and spotted with golden yellow. A very vigorous tree, with a beautiful effect. 4. ABRICOT DE PROVENCE; fruit a little compressed, small, red on the sunny side, with melting reddish-yellow flesh; sweet almond. Ripe in the last fortnight of July. A fairly vigorous tree that can be grafted onto Saint-Julien. Pleinvent and espalier. 5. ABRICOT DE PORTUGAL; small, rounded fruit, not very colorful even in the sun; yellow flesh, melting, delicate, excellent. Mür in mid-August. A delicate tree in the Paris climate. 6. EARLY ABRICOT or ARRICOTIR; small, red fruit on the sunny side, quite fragrant, slightly musky, with flesh very attached to the stone. Ripe at the end of June. Its earliness is its greatest merit, and to encourage it, it should be grown in espaliers. 7. ABRICOT BLANC, Duhamel's apricot-peach; fruit small, white, or slightly tinted red, browned on the sunny side; flesh a little fibrous, approaching the flavor of the peach. Ripe in early July. Medium-sized, fairly vigorous tree. Espalier and plein-vent. 8. LARGE WHITE ABRICOT; fruit more elongated and larger than the preceding one, ripening at the same time, with sweet almond. A vigorous tree. 9. VINEY ABRicoT; very large, elongated fruit, compressed at the sides, with a sunken, lobed top, one lobe higher than the other; yellow skin, speckled with red on the sunny side; yellowish-white flesh, melting though quite firm, sweet, fragrant, excellent. Ripens in early August. Large, vigorous tree. We believe that, when properly grafted and cultivated, it will be one of the best apricots known. We obtained it from seed, and it bore fruit for the first time in 1825. 10. ABRiCOT ANGOUNOIS; small, slightly elongated, pale red fruit, spotted with purple on the sunny side, amber-yellow in the shade; reddish-yellow flesh, melting, vinous, very pleasant; sweet almond, tasting a little like filbert. Ripe in mid-July. The low tree has thin buds and medium-sized leaves. 11. ABRICOT ALBERCE; fruit small, with thick, hard, brown skin, strongly scabby on the sunny side; flesh firm, crisp, vinous, spicy and quite good; almond sweet, fragrant, excellent. Ripe by mid-August. The tree reproduces its variety by its kernels; it is widely used as a subject for other varieties. It is the only large, vigorous tree from seed. 12. ApRICOT DE MONCAMET; fruit larger, less rounded 000 than the preceding one, of which it is only a sub-variety, less scabby, less firm flesh, just as good; sweet almond. Ripens a little later. Tree with the same qualities. 13. ABRICOT AVELINE; differs so little from the mongamet that it can only be a very light sub-variety. Same shapes and qualities. 14. ABRicOT PÉCHE, Nancy apricot; the most beautiful and the best of all; very large, compressed, red or brown, scabby on the sunny side, but only in windy and humid years; reddish-yellow flesh, melting, sweet, vinous, excellent. Ripe around mid-August. Medium-strength tree, despite Duhamel's claim that it is the largest of the apricot trees, with broad, hanging foliage. Espalier and flein-vent. It reproduces its seed variety, but almost always of inferior quality. 15. FISHING ABRICOT WITH WHITE MARBLED LEAVES; fruit slightly smaller than the preceding one, of the same quality and ripening at the same time. Less vigorous tree, with variegated or rather white marbled foliage, producing a very picturesque effect, and appearing as if covered with small snowflakes. 16. ABRICOT ROYAL; fruit almost as large as the peach apricot, greenish yellow near the tail, pure towards the top, little colored in the sun; yellow flesh, melting, more acidic, quite good. Ripens in August. Very vigorous tree, with broad leaves supported by very long petioles. 17. ABRICOT DE PARIS; medium-sized, lemon-yellow fruit, speckled with brown on the sunny side, yellow flesh, semi-melting, sweet, pleasant, a little pasty; bitter almond. Ripens in August. Small tree. This variety, which we obtained from apricot-peach seedlings, first produced fruit in 1825. 18. MUSCH-MUSCH APRICOT. This variety originates from the town of Musch, on the borders of Turkey, on the Persian side. We received it, in 1812, from M. Lacour-Gouffé, director and professor at the botanical garden in Marseille, who himself had obtained it from the royal gardens in Madrid. Rounded, dark-yellow fruit with transparent pulp, revealing a hint of stone; flesh probing, fragrant, veryO pleasant; bitter almond. Mür at the end of July. The tree is delicate and wants to be espaliered. 19. ABRICOT GROS MUSCH; fairly large fruit, deeply furrowed on one side, compressed on the other; melting flesh, fragrant, very-good, sweet almond. Ripe at the end of July. 20. ABRICOT DU PAPE, or black; small, round, almost black violet fruit, with red flesh around the circumference, yellowing near the stone, melting and pleasant in warm years, but bland and very mediocre in ordinary years, very adherent to the stone. If one were to judge by the shape of the stone and the flavor of the fruit, it would be more likely to be mistaken for a plum than an apricot. It is grown only as an ornamental tree and an object of curiosity. 21. PAPER ABRICOT WITH WILLOW LEAVES; fruit similar to the previous one, but a little smaller. Medium-sized tree, producing a charming effect with its long, narrow, yellowish-veined leaves. 22. ABRICOTIER DE SIBERIE; we have recently acquired this new species, which has not yet produced any fruit. The tree is small, with rounded, acuminate leaves. 23. NYPAUL ABRICOTIER; this is the pygmy of the apricot trees. We received it a short time ago, and we don't know what its fruit will be. CULTIVATION. This tree likes light, sandy soil; however, it does well in all types, provided they are not too wet or too clayey. It can hardly be expected to do well above 50' latitude, if grown in the open air and in full wind. It likes heat, and its fruit loses much of its quality in cold soils. It is propagated: 1° from seed, 2° by grafting onto plum trees, 3° by grafting onto almond trees. We only sow varieties that reproduce free of stones, such as alberge, peach, early and common apricots. Rarely do we graft on franc, because the trees are more prone to gum and much less vigorous. We choose the stones of the most 000 FRUIT GARDEN, beautiful fruits for sowing, and stratify them immediately for planting in spring or autumn; we put them in good, well-disturbed soil, loosened and amended as much as we can, and bury them two inches deep; during heavy frosts, we cover the seedling with leaves or litter. You can sow in place, or in a nursery, and in the latter case or space the young plants from eighteen inches to two feet apart on the rows, depending on whether you want to raise them for low or high stems. However, a tree can never be too vigorous when it is managed by a man who understands its condition and knows how to tame excess vegetation by distributing the sap evenly. Apricot trees can be grafted into scions using grafting methods n°1, 2, 3, 74, 108. In any soil, plum, black damask, Saint-Julien and cherry trees are grafted from stones and not from offshoots, as the latter are always weak and therefore prone to gumming. If trained in espaliers, they should be planted facing east in suitable soils, or south in cold, damp soils. Sometimes the apricot tree is raised on a five- or six-foot stem, so that it can be placed against a wall between peach trees trellised below its branches. In all cases, it is pruned and trained as described on page 224. In countries where it does not do well, either because of the cold, as in Russia, or because of excessive heat, as in some provinces of Spain and Italy, it should be trained in espaliers with frames, using the methods we have described on page 203. Apricot trees are subject to the same diseases as peach trees, with the exception of blistering. When its blossoms are caught 71 by frost, we try to restore them using the procedures indicated in the article on plant diseases. They are much more susceptible when the tree grows in damp, clayey soil. PLUM (Prunus) b. Plum trees, the largest of which reach only average size, produce fruit that has been greatly improved by cultivation. Plums can be eaten raw, cooked, as prunes, etc., but are never eaten dry. Plums can be eaten raw, cooked, as prunes, etc.: dried, they are never harmful, but some are thought to be febrile when ripe. Plum wood is hard and pleasantly red-veined, and is used in joinery and cabinet-making. Several species and a large number of varieties are grown. NoTA. The best or most beautiful fruits are marked with an asterisk. I. black thorn (prunus spinosa, L.). Indigenous. A shrub that grows to no more than seven or eight feet, and whose sharp thorns make it suitable for making impenetrable hedges. In some parts of France, its small, very acerbic fruits are prized by the population as a pleasant drink. Their juice, cooked and thickened to the consistency of syrup, is known in the druggist's trade as German acacia. 1. LARGE-FRUITED BLACKTHORN. It differs from the previous variety in that its development is larger, and in the size of its fruit, known as plos se in the south of France, which is slightly less sharp than the previous one, and has the same uses. There is another variety with double flowers, which we will describe in the ornamental garden. II. MYROBOLAN PLUM, (Prunus myrobolana, aut cerasifera, WILLD.). From North America. Cultivated more for pleasure than for utility. Its cherry-sized fruit is turbinate, with a pleasant flavor and an attractive red color. 1. myRObOLaN ViOlEr. This tree differs from the previous one in that its leaves are slightly smaller. Its fruit, of the same shape, is more covered with glaucous dust; it has a very pretty violet color, and a rather acid taste. It is a hybrid of the black or pope apricot and the ordinary myrobolan. We obtained it from seed, and it fruited for the first time in 1825. III. SPHERIC FRUTT PLUM (Prunus sphærocarpa; MICH.). From New England. Fruit small, short-stalked and hairy, purple-red, acerbic, of very-mediocre quality. IV. CANADIAN AUTUMN PLUM. (Prunus hiemalis; MICH.). Fruit almost black, acerbic, of mediocre quality, small. V. CHRASAU PLUM (Prunus chicasa; MicR.). Native to India, from where it was brought to Carolina. Small, yellow fruit, ripe in summer, of mediocre quality, though sought after by Americans, who call it chicasaw. We have a variety with red fruit. VI. CULTIVATED PLUM. (Prunus domestica; L.). Indigenous. It is grown only as a grafting stock for the 1008 peach, apricot and plum trees that are to be raised in cattails or espaliers. CULTIVATION. Plum trees are trailing-rooted trees, so they don't need very deep soil, but clean, light, chalky soil that's cool but not damp. However, they do well in all types of soil, provided they're not too sandy, swampy or dry. In the south of France, they thrive in northern exposure, but above the 46th degree of latitude, they need exposure to the east or west, and sometimes even, for delicate varieties, to the south. It should be noted that the quality of the fruit depends very much on the quality of the soil in which they are planted, and their more or less favorable exposure. Plum trees are propagated by seed, offshoot, shield grafting (n° 1, 2, 3, 108) and slit grafting (n° 5, 8, 18). To sow, the stones are stratified and then planted and cultivated, either in situ or in a nursery, like almonds and peaches. Large and small Saint-Julien, large and small black damask, and cherry are usually sown to graft their own varieties or those of peach and apricot. The offspring have the advantage of coming much sooner; but they also have the disadvantage of producing smaller trees, more prone to gum, and more difficult to get rid of the numerous trees whose roots grow abundantly on all sides. They are rarely used for anything other than espaliers, cattails and small pyramids. Whether you are grafting from cores or offshoots, you need to grasp the exact moment of sap flow, because it passes very quickly, despite the precaution of watering to prolong its life. Crest grafting is the preferred method for young trees, while cleft grafting is ideal for larger trees in spring. Plum trees should be trained as described on page 22. They are quite prone to gum. CHERRY (Prunus cerasus; L.) b, and MERRY (Prunus avium; L.) 5. The former is said to have originated in Africa, where it was brought back by Lucullus. As it was found in the vicinity of Cerasonte, it was given the name cerasus. The latter grows naturally in forests throughout Europe, and has given rise to two main varieties, the cherry and the sour cherry; the former appears to be the type of the cherry and sour cherry of the north. The merits of these trees' fruit are well enough known without needing to be mentioned here; their wood, especially that of the black-fruited cherry, takes on a beautiful red color. It is widely used by carpenters and turners. Nora. The best species are marked with an asterisk". A. MERISIERS; flowers in sessile clusters, developing on the wood of the penultimate year. It is grafted onto Sainte-Lucie, and placed in a northern position to prevent heat from drying out the fruit. We have sometimes had them until November 15th on one of our espaliers, which is over thirty feet high. 47. In August and September; small, oval fruit of mediocre quality. Small tree, but very pretty in shape, making it suitable for ornamental purposes. 48. OF SIBERIA WITH ROUND FRUIT; in August; fruit smaller than the previous one, just as bad. A shrub that grows to only a few feet, and is also cultivated for pleasure. 49. COMMON GRIETTIER; in July; small, very acid, mediocre fruit. Strong, vigorous tree, very low yield. 50. * GERMAN GRIOTTIER; July 15th; fruit with red, very watery, bitter pulp, medium size, poor quality. Tree of medium size, with very multiplied branches, not very productive. 51 "GRIOTTIER DE PORTUGAL; in August; fruit with firm pulp, a little less acidic than the others. Quite vigorous tree, with gathered branches, not very productive. CULTIVATION. Cherry trees do well in all types of soil, but must be deep and dry for cherry and cherry-cherry trees; cool without being damp for the others. They fear clayey, cold, marshy or sandy soils, and thrive in 3008 calcareous and granitic soils. It is especially in countries interspersed with hills and mountains that cherry trees grow, sometimes prodigiously. They are propagated by seed, offshoot or grafting. The first method produces taller, more vigorous trees that are less prone to gum disease; the second produces smaller trees, especially if grafted onto a sour cherry tree; finally, if dwarf trees are required for cattails, espaliers or pyramids, the mahaleb is chosen as the subject. For grafting, we use escutcheons n°" 1, 2, 74, and more often the dead-eye escutcheon, n° 3. If the subject already has a certain strength, it can be grafted in slit n°' 5 and 8, or the branches are folded back to the stem, and the new shoots produced are budded. For sowing, stratify wild cherry, sour cherry and mahaleb kernels. In spring, in loose soil that has been fertilized since the previous autumn, they are sown in rays spaced fifteen to eighteen inches apart for mahalebs, and two to two-and-a-half feet apart for the others. As for the cores, they are spaced only five or six inches apart in the rows, as there are often many missing. We bury them by an inch or an inch and a half; and, if we want to hasten their growth, we mix a little potting soil into the earth we cover them with. Weed carefully and water as needed, as young plants are very sensitive to drought. The following spring, thin out and replant where necessary, continuing to give the usual care to a nursery, but always watering in drought conditions, especially griot seedlings. As for offshoots, they are detached in autumn from the base or roots of old trees; and, if they have a lot of hair, they can be grafted in slit the following spring, and in escutcheon at the second 1 sap, if the first grafts have not grown. Cherry trees are trained and trained as described on page 228. The ManaleB, Bois de S**.-Lucie (prunus mahaleb, L.) b, is a third-growth tree that grows naturally in the hedgerows and woods of southern France. Its bark is brown, speckled with gray when new. Its wood is hard and fragrant; its leaves are hairless, shiny, subcordiform, rounded and ovate; it bears four to six flowers in corymbs on a common, leafy stalk. Fruit small, blackish and bad. We mention this species because it is used as a grafting subject for varieties of other cherry trees. MICOCOULIER DE PROVENCE, or austral. (Celtis australis; L.). b. This taprooted tree grows to thirty or forty feet, flowers in April and May, and in autumn produces an oval, fleshy fruit the size of a small cherry and of a blackish purple; its flesh is white and its flavor pleasant, but a little astringent, it is of very-mediocre quality. The wood of the hackberry is blackish, hard, binding and very elastic, making excellent vat rings with the young branches. The trunk takes on a beautiful polish and is particularly useful for making wind instruments; it is also highly regarded for sculptural work, as it is not prone to splitting or cracking. This tree thrives in deep, open, rich, moist and warm soils. In the north, it needs a warm exposure. It can be propagated by sowing seeds in pots or terrines as soon as they ripen, or by stratifying them like other pits. The first sprout in a year or two, and the others in the spring of the same year. The safest thing to do would be to shelter the young plants for the first two winters, by putting them in an orangeU 511 rie; but, in the absence of a greenhouse, it may be sufficient to cover them with a large bed. When they are eighteen inches or two feet tall, we put them in nurseries, in beds with good exposure, and treat them like other fruit trees. In Provence, where the southern hackberry is fairly common, it is used as a subject for grafting other species, using the same methods as for pear trees. MALE CORN, CORNIER (Cornus mascula; L.). b... Indigenous. A small tree twelve to fifteen feet tall, flowering in February and producing a fruit the size and shape of an olive, but with transparent, watery pulp and a pretty cherry-pink in the ordinary species. This fruit, called dogwood, coriole or horn, has a sweet, slightly sour taste, quite pleasant when perfectly ripe; it makes excellent jams. Dogwood is extremely hard and quite bendable; it is used to make stiles, hoops and so on. Several species and varieties with edible fruit are grown. 1° SAUVACE HORN; fruit small, red, a little sharp. 2° FRANC HORN; fruit larger, softer, red. 3° CORNOUILLER A FRUIT JAUNE; differs only in fruit color. 4° WHITE CUCUMBER (cornus alba; L.); from North America; smaller, earlier fruit, white, sweeter flavor. GROWING. This tree is very sturdy, unaffected by the inclemency of the seasons, and thrives in all soils and all exposures, even shady ones; but its growth is extremely slow, and it hardly rises more than ten feet in the space of fifteen years. It is propagated by stratifying cores, which are then trained like other trees; by scions 70, which are detached from the stock in autumn or spring; by strangulation; and its varieties by slit and escutcheon grafts, nos. 3 and 5. It is easy to prune and shear, and can take on any pleasing shape. FIFTH DIVISION. Fruits with seeds and more or less firm flesh. COMMON QUINCE. (Pyrus cydonia; L. Crdonia vulgaris, HORT. PAR.) D. Southern Europe. A low, twisted tree with brown bark and cottony young shoots. It flowers in April and May, and ripens in autumn; its fruit is large, more or less elongated, very slender or rather cottony when young, fragrant, aromatic, acerbic and can only be eaten cooked. It is used to make liqueurs and a very pleasant jelly. This tree is grown more for grafting pear varieties than for its fruit. A. COGNASSIER DE PORTUCAL; large flowers; ribbed fruit, larger, less gravelly and less acerbic than the common quince; tree larger, more vigorous, less twisted, but afraid of the cold. a. COGNASSIER D'ANGERS; similar to the previous one, but larger, less acerbic, could perhaps be eaten raw, having the defect of splitting a little. B. COCNASSIER COMMUN; flowers and fruit smaller, the latter more cottony, gravelly and acerbic than the former; tree not afraid of the cold. It has two sub-varieties. a. COING-POIRE; elongated fruit, not as bad as the next. b. COING-POMME; rounded fruit, the worst of all. CULTIVATION. Quince trees are not difficult to grow, either in terms of soil quality or exposure, since they thrive wherever pear trees are grown, and even in places where pear trees are unsuccessful. However, they prefer light, chalky, cool soil. They thrive best in sunny and sunny exposures, but the Portuguese and Chinese quince trees do better in southerly exposures. They are propagated from seed, cuttings, offshoots and marcottes; the ease of reproducing free varieties by means of cuttings means that they are rarely grafted, with the exception of quince trees from China and Portugal, which are grafted onto the common quince tree to make them more robust. Slot grafts n°' 5, 8, 10, and escutcheon grafts n°' 1, 2, 3, 108, are the most commonly used, either as subjects for their own varieties or for those of the pear tree. To propagate them from seed, prepare suitable soil by good ploughing, and loosen it as much as possible; make shallow furrows in it, and throw in the seedlings, which are covered with an inch and a half of soil or compost. Weeds are pulled out as they appear, and the seeds germinate the following spring. All that remains is to give them the usual care, and to thin out the seedlings to transplant where they are lacking. Sowing produces trees that are more vigorous and less prone to disease; but it takes a long time, which is why cuttings are preferred. In spring, before vegetation has begun, young shoots from the previous year are cut and, if possible, left with a small heel of two-year-old wood. They are then planted, but not pricked, which tears the bark and causes cankers at the root collar. Place them in rows two feet apart, and fifteen or eighteen inches apart in the rows. They recover quickly, 2. 33 and can sometimes be budded the following September. If the summer is dry, they should be watered. To propagate offshoots, cut the trunk of an old quince tree and mound it, i.e. pile earth around it. It doesn't take long for a number of young shoots to emerge, which take root and grow, and are usually ready to be raised the following year, to be nursed and grafted. The most advantageous method, and the one most commonly used around Paris, consists in planting young rooted plants, cutting them back to the ground when they are ready to produce vigorous shoots, and buttering them to help the shoots take root. This "marcotte" is lifted in autumn to set up in place and in the nursery. Once a tree is in place, it is cultivated as described on page 250. COGNASSIER DE LA CRINE ( cydonia sinensis, Thouin, ANn.), is a new species whose flowers, which appear in April and May, are very red and spread a pleasant fragrance. They are followed by a very large, elongated, oval, pot-bellied, yellowish-green fruit, with a stiff, short stalk and tough, acerbic flesh that ripens poorly in ordinary years in the Paris climate; perhaps its cultivation would be advantageous in the south. It is possible that by sowing, we could later obtain an early variety that would ripen more easily. We regret that no attention has yet been paid to quince seedlings, as we are convinced that artificial fertilization of pear trees would enable us to create new varieties which, without detracting from the beauty of this interesting fruit, would improve its quality to the point of making it edible. PEAR TREE (Prrus communis; L. ). b. Native. A tree from fifteen to fifty feet tall, depending on the variety, with taproots, whose fruit and various uses are well known to everyone. It flowers in April. Its wood is heavy, tight, hard, reddish, takes on a very fine polish, and is not prone to being bitten by insects; it is therefore sought after by carpenters, cabinetmakers, luthiers, turners and wood engravers; but these qualities, all combined in the wild pear tree, disappear as the fruit becomes more mature. This tree sometimes reaches enormous size: Évelin tells of a pear tree in Erfort, England, whose trunk was six feet in diameter, and which yielded seven muids of perry annually. We saw one between Limeille and Valenton, near Brunoy, which produced more than forty thousand pears one year; it was about three feet in diameter and forty feet high. Pear trees have been cultivated since ancient times, and it seems that the oldest known variety is the rousselet, at least according to Pliny. The number of these varieties has grown so prodigiously that catalogs from the time of Louis XIV list them at nearly eight hundred, giving over fifteen hundred French names. There must have been a lot of duplication, as we don't have nearly as many today. We will list, in order of maturity, all those that are worthy of cultivation. Good varieties are marked with an asterisk". A. SUMMER FRUIT. In June. 1. T POIRE AMIRÉ JOANNET, petit Saint-Jean; small, light lemon-yellow; tender flesh, quite pleasant when at its precise point of ripeness. Ripens at the end of the month. Vigorous tree, more successful on franc than on quince. 66. * HATIVE LUCNÉ; medium-sized, elongated fruit, two to three inches long, pale green; half-melting flesh, sweet, fairly fragrant. Fruits ripen at the end of the month. Vigorous, productive tree. 67. A vigorous tree, bearing fruit in bunches, very productive. B. AUTUMN FRUIT. October. 68. * CRASSARE, crésane bergamotte crassane; fairly large fruit, two and a half to three inches in diameter, rounded, green speckled with gray, yellowing a little on the sunny side; melting flesh, sweet, fragrant, very good; but, in certain years and on individuals planted in unsuitable soil, it becomes stony and bitter. It is harvested from the eighth to the fifteenth of the month, and keeps until January. The tree likes soft, cool, damp soil; it is very vigorous; it has a very pretty variety with white variegated thresholds. 69. Ripens at the beginning of the month. Quick to set fruit, very successful in espaliers. Sunrise and sunset exposures are the most favorable for its fruit to keep well. 22."
