CULTIVATION. Pear trees grafted on franc like free soil, rather light than strong, but above all deep and not too dry. Those grafted on quince do fairly well in light, substantial, loose soil, although not very deep. All varieties can be propagated by grafting onto wild, hard or quince trees, depending on the shape you wish to give the tree. On this subject, see page 230. The sauvageon is a tree that comes spontaneously from the seed of a wild fruit or from an offshoot. It is collected from the forest and placed in the nursery. The franc is a tree that has been sown in a garden, and whose initial vigor has already been tamed by cultivation; it is the most widely used. As for quince, see cultivation on p. 512. Franks offer three degrees of strength, depending on whether they come from wild fruit seeds, perry pears or knife pears. Wild fruit pears produce robust, very vigorous trees, which do well in the poorest soils; in this case, it would be advantageous to use them for full-wind planting, especially for varieties that develop rapidly and need abundant sap. Pear trees grown from perry seeds are vigorous and suitable for full-wind grafting for orchards. If mid-wind or large espaliers and cattails are required, preference should be given to trees from knife pear seedlings; and these trees will be all the sweeter for the more advanced the fruit from which they were sown. When it's time to sow, obtain good fruit pips and expose them to the air for a few hours to dry out, after which they are placed in dry sand to preserve them until the time comes to sow them. As for perry pears, they are collected in the pomace that comes from the crushed fruit to express the liqueur, and preserved in the same way. Seedlings are sown in the first days of spring; to do this, good ploughing prepares soft, substantial, deep soil; rays are traced six inches apart, peppercorns are thrown in and covered with two fingers of soil. The whole seedling is then mulched with litter, to prevent frost or the soil from fighting and drying out. It doesn't take long for the seedling to emerge, at which point it is weeded, hoeed, purged of weeds and thinned out if necessary. Most growers lift it in late autumn to put it in the nursery, but we believe that, whatever its strength, it's better to leave it in place for two years; it roots better, becomes stronger, and produces much more vigorous trees. If the nursery is set up in dry, sandy soil, transplanting should be done in autumn; in wet, clayey soils, it's much better to wait until spring. When trees are to be trained for full-wind conditions, the pivot should be kept as short as possible, but cut back when the trees are to be trained as cattails or espaliers; this operation slows down their vigor and causes them to set fruit much earlier. Young trees are trained as described in the "Péppinière" article in the first volume of this book, and prepared for grafting, either near the root collar, or at the top of a long stem; in this case, they are pruned into hooks, according to the principles given on page 245. When they have reached the right size, they are grafted onto a high stem six or eight inches from the ground; and this last method is always the best, even for full-wind trees; for, if an accident should break them, the tree can be restored by amputating it below the fracture and reforming it again. The procedures most generally used for grafting pear trees are those described in the history of scape grafting, n°" 1, 2, 3, 74, 108; and cleft grafting, n°* 5, 6, 8. The following year, the trees are put in place, and maintained throughout their life, according to the pruning principles indicated on page 230. We have a few more observations to make on the cultivation of this precious tree. Pear blossoms, which appear early, are sometimes affected by late frosts, to which they are very sensitive, especially when the sun strikes the pistils. We must try to protect them from the sun's rays by means of canvas and straw mats, which is only practical for espaliers and cattails. Pear trees often alternate their production, i.e. they produce a lot of fruit one year and rest the next. This is often the result of exhaustion caused by too much production, and the intelligent gardener can prevent it by removing some of the fruit that would overload the tree. However, it's natural for certain varieties to alternate, without art being able to effectively oppose it. Here's the simple reason: we know that it takes two years for a fruit bud to form; if all the buds on a tree flower at the same time, it's clear that others won't flower until two years later, and the tree will alternate without exhaustion. Prolonged rain at flowering time also aborts harvests by dispersing pollen before fertilization, as we said in our first volume. This disadvantage can be overcome by sheltering the trees with straw mats, if possible. When continuous rainfall occurs during the development of the fruit, the sap often abandons the fruit and travels to the wood, with the result that a large proportion of the pears fall off, and the others grow little, crack, remain insipid and even become stony. Only a few espaliers can be saved from this disadvantage. When the tips of a tree's branches dry out and die, the reasons must be sought in its temperament. If the damage is caused by insects, cankers or other diseases, it should be treated as described in the Plant Diseases article; the branches should be cut back, the soil on the roots renewed, and good fertilizers used. Sometimes this decline is caused by old age; in this case, by bringing the tree up to its trunk, you can prolong its existence for a few more years. Pear trees are quite prone to moss and lichen, especially in poor or damp soils: they must be scrupulously cleared of them; finally, they should be given suitable ploughing and good manuring every four or five years. COMMUR. APPLE (Malus communis, L.) b. Indigenous. This second-growth tree has roots that are more tractive than taprooted. It thrives only in temperate and even slightly cold countries. In Italy, Spain and the south of France, it is unsuccessful or only very mediocre, whereas it produces considerably in our northern departments and in England; this is because its flowers, which appear in May, are very sensitive to sunlight and heat. We are sufficiently familiar with its fruit and the uses to which it is put that we need not deal with it here. The wood of the wild apple tree is somewhat similar to that of the pear tree, but is less hard and much less pleasing in color. It is much sought-after by carpenters and turners. The number of apple varieties has increased considerably1 in the last century. It seems that in the thirteenth century, only two knife apples were known, in the sixteenth four; in La Quintinie's time, twenty-five were known, but only seven were estimated, and today we have a considerable number, which we will describe. CULTIVATION. Apple trees like open, strong, soft, slightly cool soil, which need not be very deep. In sand, clay or chalk, they do very poorly. They multiply by seed, offshoots and grafting. When you want to sow, the choice of seedlings is not indifferent; for it is on this choice that depends the greater or lesser vigor and elevation of the trees that you graft onto the subjects they provide. See what we say about this on page 245. Depending on how strong and tall you want your trees to be, you should choose: 1° wild fruit peeps, if you can't find wild ones in the forests; 2° cider fruit peeps; 3° knife fruit peeps. These first three seedlings are often given the name "francs". 4° doucin seedlings or offshoots; 5° paradis seedlings or offshoots. In all cases, they are sown and cultivated in the nursery in exactly the same way as pear trees, with the slight difference that they are ploughed less deeply, because their roots extend further into the earth. When young trees are planted, after they have been grafted, they are spaced thirty to forty feet apart, depending on whether the soil is mediocre or good, and whether the tree is grafted on sauvageon, egin or franc. Counter-palms, half-stems, etc., grafted on doucin, are planted from twelve to twenty feet; cattails, pyramids, from six to ten; finally, paradises from four to five. In light, hot soils, doucin-grafted trees are preferred, as the roots of this species sink deeper than those of paradise, and are therefore less prone to drying out during hot, rainless summers. As for exposure, apple trees are not dissimilar; they do equally well in the north, east and west, in the lowest and coolest locations; however, they fear hot exposures in the south of France, and even in the climate of Paris. They are pruned and trained as described on page 245 ff. Every year, they are given a shallow ploughing so as not to injure the roots, and every three or four years, the soil covering their first roots by five or six inches is removed and replaced by another in which a well-consumed fertilizer, analogous to the nature of the soil, has been mixed. Although this tree is rather afraid of amputations, when it is old and starting to dry out at the end of its branches, you can prolong its life by a few years, by cutting it back to near its trunk. It is very susceptible to insects, decay and cankers, and in these different circumstances, it is treated as we said in our first volume in the article Plant diseases. DOMESTIC SORBER, CORMY ( Sorbus domestica; L.). b. Native. This precious tree, unfortunately all too neglected, usually reaches a height of forty to fifty feet, and much more in soils well suited to it. Its fruit, called corme or sorbe, is picked in autumn; it is stored on straw and eaten when it is soft. Fermentation produces a cider that is stronger than apple cider, but cannot be preserved. In Sweden, it is used to make a liqueur that replaces wine. Mountain ash wood is heavy, tight, hard as horn, and takes on a superb polish. It is much sought-after by turners, carpenters, cartwrights, óbénistes, wood engravers, gunsmiths and carpenter-mechanics. The latter use it to make wheel teeth, lantern spindles, and generally all machine parts exposed to the greatest friction. Its branches make a beautiful black dye. Five varieties are known. 1° The SORBIER FRANC, which grows spontaneously on the edge of woods; it becomes very large, and its wood is one of the best, but the fruit is very small. 2° The SORDIERA FRUIT PYRIFORME, whose slightly larger fruit is shaped like a small pear. 1 3° The SORBIER A FRUIT OVALE; its fruit is more elongated, egg-shaped. 4° The SORDIER A PETIT FRUIT ROUGE; fruit very-small, fibrous, late, of a bad taste, very acerbic like those of the three preceding." 5° The SORBIER A GROS FRUIT BOUCE; the fruits of this one are much bigger, earlier, less acerbic and better than those of the preceding ones. GROWING. Sorb trees like open, light, cool soil, with a sunny or south-facing exposure. They do wonderfully well in fields, vineyards, paddocks and semi-cultivated areas, such as roadsides, hedges, etc. Perhaps the reason for abandoning their cultivation is that they are extremely slow-growing, and hardly bear fruit before they have been planted for thirty years: this long wait is incompatible with the impatience for pleasure and egoism that characterize our century. They are propagated from seedlings sown in nurseries, and governed absolutely like those of pear trees, and the replants are even less afraid of transplanting. They set perfectly, even when the stems have already reached three or four inches in diameter, which means they can be planted when they are already old, and therefore enjoyment can be greatly accelerated. They produce even earlier if you decide to graft them at the top of the stem, so as not to damage the quality of the wood. To graft them, use the same procedures as for pear trees, i.e. methods n° 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 10. NÉFLIER, MESPLIER, MESLIER. ( Mespilus germanica; L.) B. Grows naturally in hedgerows in France and Germany. Its fruit, which varies in size depending on the variety, is bitter and acerbic until it reaches the chard stage, at which point it acquires sweetness and becomes very pleasant. There are several varieties. Oe 1° NÉTLIERA FRUIT SANS PEPINS; singular fruit, but small and of mediocre quality. 2° A FRUIT PRÉCOCE; fruit of mediocre size, ripening long before the others. 3° A FRUFT LONG; oval, medium-sized fruit. 4° A GROSS FRUIT ROUND; very large fruit, the best and most cultivated of all. CULTIVATION. Medlars do very well in any soil, provided it is not too marshy, and at any exposure. However, they become larger and more productive in warm, free limestone soils. They require almost no special cultivation, apart from the care mentioned on page 251. They can be propagated 1° from seed, but this takes a long time, as they take two years to germinate; 2° from marcottes, which are only used for ornamental species; 3° from slit grafts, nos. 5, 6 and 8, or from escutcheon grafts, nos. 1, 2, 3 and 108, on hawthorn, medlar, quince and pear trees. Their fruits are picked at the beginning of October, and placed in a dry place on straw, where they ripen. BIBACIER, JAPANESE NEPLAR. (Mespilus japonica; THUNB.) B. From China. Shrub hardly reaching eight to ten feet in height. Its leaves are large, oval, elongated and cottony on top; it flowers in May, and produces a yellow, slightly hairy fruit, shaped like a small medlar, which is very pleasant to eat. This medlar tree fears the cold, especially when spawned. Grafted from hawthorn using the same procedures as the previous variety, it has withstood the winters of the Paris climate quite well, at least for the last five years. It is planted in light, warm soil, against a wall facing south, and is stocked during the cold season. Its vegeta908 tion is very beautiful, which means it can be pruned regularly in espaliers. It can be propagated from seeds sown in light soil and terrine as soon as they reach maturity, and placed in the orangery during the off-season. They germinate the following spring, and all that's left for the young plant to do is to take the usual care of greenhouse shrubs. AZEROOT, NAPLES MEDLAR, SPANISH THORN. (Mespilus azarolus; L.) b. Native to southern France. Third-generation tree. Blooms in May, and in autumn gives a fruit of rather mediocre quality, called ponsmette. Several varieties are available. I° WITH LONG FRUIT. 2° WITH RED FRUITS. 3° WITH YELLOW FRUIT. CULTIVATION. This tree is grown in the same way as the medlar; it can be propagated from seed, or by grafting onto hawthorn, quince or medlar. It prefers light, warm soil and a southern exposure if its fruit is to be edible. HORSETAIL (Cratœgus) 6. We grow several species of this genus, whose fruit is edible and even has a flavor that pleases many people, but only when they have been left to ripen perfectly on straw, otherwise they are harmful. These trees provide a very hard, supple, bindable and tough wood, capable of receiving a very fine polish; it is therefore sought after by turners, sculptors and mechanics. 1° ALISIER TERMINAL, allouchier des bois (Cratœgus terminalis; L.) Native; red, rounded, cherry-sized fruit; twenty-foot tree with taproots. 2° ALISIER BLANC, allouchier (Cratœegus aria; L.) Native; red, rounded fruit, about the same size as the preceding one; straight tree, twenty-five or thirty feet tall. It has given rise to a long-leaved variety known as Allouchier de Bourgogne, whose fruits differ little. 3° ALISIER DE FONTA!NEBLEAU (Cratœegus latifolia; ExcYcL.) Native; orange-red fruits, slightly larger and longer than the preceding ones. Twenty-five to thirty feet tall. 4° ALISIER AMELANCHIER (Cratœgus amelanchier; H. P. Cratœgus rotundisolia; Exeycl.) Native; blackish-blue fruit, smaller, of even poorer quality. An eight- to nine-foot shrub that also grows in Sumatra, whose inhabitants eat the fruit, which they call gamomong. CULTIVATION. Alisiers do well in all types of soil, but prefer open, light soil and an open, airy, northerly exposure. They can be propagated from seed, offshoots, marcottes and by grafting onto hawthorn. To sow, stratify the seeds, and if you want them all to sprout at once, leave them in the sand for eighteen months. For the rest, it is grown in the same way as medlar. SIXTH DIVISION. Berry-shaped fruit with more or less liquid pulp. MURIER (Morus) 6. Trees of this genus are highly cultivated, but more for their leaves, which are fed to silkworms, than for their fruits, which are nevertheless quite pleasant. Most are dioecious, i.e. the male flowers are on one individual and the female flowers on another. Their cultivation in France dates back only to the reign of Charles IX. Mulberry wood is yellow, fairly hard and suitable for various lathe and engraving work. By rolling the young stems like hemp, we extract from the bark a coarse filasse Pe. suitable for making ropes. There are several species and a large number of varieties, but they differ only in the quality of the leaves for feeding silkworms. 1° MurieR BLaNc. (Morus alba; L.) From Asia Minor. A vigorous, tall tree with small, sweet, slightly bland white fruit, much sought-after by poultry. Silkworms in particular are fed on the leaves of this species. - 2° ITALIAN MULBERRY. (Morus italica; ENCYCL.) A taller, more vigorous tree than the previous one, with larger, white fruit of the same flavor. The pink-fruited Italian mitrier is an interesting variety; its fruit is smaller, but a little less sweet. 3° MUriEr BLANC D'ESPAGNE; a very vigorous tree, with broad leaves; fruit slightly larger than the previous variety, but of the same quality. 4° BLACK MURDER (Morus nigra; L.) From Asia Minor. Twenty-five to thirty-foot tree, with very large, black, pleasant fruit, ripe from July to September. 5° RED MULBERRY. (Morus ruba; L.) From North America. Tree from forty to fifty feet tall, with fairly large, slightly dark red fruit, pleasant to the taste. We have two new varieties from China, but we don't yet know the fruit. CULTIVATION. We must warn our readers that we are considering the mulberry here only in terms of its fruit, and not at all in terms of its leaves. Those wishing to raise silkworms will find sufficient works to consult, if they wish to learn about the mulberry varieties to be preferred for this kind of industry, and the particular care to be given to the trees. Mulberry trees like strong, moist, warm soil; however, they do quite well in all, and especially in yards, rubble and upturned ground. They can be propagated by seed, cuttings or layers, the latter being the quickest and most widely used method. For sowing, we choose the most beautiful blackberries that have been fertilized, as the others, sometimes larger and always more pleasant to eat, contain no seeds. We extract the seeds by crushing them with our hands in a vase full of water, and sow them in spring in good, well-disturbed soil. The same care is given as to ordinary sowing, until the young plants are strong enough to be transported to the nursery, which usually happens in the second or third year; they are placed there in spring, and the following year they can be grafted in slit by procedures n° 5, 6, 8; in escutcheon, n°e 2, 3, 108, and in flute, n° 6, 63, 64, 65 and 113. When they have reached a suitable size, i.e. when their stems have grown from two and a half to four inches in circumference, they are placed in any exposure, but best sheltered from north winds and in half-sun; all that remains is to give them the care mentioned on page 269. Cuttings are taken in spring, using wood from the previous year, in light, cool soil in the shade. They are given some watering, but only to maintain the freshness of the soil, and not enough to expose them to rot. When they are restarted, they are treated in the same way as nursery stock. They can be pruned in the same season, either by strangling them in the usual way, or by cutting them off at ground level and staking them, as we did for quince pruning. PAPER MULBERRY. (Morus paprrifera; L. Broussonetia papyrifera; WillD.) 6. From China. In Japan, where this tree is widely cultivated, the bark of its branches is used to make a paper strong enough to cover the parasols used by 2. 36 its inhabitants. In Louisiana, the young shoots are selected, retted and spun into yarn, which is used by women to make cloth. In any case, the broussone tree grows faster than mulberry trees, is no more sensitive to the cold, and succeeds perfectly in warm, light and even sandy soils. Its leaves are also used as silkworm food. They have the peculiarity of taking on completely different shapes: some are whole, others lobed, digitized, indented, as if eaten away, etc. Its fruits are globular, formed by the aggregation of small, sweet, pleasant berries, which pull away from the receptacle when ripe, and hang there by a small net. They ripen in September. This tree multiplies by seed, sprouts and offspring. It is grown in exactly the same way as the mulberry tree. A male must be placed next to the female if fertile seeds are to be collected. For some years now, we've been growing a new variety with hooded leaves, whose fruit we haven't yet seen. COMMON ARBUS, of the Prrenaea family, strawberry tree. (Arbutus unedo; L.) b. Indigenous. Evergreen shrub, twelve to fifteen feet tall, with white, bell-shaped, fragrant flowers appearing from July to January. They are followed by round, hanging berries of a beautiful red when ripe, somewhat resembling large strawberries. They are good to eat, and are known as arbouses. This charming shrub likes open, light soil and north-west exposure. In its youth, it is rather delicate and somewhat afraid of frost in the Paris climate; it is therefore best to protect it with dry leaves and litter, or even by placing it in a pot or orangery close to the sun for the first three or four years. It can be propagated by seed, sprouts and cuttings. As soon as the seeds are ripe, sow them in a terrine on a warm bed. When the seedling is an inch or two old, transplant it into a pot and leave it there until it is strong enough to be planted in the ground. THE ALPINE SHRUB, (arbutus alpina; L.), is a creeping sub-shrub that grows in the Pyrenees, the Alps, Siberia and Lapland. Inhabitants of Lapland feed on its fruits, which are almost identical to those of the previous species. It is grown in the same way, but is much more delicate and difficult to preserve in the Paris climate. The IRELAND SHRUB, (arbutus andrachne; L. ), is a shrub of fifteen to twenty feet, with red fruit, firmer than the former, with less prominent seeds; its quality is even more mediocre. It is grown in the same way, and seems to us to be even more robust. RASPBERRY (Rubus) 6. This genus offers more or less thorny shrubs, whose excellent fruit, ripening in July, rivals the strawberry for fragrance and flavor. There are several species and varieties. 1° FRANBOISIEA ROUGE CONUR (rubus idœus; L.); small, very sweet fruit. It has given us the following varieties: a. Large-fruited red raspberry; fruit larger than that of the preceding species, sweet, fragrant, excellent. We brought it from Holland. b. Raspberry with large, flesh-colored fruit; larger, better fruit, very plump and never lacking in flavor. We obtained this precious variety from seedlings of the previous one. c. Alpine raspberry, available every month; pedunculated, red, very good fruit. Darbrisseau produces twice, in spring and autumn. 908 d. Raspberry with white fruit. The fruit differs from that of the common raspberry only in color; it is a little less fragrant. e. Raspberry with amber-white fruit; fruit much larger and better than that of the preceding variety, tending slightly to yellow. GROWING. Raspberries thrive in all types of soil, although they prefer light, cool, shady soils, in which they are grown as described on page 269. They can be propagated from seed if you wish to obtain new varieties, in which case they are sown like mulberries. More commonly, we use the numerous offshoots that grow at the base of the tree to propagate them. These are detached from the stumps, and planted from November to February or March, depending on climate and temperature. These shrubs have the disadvantage of crumbling and considerably exhausting the soil, to the point of harming neighboring plants and themselves. They therefore need to be moved every four or five years, otherwise they degenerate, the fruits shrink and diminish considerably in number. It's true that you could leave them in place by changing the soil or breaking it up deeply to put the soil underneath back on top, and giving them fertilizer; but then the raspberries lose some of their fragrance. Every year, they are given a shallow ploughing. In England, raspberry cultivation is very important, and is carried out as follows: raspberry bushes are planted in rows five feet apart, with the clumps three feet apart, and are raised against a strong stake around which they are tied in sheaves. Preference is given to varieties a and b. HEDGE RONCES (rubus fruticosus: L.), BLUE RASPBERRIES (rubus cœsius; L.) and their thornless, curly and multiflorous varieties produce fruit similar to raspberries, but with blackish pulp, firmer and more acidic. They are used to color wines, and alcohol is distilled from them. We've brought back this latest and newest variety from England. These spiny, creeping shrubs are cultivated like raspberries. ROSEMARY (rubus rosœfolius, HORT. PARIS.), from China, is a pretty little shrub, two to three feet tall, whose fruits are large like those of the common raspberry, but brighter red and with a much less pleasant flavor. Same cultivation, but needs to be sheltered with a little bedding in harsh winters. LOTOS OR ITALIAN PLAQUEMIN, (Diosprros Lotus; L.) b. Oriental. This tree, twenty-five or thirty feet high, with very beautiful green foliage, flowers in June and July. Its flowers are followed by a soft fruit, the size and color of a wild plum, with a mild, sweet, pleasant taste. THE VIRGINIA PLAQUEMIN ( diospyros virginiana; L.), grows higher than the preceding one; its fairly large, slightly elongated fruit is yellowish and transparent. THE KAKI PLAQUEMIN (diospyros Kaki; L. F.), from Japan; produces fruits called figs -Kakes, of a beautiful cherry red color and excellent flavor. It is unfortunate that this tree cannot withstand our winters around Paris, where it must be kept in orangeries. In the south of France, it could be grown in the open ground. CULTIVATION. Plaqueminiers are not difficult to grow, but they do require clean, light, cool soil. The first likes a southern exposure, the second a northern one. All three are propagated from seed, which is sown as soon as ripe like mulberry 908, but in a terrine with a warm layer; from marcottes by strangulation, and by approaching grefse in the case of Virginia. The rest of their cultivation is unremarkable. VINE (Vitis vinifera, L.) D. Native to Asia. We won't go into detail here on the history of this precious shrub, nor on the uses to which the grape is put; suffice it to say that, according to Strabo and Justin, it was the Phocaean colonies that brought the vine with them when they came to found the city of Marseille, and that it soon spread from southern Gaul to all other parts where it could be cultivated. We would also like to point out to our readers that France is the country where wine is best for the ordinary purposes of life. In warmer climates, grapes provide only a spirituous, very heady liquor, of which only a very small quantity can be drunk without danger. A considerable number of grape varieties are cultivated in vineyards for their usefulness. So as not to go too far afield, we'll only talk about those grown in gardens for table use. A. WHITE GRAPES. 1. CHASGELAS DE FONTAINEBLEAU, golden, Champagne grape; the most highly esteemed for table use; large, elongated, loose bunches, with round, loosely-packed berries of varying size, light green or yellowish, slightly shaded towards the sun, with soft, sweet pulp. It ripens in late September and early October, and keeps for a very long time. 2. CHASSELAS DE BAR-SUR-AUBE; stronger bunches, but looser, with larger, compressed berries, same color, firm pulp, soft, sweet, pleasant, ripening fifteen days earlier than Chasselas de Fontainebleau, which gave it preference in Brunoy gardens. It is more prone to run. 3. CHASSELAS CIOUTAT, an Austrian variety, differs from Chasselas de Fontainebleau only in its laciniate leaves, and in its bunches, which are smaller, less plentiful, and of the same quality as Chasselas de Fontainebleau. A. CHASSELAS MUSQUÉ; bunches closer together than Chasselas de Fontainebleau, with round, slightly compressed berries, less large, yellowish-white, less transparent; sweet, slightly musky pulp. 5. PETIT CHASSELAS HATIT; elongated, loose bunches, with small, whitish berries, less colorful than Chasselas; soft pulp, not very spicy, a little bland. 6. BLANC DOUX; fairly elongated, compound bunches, with small, light-white berries, liquid pulp, sweet, not very tasty. Quite early and very productive. 7. PANSE MUSQUEE; long, compound, loose bunches, with oval berries, swollen in the middle, transparent white, revealing whiter veins; very firm pulp, good in warm years. 8. Muscar Blanc, or Frontignan grape; medium bunch, elongated, narrow, ending in a rounded point; very tight, hard, crunchy berries, green to dark yellow on the sunny side, bluish-white pulp, very musky. It ripens with difficulty in the Paris area, especially if you don't take the precaution of removing the tightly packed seeds. 9. MUSCAT D'ALEXANDRIE, musky long-pass; very large, stalky, elongated bunches, with large, oval berries, nearly an inch long, light green with a slight shadow, hard, musky and pleasant. It rarely ripens around Paris, unless it is in a very warm location. 10. JERICHO or Promised Land grape; compound bunches of considerable length (said to be from twenty-four to thirty inches), with berries far apart, oval in shape, white; sweet pulp. It does not ripen well in the 7000 climate of Paris. This curious variety deserves to be grown in greenhouses. 11. CORINTHE BLANC; fairly large, elongated, very tight bunches; round, small, yellowish-white berries; sweet, melting, seedless pulp, very pleasant. Ripens in September. 12. VERDAL; large bunches; very large, russet-green berries; soft, sweet, fragrant pulp; excellent. In the Paris area, it ripens only in very warm years. 13. MORILLON BLANC; composite bunches, slightly elongated, with loosely-packed, rounded, medium-sized berries, whitish-green in color, with soft, sweet pulp. 14. PEARLED GRAPE; sparsely packed bunches with very green stalks; berries of variable size, usually small, oval, pale green, with soft, sweet pulp. 15. MORNAIN BLANc or mornan, mornanche; very similar to chasselas de Fontainebleau. Its berries are rounded, pale yellow, more or less reddish on the sunny side, with a soft, very pleasant pulp. It ripens easily, even north of Paris. 16. GOUAIS; fairly large bunches, with rounded, greenish-yellow berries, and abundant, not very tasty, liquid pulp. 17. GRISET BLANC; small, irregular, tight bunches with round, grayish-green berries; soft, sweet, fragrant, very pleasant pulp. 18. MEUNIER; bunches short, thick, tight, with large, round berries, very pale yellow. Pulp soft, sweet, pleasant. 19. VERJUS, bordelais, bourdelas; composite bunches, often extremely large, fairly tightly packed; oval berries, slightly more rounded at the top, large, yellowish-green, with firm, greenish-white pulp. It rarely ripens, so it is used only in green for cooking. 20. CORNICHON BLANC; long, loosely-packed bunches; very long berries, large towards the middle of their length, amiucis and a little curved at the top and base, giving them the shape of gherkins, yellowish-white, 4009 with a soft, sweet, very pleasant pulp; it needs a good harvest to reach full ripeness. 21. SAINT-PIERRE; large, tight bunches, round berries, yellowish-white; soft, sweet pulp, very good. B. VARIEGATED GRAPES. 22. RAISIN D'ALEP; medium-sized, loosely-packed bunches; white, black and variegated grapes of both colors on the same bunch; liquid pulp, not very fragrant. 23. CHASSELAS PANACRÉ; long, loose bunches, with small, whitish berries, variegated with red; pulp soft, sweet, a little bland. C. BLACK CURRANTS. 24. PRECOCE DE LA MADELEINE, July grape; small, tight cluster, small, rounded oval berries, black violet, very covered with glaucous dust; greenish pulp, not very sweet. The fruit's merit lies in its ripening in July and August. 25. CHASSELAS ROUGE; it differs from Chasselas de Fontainebleau in the color of its berries, which are red as soon as formed, and in that it is not musky. 26. VIOLET MUSCAT; elongated bunch of very tightly packed, russet-purple berries; semi-firm, sweet, fragrant, very good pulp. It ripens perfectly only in hot summers. 27. MUSCAT ROUCE; bunches slightly less elongated and less tightly packed than Muscat Blanc; rounded berries, brick-red or pale in the shade, purple-purple in the sunlight; sweet, musky pulp. 28. MUSCAT NOIR; elongated, narrow, loosely-packed bunches; small, round berries, black or dark purple, covered with glaucous dust; reddish pulp next to the skin, not very musky. 29. VIOLET CORNISHON; fruit similar to the white corniccon, but violet seeds, sometimes greenish towards the head. Ripens with greater difficulty in the Paris area. 30. CORINTHE VIOLET; fruit similar to white corinth, O08 but with slightly larger, light purple berries; also seedless. 31. BOURGUIGNOR ROIR; small, shortened, tight bunches with dark red raste; oval, dark red bunches. 32. PINTAU FLEURI DE LA CÔTX D'OR; medium-sized bunches; oval, black berries, fairly tightly packed, covered with a glaucous dust, liquid pulp, soft, sweet, fragrant. 33. TEINTURIER; short, slightly tight bunches, dark violet-red berries; same-colored pulp. This grape is used to color wines. 34. FRANCONIE A FRUIT LONG; long, loose, compound bunches; long, black berries, very covered with glaucous dust, with hard skin, white pulp and large seeds; mediocre quality in the Paris climate, where ripening is difficult. 35. PETIT CAMÉ ROUCE; small, slightly elongated, very square bunches, not very dark violet-red, with round, medium-sized pips; soft, not very sweet pulp. 36. VERJUS VIOLET DU JURA; composite bunches, very large, with tight, round, slightly compressed berries; pulp sweeter than in white verjuice. There are nearly six hundred varieties of grapes grown in vineyards; we have chosen from this list those we consider most suitable for growing and nurturing in gardens, to provide dessert fruit. CULTIVATION. The vine is unique in that in every province, every département, one might even say in every village, it is cultivated using specific methods that differ from one to the next. The result is that authors, considering as the best the methods of the country they have lived in or loved the most, have adopted them without further information, have recommended them as the only really good ones, and, for this ralson, have all contradicted each other. We do not believe, as they do, that vines should * - - - - - 1 - 4 4 - - P - 7 VINEYARDS. 571 be cultivated in the same way in all countries; we even believe that experience has taught each one the practices best suited to the quality of the soil, the usual temperature and the influences of the climate. Ask an Italian why he raises his vines twenty or thirty feet around the trunk of a tree; ask an inhabitant of the south of France why he raises them five or six feet on stilts; ask the inhabitants of Paris why they keep them as close to the ground as they can; they will all answer that experience has taught them these methods, but not one of them will deduce the physical reasons we have given on page 266. From this, and from the observations we have made in all the parts of Europe we have visited, we have concluded that wherever the vine has been cultivated for a long time, it is done in a way that is advantageous to the locality, and that experience is the best teacher. We do not claim, however, that its cultivation cannot be further perfected, but that this perfection will result in each country from observations made on the spot, by the most intelligent indigenous cultivators, and not from the multitude of books in which authors, who often lack the first notions of the laws of vegetation, devise new practices. Consequently, we leave to the estimable men who, in the agricultural societies of the départemens, have devoted their time to observation and their vigilance to their fellow citizens, we leave to them, we say, the difficult but honorable task of eliminating the viciousness of some particular practices, and of perfecting vineyard cultivation. We shall confine ourselves here to the more uniform cultivation of table grapes. The vine is more sensitive to climatic influences than to those of the soil: it succeeds fairly well in all Goo soils, provided they are not too wet or marshy. However, it prefers light, deep, warm soil. Its roots thrive on rocky outcrops and stones, sinking to great depths through cracks in rocks. It is usually propagated by cuttings, to which we give the name crossette, capon, etc., and by marcottes called provins, recouchées, couchage. It is rarely sown, as this method is time-consuming, and we cannot be sure of the quality of the fruit. Cuttings are branches from the previous year, well seasoned, about eighteen inches long, with a heel of one or two inches taken from two-year-old wood. They can be cut in autumn, or even better, after a hard frost. They can be kept with the heel in water or buried in cool, shaded soil, until they are ready to be planted. This is done in spring, just as the vines begin to show signs of growth with the swelling of their buds. In mined soil, or at least well-developed soil loosened by good ploughing, dig a twelve- to fifteen-inch hole, place the crossette in it with the lower part inclined towards the south, cover with soil and leave only the two upper eyes above the surface. All other care is limited to weeding, hoeing and watering if the plant has been planted against a wall facing south and the season is hot and dry. Pruning is nothing more than what we have called "marcottes simples" in our principles. When pruning, the most vigorous sarmen are left in their entire length to provide bedding. In spring, we dig a small pit eight inches deep against the stock, lay the marcotte in it, and cover it with earth, leaving only the two eyes of its tip sticking out at the surface. The following year, it is sufficiently rooted to be detached from the mother, removed and transplanted elsewhere. If you don't want this transplanting to stunt its growth, or if the young pupil is destined to travel, it's much better to make the marcotte in a basket or pot that you bury next to the stump. In this way, it can be removed with the rootball, either for packing or for immediate replanting. Sowing, an invaluable means of obtaining new or at least more vigorous varieties, is done in exactly the same way as for seed trees, and the young plants behave in the same way until they are put in place for cultivation like marcottes and cuttings. In the Paris climate, a seedling hardly bears fruit before the sixth or seventh year. There is a fourth means of propagation, little or not at all used in some countries, but in general use in others. We refer to grafting. See page 61. It should be noted that vines should never be smoked with fresh fertilizers, as the exhalations they produce attach themselves to the grapes and give them a bad taste which sometimes even passes into the wine. For each type of soil, the most suitable fertilizers should be used, i.e., divide the soils that are too strong with very light manures, such as ashes, soot, calcareous marl, sandy earth, etc., and give body to those that are too light, by mixing fatty manures, earth and clayey marl. If you wish to plant on land where vines have already been grown, you must wait at least three years until the roots of the old stock have completely decomposed, and the soil has acquired new nutrient sues. However, you can plant right away, provided you change the soil at least three feet deep, or, if it's in very good soil, by mixing the soil from the dig with a good quantity of much-consumed fertilizer. Every year, two or three good ploughings should be made, and the vines should be fertilized whenever necessary, as can be seen from the languor of the plant and the weakness of its shoots. As for the care to be given to the vine for training, pruning and disbudding, we have already dealt with this in detail (page 257), and we won't return to it here. The distance to be set between each vine stock cannot be specified, as it varies according to the quality of the soil, the climate and even the exposure, all of which have a considerable influence on the development of this shrub. Grapevines are subject to a number of accidents that can affect them to a greater or lesser extent. Late spring frosts are one of the most feared; their damaging effect is particularly noticeable when the cold of the night is followed by a blazing sun. It is said that burning straw smoke before sunrise is enough to slowly thaw out the affected buds, with no ill effects. Spray watering, in the form of fine rain, is even better, but can only be used on trellises or other vines covering a small area. A number of diseases still attack this interesting plant, for which we recommend the remedies indicated in the article on plant diseases. Birds attack grapes as soon as they ripen, and considerably reduce the harvest unless the trellises are covered with nets. The fruit can be protected from their voracity by enclosing it in paper or horsehair bags a little before ripening and when it is very dry, so that it does not rot. This method has the added advantage of preserving it on the shrub, in all its freshness, until the first frosts. GROSEILLIER. (Ribes) b. This genus is made up of small thorny or non-spiny shrubs, all interesting for their fruit, some for their attractive appearance, and capable, by pruning, of taking all the forms of fruit trees in miniature. The English have over three hundred varieties of thorny currant; we brought most of them over in March 1825, but have not yet seen the fruit. CULTIVATION. Redcurrants are not difficult to grow in any kind of soil; they grow well everywhere, but they prefer soft, fresh, sandy soil, where their fruit is larger and less acidic. They can be propagated from seed, which is sown like other pepines, or from cuttings made in autumn or after the frosts, or more usually from offshoots detached from old plants at the same time, or from marcottes, which root easily. Care is limited to a little ploughing, and pruning as described on page 269. As these shrubs age, they become overgrown with moss and the fruit becomes small. Young students give very good fruit, and in large quantities, from the second year of planting. This operation should be carried out every five or six years. You can keep currants for a very long time on bushes by wrapping them in straw a little before the fruit is fully ripe. VINE-PINE, MNRTIER (Berberis vulgaris, L.) 6. Native, thorny shrub whose oval, elongated, clustered fruits are filled with a pleasant, acidic pulp that makes very good jam. The bark of the shrub is used to make a fairly fine yellow dye. Several varieties are grown. 1° ORDINARY VINEYAR; red fruit. 2° VINETIER A CROS FAUrTS; larger, more attractive red fruit. 3° VINETIER A FRUITS VIOLETS. 4° VINETIER DU CANADA. 5° VINETIER A GROS TRUITS ROUCES SANS PEPINS; it lacks pepins only when the tree has reached a certain ageWe have new species in Nypaule whose fruiting we have not yet seen. CULTIVATION. Vineyards are even less difficult than currants in terms of soil quality; they succeed everywhere if you give them the care mentioned on page 270, and some ploughing. They can be propagated from seed, but as they take two years to germinate, and young plants require a great deal of care during their first few years, it is customary to propagate them by offshoots, marcottes or splinters, which are separated and planted in autumn. MYRTILLE LUCET, cranberry, wood or hind grape, brimbelle, moret, mouretier. (Vaccinium myrtillus; L.) 5. Indigenous. Small, two-foot shrub producing grape-like berries in July, similar to those of myrtle, blackish-blue in color, with liquid pulp, a little acidic and sweet, quite pleasant in taste. They are used to make jam, and by innkeepers to increase the color of their wines. The berries of the AIRELLE CANNEDERCE Ou COUSCINETIE, (vaccinium oxycoccos; L.) can be used for the same purposes; and if you live in the south, or have an orangery, those of the Airelie Do CaNADA (vaccinium macrocarpon; L.), whose berries are larger. CULTIVATION. These shrubs are quite delicate and only do well in very light, cool, shady sandy soil, better in heather soil. They can be propagated from seed sown in terrine and fralche soil, sheltered from the cold until the 900 seedling is strong enough to be planted. They can also be propagated from sprouts and marcottes, but in general, their cultivation is more difficult than important. SECOND CLASS. Trees that fear the cold and only do well in the open below 43 degrees latitude. CULTIVATED OLIVE TREE. (Olea europœa, L.) b. An evergreen tree in southern Europe, from fifteen to twenty-five feet high, whose fruits, called olives, are eaten when pickled, are used in cooking, and provide an excellent oil, the latter use accounting for most of their importance and the wealth of Provence, Languedoc, Italy and Spain. Olive wood is veined, fragrant and highly polished, making it much sought-after by cabinet-makers and table-makers. A fairly large number of varieties are cultivated, of which we will only mention those that are widespread in the south of France. 1 - WILD OLIVE, small, scanty fruit of mediocre quality. 2. OLIVIER TRANCANELOU, amélingue, amelode, almond-shaped fruit, excellent candied. 3. PICHOLINE OLIVE TREE; small, long fruit, the best candied, but keeps poorly. 4. CAYANNE or aglaudan OLIVE TREE; small, round fruit, yielding the best oil. 5. CORMEAU or CORNIAU OLIVE TREE; fruit somewhat resembling that of the corm tree, yielding very fine oil. 6 AMPOULLEAU OLIVE TREE; large, rounded fruit, yielding very fine oil. 7 MOUREAU OLIVE TREE; round, early fruit, yielding excellent oil. 8 SPANISH OLIVE TREE; fairly large, very bitter fruit. 206 9. BUIS LEAF OLIVE; medium-sized fruit; very robust tree. 10. LAURINE OLIVE; large, long, bumpy fruit, very candied. 11. SAUVACE D'ESPACNE; fruit remarkable for its truncated top. 12. OLIVE TREE; dark green fruit. 13. WHITE FRUIT OLIVE; medium-sized fruit. 14. ROYAL OLIVE; large, very fleshy fruit. 15. VERDALE OLIVE TREE; round, very good candied fruit. 16. OLIVIER BOUTEILLEAU; fruit in bunches. 17. OLIVIER PICAU; small, round fruit, variegated with red and black. 18. OLIVIER SALIERNE; small, round, blackish fruit. 19. OLIVIER DE LUCQUES; medium-sized, fragrant fruit. GROWING. These trees do fairly well in all types of soil, but do best in light, warm soils, especially by the sea. The tree grows tall and strong in solid, open soils, while in sandy, poor soils, the fruit is of much higher quality. They are only cultivated with advantage from the 25th degree of latitude to the 43rd, which proves that they are equally afraid of cold and too much heat. It has also been observed that sea air is favorable to them, and that they produce less inland than on the shores of the Mediterranean. Olive trees are propagated from seed, offshoots, marcottes and cuttings, and varieties are propagated by grafting, n° 1, 2, 3, 32, 65, 66, 67. An even easier method, which we have been using for the last ten years with great success, is to graft into the common privet. We invite growers in the south of France to try this method, which may be of great benefit to them, especially when they can use Japanese privet as a subject, because it is O0 more vigorous, grows faster and is therefore more in balance with olive trees. For sowing, it is necessary to obtain pits of the most vigorous varieties, growing naturally on the mountains, and better those of the wild fruits of the Greek Archipelago. Either sow them as soon as they ripen, or have them stratified, as they would otherwise not ripen until the second year of planting. They are spaced so that the plants are three feet apart in all directions. They are treated in the nursery in the same way as other fruit trees. When they are strong enough, they are lifted with extreme care, so as not to break the pivot; they are set in place, spaced thirty-six to fifty feet apart, depending on the quality of the soil, and are grafted once they have fully recovered, if they have not been grafted in the nursery. They are ploughed and fertilized as for other trees. Sowing takes a long time, as an olive tree hardly bears fruit for fifteen years; but if it were used, it would be the best way to prevent the degeneration that the inhabitants of the south of France complain about. Olive trees grow a lot of offshoots, which are removed from the base if they are not intended to provide new trees; otherwise, they are left to strengthen for three years, after which they are lifted and put in place. Marcots are made from branches the size of an arm, which are placed in the ground over winter to root. They are then detached from the stump and raised the following year. Cuttings are taken in autumn and spring from young wood. Place them in a nursery, and leave them there until they are large enough to be planted. This tree can also be propagated by its roots. 7 By cutting one close to the stump and raising its truncated parcel to the surface of the earth, it soon emits a bud, which rises with it when it forms a subject that is vigorous enough for this. This last method of propagation is preferable to the first, because it is less exhausting for the trees, especially if practised on small roots. Once a quincunx of olive trees has been planted, all that remains to be done is to give them the care mentioned on page 260. Cereals and vegetables can be grown under their shade; the trees thrive all the more, because they benefit from the fertilizers and ploughing these crops require. CULTIVATED JUJUPIER. (Ziziphus sativa; HoRT. PARIS. Ziziphus vulgaris, ENcrct. Rhamnus ziziphus; L.)5. From the East. A very thorny shrub, ten to fifteen feet tall, naturalized in Provence and Languedoc, where its fruits, called jujubes, ripen very well. They are oblong, olive-shaped and olive-sized, first green, then yellowish, and finally red; their whitish pulp is soft, sweet, pleasant when dried, and excellent when fresh. The jujube tree multiplies from pits that are smelled as soon as they ripen, or stratified during the winter. In southern France, it can be sown in nurseries and managed like the olive tree, but in northern France, where it must be grown in orangeries, it is sown in pots, on beds and under frames. It can also be propagated by strangulation or by offshoots. GOYAVIER POTRE, guaravier, Indian pear tree. (Psidium prriferum, L.) b. Indies. A tree in its country, a twelve- or fifteen-foot shrub in southern France, where it is naturalized. It produces a fruit as large as a crabapple, crowned like a medlar, greenish at first, then yellowish, with a soft, fragrant pulp, mildly acidic and quite pleasant. Eaten raw or cooked with sugar. The guava tree, as well as its more robust apple-shaped variety, can be grown in exactly the same way as the orange tree, and in the same soil. ORANGE (Citrus). b. Native to Asia. This name covers seven species which have given rise to a very large number of varieties, but many of which produce fruit that is bitter or so acidic that it is of no use, at least as a food substance. All are cultivated in pleasure gardens, where they are the most beautiful ornament: we will therefore describe them without exception in the history of the families, and following the botanical procedure we have imposed on ourselves in the last two volumes of this work. In the fruit garden, however, we shall only discuss edible species and varieties, and we shall only give their names to avoid unnecessary repetition. We will quote here only the French synonyms for 1. FRENCH ORANGE; fruit containing an abundant, pleasant, sweet juice. 2. DE LA CHINE; very good fruit, with a rind soft enough to be eaten. 3. DUBOISVIOLETTE; a slightly pyriform fruit, whose pulp detaches itself from the rind, to the point of playing in it like a bar of soap in its box. Soft, sweet, fragrant, excellent pulp; the best of all oranges. We received it directly from China from Mr. Dubois violette, to whom we dedicated it. 4. EARLY FRUIT; large, very good fruit, with thin rind. 5. DEPRIMED FRUIT; medium-sized fruit, very sweet, very good. 6. PYRAMIDAL; fruit small, firm pulp, mediocre. 7. WITH PYRIFORM FRUIT; fruit with red pulp around the circumference, yellow in the center, very pleasant. The tree has little fear of the cold around Nice, and its oranges keep very well. 10. PORTUCAIS; medium-sized fruit, yellow or reddish pulp, good, but a little acidic. There are other edible fruits, and even quite good ones, in the families that authors have named pompelmouse, lumie, limon, etc.; but, as they are not very widespread, we will only talk about them in the volumes dealing with ornamental plants. CULTIVATION. In most of France, the orange tree is grown only in crates or pots, so that it can be squeezed into orangeries during the winter, and in co eas it is strictly speaking only an ornamental tree; it requires special care, details of which will be given in the family histories. Here, we must only use it as a fruit tree, and only grow it in the open ground. Wherever the orange tree can be grown in the open ground, it is not fussy about the choice of soil, and does very well in all types, provided they are neither damp nor cold, neither too light nor too compact; all free, airy exposures suit it, except in its early youth, when it absolutely needs the south. The temperature that is most favorable to it is the one whose most constant average term is ten to eighteen degrees of Réaumur's thermometer, and it seems that it can only be found in France on the shores of the Mediterranean, or at most fifteen or twenty leagues from its shores, because further inland it ceases to be productive. Orange trees are propagated by seed, cuttings and marcottes; the varieties are propagated by means of the grafts we have described no. 1, a, 3, 67, 75, 108, which are common to them and most other fruit trees, and by those specific to them, see page 75. For the sowing operation, select and prepare the seeds as follows. We take a certain quantity of the best fruits, put them in a heap in the sun, and leave them to ferment for eight to ten days, after which we throw them into O water to macerate them for a few hours; we crush the oranges in our hands, and leave the whole thing to rest for a few moments; we then pick up the seeds that have sunk to the bottom, and throw away those that have floated to the surface.

If the season is right, i.e. if it's spring and the temperature is 15°C, sow immediately; if not, wait. Dry the seeds in the shade, and keep them in well-stoppered bottles until the right moment. When you're ready to sow, prepare the ground as evenly as possible, bury the pots or crates in which you'll sow the seeds, and cover the whole with an inch of light, loose soil, or better still, compost. Water and, if the temperature is right, the seedlings sprout in fifteen or twenty days. For vigorous trees, preference is usually given to pepins from wild or cultivated sour orange trees; however, some growers prefer orange pepins, even though they grow more slowly, because they are more resistant to cold, sturdier and produce more fruit. Finally, others recommend using seeds from pompelmouses, particularly pompoleon. During the first year, we simply pull out the weeds and water the plants to prevent the soil from drying out. In the second year, we lift them, taking care to leave a small root ball; we cut off the small twigs and lower leaves, and replace them in the ground eight or ten inches apart. In the spring of the third or fourth year of germination, the plants are usually strong enough to be transplanted into a larger nursery, or into the ground. In February or March, they are removed with the root ball and spaced eighteen inches apart in the nursery; in the field, six or seven feet apart if they are to be trained as espaliers; nine or ten feet apart if they are to be trained as counter-paliers, and eighteen or twenty inches apart if they are to be trained as plein-vent. These distances vary according to the species of orange and the quality of the soil. Finally, in the fifth year after sowing, they are ready for grafting. Experience has shown that it is better to graft orange trees a year or two after they have been planted than in the nursery. Propagation by cuttings is mainly used for pumice and citron trees, although it is equally successful for all species. From December to February, cuttings can be taken. This is how it works: prepare good soil with fertilizers and deep ploughing; open furrows eighteen inches apart and fifteen inches deep; select small branches from the orange trees you want to propagate, or better, the long buds that have grown during the summer, called plumets or gourmands, and which are usually removed in autumn, unless they have been kept for this purpose. They are cut to eighteen inches in length, and beveled at the base; they are buried in the furrows, with only two or three eyes emerging from the surface, which are left leafless; the plantation is watered as needed, and covered with litter at night to protect it from the cold. As soon as the cuttings have grown, all you have to do is give them the usual nursery care. Sometimes, root shavings are used for the purpose just described. Propagation by cuttings is widespread throughout southern Europe, because it is quicker than sowing; but it nevertheless produces much less vigorous trees. As for marcotting, this is rarely used, because it produces weak, puny plants that require a great deal of care; it can be done by strangulation in the ordinary way, or by cepée; and in the latter case, here's how it's done. When the stem forms a trunk, it is cut three or four inches from the ground, giving rise to a large number of shoots which are left to strengthen for two years. A tight ligature is then made at the base of each one, and good soil is mounded over it, bringing it close to the stump so as to form a casing seven to eight inches high. All that remains to be done is to give the necessary care until the marcottes have taken root. They are then lifted and placed in a nursery. The young trees are often visited, whether obtained from seed, cuttings or marcottes; slugs and insects are destroyed, weeds are pulled out, hoeing is carried out two or three times a year, and frequent watering is given during hot spells. Once the orange trees are in place, they are shaped, pruned and trained, as described on page 52. Twice a year, deep ploughing is carried out, penetrating right down to the roots: the first is done in spring, the second in autumn; fertilizers are indispensable to them, and all suit them very well, although each grower gives preference to a particular combination, which his neighbor often rejects in favor of another. This dissidence of opinion and practice is simply the result of the differences in soil types. It is clear that, in strong, compact soils, vegetable fertilizers capable of lightening and dividing the earth will be the best; in those that are burning, cold fertilizers will be more suitable; in damp, cool soils, detritus of animal matter and powders will produce more effect, etc. It is up to the grower's intelligence to decide which fertilizers to use. It's up to the grower's intelligence to choose the most appropriate fertilizers for his land: the essential thing is to use them only when they are well consumed. Orange trees are only smoked in the months of September and October, or February and March, at ploughing time. A circular pit is dug around the tree, a foot or eighteen inches from the trunk, and eight or ten inches deep; it is filled with suitable fertilizers, and covered with the soil from the digging. Plant fertilizers are usually used in autumn, and animal manures in spring. The age and vigor of the trees, as well as the exhaustion of the soil, should be taken into account in this essential operation. Watering should be carried out whenever the shrubs appear to need it, which is perfectly recognizable by the leaf, but moderately, and with clear water at about atmospheric temperature. Watering is only necessary from the first days of June until September, and then only when the temperature remains at twenty degrees and above daily, GRENADIER. (Punica granatum; L.) D. From near Tunis. This tree, which often reaches twenty or twenty-five feet in regions where it grows in the open ground, usually remains a shrub when grown in crates in northern France. Its beautiful red flowers are followed by fruits the size of a fist and larger, with hard, brittle rind, enclosing a red, liquid pulp, mixed with seeds of a pleasantly sweet, acidic flavor. GROWING. Pomegranates are not extremely sensitive to cold, and even overwinter quite well in the open ground around Paris, provided they are sheltered with straw; however, they only produce fruit below 43' degree of latitude. They are propagated from seeds sown in the spring and treated like orange trees, from cuttings and marcottes by strangulation using ordinary methods, and from the numerous offshoots that grow at the base. They are grown in the same way as oranges, and do well in substantial, dry, warm soils. They are pruned and trained as described on page 256. FIGHER CUITIVÍ. (Ficus carica; L.) b. Native. This tree, growing from fifteen to twenty-five feet high, produces delicious fruits twice a year, maugéa fresh, very sweet and recommended in certain cases in medicine when dried out. The wood of the fig tree is white, soft and mellow, and serves almost no purpose; however, locksmiths and gunsmiths sometimes use it to rub and polish their work, because being spongy and soft, it easily takes oil and emery. In warm countries such as Provence, Languedoc, Italy and Spain, a large number of fig tree varieties are grown, but not all succeed above latitude 44. We will mark with an asterisk those varieties that can be grown advantageously in the average climates of France. We have taken the description of all cultivated varieties from M. Loiseleur des Lonchamps' excellent monograph on the fig tree genus. A. WHITE, YELLOW OR GREEN FIGS. "Fruits as long as they are wide, or wider than they are long. 1.a FIGUE BLANCHE or grosse blanche ronde; large fruit, two inches long and about as wide, swollen towards the head, pointed at the base, smooth, pale green or whitish, sweet, very pleasant. Autumn varieties are better than spring varieties. 2.* D'ARGENTEUIL. A variety of the previous one, differing only slightly, and only in the influence of climate. The inhabitants of Argenteuil, who grow it extensively, supply it almost exclusively to the capital. 3. Fruits of the second sap are smaller. Dry soil. 34. NEGRONE; small, brownish-red fruit, pale red inside, of mediocre quality, but never attacked by insects when dry. Tont terrain. 009 35. FIGUE BRAYASQUE, bouffoz; oval fruit, abruptly tapering towards the stalk, of mediocre quality, but early and not very prone to sinking. All terrain. 36. COUCOURELLE BRUNE; brown, elongated fruit, ten to twelve Tignes in diameter by twelve or fourteen in length, early. Second-harvest figs are less elongated, smaller and less darkly colored. Dry soil, very productive tree. 37. MoUrENAoU; globular, compressed fruit, barely taller than it is wide, thick-skinned, purple-purple, with white pulp, not very pleasant. GROWING. Fig trees are not very sensitive to ordinary frosts, and can withstand Paris winters fairly well, provided a few precautions are taken. They generally like light, sandy, warm soil, but they do well everywhere. In the north, they grow vigorously in rubble, at the foot of walls in the south, and even in paved courtyards. In the south, they thrive in the most arid soils, and can even be found in rock crevices and holes in old walls. They can be propagated in several ways: 1° by seed, 2° by offshoots, 3° by marcottes, 4e by cuttings, 5° by grafting. Sowing is a long process, with uncertain results, as varieties do not reproduce freely, but it is the only way to acquire new varieties. The best species are selected, and only from those that are very ripe, or rather that have withered on the tree; they are crushed in a vase full of water, and only the seeds that sink to the bottom are collected; those that float to the surface must be discarded. In the south of France, they are simply sown in well-disturbed soil, lightly covered and watered. In no time at all, they sprout and produce young trees that require no special care. This is not the case in the north, where the seedlings must be sown on a bed and in a terrine, so that they can be stored in an orangery, at least during the first winter. The following spring, it can be placed in the ground and treated like any other plant. The offshoots are removed from the old plants before they are big enough to wear them out, but when they are strong enough not to keep their fruit waiting too long. They are usually ready for harvesting at the age of two. Picking takes place in March or April, on two-year-old fruiting branches. They are placed in the ground without further precautions, or in pots and baskets, so that when they are removed, they can be planted with the root ball. In spring, wean them from the mother, plant them in holes two feet wide and two feet deep, and give them a few sprinklings until they recover perfectly. Cuttings are taken in March or April, using two-year-old wood. Choose a branch two-and-a-half or three feet long, with one or two twigs at the bottom, which should not be cut, as they will provide the most vigorous roots. Care should also be taken with the top bud or eye, as this is the one that should provide the stem, the others giving only weak buds. Prepare a hole in the same way as for the root ball, and press the cutting into it so that no more than six inches protrude above the surface. Water, especially if the spring is dry, until the plant has recovered. With the exception of approach grafts, all the others generally used for fruit trees can be used on fig trees; however, preference should be given to split, crown and whistle grafts. Care should be taken to secure the young buds of the grafts to a stake, otherwise the wind will break them off very easily. This tree has a rather difficult recovery after planting, so young plants should never be raised in the nursery like other fruit trees, but in the field. In the southern provinces, it is extremely easy to grow: all it needs is a little ploughing, to get rid of the offshoots that grow at the base and exhaust it, and to be managed as described on page 268. In parts of France where the fig tree may be affected by frost, it must be protected from it; and to do this, two methods are employed. The first is to wrap it in straw, and the second, which we're about to describe, is to bury it; but this procedure can only be used in dry, healthy soil. As soon as there's a threat of heavy frost, clear the tree of any remaining leaves and fruit, and dig a small pit at its base; by bending the branches gently, so as not to break them, it's easy to lay them down; Then cover them with at least six inches of earth, mound up the parts you haven't been able to bend, and, if the season becomes very harsh, throw dry litter, leaves or fern on top. You can leave the tree in this state for two and a half or three months without doing it any damage. When the cold has passed, dig it up and straighten its branches. In Argenteuil, near Paris, where huge fields of fig trees are cultivated, the inhabitants have no other method of protecting them from the cold. If any are frozen, they cut them down to the root collar, along with branches that have become too strong to bend, and these trees quickly sprout new shoots that bear fruit after two years. O Figs are often prone to falling off before they reach maturity; this can be prevented by pinching the terminal bud of the branch bearing them, or by watering the tree if this effect is due to dry soil, which happens quite frequently. If you want them to be ripe a fortnight earlier than usual, or even to harvest them in late years, prick their heads with a pin dipped in oil; or, better still, when the fruit is a third of the way full, use the tip of a penknife to circle the part of the head where the male flowers are located, and remove it. The wound is covered, and the fig is no less large. An artificial method of fertilization, known as caprification, is used on wild fig trees in Greece, but as these trees are not cultivated in other parts of Europe, we will only mention it in the family history section. JAMBOSIER. EUGENIA, JAMROSE, JAMBOSE, POMME-ROSE (Eugenia jambos; L.) b. From India. This tree, ten to fifteen feet tall, can only be grown in the open ground in the south of France and in the warmer parts of Europe. It flowers from May to September, and produces a fairly large, yellowish-white, rounded fruit with a soft, sweet pulp and a pleasant rose fragrance. It thrives in free-light soils, with warm, sheltered exposure, where it is grown like the orange tree, except that it requires more watering. It is also propagated by seed, cuttings and marcottes. The French in India call its fruit pommes-roses and jambes-rosades. It would be advantageous to naturalize this tree in parts of France where the temperature permits.
